# COMMENTARY

->resolutory regarding usury, on the<-
->first chapter of the third question of the fourteenth<-
->cause, composed by Doctor Mar-<-
->tin de Azpilcueta Navarro.<-
<note>Martin de Azpilcueta (1491–1586), known as "Doctor Navarrus," was one of the most influential canon lawyers of the 16th century and a key figure in the School of Salamanca, which pioneered early economic theories.</note>

*Directed together with four others regarding the beginning of the final chapter <term>On Usury</term> <note>original: "De usuris"; a section of the Decretals of Gregory IX, the primary source of Catholic canon law at the time.</note>. And the final chapter <term>On Simony</term> <note>original: "De symonia"; the sin of buying or selling sacred things or church offices.</note>. And the Chapter <term>Not in inflicting</term> <note>original: "Non in inferenda"; a specific legal text regarding the use of force, found in the 23rd question of Gratian's Decree.</note>, 23rd question, 3rd. And the final Chapter, 14th, final question.*

->los, Prince of Castile, and of many o-<-
->ther and very great Kingdoms,<-
->our Lord.<-

->in his <term>Manual for Con-<-

<summary>This title page introduces Martin de Azpilcueta's legal treatise on usury and ecclesiastical law, dedicated to Prince Don Carlos of Spain. It serves as an explanatory supplement to his famous "Manual for Confessors," focusing on the ethics of money lending and religious office.</summary>

--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page and contains the legal and ecclesiastical authorizations for the work. It establishes the author's copyright-like protections through a Papal Privilege and local episcopal approval, ensuring his intellectual labor is protected across Christendom.</meta>

# Apostolic Privilege granted to the Author so that
->no one may print or sell his works without his con-<-
->sent within seven years, under penalty of excom-<-
->munication <term>latæ sentenciæ</term>.<-
<note>*Latæ sentenciæ* refers to a penalty that is "automatically" incurred the moment a law is broken, without needing a formal trial or decree.</note>

->*To our beloved son Martin de Azpilcueta, Doctor of Decrees, currently presiding over the*<-
->*Primary Chair of Canon Law in the University of Coimbra.*<-
<note>The University of Coimbra in Portugal was one of the oldest and most prestigious universities in Europe, where Azpilcueta taught for many years.</note>
->POPE PAUL III.<-

Beloved son, greetings and Apostolic blessing. Since, as you have explained to us, you have written and compiled several lectures on the volumes of the <term>Decree and Decretals</term> <note>These were the foundational textbooks of medieval Canon Law (church law).</note>—not without the greatest labor and sleepless nights—to the praise of the Divine name, by whose help you believe you have consulted and advanced the cause of literature and the Christian Republic, and since you intend to bring these to light and have them printed very soon at your own expense; a humble petition was made to us on your behalf that we might deign, out of Apostolic kindness, to grant that such lectures cannot be printed without your consent, so that you may enjoy the fruit of your vigils. We, wishing to favor you with special grace and being inclined toward such petitions, grant to you by Apostolic Authority through these presents—as a special favor—that for a period of seven years, to be calculated from the day you have these lectures printed, no other person established throughout the entire Christian world may print these lectures or any part of them, or cause them to be printed, or have and hold them once printed in their home or elsewhere, nor sell them, or give them as a loan, gift, or otherwise. We strictly forbid this in virtue of holy obedience and under penalty of excommunication to be incurred by the very act if it is done to the contrary, applying to each and every person of whatever status, grade, order, and condition they may be, even those acting with Apostolic authority or faculty throughout the world: they shall not presume to print, cause to be printed, sell, or give away these lectures or any part of them without your express consent and license during the said seven years. This is notwithstanding any Apostolic constitutions and ordinances, or the statutes and customs of any provinces and places, even if strengthened by oath, Apostolic confirmation, or any other weight, nor any privileges, indults, and Apostolic letters granted to any provinces, persons, Universities, and Colleges even by us and the Apostolic See under any wordings or forms, including those with clauses that revoke previous revocations. To all these things—even if specific and individual mention of them or their entire contents should have been made word-for-word rather than through general clauses—we specifically and expressly revoke them for this occasion only, while they remain in force for other matters. Given at Rome, at St. Peter's, under the <term>Ring of the Fisherman</term>. <note>The Ring of the Fisherman is the official signet ring used by the Pope to seal private or administrative documents known as "Briefs."</note> The 8th day of January, 1553. In the 9th year of our Pontificate. L. de Torres.
<note>There appears to be a chronological discrepancy in the original printing: Pope Paul III died in 1549. The year 1553 and the 9th year of a pontificate would correspond more closely to Pope Julius III, or the 1543 date for Paul III.</note>

->Pedro de Illanes, Scholar of Oviedo, Licentiate in Decrees, official,<-
->and Vicar General in the Church and Bishopric of Salamanca. Greetings to the Reader.<-

We have seen the five resolutory commentaries on as many chapters, composed by the most learned doctor Martin de Azpilcueta of Navarre, and we grant him the power to print them and, once printed, to publish them, by the authority of the most illustrious and most reverend Lord Francisco Manrique, for whom we act as Pro-Bishop. Furthermore, we forbid everyone from printing them or selling them once printed without the permission of the aforesaid doctor, under the penalty of excommunication which we carry out in these writings according to Canon Law, the same having been preceded by a triple warning, as fully as we are able. Given at Salamanca, the 7th day before the <term>Kalends of August</term> <note>July 26th.</note>, in the year of our Lord 1556. Licentiate P. de Illanes.

<summary>This page presents two legal authorizations: a Papal Privilege from Rome protecting Martin de Azpilcueta's work from unauthorized copying for seven years, and a local licensing approval from the ecclesiastical authorities in Salamanca.</summary>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal dedication of the work to Prince Don Carlos, the son of King Philip II of Spain. Following the legal protections shown on the previous page, the author, Martin de Azpilcueta (popularly known as "Doctor Navarro"), now establishes his personal and professional relationship with the Spanish Royal family, transitioning from his long career in Portugal back to his native Spain.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<sig>A 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: Commentaries</meta>

# To the very high and very powerful Lord Don Carlos, Prince of Castile, and of many other great Kingdoms. Our Lord, Doctor Martin de Azpilcueta Navarro, wishes sovereign glory, both temporal and eternal.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'A' at the beginning of the text, featuring ornate floral or vine-like patterns within a rectangular border.</image-desc>
Although I well know, very high and very powerful prince and Lord, that these five Commentaries are not such high and mature fruit as is fitting to present to Your Highness, nor to humbly beg you, as I do, to authorize them by receiving them with your Royal kindness; nevertheless, I have been emboldened to do so primarily by that very noble humanity with which, at the end of last Lent, you favored me by asking many things about my Order of Saint Mary of Roncesvalles, <note>Roncesvalles is a famous Augustinian monastery and hospital in the Pyrenees, a key stop on the Way of Saint James.</note> and about my profession, and what I did in the Kingdoms of Portugal while I was there, and what I was doing then in these realms of Your Highness since I arrived. You signaled to me that you would be pleased to see the *Manual for Confessors and Penitents* <note>original: "Manual de Confessores y penitentes." This was Azpilcueta's most famous work, a best-selling guide for priests on how to hear confessions and judge sins.</note> with the additions I told you I was then making. It seemed to me that seeing it would be more pleasing to Your Highness if I dedicated these Commentaries to you, in which answers are given to many questions that Your Highness might ask regarding its contents—you being such a skillful imitator in this regard of Cyrus, that great Prince of the Persians, who was so highly praised for it by Xenophon. <note>Xenophon was an ancient Greek historian who wrote the "Cyropaedia," a biography of Cyrus the Great used in the Renaissance as a manual for how a prince should behave.</note>

I was also incited to this because God showed me favor in the Kingdoms of Portugal, where, by the command of your most glorious grandparents—the Emperor and Empress, forever August—I served by lecturing on the <term>sacred canons</term> <note>The "sacred canons" refer to Canon Law, the legal system of the Catholic Church.</note> for nearly twenty years. To your other most Christian grandparents, the most wise grandfathers of those Kingdoms, I was the first to offer printed fruit from their new University of Coimbra to their Highnesses, their Prince and Princess, and even to the Infantes who live today. Thus, I have endeavored not to be the last in offering to Your Highness, my natural Prince and lord, some work from this, your very ancient University of Salamanca, since I was occupied in composing and printing some of my works here. Here, before I went to Portugal, I served as <term>Primary Professor</term>. <note>original: "Cathedratico de prima." The "Prima" chair was the most prestigious and highest-paid academic position in a Spanish university, so named because the lectures were held at the "first hour" of the day.</note>

My daring was further aided by the fact that these works emerged from the forge differently than I intended—contrary to what the Poet said about "singing for jars"—and in the number of five. This gave me hope that they would please Your Highness simply by bringing to your memory those <term>five wounds</term> <note>The "Five Wounds" refer to the piercings Christ received during the Crucifixion (hands, feet, and side), a central devotion in 16th-century Spain and Portugal.</note> of your supremely beloved Jesus Christ our Lord. This may give occasion for Your Highness, from this moment forward, to close the doors of your royal breast to everything that the *Manual* briefly tells you in each matter is mortal poison. May you begin to conceive most just purposes to finally exile from your Kingdoms the whirlpools of <term>usury</term> <note>Usury: the practice of charging excessive or illegal interest on loans, which the Church then considered a grave sin.</note> along with the leeches of <term>illicit exchanges</term>, <note>Azpilcueta was a pioneer of the "School of Salamanca," which developed early economic theories. "Illicit exchanges" refers to unethical currency manipulation.</note> and the excommunicated <term>simonies</term> <note>Simony: the act of buying or selling church offices or spiritual things.</note> which three of the said Commentaries address. May you also form the strongest resolutions to open your heart to the defense of your most faithful subjects, and of their honors and estates, which the other two address.

And above all, may you form the most generous and heroic resolutions, conforming to your natural and inherited magnanimity and high spirit, to imitate your ancestors. I speak both of those who adorned themselves with the <term>five quinas</term> <note>The "Quinas" are the five blue shields on the Portuguese flag, which traditionally represent the five wounds of Christ.</note> and those who adorned themselves with castles, chains, and such varied lions and bars and other insignias <note>These are heraldic symbols of the Spanish kingdoms: Castles (Castile), Chains (Navarre), Lions (León), and Bars (Aragon).</note> in defending, exalting, and extending through Europe, Africa, and Asia the honor and glory of the said five wounds. Consider as your sovereign insignia that of the most glorious cross, upon which those wounds were received by that eternal Prince. May He confirm the temporal princedom of Your Highness with His sovereign spirit; and, uniting your royal heart with His divine one, may He unite the hearts of all Christian Princes with yours. May He constitute Your Highness, as is expected, as His great and most fortunate captain against the demons, and any demoniacal appetites, and men who are capital enemies of both of you, so that Your Highness in Him, and He through Your Highness, may both always conquer them and triumph over them on earth and in heaven.
->Amen.<-

<summary>In this dedication, Martin de Azpilcueta presents his "Five Commentaries" to Prince Don Carlos, linking his academic service in Portugal and Salamanca to the moral and economic reform of the Spanish Empire. He uses the religious symbolism of the "Five Wounds" to frame his legal teachings on usury, simony, and the duties of a prince.</summary>
<keywords>Martin de Azpilcueta, Don Carlos, Manual for Confessors, University of Salamanca, University of Coimbra, usury, simony, Five Wounds of Christ, economic ethics, Canon Law</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the formal dedication to Prince Carlos into the actual substance of the work. The author, Martin de Azpilcueta, explains that while revising his famous "Manual for Confessors," he found it necessary to expand on the complex legal and moral issues of usury and simony. He frames these additions as five distinct commentaries, beginning here with a study of the Church Fathers on the nature of lending.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>Resolutory Commentary on Usu-</header>

# ry regarding the first chapter.
### 14.q.3. <note>This refers to the "Decretum Gratiani," the foundational collection of Canon Law. Specifically, Case 14, Question 3.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial "P" depicting a scene with two figures, one seated at a lectern or desk, possibly a scholar or a saint, and another figure nearby.</image-desc>BECAUSE in the Revision of the Manual for this third edition, some necessary things have occurred to us for greater clarity and defense in the matter of usury, exchanges, simony, theft, and defenses. These are matters that we noted while reading, repeating, and annotating the fourteenth Case and the titles on simony and usury, along with other chapters, in the year 1532—when this most celebrated University of Salamanca, with very distinguished honor, favored us with its Chair of the *Decretum* <note>The "Decretum" was the primary textbook for studying Canon Law.</note>—and a year or two before then. These additions could not fit in their proper places without disrupting the numbering. Therefore, we agreed to create five brief commentaries and refer back to them. The first is on the chapter *Si feneraveris* <note>Latin: "If you have lent."</note> 14. q. 3., which is by our great father and most glorious doctor St. Augustine. The other two are on the final chapter of 14. q. final. and the chapter *Non in inferenda* 23. q. 3., which are by that no less glorious doctor, most holy interpreter, and great friend, St. Jerome. The other two are on the final chapter "On Simony" and the beginning of the last chapter "On Usury," which are by Gregory IX—a very renowned Pope for many reasons (one of which is having canonized the most glorious St. Dominic and St. Francis). To the correction of his sovereign See I submit myself, and I humbly implore the favor of the said four marvelous Patriarchs and the guidance of St. Thomas <note>Thomas Aquinas.</note>, the excellent light of his doctrine and children, to declare with few and clear words many obscure sentences, for the service and glory of our Lord Jesus Christ and his five wounds—may these five commentaries make us feel them. Amen.

---

### 14.q.3. cap. 1. Augustine. On Psalm 36.
#### in the third sermon.

> If you have lent at interest to a man, that is, if you have given your money as a loan, from whom you expect more than you gave: not money alone, but anything more than you gave, whether it be wheat, or wine, or oil, or whatever else; if you expect to receive more than you gave, you are a usurer, and in this you are to be disapproved, not praised. <note>original: "Si fœneraueris homini, id eſt, ſi mutuo dederis pecuniam tuã..."</note>

->S U M M A R Y<-

A usurer is one who expects something more than what he lent. num. 1.

<margin>
a In the title "on usury."
b In the Code of Justinian and the Digest, as well as in the Decretals, and in this same Case, following question, and often elsewhere. For although all the aforementioned texts condemn usury, they say that any profit beyond the principal is usury.
</margin>
IF you give at interest to a man: That is, if you lend money to one from whom you expect more than 1 you gave—not only money, but even some other thing more than you gave: whether it be wheat, whether wine, whether oil, whether any other thing—if you expect to take more than you gave, a usurer <term>logrero</term> <note>"Logrero" is an older Spanish term for a person who profits excessively or a usurer.</note> you are, and worthy of being reproved and not praised in this. 

First, let us note the summary intention of this chapter, which according to that great doctor Gratian, Compiler of this great book (which we call the *Decretum*), is: Whoever takes more than he has given, desires usury. Although (in our view) for many reasons that can be gathered from the text, a better summary would be: Whoever expects more than he lent (whatever it may be) is a usurer. St. Jerome says the same regarding Ezekiel, referred to in the following chapter in those words: "Whatever it may be," etc. <note>original: "Quicquid illud ſit, &c."</note> Whatever it may be, if it is more than you gave, it is usury. St. Ambrose says the same regarding Tobias, referred to in chapter 3 of this same question: That what is taken beyond the loan, even if it is not money but something to eat—such as a jar of wine from the tavern-keeper, or a <term>hila</term> <note>original: "hila" - a specific type of stuffed intestine or sausage link.</note> (which is a certain stuffed intestine) from the butcher—is usury. The Council of Agde determines the same in the final chapter of this same question, with which many other texts agree.

<meta>catchword: SUMMA-</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta introduces five new commentaries on usury and simony to supplement his "Manual for Confessors," beginning with a strict definition of usury based on St. Augustine. He argues that usury constitutes any expectation of receiving back more than was lent, whether in money or goods like food and wine.</summary>
<keywords>usury, Martin de Azpilcueta, Canon Law, Decretum Gratiani, St. Augustine, University of Salamanca, lending, interest, restitution, simony</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues Martin de Azpilcueta’s commentary on the legal and moral definitions of usury. While the previous page introduced the project as a revision of his "Manual for Confessors," this page dives into the etymology of "usury" and the legal distinctions between different types of loans (Commodatum versus Mutuum).</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>Regarding Chapter 1, Case 14, Question 5</header>

### SUMMARY.

The word "Usury" and what it signifies. num. 2.
The esteemed Doctor Soto. num. 2.

<margin>2</margin>
<margin>a According to Cicero.</margin>
<margin>b *Pro Rabirio Postumo*: "Usury of this light," etc.</margin>
<margin>c See Charles Dumoulin in the book *On Commerce*, at the beginning.</margin>
<margin>d Book 6, question 1, article 1, *On Justice and Right*.</margin>
SECONDLY, † let us note that although this word **Usury** in Latin (according to its original meaning) signifies the use of any thing <sup>a</sup>: commonly here, and in other texts, glosses, and doctors (both Theologians and Canonists), it signifies the profit taken from a loan where the ownership passes to the one who receives it. Thus, the Spaniard calls it <term>logro</term> <note>A Spanish term for profit or gain, often associated with usury.</note>, from the Latin <term>Lucrum</term> <note>original: "Lucrum"; meaning profit or gain.</note>, and in Greek <term>Tokos</term> <note>original: "Tocu"; literally meaning "offspring" or "childbirth," reflecting the idea that money "gives birth" to more money.</note>, which signifies birth, because the thing lent gives birth to it. The Hebrew uses another word signifying a "morsel" or a "bite," <note>original: "bocado, y mordedura"; referring to the Hebrew word *Neshek*, which means a bite.</note> with which the lender bites the one to whom he lends. This is written at greater length by a modern author <sup>c</sup>, though he infers from this last point things which (in our view) he ought not have. We also note the illustrious doctor <term>Soto</term> <sup>d</sup> <note>referring to Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a key figure in the School of Salamanca.</note>, whose profound knowledge, accompanied by great virtues and a holy life, is no small ornament to the illustrious Dominican order.

### SUMMARY.

Two types of loans, both of which must be gratuitous. num. 3.
Manifest usury, and the types of loans in which it is found. num. 3.
Cloaked or hidden usury in every contract. num. 4.

<margin>3</margin>
<margin>a Chapter 17, n. 282.</margin>
<margin>b According to the gloss on chapter 1, *On Commodatum*, and the entire title in the Digest *On Commodatum*.</margin>
<margin>c As gathered from the text and glosses of Law 2, at the beginning, and § 1, in the Digest *On Credited Things*.</margin>
<margin>d In Law 2, § *Appellata*, *On Credited Things*.</margin>
<margin>e And many other places in this and the following question, and the chapter *Consuluit*, and others regarding usury.</margin>
THIRDLY, we note † that all loans must be <term>gracious</term> <note>meaning "gratuitous" or "free," given as a favor without charge.</note>, because there are only two types of loans. One is that which in Latin is called <term>Commodatum</term> <note>A loan of a specific, non-consumable object that must be returned in itself.</note>: in this, the ownership of the thing lent does not pass to the person who takes it. Rather, the very same thing in its specific form (which the scholars call the *individual*, and the jurists call the *species*) must be returned to the lender. An example is the loan of a mule for riding, which was discussed in the *Manual* <sup>a</sup>; this must be gratuitous <sup>b</sup>. The other type of loan is that in which ownership passes to the person to whom it is lent. This is not returned as the same specific individual item, but as another thing of its kind—which scholars call the species <sup>c</sup> and jurists call the genus. This is called in Latin <term>Mutuum</term> <note>A loan of fungible goods (like money or grain) where the borrower consumes the items and returns an equal amount of the same kind.</note>: because through it, "mine becomes yours" (*meum tuum*), as <term>Ulpian</term> <sup>d</sup> <note>A famous Roman jurist.</note> said. This must be done gratuitously according to this chapter <sup>e</sup>.

<margin>4</margin>
<margin>f Institutes, *In what ways an obligation is contracted*, at the beginning.</margin>
<margin>g Chapter *In Civitate* regarding usury; chapter *Ad Nostram* regarding sales.</margin>
<margin>h In the *Manual*, chapter 17, n. 223, and chapter 23, n. 78.</margin>
¶ FOURTHLY, † let us note from those words *Mutuum dederis* <note>Latin: "You shall have given a loan."</note> that usury is only committed in the second type of loan mentioned above, called <term>Mutuum</term>. From this it follows that, since this loan is clearly found only in things consumed by use and traded by weight, count, or measure—such as money, gold, silver, wine, oil, bread, wheat, and similar things <sup>f</sup>—manifest usury is also only found in the trading of such things. It also follows that, since the said loan can be found hidden within the trading of all things, so too can **hidden and cloaked usury** be found in all of them. Indeed, it is found in all those transactions where one takes more than the highest **just price** because of waiting longer for payment, or where one gives less than the lowest just price because of paying in advance, before the item or the use for which it is given is received <sup>g</sup>. 

As an example of the first: I sell you some houses whose highest just price is one hundred ducats, but because I give them to you on credit for a year, I sell them to you for one hundred and ten. This is a covered loan and hidden usury. For it amounts to the same as if you had given me the hundred ducats as a cash loan, and I lent them back to you for a year so that at the end you would return one hundred and ten. 

As an example of the second: I lease to you or buy the fruits of your benefice, or your inherited estate, or the rent of a certain town, village, or military commandery <note>original: "maestrazo"; the office or jurisdiction of a Grand Master of a military order.</note>, the lowest just price of which is one thousand ducats. Because I pay you a year in advance, I lease them from you for nine hundred. For it amounts to the same as if I gave you nine hundred now so that you would give me a thousand a year from now. However, we did not say without reason "more than the highest just price," because, as we said elsewhere <sup>h</sup>, taking more than the *lowest* just price when selling on credit is not usury or sin. We also said "less than the lowest just price," because giving less than the *highest* just price for an advance payment is not usury or sin.

<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>Catchword: SVMA- (Summary)</meta>

<summary>This page defines usury through etymology and distinguishes between two types of loans: "Commodatum" (lending a specific object) and "Mutuum" (lending fungible goods). It explains that usury technically applies to the latter and can be hidden in standard commercial contracts through price manipulation based on the timing of payment.</summary>
<keywords>usury, loan, mutuum, commodatum, just price, credit, hidden usury, Domingo de Soto, etymology, legal definitions</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and theological definition of usury from page 5. Having discussed the etymology of the word, Azpilcueta now provides a formal definition and distinguishes between material profit and spiritual gain.</meta>

# Resolutory Commentary on Usury.

<page-num>6</page-num>

->S U M M A R Y.<-

<margin>2, 3, & 4 of this Case, question 9, etc.</margin>
What usury is, by its definition. num. 5.

<margin>Since chapter *Ne hoc*, chapter *Quid dicam* of the same Case, question 9, etc.</margin>
The sin of usury, what it is. num. 5.

<margin>He consulted, and others on usury. Geoffrey, Hostiensis, and others in the summaries, and in book 4, distinction 15, and in the rubrics on usury. cf. Argument from law 1, Digest, on testaments, and law 1, on gifts, and those which Bartolus and others in law 1, Digest, on acquiring possession. d according to that psalm, *Blessed is the man who is merciful and lends*, and that of the Gospel, *you shall receive a hundredfold*, etc. e Although it may be metaphorical, Luke 19; Thomas Aquinas, *Summa Theologica* II-II, question 78, article 1, reply 1. f Final gloss on chapter *Conquestus* regarding recovered usury, see also chapter 2 and chapter *Salubriter* on usury.</margin>
Usury is not spiritual gain, or quasi-spiritual gain, such as friendship, etc. num. 6.

FIFTH. † We note that from the intent of this chapter one can gather the definition of usury and <term>logro</term> <note>Spanish for "gain" or "profit," specifically used in the context of usurious interest.</note>. Although many have defined it in many ways according to the meaning it takes in this subject, the clearest definition—and the one most consistent with the words of our text, many others<sup>a</sup>, the glosses, and the doctors<sup>b</sup> (both theologians and canonists)—seems to us to be this: **Usury, or illicit profit, is gain that is by its nature appraisable in money, which is primarily taken by reason of a loan, whether overt or disguised.** And the sin of usury is to take, or to desire, such gain. We used the word "gain" as a more general term than the one being defined, which every good definition requires at its beginning<sup>c</sup>. Thus it is that all usury or <term>logro</term> is gain, but not every gain is usury or <term>logro</term>. We said "appraisable in money" because any gain taken from a loan is usury. For the virtue, merit, and grace earned before God is a very great gain<sup>d</sup> when lending when, how, and for the reason one ought, but it is not properly usury<sup>e</sup>. And would that people valued this spiritual gain as much as they value ten or twelve percent interest, for greed blinds them and takes away their sight of its great value. † We added "by its nature" because the gain of friendship and favor earned by lending to a recipient is not usury. For although many would give much money for the friendship and favor of many, friendship is not, by its nature, appraisable in money. We added "primarily" because the intention is illicit when it is focused on gain even in a less primary or secondary way, as we shall soon explain further. We added "by reason of the loan" because the gain taken by reason of true interest <note>original: "interesse"; here meaning legal compensation for loss or risk rather than profit for profit's sake.</note> or for another respect is not usury<sup>f</sup>. We added "overt or disguised" because of what was said in the third notable point. From this it follows that this chapter is not to be understood as referring to spiritual gain, nor even to that which is quasi-spiritual (such as friendship and any other thing not appraisable in money by its nature), but rather to temporal gain which is primarily expected by reason of the loan.

---

->S U M M A R Y.<-

<margin>a Clementines, 1, on usury.</margin>
Illicit usury is a mortal sin, and to say the contrary is heresy. num. 7.

<margin>b In the chapter *Quia* on usury.</margin>
Usury is forbidden, even in the New Law, even specifically, and even mental usury. num. 7.

<margin>c In the chapter *Super eo* of the same title.</margin>
St. Thomas Aquinas greatly respected the canons, which was a mercy from God. n. 9.

Charles Dumoulin is very suspicious of heresy. num. 10 & 11.

<margin>d In Counsel 1. 2.</margin>
SIXTH. † We note from those words *In hoc improbandus* <note>original Latin: "In this you are to be reproved."</note> that the gain from usury is illicit gain, and by its very nature a mortal sin, so much so that to say the contrary is to speak heresy<sup>a</sup>. For it is not only a mortal sin according to human canon law, but also according to natural and divine law in the Old and New Testaments. This was the view of the Lateran Council<sup>b</sup> and Alexander III<sup>c</sup>, regardless of what Alessandro d’Imola<sup>d</sup> and others he cites may say. This is because it is forbidden by the Seventh Commandment of the Decalogue, which is "Thou shalt not steal," given in the Old Law<sup>e</sup> and confirmed in the New<sup>f</sup>. By this commandment, all illicit usurpation of another's property (such as usury) is forbidden. & Also because it is against natural justice to take something for what is not yours (neither in terms of direct or useful ownership, nor usufruct, nor use, nor possession, nor any other servitude). In usury, profit is taken for the money, wheat, or other thing lent, the ownership, possession, and use of which passes to the person to whom they are lent<sup>h</sup>, and ceases to belong to the lender. Furthermore, usury is specifically forbidden in many parts<sup>i</sup> of the Old Testament; and since that prohibition was not ceremonial or judicial, but moral, it remains in effect in the New<sup>k</sup>. It is also specifically forbidden in the Gospel.

<margin>e Exodus 22, regarding which see the previous chapter.</margin>
<margin>f Matthew 19.</margin>
<margin>g Chapter *Penale*, Case 14, question 5.</margin>
<margin>h Law 2, section *Appellatu*, Digest, on things credited; Institutes, on the ways obligations are contracted, at the beginning.</margin>
<margin>i Deuteronomy 23; Ezekiel 18; and Psalms 43 and 71.</margin>
<margin>k Final section, distinction 9.</margin>
<margin>l Luke 6, there: "lend, expecting nothing in return."</margin>

<summary>This page defines usury as any material profit primary to a loan and distinguishes it from spiritual benefits like friendship. Azpilcueta argues that usury is a mortal sin and a form of theft forbidden by natural, divine, and canon law.</summary>

He strongly rebukes contemporary jurists like Charles Dumoulin and Protestant reformers like Melanchthon for suggesting that moderate interest could be legal.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal analysis of usury, shifting from general scriptural prohibitions to a technical classification of usury into "real" and "mental" types, followed by a critique of contemporary banking practices that circumvent these laws.</meta>

# Resolutory Commentary on Usury.
<meta>catchword: use of</meta>

<margin>m. Chapter *Debitores* regarding usury. v. last chapter regarding usury, and many others.
n. According to the opinion of all in the chapter *Consului* regarding usury, and the last chapter regarding simony.
a. Chapter *Periculose*, chapter *Homicidiorum*, and chapter *Noli* regarding penance, distinction 1.
b. Chapter *Si propter ea*, chapter *Si cui* regarding penance, distinction 1.
c. In the commentary on the last chapter regarding simony, note 3, which we are now composing in the revised Manual.</margin>
**Point VI.** † We note from that word "Expenses" repeated twice, joining with it other texts <margin>m</margin>, that usury is divided into **actual usury** and **mental usury**. **Actual usury** is usury that is taken by a tacit or express pact, whether public or secret. **Mental usury** is usury that is taken without an express or tacit pact, by the sole principal intention <margin>n</margin> of taking something in exchange for lending. From this it follows that we do not call it "mental usury" for the same reason that we commonly call other sins "mental": for we call others mental because they are interior sins of the will without speech or action. We call **mental homicide** the will <margin>a</margin> to kill, without death following; **mental theft** the will to steal <margin>b</margin>, without the theft following; and it is both mental and actual when one and the other concur. However, we do not commonly call it "mental usury" simply for being the will to commit usury without the act following, but rather for being usury that is taken without an express or tacit pact, through the sole principal mental intention that the lender has in lending: that they return to him something more than what he lends. From this it is inferred that there are two species of mental usury. One is the aforementioned. And the other is the will to take usury, even if it is not taken: which is such a mental sin as is found in all those sins which are completed by an external act. It follows † that some usury will be called "solely mental" even though the act of receiving the usury follows. Suppose I lend you ten with the principal intention that you return eleven to me, without an express or tacit pact, public or secret; you return the eleven to me and I receive them: I commit solely mental usury. ¶ And it should be known that just as no sin of usury, however mortal it may be, obliges one to restitution if nothing is taken, so all usury received (even if it be solely mental) obliges one to restitution, although mental simony <note>Simony is the sin of buying or selling sacred things or church offices.</note> does not oblige one to it, as we prove elsewhere <margin>c</margin>.

### Forbidden and cursed usury: but it is used more than ever. num. 14.

<margin>d. Distinction 1, law *Eos*.
e. Which Bartolo Socini shows diligently and copiously in the law *Si haeres*. 6. Item. *Digest* regarding the Falcidian law, with which Budé agrees in *De Asse* and Alciato in book 3, *dispunctio*, and other more recent authors.
f. Law *Ut nullo*, Code regarding usury, and law *Placuit*, *Digest* on the same.
g. In the commentary on the last chapter regarding usury, which we are composing together with this in the revised Manual.</margin>
**Point VIII.** † We gather from this chapter, and from its poor observance, that it is a pity to consider on one hand that all of Christendom holds usury to be illicit and considers him a heretic who would say it is licit; and that even those which the civil law <margin>d</margin> <note>Referring to Roman Civil Law, which allowed certain interest rates that Canon Law (Church law) forbade.</note> permits cannot be taken with a good conscience; and on the other hand to see that throughout Christendom much higher rates are taken than those. For the highest usury that the civil law permits is that which they call the **centesima**, which is that which in a hundred months equals the loan <margin>e</margin>, and is worth one percent each month, and twelve percent each year. And this is not permitted except to those who lend and insure; that is, those who lend money or merchandise to be carried over the sea at the lender's risk. And to other merchants it permits two-thirds of the *centesima*: that is, eight percent per year. And to other common men half of it, which is six percent per year. And to the illustrious <note>Members of the nobility or high-ranking officials.</note> a third, which is four percent per year. But now, sometimes ten and even twelve percent is paid from "fair to fair" <note>Financial settlements made during the great seasonal trade fairs.</note>, there being three or more fairs in the year, which comes out to thirty percent or more. And previously, usury upon usury <margin>f</note> <note>Compound interest.</note> was not taken, but now "re-exchanges of exchanges" <note>A "re-exchange" (*recambio*) was a banking maneuver involving bills of exchange to hide interest charges.</note> are. I know well they will answer us that this is not taken as usury, but as **interest** or **exchange**; but we believe that all those who change the name do not change the essence. And regarding exchanges, we say elsewhere <margin>g</margin> what we think of them.

->SUMMARY<-

Usury seems better defined above than in two other places. num. 15.
Usury is taking something for the good deed of lending, even if it is not taken for the use of the thing lent. num. 16.
Mortal usury is lending for notable gain, and venial usury, etc. num. 17.
Simoniacal usury is lending in order to obtain a benefice <note>An ecclesiastical office or the income from it.</note>. num. 17.
Usury exists without a pact, by the will to do it. num. 18.

<margin>a. In the rubric regarding usury and in the summary of this case.
b. Soto, book 6, question 1, article 1, *De iustitia et iure*.
c. *Secunda Secundae*, question 78, article 1.</margin>
**Point IX.** † From all this it follows that not without cause did we say that the definition given above seemed clearer and more conforming to the law than two others. For it seems that the one we once gave <margin>a</margin>—namely, that it is the "will to take profit by reason of a loan"—though good, does not define usury itself, but rather the sin committed in desiring it. And because another definition, which a celebrated doctor <margin>b</margin> <note>Likely referring to the theologian Domingo de Soto.</note> has since given, new and cautious, which he may have taken from a saying of St. Thomas <margin>c</margin>: that usury is the price of the...

17 It also follows † that although lending is commonly a matter of "counsel" <note>An act that is spiritually recommended but not strictly required by law.</note> when there is no extreme necessity, the act of *not* primarily expecting to receive more than what is lent is a matter of "precept" <note>A mandatory religious or legal command.</note>. However, it is not a mortal sin when the amount expected is small, just as the theft of an insignificant amount is considered a more venial sin ᵍ. It also follows that lending primarily to obtain a spiritual benefit can be called usury; for although a benefit may be a thing beyond estimation by law ʰ, it is not so by its nature, since it is the right to receive certain fruits and rents ⁱ. Similarly, all sacred things are said to be beyond estimation because the law removes them from human commerce ᵏ. Although this sin is also <term>simony</term> <note>The sin of buying or selling sacred or spiritual things, named after Simon Magus.</note> ˡ, in our opinion, it effectively constitutes two sins, or one sin with a necessary circumstance that must be confessed ᵐ.

-> S U M M A R Y <-

Usury is not lending with a less-than-principal intention of gain, against the view of some; but it is lending with the principal intention of gain, against the view of others. Number 19.
A less-than-principal goal can be something that a principal goal cannot be. Number 20.

<margin>
a. Book 6, question 1, article 2, regarding justice and law.
b. Question 2 is in the chapter *Consuluit*, regarding usury, which Panormitanus and all others there and elsewhere magnify.
c. Cited above.
</margin>

19 The tenth point † which also follows from what has been said is that for usury to exist, there must be an express or tacit pact, or the principal aim of the lending must be gain. Otherwise, even if the secondary and less-than-principal aim is gain, it is not usury. Recently, however, the Lord Domingo de Soto ᵃ has held the opposite, saying that both intentions cause the sin of usury. Firstly, because this goes against Innocent [IV] in the first [part] and a singular gloss ᵇ received by almost all the Theological Doctors and Canonists, who establish the said distinction between principal and secondary [intentions]—except for Molinaeus ᶜ, who took the other extreme, claiming that neither the principal nor the secondary intention induces an obligation of restitution for usury without a pact.

h. Session 6, canon 31.
</margin>

20 ¶ Secondly, because Cajetan—whom Soto praises for a statement in one part ᵈ to confirm his own view—immediately in the following question ᵉ expressly holds the same view as that gloss and the common opinion. And even in that same question where he makes that statement, he supports the common view with those words: <note>original: "oculus sinister, seu spes secundaria potest dirigi circa aliquam remunerationem."</note> "*the left eye, or secondary hope, can be directed toward some remuneration*" <note>Scholastic metaphor: the "right eye" is the pure, primary intent (God/charity), while the "left eye" is the secondary, worldly hope.</note>. Indeed, if one weighs the words, it means that St. Thomas did not doubt this. Furthermore, there are texts ᶠ and glosses that quite expressly prove it is lawful to serve the Church and the prelate with a secondary (though not principal) hope that a benefice will be granted. Finally, it cannot be denied that we can often have a less-than-principal regard for many things without it being the principal one; for we can say Masses or hear divine offices principally for God, and less principally for the pittances and daily distributions ᵍ. We can serve God principally for the reward of the earth and of heaven, as the Council of Trent ʰ declares, labeling as a heretic anyone who says a just man sins when he serves God out of regard for an eternal reward; because both temporal and eternal rewards can be usury. <note>In this context, the author argues that just as hoping for a heavenly reward doesn't ruin a good deed, a secondary hope for a gift doesn't necessarily make a loan usurious.</note>

<summary>This page analyzes the role of intention in the sin of usury, arguing that while a primary intent for profit is sinful, a secondary or incidental hope for a reward does not necessarily constitute usury or require restitution.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the morality of intention when lending money. It addresses whether having a secondary hope for profit, such as gratitude or a return favor, constitutes the sin of usury. Azpilcueta relies on the authorities of Pope Adrian VI, Thomas Cajetan, and Domingo de Soto to define the boundary between a "vicious" (sinful) act and a virtuous one.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>Resolutory Commentary on Exchanges.</header>
<meta>catchword: and of the</meta>

<margin>i Quodlibet 10, column 4.</margin>
<margin>k In the repetition of the chapter *Inter verba*, question 4, number 258.</margin>
...can be the less principal and secondary end: provided that the principal end be God alone for His own sake, and because He is most worthy of being served. This is a very fine and well-founded conclusion of Adrian’s, ᵏ which we follow elsewhere, ᵏ namely: that every act of any virtue is flawed if its total or principal partial end is some temporal good. It is clear that there are few who, through their virtuous works, do not intend some temporal thing as a less principal end—such as honor, fame, glory, health, property, or the maintenance of themselves or others—and no one would dare say that all these people sin in this. Even 
<margin>a 2. Secunda, question 78, article 1.</margin>
Cajetan <note>Thomas Cajetan (1469–1534), a major Italian theologian and cardinal known for his commentaries on Thomas Aquinas.</note> himself in another place ᵃ affirms this common opinion at length, noting that he had not forgotten what he had said in that place where the aforementioned Soto <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a Spanish Dominican priest and philosopher who, like the author, was a key figure in the School of Salamanca.</note> cites him for that point, with which he confirms his own. I do indeed confess it to be true that one cannot expect profit from a loan, either principally or less principally, as something legally owed, but rather as something naturally owed by way of gratitude, and not by way of justice. If this is his meaning, we all agree: but we do not depart from the Common Opinion, by which we say that we can principally and secondarily hope for the profit of friendship and gratitude from the loan, and also principally and secondarily for the profit of money arising from that friendship and gratitude: but not as a profit principally or secondarily due by reason of the loan itself.

### SUMMARY.

<term>Mental usury</term> <note>The internal sin of intending to profit from a loan, even if no interest is explicitly charged in a contract.</note> is undone by changing the intention. num. 22.
It is not usury to hope for temporal gain from a friendship that was principally intended. num. 12.
A principal hope is not simply any hope without which one would not have lent. num. 23.
<margin>b Under the word "usury" 1. § 1. c Cajetan 2. Secunda, question 78, article 1, and in his small works, Volume 3, questions 2 and 3 on usury; and Soto, book 6, question 1, article 2 on justice and law.</margin>
Usury is that which is taken without the free will of the giver, even if the one who takes it thinks it is given freely. num. 24. But not that which is received for the payment of what is owed, provided that etc. num. 25. Or for the labor of counting or sending [the money]. num. 25.

<margin>22</margin>
The 11th: That † whoever, after lending principally for gain, recognizes their sin and changes their intention—hoping for nothing principally for having 
<margin>a In the repetition of the chapter *Inter verba*, question 3, number 284; and in the repetition of the chapter *Quando de conf.*, distinction 1, note 15, number 13.</margin>
lent, but secondarily expects and takes some token of gratitude—shall not be a <term>usurer</term> <note>Someone who charges illegal or immoral interest on a loan.</note>: because they do not expect or receive it principally for the act of lending, as Angelus <note>Angelus de Clavasio (1411–1495), author of the *Summa Angelica*, a famous manual for confessors.</note> rightly says. ᵇ
¶ That it is lawful to lend even principally (as has been said) to gain the friendship and favor of the one to whom it is lent: because this gain is not by its nature estimable in money; and it is even lawful to lend principally to gain friendship, while even principally hoping for a gain from that friendship that *is* estimable in money, as two solemn men ᶜ finely determine, which is a common conclusion. Against which some things could be considered, and others to resolve them, if the brevity aimed for in this work permitted it. From which it follows that it is never a sin to hope for some gain from the loan by way of friendship and gratitude as a gift, which arises from the free will of the receiver. ¶ That † he is not a usurer who lends with the hope that they will give him something more 
<margin>23</margin>
<margin>17. and in his additions, number 324. b According to the mind of Antoninus, part 2, title 1, chapter 7, § 9, with that which Sylvester adds under the word "Usury," 6, questions 3 and 5. c Cajetan 2. Secunda, question 78, article 1. Laurentius in the chapter *Salubriter* concerning usury. Antoninus, as above.</margin>
than what he lent: but he would not stop lending even if he knew they would return nothing more than what he gave, for such a hope is secondary and not principal. And we even say that not everyone who lends with a hope of gain without which they would not lend is a usurer: because to be a "principal end," it is not enough that the act would not be done without it, unless that end is as much or more esteemed than the other end that moves the person to act, as we have declared elsewhere more at length than others. ᵃ
<margin>24</margin>
¶ That † neither does the lender sin who does not lend principally for gain, but receives something in good faith from the one who borrowed, thinking it is given out of love and favor, even if the other gives it not so much for that reason as out of fear that if they do not give it, the lender will take back the money or not lend it again. Although if later (before spending it) the lender should realize that the debtor did not give it freely, he would be obliged to <term>restitute</term> <note>The legal and moral obligation to return something to its rightful owner.</note> that by which he found himself richer, and no more. ᵇ But if, when it was given, he presumed it was not given of free will but by constraint, he would sin by taking it, even if at the beginning he lent solely out of charity. ¶ And we add, that from the quality of what is given, and of the...

<summary>This page explores the nuances of intent in lending, distinguishing between primary motives (like God or charity) and secondary motives (like friendship or small gifts). Azpilcueta argues that a loan is not usurious if the hope for gain is secondary to the act of lending, or if the profit stems from natural gratitude rather than a legal requirement.</summary>

Et si quæstiones. de symo."</note> Since the intention is gathered from conjectures. From the Chapter "Unico" regarding renunciations in Book 6; the Law "Donations" in the Code regarding fraud and contumacy; and the Chapter "Et si quaestiones" regarding simony.

<margin>25</margin>
¶ He who cannot collect from his debtor what is rightly owed to him, and for this reason lends him more money so that the debtor may give him as much more as he owes, does not sin; this is because he takes nothing principally for having lent it, but rather because he cannot collect what is his own in any other way.<gloss>e</gloss> We add, however, that he must set things in order so that the other person is given a release from what he owed, or that the payment of his debt is recorded in such a way that neither he nor his heirs are made to pay it again, whether out of a remorse of conscience or by law.<gloss>f</gloss> Neither does it appear to be usury to receive something extra for the labor involved in counting a large quantity of small change by himself or through his servants; he does not receive this for the act of lending, but for the work of counting. Nor is it usury for someone who is far from the person to whom he lends to receive back as much more than the loan as the costs and labor of the journey amount to.<gloss>g</gloss>

<margin>
e John Major on Book 4, distinction 15, question 29, at the beginning. <note>John Major (1467–1550) was a Scottish philosopher and theologian highly influential in Spain.</note>
f Through those things said in a similar case in the *Manual for Confessors*, chapter 17, number 113.
g The same John Major, as cited above.

### S U M M A R Y

->Usury is everything taken in excess for waiting, or given in less for advancing payment in any contract, with many particular examples. Number 26.<-
->Palliated <note>Hidden or disguised under a legal cover.</note> usury is worse than the overt kind, and more common. Number 26. Doctor Montemayor <note>A contemporary jurist of the School of Salamanca.</note> is praised. Number 29.<-

<margin>26</margin>
THE TWELFTH. Every buyer, every lessor, and every exchanger who—by paying beforehand and before receiving the profit of what he buys, leases, or exchanges—gives something less than the lowest <term>just price</term> <note>The 'just price' was a central economic concept in Scholasticism, often determined by common market estimation rather than individual desire.</note> for the full payment of it, is a usurer. Such also is every seller, every hirer, or one who gives something for rent, who, for granting a longer term for payment, takes something more than the highest just price: because all the aforementioned take, or desire a gain estimable in money, principally by reason of a palliated and hidden loan. Thus, the entire definition of usury applies to this gain which these people take or wish to take, and the entire definition of a usurer applies to them.

<margin>27</margin>
¶ It follows, † how much reason there is to pray to Our Lord that He grant repentance with restitution to those who lease out bishoprics and benefices, masterships, provinces, lordships, places, taxes, <term>alcabalas</term> <note>A heavy Spanish sales tax.</note>, and other rents belonging to Kings, Bishops, Lords, and beneficiaries, or other needy individuals, for much less than the just price in exchange for advancing the payment by one year—and for even less if they advance it two years, and for less still if they advance it three, etc. How much reason there is also to pray that He do the same for those who, conversely, sell spices, cloves, pepper, cloths, silks, tapestry, books, paper, and other similar things, or oxen, cows, mares, sheep, and other livestock to those who have need of them for their sustenance, or to <term>mohatrar</term> <note>The 'Mohatra' was a sham contract where a person 'buys' an item on credit at a high price and immediately 'sells' it back to the seller for a lower cash price to obtain a loan.</note> and sell it later at a lower price; and they charge them more than the highest just price for granting them a term for payment, and they charge even more as the term grows longer.

<margin>28</margin>
¶ How much reason † also to pray that He forgive so many rich and powerful ecclesiastics and laypeople who lease lands to their laborers for more than the highest just price for extending the deadlines of their debts, or for lending them wheat to sow and eat, or money for other necessary things with a long term. By this, they compel them to work excessively and live more miserably than if they were their slaves. And even to the said rich and powerful who lease their own rents for as high a price as they can, inviting price-hikes through promises and the gains from competitive bidding and extensions of terms, making them sign outrageous waivers <note>original: "renunciaciones defaforadas," referring to legal clauses where the poor waived their rights to traditional protections or fair price laws.</note>—few of which Spain saw before our current age—without any regard for whether what they are given is more than the just price or not. Nor do they consider that those rents are worth much less with those waivers than without them, and that those rents cannot be worth as much in the hands of one who would gather and sell them virtuously, even if they amount to more when gathered and sold with much diligence and little conscience, with many lies and little truth, with many extortions of the miserable to whom the collectors sell on credit. They consume much of their small estates, lodging themselves with them and taking gifts from them, and showing little charity that does not bleed them dry.

<meta>catchword: He remembers</meta>

<summary>This page defines palliated usury as the manipulation of prices based on the timing of payment in sales, leases, and loans. Azpilcueta condemns the exploitation of the poor and laborers by wealthy individuals and clergy who use high-interest credit disguised as commercial contracts.</summary>

<header>Resolutory Commentary on Usury.</header>

<margin>29</margin>
<margin>a <note>original: "Quod nobis aliqua ex parte, tanquã quodammodo conſentientibus imputari poſſet. arg. cap. 1. Ad Rom. & c. 2. de offic. deleg. & c. Notum. 2. q. i."</note> Which could be imputed to us in some part, as if we were in some way consenting. Arguments from Romans Chapter 1, and Chapter 2 regarding the "Office of the Delegate," and the Chapter "Notum" in Question 1 of the second Case.</margin>

¶ I remember that one day that Doctor Monte Mayor—whom I always imagine shining with glory in heaven, just as I always knew him to shine with virtue on earth, and whose great successor I followed in the <term>Prima chair</term> <note>The "Prima" chair was the most prestigious and highest-paid professorship at the University, so named because the lectures were held at the "first hour" of the day.</note> though I was unworthy—I remember, then, that he and I were the only ones to vote one year in the cloister of this most celebrated University of Salamanca that the fruits of the university's rents should not be leased out as dearly as was expected. We voted this way because, without a doubt, certain <term>speculators</term> <note>The original Spanish "trampeadores" suggests those who use tricks or traps to manipulate market prices.</note> were driving up the price of certain known measures of grain to ten <term>reales</term> <note>A Spanish silver coin.</note> when it was certain that, if paid in cash at the time of harvest, they would not be worth five, nor likely more than seven at the time the university would have sold them had it not leased them. Furthermore, the labor, costs, and risks, along with the outrageous waivers of rights, ought to have diminished the price. We feared that by taking more from them than was fair, we would in some way place them under the necessity of practicing usury, cheating, and giving themselves to the devil.<a> But we were not heard.

->S U M M A R Y.<-

The Emperor and King Don Philip, along with those of his council, are worthy of blessing for the <term>pragmatic</term> <note>A "pragmatic" or "pragmatic sanction" was a sovereign's edict that had the full force of law.</note> against leasing, etc., with a warning that it will be weakened unless, etc. Number 30 and following.

<margin>30</margin>
The 14th Point.† That a thousand thousands of blessings are deserved by the Imperial Majesty of the Emperor Charles V and the Royal Catholic Majesty of his son and King Philip our lords, and those of their royal council, for the pragmatic decree stating that no one may lease grain for the purpose of reselling it. This was the salvation of all their kingdoms and a certain bridle upon those cursed "petty usuries." And because I see a door opening through which the virtue of this law will escape (if it is not closed), I beg the holy prelates, preachers, and confessors who are near His Royal Highness to advise them—as I have also advised: namely, that good and daring judges do proceed against and condemn those who trespass against it; however, the penalty is so small (for the sake of ensuring they do not appeal) that the appellate judge ends up taking the profit of the first judge's hard work, which amounts to nothing. Moreover, some religious figures even beg the judges to either drop the charges entirely or lower them significantly. With such <term>unjust mercy</term><b> <margin>b <note>original: "De qua in cap. Eſt iniuſta miſericordia. 23. q. 4."</note> Concerning which, see the Chapter "It is an unjust mercy" in Question 4 of the 23rd Case.</margin> they prevent the true punishment of fifty men from serving as a warning to fifty thousand and bringing relief to a million.

<margin>31</margin>
I therefore† charge all the aforementioned, for the service of God and love of the commonwealth and the poor, to persuade their Presidents and Judges to establish an order. By this order, the first-instance judges should not lose the will to uncover delinquents and condemn them (thereby incurring their hatred), nor should the higher judges lose the will to confirm their sentences or reform them for the better. A convenient order for this (saving whatever better one His Highness might establish) seems to be that the appellate judges should keep any additional penalty they add to that of the first judges. If they add nothing, or moderate the fine, or if what they add does not reach a third of the first fine, they should always be given what is necessary so that they retain a third of what the first judges are to receive.

->S U M M A R Y.<-

Profit is not always usury when there is a certain gain from a partnership with guaranteed capital; and how this can be done through three contracts, etc. Number 32.
Money placed in a partnership does not become a loan simply because of a guarantee given by the one who receives it. Number 35.
Ownership of a thing does not pass to another simply by receiving it at one's own peril and risk. Number 37.
How a full owner cannot always dispose of what is his own. Number 40.
The obligation of a debtor is not removed by the guarantee of another. Number 40.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of financial contracts and usury, specifically addressing the "triple contract" (contractus trinus) used to secure capital and profit. It references the author's previous work (the "Manual") and responds to the critiques of Domingo de Soto.</meta>

# Regarding Chapter 1, Section 14, Question 3.

<margin>b. John Major on Book 4, Distinction 15, Question 49; Sylvester under the word "partnership," Question 2; and Cajetan, Book 17, Response 11.</margin>
...of some Doctors, who based on weak foundations affirmed that it was licit to take and give money in a certain way with guaranteed profit and capital, in which later very large sums of money were taken and given. And we proved that this was not tolerable unless it were reduced to another form, which is gathered from some very learned men <note>original: "varones muy doctos"</note>; but because the profit resulting from it seemed small to them, it was not used, and many turned away from the other form
<margin>33</margin> because it did not seem just to them. We said, then, that with three licit contracts, one partner can secure the capital of the other with a certain profit, in this manner: first, that the first contract be one of partnership <note>A "societas" contract where risk is shared.</note>, where one provides the money and the other provides the labor and industry, sharing the uncertain profit and loss justly. The second, that the one providing the labor insures the capital for the other, for a fair amount, or because he takes a fair portion less of the profit. The third, that to remove suspicion and trouble, the one who trades leases the uncertain profit for a reasonable certain price, or the owner of the money takes a smaller certain profit instead of the likely and uncertain profit. We understood this to be licit provided there is no fraud, simulation, or danger of infamy. This conclusion seems to be effectively proven: because everyone confesses that these three contracts can be made justly with three different men; and there is no text in the world, nor reason, that necessarily proves why they cannot be made with one single person, provided there is no fraud or simulation, and it being the truth before God that that certain profit is desired in exchange for the true and uncertain or likely profit of the dealings of that partnership, and not for any other unjust reason.

<margin>34 a. In the said Chapter 17, number 255. b. John Major, as cited above. c. Because regularly, a silent agreement has the same force as an express one. Law "siina," Digest, on mandates; chapter 2, on rescripts.</margin>
We added further in the said Manual <note>The author refers to his "Manual for Confessors."</note> that they could be made at the same time (following the Parisians <note>The theologians of the University of Paris.</note>) for the same reason, provided all fraud ceases, etc. And for the same reason, just as these three formal and express contracts could be made, they could also be made with three silent and equivalent ones, as we declared there. Where we also brought forward for this a custom from many parts of France, which we heard of when we were lecturing in Toulouse (before we came to Salamanca and Coimbra), and even a decretal letter from Innocent III which can be seen there, understanding (as we have said) all of this as long as all fraud ceases, etc. Because if that does not cease, not even two can be made, nor even one.

<margin>35 d. Chapter "per vestras," on donations. e. Book 6, Question 6, Article 1, on institutes and law. f. "Ad vostram," chapter "illo vos," with those annotated, on pledges.</margin>
After which the most learned and no less pious Doctor Soto <note>Domingo de Soto, a key figure in the School of Salamanca.</note> wrote three things about this. First, that it does not matter whether these three contracts are made at one time or successively. This seems very right to us regarding the forum of conscience before God: although they could matter much for the external forum, where more evil might be presumed when they are made at one time than when they are made at different times. Second, that the said contracts made with three different men are licit; and even if the first (the partnership) and the second (the profit) were made with one person, and the third (the insurance) with another. This is also commonly held. Third, that it is not licit to make the first of partnership and the second of insurance with the same person, neither at one time nor at different times, for a new and very strong reason: namely, that putting money into a partnership with the partner's insurance, or having it placed and insuring it thus, is in effect lending it, or beginning to have it as a loan.

<margin>36</margin>
Because whoever thus places or begins to have money placed, transfers the ownership <note>original: "señorío"</note> of it to the one who receives it, since it is at his risk, and consequently, he can do with it what he wishes (as if it had been lent to him), trading it or leaving it out of trade, and buying estates or annuities; for if it is lost, it is lost to him, and if it is kept, it is kept for him, and he must restore it even if it is lost. This is not the case when the first of partnership and the third of profit are made with one person, and the second of insurance with a third party: because then the one to whom the money is given is not obliged to restore it, and the one who is obliged to it is not given the money, and thus there is no loan <note>original: "emprestido"</note>.

<margin>37 a. In Chapter 1, on commodatum. b. In Law 1, Code, on commodatum. c. Accepted gloss, on said chapter 1, on commodatum. d. In the final chapter, on deposits; see also Law 1, section "If it is agreed," Digest, same title.</margin>
The reason is certainly plausible, but it does not satisfy us, though perhaps the cause of it is our failure to understand its force. Firstly, because it is against clear law to say that the ownership of a thing passes to the receiver simply because there is a pact that all danger of it shall be his. For Gregory IX expressly determines (following the Emperors) that to lend you a mule for a certain use with a pact that all danger be yours, even if it is lost by a fortuitous event, is a loan for use <term>commodatum<gloss>A "commodatum" is a type of loan where an item is given for free use and must be returned, as opposed to a "mutuum" or monetary loan where ownership of the specific coins passes to the borrower.</gloss></term>, by which the ownership does not pass to the receiver; and thus in this case, you are not the owner of the mule, even though you hold it at your total risk. Likewise, the same Gregory IX determines that to entrust something to you so that you may keep it,

[with a pact...]

<summary>This page discusses the legality of the "triple contract," a financial arrangement designed to bypass usury laws by combining partnership, profit insurance, and capital insurance. The author debates with the theologian Domingo de Soto over whether transferring financial risk also transfers legal ownership of the money.</summary>
<keywords>triple contract, usury, partnership, insurance, capital, risk, ownership, Domingo de Soto, commodatum, canon law, School of Salamanca</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and moral analysis of the "triple contract." The author is refuting the argument that transferring the risk of capital to the borrower automatically transfers full ownership (and thus makes any guaranteed profit usury). He argues that one can remain the owner of the money even if another party bears the risk through insurance or a deposit agreement.</meta>

-> # Resolutory Commentary on Usury. <-

<margin>e In the said chapter 1 and in the said final chapter.
f Section "Placuit." On obligations arising from crimes. Law "Qui vas." Section "Vetare." Digest: On theft. Final law. Section "Sed siquidem." Code: On obtaining ownership. Authentic "presentse." Code: On sureties.</margin>
...with a pact that all the risk be yours, it is a contract of deposit, in which the ownership <note>original: "señorío," referring to legal dominion or title</note> does not pass to the receiver. Likewise †, fault and delay commonly transfer the risk <margin>38</margin> to the receiver of the thing, as [Gregory] himself affirms: but ownership is not transferred because of this. Furthermore, it is also against the law to say that a person may do whatever they wish, as an owner, with that which is at their total risk. For that which has been lent to you for a certain use, or deposited with the said pacts—that it be at your total risk and peril until it is returned—and everything that is in your power for which you have committed fault or delay in its restitution, is at your total risk; but you cannot use it except for the purpose for which it was lent and delivered to you. On the contrary, if you profit from it for another use against the will of the one who lent or delivered it to you, they can sue you for theft and condemn you to double or fourfold restitution <margin>f</margin>. Furthermore †, <margin>39</margin> it is also against the law to say that the partner, into whose power I have placed the money, is not obliged to restore it to me because a third party has insured it for me. Because <margin>g And things introduced to increase do not work a diminution, nor vice versa. Law "Legata inutiliter." Digest: On the administration of legacies. And final chapter: On the significance of words. Nor should a thing introduced for one end work the contrary. Chapter "Ad nostram." On appeals. Law 1. Digest: on the same.</margin> he remains just as obliged to it as if no one had insured it for me. For there is no text in the world, nor any reason, that proves one is freed from an obligation because another person insures my [capital], just as a guarantee or pledges do not remove or diminish it. For all of this is added to fortify, and not to weaken <margin>g</margin>. Furthermore †, because my partner cannot <margin>40</margin> use the money that I have given him for a certain trade in another; nor use that which I have given him for commercial dealing to instead buy annuities <note>original: "comprar rentas," likely referring to fixed-income assets or census agreements</note>, no matter how much he has guaranteed my profit and my money. Because this is to use my property for a different use than that for which I <margin>h Law "Si convenerit." Digest: On partnerships. And through the aforesaid Section "Placuit" and Law "Qui vas," Section "Vetare." And the argument in Clementine "Quia contingit": On the residences of religious orders, and those things which [Cajetan] beautifully teaches there.</margin> gave it to him, which is a sin and theft <margin>h</margin>. Furthermore, it is also against the law to say that anyone to whom ownership of a thing passes can always do with it what they wish: because this does not apply when it passes with some reservation and charge to use it for a certain end, or in a certain manner: just as ownership of things passes that a first husband gave to his wife <margin>a</margin>; and as ownership of entailed estates <note>original: "mayorazgos," a system of impartible inheritance</note> passes, and many other things. And consequently, even if the ownership of the money passed to the partner, because it passes with the charge and reservation that he use it in such a trade, or in commercial trade, it cannot be used in another against the will of the one who gave it <margin>b</margin>. Now † then, if <margin>41</margin> the ownership of the thing does not pass to the receiver by taking it with a pact that all risk and peril be at his charge—and this is what it means to insure—and if the one who receives the thing at total risk cannot use it except for the purpose for which it was delivered; and if even when ownership of something passes with some reservation, the owner cannot use it without [observing] that reservation; and if the insurance from a third party does not take away or diminish the obligation to restore it to the partner: it is clear that the said reasoning presupposes four things contrary to law, by which it concludes nothing. All this is confirmed †, as it is inferred <margin>42</margin> from what is said above, that the owner of the money can compel the trader to deal in the trade for which it was given and not in another, and can withdraw from the partnership <margin>e</margin>, notwithstanding the insurance and the leasing of the profit, or take back his money and give it to another, according <margin>a Law "Femine." Code: On second marriages.
b Law 1 and Law "Legatum." Digest: On the administration of things pertaining to the city. Law "Legatum": On the legacy of usufruct. And the said Clementine "Quia contingit."
e Law "Si convenerit" and Law "Si socium." Digest: On partnerships.</margin> to what the law and the pacts of the partnership allow. And he can add pacts by which it is a very clear partnership, and declare what is understood by law: for example, that he does not want that money used except in such trade or trades for which it is given; and that he wants neither certain nor doubtful profit, but rather the true or likely profit that comes from that trade, or is likely expected from it; and he can even add penalties to the contract if the person does not trade as expressed in the partnership contract, etc. By all of which, and even half of it, the foundation of the said Doctor is removed, and what was said above remains further corroborated. We † do not say this out of a whim or a <margin>43</margin> desire that this be used extensively, nor do we believe that those who have money are commonly content with the small profit that remains for them once the amount given or left for the insurance and for the exchange of uncertain profit into certain profit is removed; rather, we say it because the truth and force of the law obliges us to it, under due correction. And because it is good that people turn away from illicit ways of gaining much for the sake of lawful ways of gaining little. Perhaps they would not turn away [from illicit gain] for the sake of lawful ways of gaining nothing; and although we may perhaps be mistaken in this, we certainly hold that this is not as close to usury as are personal annuities <note>original: "censos personales," a controversial form of fixed-return investment often accused of being disguised usury</note>, which...

<meta>catchword: ...them, which</meta>

<summary>The author argues that transferring financial risk to a partner or third-party insurer does not grant that partner the right to use funds however they wish, nor does it automatically constitute usury. He asserts that legal ownership can be transferred with specific restrictions and that lawful "small gains" are preferable to illicit "large gains."</summary>
<keywords>usury, ownership, risk, insurance, partnership, contract of deposit, triple contract, commercial trade, personal annuities</keywords>
