<meta>This page continues the legal and theological debate over the "triple contract" and usury, specifically moving into the definitions of "interest" (compensation) versus "usury" (illicit profit). The "Doctor" mentioned is likely the author himself, Martín de Azpilcueta, referring to his own previous writings.</meta>

### Concerning Chapter 1, Question 14, Case 3.

<margin>d In the Commentaries, at the end of the section on usury.</margin>
...the arguments which the said Doctor introduces anew, which we will discuss below <gloss>d</gloss>.

-> **S U M M A R Y** <-

**Interest**, what it is in this matter. number 44.
**Interest of loss** <note>original: "interesse de daño," equivalent to the legal concept of *damnum emergens*</note> and **interest of profit** <note>original: "interesse de ganancia," equivalent to *lucrum cessans*</note>. number 45.
**Interest** may be taken for lending in three cases according to everyone: 1) when there are losses, 2) when there was a delay in repayment, and 3) when the loan is made by force; and even in a fourth case, when it is made without force, etc., according to the common opinion, based on nine reasons found here. number 46. etc.
**Extrinsic interest** (outside the thing itself) is not commonly owed by law. number 50.
**Usury** cannot be taken even by dispensation; thus, being forced to lend does not excuse one from this sin. number 51.
**Interest and usury** differ according to **Roman laws**. number 51. And interest is not owed simply because usury is owed, nor by the mere delay of payment. number 52.
**Money** is worth more in the hands of a merchant than in others. Just as **seed-corn** is worth more to the one who wants to sow it and has no other. number 52.
**The value of a thing** does not increase because you are forced to give it, nor because it is stolen or robbed from you. number 54.
**A person may sell** their own property for what it is worth to them, even if it is not worth as much to others; but not for what it will be worth to the buyer, if it is not commonly worth that much to the seller and others. number 55.
**Money** has two powers for making profit. number 55.
**Virtue** is more favorable than vice. number 55.
**Selling** that which is on the path to profit. number 56.
**Lending** when compelled by love and charity. number 56.

<margin>44</margin>
<margin>a In chapter 17, number 206.</margin>
<margin>b Concerning which Bartolus, Baldus, Salicetus, and other more recent authors write extensively on the first law of the Code: "Concerning the sentence for that which is of interest."</margin>
**15. † That it is lawful for the lender to take**, and even to demand, something by reason of **interest**, both for profit and for loss. Because some have desired a declaration in the *Manual* <gloss>a</gloss> <note>The *Manual de Confesores y Penitentes*, Azpilcueta's most famous work</note> regarding what we call "interest," we say to one and all that—leaving aside the subtleties and fine points of its definition and the divisions and subdivisions <gloss>b</gloss> that partially obscure the matter—**interest** for this purpose is defined as that which the lender loses from his estate, or fails to gain, by lending or by not being paid what he lent at the due term. And thus there are two species of interest: one is **interest of loss** <gloss>c</gloss>, and the other is **interest of profit**.

<margin>45</margin>
<margin>c Which is called "emergent loss" <note>Latin: *damnum emergens*</note> and the other "ceasing profit" <note>Latin: *lucrum cessans*</note>. Gloss 4 on the said law 1, the end of which proves this.</margin>
**An example of interest of loss:** I have money † with which I intend to repair or improve my house so that it does not collapse, or with which I want to buy wheat in the summer for the whole year’s provision, or hay for my livestock, or to repair the dams of my mills, or to pay my debts. I lend this money to you to remedy your own greater hardships, telling you that I fear loss if I lend it to you, or if you do not pay me by a certain time. Afterward, either because I lent it to you or because you did not pay me at the agreed time, my house falls down, I must buy wheat at double the price, my livestock dies of hunger, the mills do not grind, I pay interest to my own creditors, or I am forced to sell my estate at a poor price to satisfy them. That which I have lost because of this is called "interest of loss received."

**An example of interest of profit:** I am a merchant, I have money, and I want to buy merchandise to trade. I lend the money to you because of your persistence, warning you how much I will fail to gain by lending to you or by your not paying me at the agreed time. You take the money and do not return it to me at that time; that which I fail to gain because of this is my interest, which is called "of profit."

<margin>46</margin>
¶ **All authorities** † (without exception) agree that in both of the said types of interest, one may demand...

<meta>The catchword "fin duda" (without doubt) indicates the start of the next page.</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta defines "interest" as legal compensation for a lender's losses (damnum emergens) or missed profits (lucrum cessans), distinguishing it from usury. He provides practical examples of how a lender might suffer financial harm when a borrower fails to repay a loan on time.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal and theological investigation into the distinction between usury (illicit profit) and "interest" (rightful compensation). It focuses specifically on the concept of "missed profit" (lucrum cessans) and whether a lender can claim compensation for business opportunities they gave up in order to provide a loan.</meta>

<header>Resolutory Commentary on Usury.</header>

<margin>
a Famous gloss on the chapter "Conquetus" regarding usury, via the chapter "Dilecti" on competent forum, and the chapter "Pervenit" and the chapter "Continuus" on suretyship, and law 1 of the Digest, if finished; also the chapter on sentences which for him, because it is an excellent text in law 3, paragraph "if," of the Digest on that which is in a certain place.
b Book 6, question 1, article 3, on justice and law.
c In the final chapter on usury.
47
d In the said final chapter.
e Second part of the Second part, question 76, article 2, reply 1.
48
f In the Commentary on the Sentences, book 4, distinction 15, question 2.
g In book 3, distinction 37, question 1.
h On contracts, question 30.
</margin>
...without doubt for the loan in two cases. The first, when there was fault or delay in payment, and the interests accrued after that delay <a>. The second, when they accrued after or before the delay, but the loan was made by some force exerted by a King, a City, or someone else. ¶ Everyone also agrees that "interest for loss" <note>original: "interesse de daño," refers to *damnum emergens*, or compensation for an actual loss incurred by the lender</note> can be taken, even if neither force nor delay occurs. This is provided that, having been warned of the damage that could come, the borrower is willing to receive the loan and pay the lender back for it. Not only can one take and ask for it in these three cases, but even from the beginning, one may agree upon a sum—beyond the principal—that must be returned for that probable "interest for loss" or profit. As the sainted Doctor Soto <b> <c> wrote at length, even though he strove more than others to narrow the scope of this matter. All † the difficulty lies in another case: namely, when someone by their own will and without force lends money, stating that they intended to use it for trade, or were about to put it into trade, and by lending it to another, they cease to trade and gain a profit from it. The question is whether they can, with a good conscience, take "interest for that profit" <note>original: "interesse de aquella ganancia," refers to *lucrum cessans*, or compensation for the profit the lender missed out on</note> which occurs before any fault or delay in payment happens. On this point, it seemed to Innocent IV <d> that they could not; the said Doctor Soto praises and follows him, bringing some fundamental proofs for his position and responding to those of the contrary opinion. However, Soto says he does not condemn the opposing view, though he would wish many to align with his own: which is that all those who lend without force are usurers if they require payment or a pact of compensation for the profit they would have made from trading their money up until the time of repayment (provided they suffered no loss to their already earned wealth). For † this position, he cites Innocent <e>, who affirms this, and St. Thomas <f> <note>St. Thomas Aquinas</note>, who distinguishes between interest for loss and interest for missed profit regarding this matter. He argues there seems to be no text that permits taking this interest for profit from one who was not at fault nor committed a delay in payment. He further claims no ancient doctor clearly held the contrary; rather, Scotus <g> and Durandus <h> followed St. Thomas. Furthermore, he argues that in this case, no one is ceasing to gain against their will, since they voluntarily and without force lend their money which they had intended for trade.

<margin>
i Under the word "usura," question 19.
k Second part of the Second part, question 78, article 2, very extensively.
l In book 4, on restitution and usury.
m In the final chapter on usury.
n In the chapter "Salubriter," same title.
o In the said final chapter.
49
</margin>
¶ For the contrary opinion, however, it is noted that—as Soto himself admits—it is held by Conrad <i>, Sylvester <k>, Cajetan <l>, and Adrian <m>. We add that it is also held by Panormitanus <n>, and even before him by Hostiensis, Antoninus <n>, and Johannes Andreae <o>, insofar as he says that the statement of Hostiensis proceeds when the debtor is late in paying; otherwise, anyone could pretend they want to trade and then give the money at interest. This reasoning † only concludes that our opinion does not apply when, in truth and before God, the lender does not actually intend to trade and merely feigns it to give money at usury—and all of this we confess. Consequently, Johannes Andreae must either confess that his reason is worthless or that our opinion is good. Also, Giovanni di Lignano, a most grave doctor, and Lorenzo de Rodulphi <a> hold the same as Hostiensis, and even St. Antoninus <b>, citing three reasons for it, although he says it would be well to dissuade people from these contracts. We also confess this, except when it is clear that making them would be a greater service to God—which happens every day, where for a small interest, a neighbor's great harm might be avoided by lending them the money that was intended for trade. The same is held by the Parisians <c>, Gabriel, and others.

<margin>
a In the said chapter "Salubriter."
b Part 2, title 1, chapter 7, paragraph 15.
c In book 4, distinction 15.
50
d Bartolus and others on law 1 of the Code, on sentences which are for that which is interest.
e Law "If sterile," paragraph "when through the seller," of the Digest on the action of purchase.
f Argument from law "That," of the Digest regarding the Aquilian law.
51
p As it is said above in this Commentary, number 7, which he himself proves more extensively.
b In the chapter "Super," on the same [title] regarding usury.
</margin>
Of the principal reasons that move us toward this [contrary opinion], the first is that the said Doctor Soto confesses that I may take interest for the damage I receive by lending—such as if my house falls down. Since this interest is what is called **outside the thing itself** <note>original: "extra rem," referring to extrinsic titles for interest that do not arise from the loan itself but from external circumstances</note>, it is no more due by law than the interest for missed profit <e>. Therefore, if that can be taken without preceding fault or delay, so can this <f>. The second reason is that Soto confesses that one who lends by some force may take interest for profit without preceding fault or delay. It is well known that force cannot make usury lawful, as it is forbidden by natural and divine law <g>. Therefore, either it will not be lawful to take this interest for profit even when force intervenes, or it will be lawful even without force. The third reason is that Pope Alexander III expressly determined <p> that no dispensation can be given regarding the divine prohibition of usury; and just as one cannot lie for any reason, neither can one give at usury. Consequently, as force does not make lying lawful, it will not make giving at usury lawful. From this it follows that taking interest for profit—which one ceases to gain because they lent money prepared for trade—is not usury forbidden by divine and natural law, or else force would not make it lawful. Since he confesses that force makes it lawful, he must confess that without force, it is not usury. The fourth reason is that it is a different thing <b> to take in...

<meta>catchword: to take in</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the theological debate over whether a lender can be compensated for "missed profit" (lucrum cessans) when making a voluntary loan. Azpilcueta argues against his contemporary, Domingo de Soto, claiming that such compensation is not usury because if it is permitted in "forced" loans, it must be inherently lawful under natural law.</summary>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues Azpilcueta’s defense of "interest for profit" (lucrum cessans). He argues that money has a different value depending on who holds it and what they intend to do with it, specifically distinguishing the "interest" a merchant may rightfully claim from the "usury" forbidden by the Church.</meta>

<header>Regarding Chapter 1, Question 14, Part 3. 17</header>

<margin>
i in Law 3, final paragraph. Digest on that which is in a certain place. <note>original: "ff. de eo quod cer. loc." refers to the *Digest* of Justinian.</note>
k in the said Law 3, final paragraph.
</margin>
...is to collect interest on profit, and another to collect usury, as the Jurisconsult Scaevola <note>Quintus Cervidius Scaevola was a distinguished Roman jurist often cited in the School of Salamanca's legal-economic theories.</note> clearly stated; saying that a debtor, by failing to pay what he owes, is sometimes obliged to pay interest on profit, even if it is a greater quantity than the permitted usury. Thus, he feels that usury is one thing, and interest on profit is another.

<margin>
52
a According to the mind of all, which Cajetan explains at length in the Second Part of the Second Part, question 78, article 2. <note>Thomas Cajetan was a leading theologian who influenced Catholic economic thought.</note>
b Because against the aforementioned Law 3, final paragraph, there: "That which was of interest shall come, and indeed beyond the legitimate usurious limit. Because if merchants were accustomed to purchasing, I think, etc., the calculation of profit must be held."
</margin>
The † fifth [point] is that the same Jurisconsult <sup>k</sup> clearly signifies that not all creditors can collect interest on profit when a debtor fails to pay what is owed. This is because merchants may collect it, while others may not; consequently, it signifies that more is owed to a merchant for the money with which he trades than to another person who does not trade. From this, it seems to follow that a certain amount of money is worth more in the hands of a businessman prepared to trade with it than the same amount in the hands of another. From this, it follows that if he receives something more than another would, he does not thereby receive more than what he originally possessed and gave. The sixth [point] is that, as the said Jurisconsult signifies, money in the hands of a businessman who wishes to trade with it immediately is worth more than the same amount in the hands and power of another—or even in his own hands, if he does not wish to trade with it or cannot do so immediately. For this reason, a thief who steals that money would be obliged to restore more to him than if he had stolen it from someone else. Similarly, seed wheat <note>original: "trigo semental," grain intended for planting rather than eating.</note> which one person holds to sow immediately, having no other such grain for it, is worth more than it would be to another from whom it was stolen; the thief would be obliged to restore more to him than if he had stolen it from someone who was not going to sow it, or even from the same person if he had plenty of other grain for that purpose <sup>a</sup>.

<margin>
53
c Law on Delay, paragraph "in good faith." Digest on usage; and the final law of the Digest on the risk and convenience of the thing sold; and the Law on Fruits. Code on the action of purchase.
d Through the said Law 3, final paragraph. For the common opinion in the said Law 1, Code on sentences which for him, against his gloss and others similar to it, holds that nothing has been changed by that law regarding the payment of interest that was not owed before it.
</margin>
The † seventh [point] is that it is against the law <sup>b</sup> to say that interest on profit is owed solely because of the fault or delay <note>original: "tardança," referring to the legal concept of *mora* or default.</note> of the debtor in paying. For although the Roman laws command the payment of ordained usury solely for the delay of payment in certain types of contracts <sup>c</sup>, this does not apply to interest on profit, except when at the very least the debtor's delay coincides with the lender's near and likely power and will to have made a profit <sup>d</sup>. The eighth [point]: because from all this it follows that although the aptitude for profit that money has, considered absolutely, does not make it worth more than its face value; however, the aptitude for profit it has when considered in the power of one who knows how and wants to use it *does* make it worth more. Just as the ability to produce wheat, which is in the wheat considered absolutely, does not make it worth more; but considered in the power of one who has it planted, or prepared for it, without having any other to sow, it makes it worth more. The effect is that he can sell it, and even lend it, at a higher price, and whoever steals or robs it from him must restore more to him than to another. The ninth [point]: that there is no text or reason in the world to prove that stealing from me secretly, or robbing me publicly, or forcing me to give something out of fear, increases the value of what is stolen, robbed, or forced from me. For although by way of penalty or injury the perpetrator could be compelled to pay me more because of the crime committed, it is not because the value of the stolen item grows because of the act. Nor in the court of conscience <note>original: "fuero dela consciencia," the internal forum of moral and religious judgment, distinct from civil law.</note> will he be obliged to do more than return what was taken, without any other increase <sup>f</sup>, and to restore my honor if he took any from me by the act.

<margin>
54
e According to the whole title of the Digest on theft; and the title on goods taken by force; and the title on that done through fear; and the Institutes on obligations arising from crimes and goods taken by force.
f According to the singular and repeated gloss on the chapter "Facientes," 12, question 2.
a Second part of the Second part, question 77, article 1.
b In book 4, distinction 15, question 2.
c Argument of those who do not
</margin>
And † everyone confesses that if someone, through theft, force, or fear, were to take or compel me to give the money I had prepared for trading (having no other for it), or the wheat I had for sowing (having no other for it), he is obliged to restore more to me than to someone else who did not have them for those purposes. Therefore, by necessity, we must confess that money and such wheat are worth more in the power of one who wants and can trade and sow, than in the power of others who do not want to do the same; and consequently, if he lends it, he may collect something more for it as interest. The tenth [point]: that although someone who has a thing that commonly is not worth more than ten—neither to him nor to others—cannot sell it to another for more, even if to him it is worth much more; yet, someone who has a thing that commonly is not worth more than ten for others, but for him it is worth more, may well sell it for what it is worth to him, taking as much more for it as it is worth to him, according to Saint Thomas [Aquinas] <sup>a</sup> and [Duns] Scotus <sup>b</sup>, who are accepted authorities. And as has been said and proven, one hundred ducats <note>A common gold coin of the era.</note> are worth more to a merchant who immediately wants to trade with them, or to another who wants to buy productive estates <note>original: "possessioes fructiferas," land that yields crops or income.</note> that he has found, than commonly to others who do not want to do the same; therefore, he may collect that extra amount for them. The eleventh [point]: that this interest is not collected for lending the money, nor even for the general and absolute virtue it has for being able to gain profit, but for the special virtue it has for being able to gain profit for *this* person, who holds it to trade with it. Consequently, it will not be usury to collect it, since the definitions of usury were placed above in number 5.

<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: do not</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that money's value is not static but depends on the holder's opportunity to use it for profit (lucrum cessans). He uses the analogy of seed wheat to show that restitution and interest should reflect the lost potential for growth, distinguishing this from illicit usury.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Azpilcueta’s detailed legal and theological defense of "lost profit" (lucrum cessans). He is responding to counter-arguments from other scholars, such as Domingo de Soto, and refining the distinction between an illegal usurious loan and a legal compensation for the "potential power" of money used in trade.</meta>

<header>Resolutory Commentary on Usury.</header>

...is not taken by reason of the loan itself, but by reason of that greater and special power <note>The term "virtud" here refers to the economic potential or utility of the money in the hands of a merchant.</note> that he holds in his hands, which would not be held in the hands of everyone. The twelfth [point] is that virtue deserves more favor than vice <margin>d Chapter 1, On the postulation of prelates; Chapter *Quanto*, 2. q. 5.</margin>: and to lend by force is commonly not a virtue, whereas to lend <margin>e in the said question 78, article 3, to the 1st [objection].</margin> out of charity and love is; and since he who lends under some degree of compulsion can take this interest (as was said above), he who does the same out of charity and love should also be able to do so. The thirteenth [point] † is that the motives of the said *Master Soto* <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a colleague of Azpilcueta and a major figure in the School of Salamanca.</note> do not stand in the way, nor does the authority of *St. Thomas*: because *St. Thomas*, although he distinguishes between interest arising from a loss <note>term: damnum emergens; an actual loss incurred by the lender.</note> and that arising from a profit <note>term: lucrum cessans; profit that the lender ceases to make because they lent the money.</note>, for the purpose that one should not take profit from it as if it were a guaranteed thing already achieved, while one may take it for a loss; but this is not for the purpose of saying that nothing at all can be taken. This is because the reason he <margin>f <note>original: "Quia non debet vendere id, quod nondum habet et potest multipliciter impediri."</note> Because he ought not to sell that which he does not yet have and which can be hindered in many ways.</margin> gives is: That he must not sell what he does not yet have, and which can be hindered in many ways. He concludes the opposite, for he concludes that one cannot take the interest of the profit as a thing already earned, but as a thing that is on the way to being so, and which can be hindered. <margin>g *Secunda Secundae*, question 62, article 4.</margin> And it is clear that the same *St. Thomas* says that whoever damages another in their sown field <note>term: sementera; a field already planted with seed.</note>, or in another thing that is not yet earned but is on the way to it, although he is not obliged to restore as much as the owner hopes to gain and harvest from it, he is indeed obliged to give him a reasonable compensation. And thus, this saying of *St. Thomas* does not contradict our conclusion, although it limits it, so that we may say: that he who lends cannot take as much interest as another equal to him might gain with the same amount of money, but only as much as is deserved by that power, or likely hope of gaining, which that money holds in his hands when he lends it, considering that it can be frustrated in many ways. Just as he who has a field sown ought not to sell it for as much as it would be worth with the fruit expected if it were already harvested, but for as much as that hope of harvesting is worth, considering it subject to many risks, because it can be hindered. Neither does what *Durandus* <margin>a in 3. d. 37.</note> says stand in the way, which is the same thing, and regarding this, it is addressed by the same words of *St. Thomas*. <margin>b in 4. d. 15. q. 2</margin> Even less does the authority of *Scotus* <margin>c in the said distinction 15, q. 2. column 6.</margin> stand in the way: because although he says other fine things on this subject (where he alleges it) as he usually does in others, he does not apply this distinction of *St. Thomas*. Rather, he says expressly that whoever has money for trading may lend it, setting a penalty of a certain amount to be paid to him if it is not returned, in order to keep himself harmless and without loss; and thus he feels that one receives a loss in lending and ceasing to gain. The fourteenth [point] † is that neither do the two strongest arguments of all stand in the way. One is that he who lends by his own will does not lend compelled; and he who does not lend compelled cannot say that another hinders or thwarts him, or makes his profit cease, until there is a delay or fault in the payment. Consequently, he who lends without compulsion cannot take anything for the profit, since it is not hindered before the fault or delay occurs. The other argument is that *Cajetan* <margin>d *Secunda Secundae*, q. 78. art. 2. to the 1st [objection].</margin> (even though he defends our opinion) says that although a thing that is in a particular state of potential for gain is worth more than another of the same value that is outside of that state—such as wheat that is sown, or ready to be sown, which is worth more and can be sold for more than other wheat not in that state—yet if it is voluntarily removed from that state, it cannot be sold for more than another of the same quality. Thus it seems that although money which is placed in trade, or ready to be put into it immediately, is worth more than other money, as soon as it is lent and voluntarily taken out of that state of profit, it is worth no more than any other, nor can more be asked for it than for any other. To these arguments, however, *Cajetan* responds with many words, which in summary contain: that he who lends out of love and charity to free his neighbor from a greater harm with a lesser one, is not said to lend <margin>e Thomas, *Secunda Secundae*, q. 78 art. 2. to the 1st [objection].</margin> voluntarily, but in some manner compelled. In favor of this, it is noted that he who throws the ship’s merchandise into the sea to <margin>f Law 1 and the whole title of the *Digest* regarding the Rhodian Law on jettison; chapter *maiores*. §. Likewise the question of jettison.</margin> save himself does so voluntarily, yet he does not throw them with an absolute will, but a conditional and respective one, to avoid a greater harm. Against this response of *Cajetan*, however, there are strong objections: First, that justice and injustice do not consist in good or bad spirits and intentions, but in the equality and inequality of exterior things and works <margin>g Aristotle, *Ethics* books 2 and 5; and Thomas, *Secunda Secundae*, q. 38. art. 10 and q. 59. art. 2.</margin>. Second, that the cause—be it mercy, piety, or cruelty—for which it is lent does not matter, but rather what is lent and what one wishes to take or takes for lending, for the purpose of it being just or unjust, and creating or not creating a necessity for restitution. Third, that although it may be a good work of charity or a bad one against it to lend or not to lend to help or harm one's neighbor, not because of this...

<meta>catchword: this</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that money's value includes its potential for profit (lucrum cessans), comparing it to a sown field which is worth more than an empty one. He disputes the idea that voluntary lending forfeits the right to compensation, likening the act to jettisoning cargo during a storm—a choice made under pressure to avoid greater harm.</summary>

<keywords>lucrum cessans, interest, usury, School of Salamanca, Thomas Aquinas, Cajetan, Domingo de Soto, opportunity cost, compensation, charity, Lex Rhodia</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues Azpilcueta’s rebuttal regarding the necessity of restitution and the conditions under which a lender may justly receive compensation for profit lost by lending their capital. He specifically addresses the nuances of "compulsion" and the legal nature of agreements regarding potential gains.</meta>

<header>About Chapter 1, 14, Question 3.</header>

...this will be just, or against justice, so that it imposes, or fails to impose, the necessity of restitution^b.
<margin>b *Because, as we said in the Manual, chapter 24, number 5, only sins against justice induce the necessity of restitution.*</margin>
The other thing, which seems to be new doctrine and contrary to the law^c and to that of St. Thomas^d (which in the *Manual*^e we apply to another matter), is that mercy, piety, and loving pleas without deceit might induce compulsion, or sufficient force, for one to be called "compelled," to the effect that because of this one might ask for restitution, or be freed from the restitution that would otherwise be owed. 
<margin>c *In the final law, Code, if anyone prohibits another from testifying.*
d *Summa Theologiae, Secunda Secundae, question 6, article 6.*</margin>
Therefore, I respond to the first argument by conceding that he who lends voluntarily to help his neighbor, and out of piety, or even out of mercy, does not lend compelled by a compulsion that induces or removes the need for restitution. I also concede that he who lends voluntarily should not be said to be hindered by another, nor to cease earning because of an impediment that another
<margin>e *Above, chapter 17, numbers 15 and 16.*</margin>
places upon him. However, I deny that he who voluntarily ceases to earn in order to do a favor for another—the other having promised to give him that same gain—cannot justly accept it.

A carpenter, stonemason, or other laborer voluntarily ceases to earn in his trade when I entreat him to come with me to this or 그 that place, promising that I will satisfy him for what he would have earned by his labor; he may certainly ask me later for what he would have earned during those days.

I voluntarily lend to my neighbor a load of seed wheat that I had intended for sowing, but I can certainly ask him for as much as it was worth to me at the time I gave it to him, even if
<margin>f *By those things which we said a little before in number 34.*</margin>
it was not worth as much to others^f. <note>Azpilcueta is distinguishing between the market price of the wheat and its specific value to the lender as "capital" for a future harvest.</note>

I voluntarily exchange, sell, or give to someone the wheat that I have already sown, for what it is worth, with the likely hope of the harvest; but I can take what it and the harvest are likely worth for that same reason.

If I voluntarily cease from buying estates, or from trading, in order to lend you the money I had for that purpose, or invested in it, and you promise me that you will pay me what I cease to earn because of you, or what that money is worth in that state, I commit no injustice. No force is necessary to justify it. It is not usury, because I take nothing for the lending itself, but because I cease to earn because of
<margin>g *According to the law Naturalis, Digest, on prescriptive words.*</margin>
you, and you promised me compensation for it. In effect, there are two contracts: one of a loan, and another of *facio ut des* <note>Latin: "I do that you may give." A legal category of contract where one party performs a service so the other will provide a reciprocal benefit.</note>, which is that I cease to earn^g so that you may give me compensation, or
<margin>h *According to the mind of St. Thomas, Secunda Secundae, question 58, article 2, to the 1st [objection].*</margin>
it is the sale of that probable gain^h.

To the second [argument], I respond that the saying of Cajetan <note>Thomas de Vio (1469–1534), known as Cajetan, a prominent Dominican theologian and commentator on St. Thomas Aquinas.</note> does not apply except to one who has his money or property in a state of earning, and simply removes it from that state without contracting with another who promises him compensation for it, and afterward wants as much as it was worth while in that state of earning.

Our conclusion, however, applies to the one who removes money from a state of earning by a pact and a promise to be given just compensation for it, whether certain or uncertain.

Regarding this, consider that he who ceases to work and earn for eight days without a promise from anyone cannot justly demand that the person who might have needed his work on the other eight days pay him for the past eight. But if such a contract preceded between them, then he can.

Let us conclude, then, that it is licit for the lender to take something more, not only by reason of interest for a loss <note>term: damnum emergens; an actual loss or expense incurred by the lender due to the loan.</note>, but even for that of profit <note>term: lucrum cessans; profit that the lender forfeits because their capital was lent out rather than invested.</note>. This applies not only to profit that would have occurred after a delay or fault in payment, but even to profit before that time; provided that it is not taken as a guaranteed gain, but as something that is on the way to being gained and could be hindered in many ways. Furthermore, the money must be taken out of trade or kept from being placed in trade or in fruitful things in order to lend it, with a pact that the borrower will give payment or probable compensation for that probable gain. However, this is not permitted if the lender first removes the money from the laborious trade and the danger of merchandising simply to have it available to lend at a secure and quiet interest, as we shall say in the following corollary.

---

### SUMMARY.

According to some, interest cannot be taken without seven conditions being met; of those mentioned, some are shown not to be necessary (num. 57 & seq.). Two others are added, which are also limited (num. 59). Interest should not be taken before the time in which it would have been earned, nor with infamy (num. 58).

<sig>B 2</sig>
<meta>The catchword "Lo.xv." indicates the first words of the following page.</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that lenders can justly receive compensation for "lost profit" (lucrum cessans) if a prior agreement exists, comparing it to a worker being compensated for time taken away from their trade. He distinguishes this from usury by framing it as a contract for the sale of a probable future gain rather than a charge for the loan itself.</summary>
<keywords>restitution, usury, interest, lost profit, lucrum cessans, damnum emergens, contracts, St. Thomas Aquinas, Cajetan, compensation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Azpilcueta’s analysis of the conditions required for a lender to lawfully accept compensation for lost profits (interest) without falling into the sin of usury or being required to make restitution. He is specifically debating a list of seven traditional conditions, arguing that many are not strictly required by law or justice.</meta>

<header>Resolutory Commentary on Usury.</header>

<margin>
v Conradus [Summenhart], 9.30.
z Sylvester [Prierias], see: Usury, 1.q.19.
y Therefore it should not be said... [original: "c. Legatum 24.q.2.ca.2. de translat.prel."] Which was later taught specifically by the Lord Doctor [Domingo de] Soto, book 6, question 1, article 3, *On Justice and Law*.
</margin>
<margin>57</margin>
¶ Lesson 15. It does not seem true what some <note>original: "v," referring to the margin note for Conradus</note> say: that for one to take the interest of profit without sin and without the need to restitute it, seven conditions must concur. By examining these, we shall identify those that are not actually proven by law.

<margin>
a Because a triple-braided cord, etc. [original: "c.1. de treuga & pac. Au.Itaq;.C. commun. de succef."]
b Cited above, question 102.3.
</margin>
¶ The first condition: that the lender does not enjoy gaining money this way <note>By receiving interest for a loan.</note> as much as through trade. However, in our view, this is not necessary. As long as he truly would have traded before God, and likely would have gained interest, and receives it for that reason, he neither sins nor is obliged to restitute. For he does not receive it primarily for the act of lending, but for the act of ceasing to trade; both are lawful. In our opinion, there is no reason or text to conclude that a means which is lawful in itself becomes unlawful simply because one wishes to use it as much as another equally lawful means—even if the other were better <note>original: "y," referring to the margin note citing Soto</note>. Moreover, one ought to rejoice as much or more in the work of helping one's neighbor while gaining, as in gaining alone <note>original: "a," referring to the margin note citing the "triple cord" of unity in purpose</note>. And because (as the Lord Doctor Soto <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a key Spanish theologian and contemporary of the author.</note> very well declared) it does not matter whether the lender is entreated and importuned by the receiver, or lends without being asked, so long as the requirements of commutative justice <note><term>Commutative justice</term>: The branch of justice governing fair exchange between individuals, requiring that one receives an equal value for what they give.</note> are met.

<margin>
c Chapter *Conquestus* on usury.
d In the *Code of Testimony*, at folio 132.
</margin>
¶ The second condition: that what he receives is truly his interest <note>Compensation for a loss occurred or a gain missed.</note>, and that he receives it as interest and not as "palliated usury" <note>A common legal term for usury disguised as a different kind of contract.</note>, according to the commonly received interpretation of the Gloss <note>The "Standard Gloss," the authoritative medieval commentary on Canon Law.</note>. However, it is enough that at the time of the loan, when the interest was specified, the profit was likely, even if it did not follow afterward, as [Juan de] Medina <note>Juan de Medina (1489–1545), an influential Spanish theologian.</note> rightly said.

<margin>
e Cited above.
</margin>
<margin>58</margin>
¶ The third condition: that the fact of having paid, or not having been paid, be the cause of not having had a profit (at least a probable one), according to everyone. This is not the case (as [Thomas] Cajetan and Medina rightly say) when the lender has other money with which he could trade. However, this point does not hold, in our view, when that other money was destined for something else, or for other fortuitous needs, and he did not wish to risk it in trade—which is wise for many reasons.

<margin>
f And thus it does not conclude. Argument from the chapter *In praesentia* on proof.
g Cited above.
</margin>
¶ The fourth condition: that the lender is not accustomed to giving usury. This is irrelevant to the court of conscience <note>The internal forum of the soul and God's judgment, as opposed to external human courts.</note>, for it may be that he is a usurer in other matters, but not in this one.
¶ The fifth condition: that he does not receive the interest before he would have likely earned it if he had traded. For it is unjust, according to Medina's considerations, to take interest beforehand, even if it were likely. Once the time has passed during which the profit was expected, the likely interest agreed upon at the start can be taken, even if, when it is received, it turns out that nothing would have been gained.

<margin>
h Argument from the chapter *In civitate* on usury; determined by Medina.
i Chapter 17, number 60.
</margin>
¶ The sixth condition: that the one who asks for the loan is not in extreme necessity. This also does not seem necessary. For just as one is not bound by precept to give freely, but rather satisfies the obligation by lending what is necessary to be paid back when possible (as said in the *Manual*), so there is no text or reason that necessarily obliges one to lend without interest to be paid when possible; for this would be, in effect, giving a gift.

<margin>
k 1 Thessalonians 5 and the chapter *Cum ab omni specie* on the life and honesty of clerics.
l As stated in the *Manual*, chapter 19, number 6.
m Thomas Aquinas, *Secunda Secundae*, question 43, at the beginning.
</margin>
¶ The seventh condition: that he who lends does not incur the "infamy of a usurer." One can incur this without actually committing usury, scandalizing the "weak" who think it is usury because of its similarity to it. As Saint Paul says, we must distance ourselves from every appearance of evil. While this condition is necessary to avoid sin <note>The sin of scandal, which causes others to stumble.</note>, it is not necessary to avoid the obligation of restitution, which arises only from injustice. The sin of scandal is against charity, not justice.

<margin>
a In the preceding corollary, from number 56.
b Above in this same work, number 57.
</margin>
<margin>59</margin>
¶ The eighth condition, which we add as being of great importance, is gathered from the fourteenth argument we brought for our conclusion above, along with a solution by Cajetan touched upon there. Namely: to take interest from a voluntary loan before there is a delay or fault in payment, the principal cause for lending must be to help with a neighbor's need for life, health, honor, or property. Thus, even if there is no compulsion of force, there should be a compulsion of love and charity. For while a person may lawfully and meritoriously prefer to take his money out of trade and lend it at interest to help his neighbor (as said in the first condition) rather than trading with it, the *principal* reason he must take it from trade (or cease putting it in) to lend it must be the necessary help of the neighbor. This is gathered from the said argument and its solution. However, as noted against Cajetan’s solution, this limitation is holy, but not entirely necessary.

<meta>catchword: ¶ The ni-</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta debates the seven traditional conditions required to take interest for lost profit, arguing that the obligation to restitute only arises from injustice, not from a lack of charity or the presence of social scandal.</summary>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues Azpilcueta’s discussion of the specific conditions under which a lender may lawfully charge interest. He is transitioning from debating general conditions to providing specific examples of "damnum emergens" (loss occurring) and "lucrum cessans" (profit ceasing), which were the two primary legal justifications for interest in the 16th century.</meta>

<header>On chapter 1, 14, question 3. [Page] 21</header>

<margin>c See above on the same topic, number 5.</margin>
¶ The ninth condition, which is also very important and which we add here, is gathered from the thirteenth argument with which we confirmed our conclusion in the preceding inference <note>original: "ᶜ. f." referring to marginal note c</note>. Specifically, that one should not collect or agree upon the total interest that is likely expected, but only what that *probable hope* is worth. For example, someone who sells a sown plot of land cannot sell it for the full value of the grain that is likely expected to be harvested; rather, they sell it for what that likely hope is worth, given it is subject to many dangers <note>Such as storms, pests, or blight</note> that could prevent the harvest. It seems to us, however, that it would be reasonable if the person who is importuned, begged, or who even offers out of a virtuous and charitable will, were to say: "I will lend you this money, taking it out of my business, or foregoing its investment in such fruitful estates that I am now refraining from buying. However, I do not want a specific interest rate to be fixed or guaranteed; instead, I want you to pay me whatever my partners earn with an equal amount—be it much, little, or nothing—or what the fruits of those estates I intended to buy would be worth, minus the costs." Such a request should be granted. This is because this interest is not demanded as a realized profit, but as something that is currently "on the path" toward becoming profit. From these final conditions, it could be inferred that a trader who lends to someone—knowing that the borrower will not spend it on things for his soul, health, honor, or estate, but on sins, vanities, or prodigality—cannot charge interest. This is because the primary cause for such a loan is not charity, nor good love, nor helping one’s neighbor, but other ends that are insufficient to say that the loan was made as if forced by fear, or by love and charity for one’s neighbor.

-># S U M M A R Y<-

It is not usury to take payment for a loss caused by non-payment, nor to take what was lost by lending, even if two conditions required by some are not met. Number 60.

It is not usury to collect what an estate (which I refrained from buying at a fair price in order to lend to you) would have paid me in rent, even if I had not lent you as much as I was going to pay for it. Number 61, etc.

It is usury to take the profit that many merchants take for lending at interest the money they were going to trade with, even if they were to trade with others, including many who lend this way every day. Number 62.

<margin>
60
</margin>
<margin>
a Which he calls "extrinsic" [extrarem]. Bartolus and others in Law 1, Code [of Justinian], "Concerning the judgment for that which is [owed]." ¶ It is not regularly owed, Law: "If sterile," section: "When through the seller," Digest, "Concerning the action of purchase."
b Argument from Law: "Wine," Digest, "If a certain thing is sought," and Law 3, section: "If," Digest, "Concerning that which is [owed] in a certain place."
</margin>
**T**HE 17th. It follows that it is also not usury to take the interest that I myself paid to others because it was necessary for me to borrow at interest due to my debtor not paying me at the agreed time. Nor is it usury to take what was lost by selling one's belongings for less than they were worth, or by buying bread and wine at a higher price because the debtor did not pay when he was supposed to. This is because such payment is taken by way of "interest of damage" <term>Damnum emergens: compensation for a specific financial loss caused by the borrower's delay</term>. This is provided that two conditions concur, according to some: namely, that the lender had warned the debtor of that potential damage, and that the lender could not have found anyone to lend to him for free. The second of these conditions is not, in our opinion, necessary. For there is no text or reason proving that a lender is obliged to seek or accept a free loan just so the debtor does not have to pay back what he borrowed. As for the first condition, it is either insufficient or unnecessary. If the interest was "extrinsic" <note>original: "ᵃ," referring to the margin</note>—such as my slaves or livestock dying because you did not pay me the wheat or barley at the agreed time—the warning would be necessary, as a promise to pay for such loss would be required. But if the interest is "intrinsic"—such as the increased value of the wine or wheat that I lent—it is understood and owed even without a specific warning or promise <note>original: "ᵇ," referring to the margin</note>.

<margin>
61
</margin>
<margin>
c Antoninus [of Florence], part 2, title 1, chapter 7, section 15. The common opinion follows his mind insofar as it equates this merchant preparing money to buy goods.
</margin>
¶ The 17th <note>The author repeats "The 17th," likely as a sub-point of his seventeenth major argument.</note>. He who is determined to buy a certain estate or house that yields a yearly income, and finds someone to sell it to him, but refrains from buying it in order to lend to his neighbor (who promises him that interest), may ask the borrower to pay him each year what that estate would have yielded in rent until the money is returned <note>original: "ᶜ," referring to the margin</note>. This is because he takes it as "interest of profit" <term>Lucrum cessans: compensation for the profit a lender misses out on by not having their capital available</term>, just like a trading merchant. Moreover, anyone who sells an estate and delivers it to a buyer who does not pay may charge as interest what the estate rents for,

<margin>
d Law 2 of the Code on Usury <note>Latin: "l. 2. C. de usur."</note>, where the gloss follows Azo, and accepted by others, says it is also permitted by Canon Law.
e In the chapter *Novit* <note>Latin: "In ca. Novit"</note> concerning judgments, note 6, from number 21.

...presents, even if he deposited the money for the payment <gloss>d</gloss>. For the court of conscience, however, this must be limited to cases where the money given for the estate constituted its fair price. For if the estate were worth one thousand ducats, and due to necessity or other considerations (not intended as a gift) it was given for five hundred, he could not take more than half of what it yielded in rent. We have written at length on this for another purpose elsewhere <gloss>e</gloss>, since in the court of conscience, he could not buy more than half of it with that amount of money.

<margin>62</margin>
We add <gloss>†</gloss> however, that if you did not have need of the full one thousand ducats, but only seven hundred, yet by lending those to you I was unable to buy the estate or the merchandise that suited me—whereas with the full amount I could have—and because I lent them to you I ceased to profit: I may then charge you interest based on the full one thousand ducats, as Medina <note>Juan de Medina (1490–1547), a Spanish theologian who wrote on contracts.</note> rightly considered <gloss>d</gloss>.

¶ XVIII. Those who sin without having the intention to trade for a profit <term>interesse</term> <note>In this context, "interesse" refers to the legal compensation for loss or missed profit, which Azpilcueta distinguishes from usury.</note>: for in such cases, there is neither a true nor a likely profit. This also applies to merchants who trade with a certain sum of money and no more, and the rest (which is often much more) they lend at interest. Likewise, those who bring much money to the fairs, and having bought their merchandise, 
<margin>63</margin>
give whatever is left over at interest: for it is certain that there is no true or likely profit to be lost there. This also applies to those who take large sums of money from other partners to give them at interest to various people, not intending or wanting to trade with those funds (even if they trade with others), but only giving them out as feigned interest. And even those who used to give me money at five percent until a certain fair, taking the five from me immediately and paying me only the ninety-five. They did this because they did not intend to trade with it; and even if they did want to trade and took it for a true or likely interest, they could not take it in advance, for the reasons stated above <gloss>b</gloss>. 
<margin>b Above, in section 11.</margin>
Furthermore, it is because they only gave me ninety-five but charged me interest on the full one hundred. They do not realize that these "small" sins are infernal greatnesses <note>A warning that even small, seemingly insignificant acts of usury can lead to eternal damnation.</note>.

<margin>64</margin>
XIX. Not <gloss>†</gloss> without a strong appearance of truth, the matter of the Mount of Piety <note>The "Monte de Piedad" was a charitable institution that provided low-interest loans to the poor, secured by pawns (pledges), to protect them from exploitative moneylenders.</note> and other similar works <gloss>c</gloss> established for the aid of the poor in Italy and elsewhere has seemed like usury to some. For they are organized in this manner: a prelate, a lord, a wealthy man, or a city gives or leaves some grain or money to be lent to the poor for a certain time upon pledges. A small amount is given for each month the loan lasts to maintain those who are in charge of guarding said grain or money, and of lending, collecting, and keeping, preserving, and returning the pledges in due time. Thus, it appears that the extra amount given each month is taken for the act of lending, which seems like usury. 
<margin>65</margin>
<margin>

</margin>
And <gloss>†</gloss> because if this were not usury, then it would not be usury if a private, pious man set aside some money or part of another sum to lend to the poor and charged them something for the burden, labor, and care involved in guarding it, lending it, taking pledges, and returning them. This seems a harsh conclusion, and the same reasoning appears to apply to both cases. Likewise, it seems that the poor person who receives the loan is no more obliged to guard the money or grain lent from that Mount than from that of any other pious man, since it is not his <gloss>a</gloss>, nor will it be until the loan is made.

<meta>This page continues Azpilcueta’s defense of the "Mounts of Piety" (charitable pawnshops), specifically addressing the legality of charging a small fee for their administration. He argues against the strict view of Cardinal Cajetan and aligns himself with the decrees of the Fifth Lateran Council.</meta>

<header>Concerning Chapter 1, Question 14, 3.</header>

...had my hands tied, so that I have not treated this subject either in my university lectures <term>cathedra</term> <note>Literally "chair," referring to a professor's formal teaching position.</note> or outside of them until now. But now I am determined to say that the contrary seems better to me. Firstly, because Pope Leo X in the Lateran Council <margin>e Session 10 under Leo X. The contents of which are referenced by the most learned Juan Medina at folio 153 near the Lateran Council.</note> by a public bull approved the said "Mounts," stating that his predecessors Paul II, Sixtus IV, Innocent VIII, and Julius II had done the same. And he did not approve them (as some feel) *in a common form*, but in that which they call *with certain knowledge* <note>Latin: "ex certa scientia." A legal formula indicating the Pope has personally reviewed and deliberately approved a measure, rather than it passing through routine channels.</note> <margin>d According to the Lateran [decree].</margin>. And he did not even do so merely *with certain knowledge*, but by recounting the arguments of one side and the other, and greatly praising those who organize such Mounts, and the preachers who induced them to do so, and the Popes who granted indulgences to increase them.

<margin>† Decio in the rubric and chapter 1 and chapter *Venerabilis* regarding custom, cited above.</margin>
The second point is † because the response of Cajetan <note>Thomas Cajetan (1469–1534), a leading theologian who argued that any charge above the principal was usury, even in charitable banks.</note> seems very daring, and such that (in my opinion) Saint Thomas would not have dared to give it, and the most learned Medina sufficiently refutes it. And I would even less dare to say what another most learned man says: that everything from that Council was not received. For that response might be appropriate regarding the declarations of human laws that the Council makes (such as the example he places there), but not regarding those of divine laws, which do not cease to be true just because they are not "received." Nor should anyone say that the Council could err in this declaration, since it concerns divine law and whether something is a sin or not <margin>69 f The decision of which belongs to the Roman Pontiff and the Council, according to what we wrote in the repetition of the chapter *Novit* on judgments, note 6, number 10.</margin> ^f^.

Thirdly † because we all agree in saying it is just that those who have the task of guarding, lending, and collecting that money or grain, and of receiving, preserving, and returning the pledges to the poor who receive them, should have an honest salary, and that they ought to have it from those who are obligated to it. The discord lies in this: Cajetan and his followers say that the Mount itself, or the city that took charge of it, is obligated to pay this, and not the poor. But we say that the poor who benefit from that Mount are obligated to it, and not the city nor the Mount, because the Council felt this way <margin>a In the said Session 5, where above, in those words: "He who feels the benefit, must also bear the burden."</margin> ^a^ based on that reasoning. "He who feels the benefit must feel the burden," and thus it was just that they should feel it. For that reasoning is a well-received rule <margin>b Chapter *Qui sentit* from the Rules of Law, Book 6.</margin> ^b^ of the law. And it is clear that that money or grain was not left for the city, nor for the citizens; and thus, neither the city nor another citizen (as a citizen) can benefit from it, but only the poor for whose help it was left, who alone can benefit from it. And if it were lost or diminished, it would be lost or diminished for them alone, and not for others. According to the law, the guarding of a thing commonly belongs to the one for whose benefit it is kept, and for whom it is lost or gained <margin>c Law 1 and law *Necessario* from the Digest on the risk and benefit of a sold thing.</margin> ^c^.

Furthermore † because a bequest or donation would be just and firm if something were given or left to the poor with some charge attached. Thus, ten thousand ducats can be donated and left to them with the charge that they always remain secure in the pile or as debts, and that they benefit from them by borrowing against pledges and returning them at their due dates so that everyone can benefit. And since the benefit is to be for them alone, they should also provide the necessary guarding of it and contribute toward it. It seems they could make no more equal and just contribution than by ordaining that each one pay a certain percentage each month that the loan lasts, provided it is so little that it likely does not exceed the salary deserved by the one who obligates himself with good sureties to <margin>90 d Clementine 1 on census; Law *Secundum naturam* from the Digest on Rules of Law; Argument from Law *Id quod pauperibus* in the Code on bishops and clergy; and Law *Si quis ad declinandam* in the same title.</margin> guard it, collect it, receive pledges, and return and preserve them.

The reasons alleged to the contrary do not stand. To the first, I respond that the poor who borrow do not pay for the lending itself, but for the guarding and management of the Mount, which is guarded and managed for their benefit. To the second, I respond that there is a great difference between a pious man and the said Mount: for his money is his own, and when he does not wish to give it, he cannot be forced to do so. But the ownership of the Mount belongs to the community of the poor, or to some other community with the charge that all the benefit belongs to them—which for this purpose amounts to the same thing—and he who has charge of it must lend even if he does not want to. To the third, I respond by denying that the guarding of that Mount belongs primarily to anyone but the poor, or at least at their expense, since it is for their benefit alone. And as the Council said <margin>e For even Lord Soto confesses one can receive something from them for the obligation he undergoes to lend; and before him, Scotus said this uniquely in his custom, in Book 4, distinction 15, question 2. f In the said Lateran Council, Session 10.</margin> ^f^, since they feel the benefit, they must feel the cost, which is very little. Thus, we see nothing to find fault with in such a holy and well-approved work, and indeed much to praise, and with the praises of some to persuade others to do the same. And although it would be a better work in itself to leave an endowment for the Mount to guard, manage, and lend so that it could lend without any charge, we nevertheless hold as good that which the Holy Apostolic See approved, considering that if that [fee] were taken from the Mount, it would become smaller, and that

<sig>B 4</sig>
<meta>Catchword: and that</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta defends the "Mounts of Piety," arguing that charging a small administrative fee to borrowers is not usury but a just way to cover the costs of managing the charitable fund, as supported by the Fifth Lateran Council.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legality of the "Mounts of Piety" (charitable pawnshops) and begins a new legal resolution (Point 20) regarding the profits derived from dowry pledges. Azpilcueta is transitioning from the general topic of charitable lending to specific cases where taking profit from collateral is not considered usury.</meta>

and that even in some places, with only what that income would cost, a small Mount of Piety could be established.

### S U M M A R Y

It is not usury for a son-in-law to take the fruits <note>In legal terms, "fruits" refers to the profits, harvest, or rents produced by a property.</note> he gathers from a pledge given to him for a promised dowry, according to a new reason provided here—leaving aside the common view—and four others. Number 71. And it makes no difference whether he is a son-in-law or not. Number 74.
All assets are held in common between husband and wife in Portugal. Number 72.
Whoever promises a dowry and does not pay it is seen as promising the profit that could moderately be drawn from it. Number 71 & following.
It is not usury to take a certain amount for a promised dowry each year. Number 74.
Which a widow may also take, etc. Number 75. And such statutes are good, etc. Ibidem.

a Chapter *Salubriter* <note>original: "Salubriter"</note> regarding usury. See Law *Pater* in the Digest regarding fraud.

20. That † it is not usurious for a son-in-law to take the fruits of a productive pledge that his father-in-law gave him as security for the payment of a promised dowry <gloss>a</gloss>. This is because he does not take them by reason of a loan, either overt or disguised, but for another reason.
On this matter, much has been written, as the most learned Doctor Diego de Leyva y Covarrubias <gloss>b</gloss>—who was a student of ours (long ago) in this most celebrated University of Salamanca—records very diligently. He is most devout, providing even greater consolation for this long and laborious pilgrimage of ours, as he publishes it so candidly in his most choice works; and now, for the great luster and approval of his immense learning and virtues, and for the great honor and benefit of the Church of Spain, he is our Most Reverend Archbishop, may God make him most holy. It seems to us, however, that the sufficient reason is still to be found. To say with the Common Opinion that he may take them for the interest of the damage that comes to him, or the profit he ceases to gain <term>lucrum cessans</term> <note>A legal concept referring to the loss of profit that would have been made if the money/asset had been available.</note>, does not satisfy. For it seems that it does not fit the text; and if it were a good reason, it would conclude that there is no difference between a dowry debt and any other debt, which seems a great inconvenience. Furthermore, if it were certain or likely that the husband was not going to lose any of his estate by not being paid the dowry, nor was he going to gain anything with it paid, he could not take the said fruits; nor even when he might lose something or stop gaining, if it did not amount to as much as the fruits. This seems contrary to the interpretation of the ancient custom of never dealing with these accounts in such cases <gloss>c</gloss>. Even less fitting is what others <gloss>d</gloss> say: that he can take them solely for the interest of the cost of maintaining his wife. Because maintaining his wife is not a loss of his estate, but rather doing that which he is obligated to do.

e In the Chapter on Restitution, in the question of usury: Whether it exists.
f Book 6, question 1, article 2, on justice and law.
a In law 4, 6, on the ultimate end of law.
b Chapter *Salubriter* regarding usury.
72
c Which the Portuguese law clearly feels. Book 4, title 14, paragraph *Eposito*.
d Law *Pomponius*. *Philadelphus*. Digest regarding the division of an inheritance.
</margin>
Nor does the new reason of Medina <gloss>e</gloss> satisfy <note>Juan de Medina (1490–1547), an influential Spanish theologian.</note>: that the father-in-law is seen as donating those fruits of the pledge, because the text is not based on any donation. Nor even the most recent reason of the Lord Doctor Soto <gloss>f</gloss> <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a key figure in the School of Salamanca.</note>, which suggests that the husband is obligated to maintain the burdens of marriage, and that among these is the guarding of the dowry. This is because the text is not based on the guarding of the dowry, and because it would follow that in Portugal † nor in other parts where all assets are shared between the husband and the wife, that Papal decision would have no place—which is against all use and custom. Furthermore, because no one until him ever called the guarding of the dowry a "burden of marriage." Rather, its guarding and good utilization is a relief, and the burdens are the expenses he incurs in maintaining himself, his wife, and his house. Nor even the reason of the most learned Fortunio <gloss>a</gloss> <note>Fortunio García de Ercilla, a jurist and colleague of Azpilcueta.</note>—whom we followed in this most celebrated university when we read the title on usury and the chapter <gloss>b</gloss> that speaks of this—is sufficient: namely, that the father is obligated to maintain and give food to his daughter, and that for this reason that text only applies to the son-in-law who receives a productive pledge from his father-in-law. This understanding narrows that text too much. It is because it was always observed in all productive pledges given to any man <gloss>c</gloss> for a dowry with a daughter, with a sister, with a niece, with an orphan, or with any other. And because the reason hinted at by the text applies equally to all. It seems to us, therefore, that the reason for that text was that, considering that the dowry is given as the wife's patrimony <gloss>d</gloss> and to help in her maintenance, and that

<meta>This page continues the legal resolution regarding the profits of a dowry. It argues that income generated from a pledge (collateral) given for an unpaid dowry belongs to the husband to support the household, rather than being counted against the principal debt, thus avoiding the charge of usury.</meta>

# On Chapter 1, Question 14, Case 3.

<margin>e On which see extensively in Baldo, *Novellum*, regarding the dowry.</margin>
<margin>g Argument from the chapter *Per vestras*, regarding donations between husband and wife; chapter *Si de divorcio*; law *Ubi adhuc* and law *Pro oneribus* in the Code, regarding the right of dowries; and law *Proponius* in the Digest, regarding the division of an inheritance.</margin>

73 and that the intention of the one who gives it is not for the dowry itself to be consumed, but that from its profit, aid may be taken for the burdens of marriage. Considering many other particularities that the dowry possesses^e, the one who gives or promises† it does not only promise that dowry expressly; rather, they are seen as tacitly promising for the said matrimonial burdens that aid which can reasonably be drawn from that dowry (while keeping the principal intact) by a man of moderate discretion and diligence. This applies from the time it ought to be paid and from the time he begins to sustain the said burdens until it is paid, without regard to any other interest of the husband. By holding and taking a productive pledge <note>A "productive pledge" refers to collateral that generates income, such as land that produces crops or a building that earns rent.</note> for the security of the promised dowry, they are seen as tacitly agreeing that all, and only, the fruits <note>In legal contexts, "fruits" refers to the profits, harvest, or rents produced by a property.</note> thereof shall be taken as payment for what was tacitly promised^g. We are moved to this: first, that whoever promises a hundred ducats for certain burdens with the intention that the principal remains intact, tacitly promises the profit that can be drawn from them until they are given, after the burdens have commenced. Secondly, that general custom conforms to this, and that the text itself hints at it by saying^g that often the fruits of the dowry are not enough to sustain the burdens of marriage. By this, it seems to presuppose that whoever promises a dowry is obliged to pay the profit that can be drawn from the dowry until it is paid: and as much of the matrimonial burdens as a man of moderate discretion and diligence can draw from that dowry, while keeping the principal intact. For this reason, without scruple,

<margin>b In those words of the said chapter *Salubriter* regarding usury: "Since frequently the fruits of the dowry are not sufficient to sustain the burdens of marriage."</margin>
<margin>a In the said chapter *Salubriter*, when Salamanca interpreted it, following the aforementioned Fortunius, as cited above.</margin>

74 we have responded against our previous position^a. That any husband (even if he is not the son-in-law of the one who gave him the productive pledge) may enjoy the fruits without counting them toward the principal sum of the dowry. And he may do this even if he gives the promisor of the dowry as long a term as he wishes, having taken some productive thing as a pledge, unless it is expressly agreed that he should count the fruits as part of the dowry: because any disposition is understood to be made in accordance with the law^b and custom. This holds provided that the promise of the dowry was for the immediate present, even if a delay were granted for the payment upon taking a productive pledge. For the same reason, we responded that the husband may reach an agreement with the one who promised him the dowry and does not pay it, that until it is paid, he shall give him each year (as aid for the burdens of marriage) roughly as much as a man of moderate discretion and diligence could draw from that dowry, while keeping it safe^c. For the

<margin>b Chapter *Cum dilectus* regarding custom; and chapter *Causam quae* regarding rescripts; chapter *Cum M.* regarding constitutions; the final law in the Code regarding bail; with many additions by Felinus in the said chapter *Cum M.* and *Causam quae* and by others elsewhere.</margin>
<margin>c Which Panormitanus holds in the said chapter *Salubriter*, as do many others cited by Doctor Diego [Covarrubias] above; and Soto also holds this, although the contrary seems more widely accepted, as Juan López says in his repetition of the chapter *Per vestras*, note 6.</margin>
<margin>d Argument from the law *Cum quid* in the Digest, regarding certain requests, with the extensive notes there.</margin>

75 †same reason, what the Lord Doctor Soto pointed out seems right to us: namely, that even the husband who received the dowry, and his heirs in case of divorce, must give the widowed or separated woman the maintenance <note>Original: "alimentos." In Spanish law, this refers to the necessary support for food, clothing, and housing.</note> that can be provided while keeping the dowry safe, until it is paid back to her. Because just as the one who promised it was seen, in case of doubt, to be obliged to provide these until he paid it; so it seems that, in case of doubt, the one who received it was tacitly seen to be obliged to provide them until he returned it to her for whose support and patrimony it was given. And thus it is not received by way of usury from a disguised loan, but by way of a promise for the safekeeping of the dowry itself, which tacitly results from its nature and the purpose for which it is given and taken, and for which it must be preserved until it ceases to be a dowry^d. For the same reason, we believe that without scruple one may observe the statutes existing in some lands, stating that whoever promises a dowry must pay a certain percentage each year until it is paid—and likewise for the husband and his heirs until they return the dowry to the widow or separated woman. Because, as has been said, they do not command this payment by way of a disguised loan, but by way of declaration and determination of a just and indeterminate debt, always subject to due correction.

# Resolutory Commentary on Usury.

<margin>Regarding the external forum, number 94.</margin>

A **real annuity** <note>Spanish: *censo real*. An annuity secured by real estate or "real" property.</note> cannot be placed upon a free person, number 83. Nor can a **personal annuity**, nor a right of pledge, number 91. Especially regarding the <term>external forum</term> <note>The "external forum" refers to the public legal courts of the Church or State, which judge based on evidence, as opposed to the "internal forum" of confession and conscience.</note>, number 94. The seller loses nothing if the purchased item perishes, number 83.

Buying with a **repurchase agreement** <note>Latin: *pactum de retrovendendo*. A clause allowing the seller to buy back the property at a fixed price.</note> and a lower price is presumed to be usurious, number 84. As is the sale of animals that do not exist, number 86.

A **real annuity** for non-essential things is a harm to the republic, number 89. And even more so a **personal annuity**, number 92 and the four following.

A free man cannot be given as a pledge, nor as a slave for debt, number 91 and 92. Although he can be sold, number 93.

Exhortation to dissuade the use of **personal annuities**, number 99 and following.

In chapter 17, from number 232.
b As reported by Innocent in the chapter *In ciuitate* (In the city), regarding usury.
c In the said chapter *In ciuitate*.
d Institutes, on purchase, at the beginning and the first section.
