**Point 21.** That $\dagger$ with good reason there is great doubt among great Doctors as to if, and when, the sale and purchase of <term>censos</term> <note>A *censo* was a financial contract where a capital sum was provided in exchange for a perpetual or long-term annual payment (annuity). Because the Church forbade charging interest on simple loans, the *censo* was a legal way to earn a return on capital by "buying" a future stream of income.</note> is, or ought to be presumed, usurious. We have spoken on this many times in public lectures, in councils, and in answers to queries, and finally, in the first and second editions of the *Manual for Confessors*^a we wrote on it, adding something for everyone. Now, in this third edition, we have removed what we put there and referred it to this work, because the many additions that occurred to us could not easily fit there. We say now: Firstly, that a **censo** is a right to receive a certain pension of money, or of another useful thing, by the year, month, or other timeframe. In ancient times, some^b doubted whether the purchase of a money-based *censo* was licit, even if it were perpetual and long-standing. This was because it seemed the same to lend you a hundred ducats for twenty years so that you give me five in profit each year, as it was to buy an annuity of five per year from you for a hundred ducats. However, through the aforementioned **Innocent**^c <note>Pope Innocent IV (d. 1254), a major figure in Canon Law.</note> and nearly everyone else, it is now accepted that the purchase of a perpetual *censo* established prior to the sale is licit. This is because it is a "saleable thing," and giving a price for it is not lending, but buying^d. Nevertheless, it bears a greater resemblance to usury than the purchase of other types of estates.

<margin>
e In that formal dispute which begins *Antiquis & modernis temporibus* (In ancient and modern times), which Giovanni di Anagni reports extensively in the said chapter *In ciuitate*.
f On contracts, question 79.
a On commerce, question 68.
</margin>
<margin>77</margin>
¶ Secondly, that $\dagger$ **Pietro degli Ancharani**^e and some others said that the sale of a *censo* that is newly created is not licit, because it appears that one is buying something before it exists, and that a "purchase" is being faked to cover a loan. But the common opinion holds that it is licit: for just as I can graciously establish for you a *censo* of one, two, or more ducats upon my estate by way of a gift, I can also do so by way of a sale, as **Conrad**^f <note>Conrad Summenhart (d. 1502), a German theologian who wrote extensively on economic ethics.</note> and others declare. However, this comparison is worth little for the **external forum**, because in the establishment of a *censo* by gift, one cannot suspect the fraud of usury, whereas in one established for money, one can—a point others do not notice. We believe, however, with the common opinion, that this alone is not sufficient evidence to presume said fraud. Yet, there is a greater suspicion of usury in the purchase of a *new* annuity than in an *old* one, as **Charles Dumoulin**^a <note>Original: *Carolo Molineo*. A famous French jurist known for his work on contracts and usury.</note> well said.

<margin>
b In the said chapter *In ciuitate*, and extensively by Panormitanus in dispute 5.
c Consultation 123.
</margin>
<margin>78</margin>
¶ Thirdly, $\dagger$ that the purchase of a *censo* for the life of the buyer, or the seller, or both, is also licit, with a pact that it shall expire upon their death without the obligation to return the purchase price, according to **Innocent** and the common opinion^b. Although **Filippo Decio**^c said that it could be presumed usurious; and this is true when other conjectures sufficient for such a conclusion concur with it. ¶ Fourthly, that the purchase of a *censo* for a certain number of years, such as six, twenty-five, or twenty, is also licit. This is clear when the total rent of all those years does not amount to more than the price taken for it. But if it amounts to more (as if for a rent of ten per year for fifteen years, one gave a hundred ducats), there would be greater suspicion. Still (if the price was just), according to the judgment of a good man, the purchase would be licit, nor would it be presumed as a way to cloak usury, as **Conrad**^d declares.

<margin>
d As cited above, questions 79 and 80.
e Martin V in the *Extravagantes*, 1, on purchases. Callixtus in the *Extravagantes*, 2, same title 11.
</margin>
<margin>79</margin>
However, this type is closer to being presumed usurious than the others. Fifthly, $\dagger$ that the purchase of a **redeemable annuity** <note>Spanish: *censo al quitar*. A contract where the person paying the annuity has the right to "cancel" or "redeem" it by paying back the original capital.</note> is also licit; that is, the seller can cancel and redeem it whenever they wish, as two Popes declared in two of their <term>extravagantes</term>^e <note>Decrees or papal constitutions that were not originally part of the main body of Canon Law but were "circulating outside" it.</note>. It is, however, to be noted that the said Popes did not explicitly declare that every purchase of a *censo*...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "censo al quitar" (redeemable annuity), specifically listing the eight conditions required for such a contract to be considered legally and morally licit (non-usurious) according to the "Extravagantes" (papal decrees). It also references contemporary theological debates involving Bartolomé de Carranza and Domingo de Soto.</meta>

# Regarding Chapter 1, Question 14, Question 3.

...of redeemable annuities being licit, and should be presumed as such. For they only declared as licit those contained in their <term>Extravagants</term> <note>Latin: *Extravagantes*. These were papal decrees that circulated outside the main organized bodies of canon law, such as the *Decretum* or the *Decretals*.</note>, which were established with certain conditions. Of these, the first was that the seller assigned a specific estate or property upon which the annuity was established. The second, that only that specific property remained obligated for the payment of it, and not the seller himself, nor his other assets. <margin>f Which the aforementioned second Extravagant expresses.</margin> The third, that a competent price was given. The fourth, that the entire price was paid immediately. The fifth, that the seller was given the faculty to redeem it in whole or in part, when and how he most desired. The sixth, that the seller was not obligated to redeem the annuity. The seventh, that if the said estate were lost, the annuity would be lost. The eighth, that the estate upon which it was placed yielded at least as much as the sold annuity. We gathered these conditions long ago in this university by reading the said Extravagants, and later they were reduced to six by the very <margin>g In the summary of the council, page 618.</margin> <margin>80</margin> renowned, most religious, and most learned doctor, Brother † **Bartolomé de Carranza** <note>A prominent Spanish Dominican theologian and later Archbishop of Toledo, who played a major role in the Council of Trent.</note>, great honor of the Dominicans and our fellow countryman from Navarre, who out of great humility and virtue declined to accept a great bishopric in days past.

<margin>h Among whose number is Master Soto in book 6, question 1, article 5, on justice and law.</margin> ¶ **Point 6.** That some h say the second and seventh conditions mentioned above are not proven in those texts: namely, that only the estate upon which the annuity is placed should remain obligated for its payment, and that if it is lost, the annuity is lost. They say this because those texts do not state that the annuity could not be demanded from those who sold it if the estates upon which it was placed were lost, but rather that the *money* for which it was bought could not be demanded; and that if their authors felt that the annuity could not be demanded either, they would have said so as well. These authors, however (in our opinion), do not have justice on their side. Firstly, because they did not fail to say it because they did not believe it, but because it was not doubted—for it seems that just as when the foundation is removed the wall built upon it falls, so when the estates upon which the annuity was founded are lost, the annuity itself is lost <margin>a Because when the foundation is removed, it is necessary that what is built upon it falls. Chapter *Cum Paulus*. 1. q. 1.</margin> a. Therefore, the only doubt was whether those who took the money were obligated to return it, since the annuity for which it was given ceased <margin>b Argument from the chapter *Cum cessante causa*, the effect must cease. Regarding appeals. Law *Agere*. section *Quamvis*. Digest on law.</margin> b, with the <margin>81</margin> loss of the estates; and to this doubt the said Popes respond: no. Secondly †. Because those Extravagants <margin>c 1. and 2. on purchase.</margin> c declared (as a matter of great doubt) that once the estates upon which the annuity was placed were lost, the sellers were not obligated to restore the money they took for it; and it is clear that there was no need to declare this if they remained obligated to pay the annuity from the lost estates: because even if they were not lost, they were not obligated to return the money, nor to redeem the annuity, as is evident from the tenor of the decrees.

For this reason, in the said editions we said that some of the said conditions were not necessary, and after this, the said **Master Soto** e, has said in effect that none of the said conditions is necessary, except for that of the just price, and that the seller does not remain obligated to redeem the annuity.

<margin>83</margin> <margin>f 1. and 2. on purchase.</margin> ¶ **Point 8.** † Having thought much upon this, it seems to us that the first of the said eight conditions is necessary: because the said Extravagants f indicate it, and because we are of the opinion that an annuity cannot be established upon a free person, as we will soon prove, at least without the presumption of usury. And because here we are treating of a **real annuity** <note>An annuity tied to "real" property or land.</note>, which <margin>g In the following section.</margin> is established upon an estate; and because an accident cannot exist without a subject <margin>h Law *Si servum*. section 1. Digest on the action of purchase. Baldus on law 2. Code on the possession of goods against the tablets.</margin> h, and the annuity in relation to the estate on which it is placed is like an accident of it. And because one never reads in law of such an annuity as we speak of that was not established upon some certain thing. The second condition also seems necessary to us. The said Extravagants <margin>i In the said 6th point.</margin> indicate it, as has been said i. And because, if the person of the seller and his other goods remained obligated for the payment of it, it would not be a purchase of a **real annuity**, of which we speak, but a **personal** one, which we are not treating of now, or the establishment of a pledge and an obligation to secure the money given and the payment of the annuity, under which name usury is cloaked, which is in effect what is intended. And because it is against the nature of purchase and sale that the seller should obligate himself and his goods perpetually to the security of the thing sold, even if it perishes: for the thing purchased, if it perishes, must perish for the buyer after...

<header>Resolutory Commentary on Usury.</header>

<margin>
a To the whole title of the Digest and Code, on the risk and benefit of the sold thing. <note>original: "Toto. titu. ff. et C. de peric. & cōmod. rei vendi."</note>
b To the whole title on eviction, in the Digest and Code. <note>original: "Toto tit. de euit. ff. & C."</note>
c Law "si," regarding buying. <note>original: "l. si. de empt."</note>

...the buyer after delivery ᵃ, and not for the seller. And because it is one thing to obligate oneself—as he who sells an annuity established upon a certain estate—to the fact that the property is his and that he had the power to place the annuity upon it, which is just ᵇ; and it is quite another to obligate oneself to pay the annuity from that estate even if the estate is lost, which is unjust. Similarly, it is one thing for the seller of an estate to remain obligated to guarantee that he had the right to sell it, which is just; and another to remain obligated to pay its fruits even if it is lost, which is unjust and against the entire nature of purchase and sale, as is appropriate according to natural, human, canon, and civil law. The third <margin>84</margin> condition, of a competent price—although it may not be strictly necessary for the purchase of the annuity to be called usurious in the <term>forum of conscience</term> <note>The internal moral judgment of an individual before God, as opposed to the "external forum" of legal courts.</note> if the buyer truly intended to buy and the seller to sell—is nevertheless necessary so that it is not considered unjust, with

</margin>
an obligation to make restitution, as we have discussed at length elsewhere ᶜ. It is also necessary so that in the external forum it is not presumed to be usurious; for an insufficient price, combined with a <term>pact of redemption</term> <note>original: "pacto de retrouendendo"; a clause allowing the seller to buy back the property or annuity.</note>, creates a presumption of a usurious purchase, as was stated in the *Manual* ᵈ, especially regarding the purchase of an annuity, which by its nature carries some suspicion of it, as we said above ᵉ. The fourth <margin>85</margin> condition, that the payment be made immediately and in full, also seems necessary, even regarding the forum of conscience. For the buyer should not believe that the seller is granting him credit by his own free will, even if he confesses it to be so, since he places an annuity on his estate out of present necessity. Likewise, one should not believe a person who takes borrowed money when they

<margin>
f Following Thomas Aquinas, *Secunda Secundae*, question 78, article 1, reply to the seventh. <note>original: "Post Tho. 2. Sec. q. 78. art. 1. Ad septimum."</note>
g In the six prior points mentioned.
h Argument from chapter 1, on seeking what is due, and the section "Furthermore" in the Institutes, on actions. <note>original: "Arg. c. 1. de p? peti. et §. Plus autē. Insti. de actio."</note>
</margin>
promise and pay usury of their own free will, even if they say so, according to all authorities ᶠ, who say the promise is made under the duress that necessity imposes. It also seems necessary (regarding the external forum) because in that context, such credit given to a man in such great need signifies a simulated loan for usury, because the purchase of an annuity is very similar to it for the reasons said above ᵍ. And because it seems that he who does not immediately pay the person who, out of necessity, sells an annuity on his assets, is giving less than the <term>just price</term> ʰ. The fifth condition, that it cannot be redeemed in parts, can also be called necessary for the effect that the purchase of the annuity would be unjust if more were not given for it when a pact is included that it cannot be redeemed in parts, than when the contrary is stipulated. It is also necessary for the effect that it be presumed usurious if more is not given when the former is included than when the latter is. This is because less than the just value is being given; for which reason, and because of the pact of being able to redeem (which is the <term>right of redemption</term>), usury is presumed, as has been said. If, however, by including a pact that it cannot be re- <margin>86</margin> deemed in parts, more is given as is reasonable, it would not be necessary to include the contrary contained in this fifth condition. The sixth condition, according to everyone, is necessary. The seventh follows from the second, and with it, it has been proven to be necessary. The eighth, that the estate yields as much or more than the annuity placed upon it, seems to some not to be necessary. This is because it is more favorable to the buyer of the annuity than to the seller, since the seller, by leaving the estate with its annuity to the buyer, remains free by the second condition. And because it does not seem to be gathered from the said <term>Extravagants</term> <note>Papal decrees circulating outside the primary canon law collections.</note>. Nor did we (when we read them here) gather it; but it is not so, because no one truly buys an annuity on an estate that they know yields nothing. And because it is held as certain—as we said in the *Manual* ᵃ,

<margin>
a Chapter 17, numbers 229.
b Under the word "usury," 1, question 7.
c In book 4, distinction 15, question 46.
d Under the word "usury," 2, question 6.
e Above in this same commentary, from number 76 to 79.
</margin>
following <term>Angelus</term> ᵇ, <term>Major</term> ᶜ, and <term>Sylvester</term> ᵈ <note>Refers to Angelus de Clavasio, John Major, and Sylvester Prierias, prominent scholastic theologians and legal commentators.</note>—that whoever buys possessions or animals from those who do not have them, or more than they have, and rents them back to them, is a usurer. To which, certainly, he who buys an annuity on land that does not exist, or that yields only as much as the annuity itself, is similar.

¶ Ninth: † we conclude from what has been said that it must be held that all the aforementioned <margin>87</margin> eight conditions, in the manner described, are necessary. First, for the reasons touched upon in their verification. Second, because (as noted above ᵉ) every purchase of annuities (even ancient ones) has some resemblance to a usurious one. This is even more true for new ones being established, even if they are perpetual and without a pact of being able to be removed or redeemed; and much more so for an annuity with a pact of being able to be redeemed and removed. This resemblance is so great that in the said <term>Extravagants</term> it is said that even when made with the said conditions—which diminish the presumption of usury and injustice—it was held to be usurious by many people who would undo them as such until it was declared by those decrees that those made with these conditions were licit, signifying that others should not be presumed as such. Furthermore † because the pact they call <term>redemption</term> <margin>88</margin> in any purchase of any thing brings such a great suspicion of usury, that it and the lack of a just price...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on whether redeemable annuities (censos) are usurious. The previous page established that for such a contract to be licit, it must involve a fair price and the transfer of real risk. Here, the author examines how the lack of these conditions creates a "presumption of usury" and critiques the practice of securing such debts against a person's body rather than specific property.</meta>

<header>On Chapter 1, Question 14, Part 1.</header>

<margin>89</margin>

...[deviations] from the just price make one presume it is usurious, according to the singular gloss **f**. And since using merchandise as a newly established annuity to take money for it, without the aforementioned conditions being met, seems to carry as much or greater presumption of usury than a low price, it follows that this—along with the agreement that it can be redeemed (which in effect is a <term>right of repurchase</term> <note>original: "retrouē-dedo" / "retrovendendo." A clause allowing the seller to buy back the asset, often used to disguise a loan as a sale.</note>)—will create a presumption of usury if done without the conditions mentioned above that would diminish this presumption. Furthermore, † even if this point could not be proven by necessity against someone holding the opposite view, neither could the opposite be proven against one holding this view; this greatly reduces the ease of giving and taking annuities. It is all the more appropriate to remove this ease for the good of souls, the honor and estates of individuals, and even of republics (as far as they can be legally protected), considering how many people burden themselves with these annuities for unnecessary, superfluous, and even evil things—to eat, dress, idle, and socialize excessively. Being unable to carry the burden of them, nor maintain the lifestyle and clothing they adopted through the sale, they easily break their faith and word and flee (leaving behind their wives and children) forever, to the great harm of the republic and private interests **a**. To this end, Innocent IV, a most grave authority—though he was among the first to say this purchase of new annuities was licit—added that all Christians should distance themselves from it, a point in which no one has contradicted him. For all these reasons, a very holy statute **b** remains justified, along with what is written in his extensive Commentary.

<margin>90</margin>
<margin>c Book 6, Question 1, Article 1, on justice and law. d On contracts, Question 74 and 75.</margin>
¶ Tenth: † from this it follows that one should not hold the opinion of the aforementioned Master Domingo de Soto **c**, who held that it is licit for someone to place an annuity upon their person alone without pointing out or naming any specific goods. Conrad **d** and Charles Dumoulin **e** also held this previously, the latter praising his Philip Melanchthon <note>A German Protestant reformer and collaborator of Martin Luther; Azpilcueta, a Catholic jurist, finds this praise "unnecessary" or "excused."</note>—a quite unnecessary thing—and calling the common opinion "superstitious." [We reject this] first, because of the preceding conclusion and all its foundations. Second, because it is affirmed otherwise by Innocent IV, Hostiensis, Joannes Andreae, Ancharano, Antoninus, and Cardinal Panormitanus, and Joannes de Anania in one part **f**, and Panormitanus in another **g**, Angelus de Clavasio **h**, Bartolomé de Cassaneo **i**, Lorenzo de Rodulphis **k**, Saint Antoninus **l**, and almost everyone else. Third, because it is a new invention never practiced in Roman governance—at least since it has been Christian—to settle an annuity and pension upon a free person as one settles it upon an estate **m**.

<margin>91</margin>
<margin>f In the chapter *In civitate*, on usury. g In dispute 5, penultimate column. h In the *Summa*, under the word "Usury," section 78. i In the *Catalog*, part 12, consideration 99.</margin>
Furthermore, † in the said governance it is ordained that a free man should not be given as a pledge **n**, nor placed under the <term>real right</term> <note>A right that stays with the property itself, regardless of who owns it.</note> called *ius pignoris* (the right of pledge). So much so, that the creditor who takes such a pledge falls under penalty **o**; and it is certain that the right of an annuity is a greater burden than the right of a pledge. Because through the latter, the debtor gives nothing, nor does anything more than secure the debt, whereas through the annuity, he does **p**. And for this reason, he who has money prefers to give it for the purchase of an annuity rather than for a pledge of gold.

<margin>92</margin>
<margin>m Wherefore, as an evil example, it should be banished. Chapter *Cum consuetudinis*, on customs. k In the chapter *Consuluit*, on usury, part 2, question 12. l Part 2, title 1, chapter 8.</margin>
Furthermore, † although (according to the governance of the Hebrews) the debtor gave himself and even his children as slaves, as Sacred Scripture signifies **q**—even though they did not become slaves properly and entirely, as Saint Thomas says **r**—and although in the time when the <term>Twelve Tables</term> <note>The earliest code of Roman law, c. 450 BCE, known for its strictness.</note> of Roman laws were made, when their governance was more barbaric and less humane, the debtor who had nothing with which to pay gave himself as a temporary slave to the creditor (as Guillaume Budé **a** and others declare). From this hardness followed great evils and great danger to the Roman people, as Livy recounts in one part **b**, and in another **c** he says it was ordered that money should not be given for profit, and in another **d** that because of a great cruelty and filthiness committed by a creditor against a young debtor, and other evils that followed from it, the Senate freed all debtors from the obligation of their bodies, ordering that the goods of debtors could be taken, but not their bodies. Even Solon <note>An Athenian lawmaker who abolished debt-slavery in the 6th century BCE.</note>, seeing that by placing the right of pledge upon their persons men found those who would lend to them under usury and burdened themselves with it, ordered that no one could obligate his body as a pledge for what he might borrow, as Plutarch declares **e**. How much more should it be ordered that one could not place—

<meta>This page continues Azpilcueta’s rigorous legal and moral argument against "personal annuities"—financial contracts secured only by a person's individual labor or status rather than tangible property like land. He argues that such contracts are not only economically dangerous but also a form of "cloaked usury" that undermines social charity and leads to a new form of debt slavery.</meta>

<header>Resolutory Commentary on Usury.</header>

<margin>93</margin>
...establish an annuity? Since (as has been said) it is a heavier burden. Secondly, because to introduce the power to establish annuities upon persons alone would be to return them to that ancient cruelty <note>Azpilcueta refers here to the pre-Roman and early Roman practice of *nexum*, where a debtor could be enslaved by a creditor, a practice later abolished for its social harms.</note> which was the cause of great evils. Furthermore, † because even if we were to hold that one could sell oneself and become the temporary or perpetual slave of another—it being licit according to <term>natural law</term> <note>The set of universal moral principles believed to be inherent in human nature and discoverable by reason, distinct from "civil" or "human" law.</note> and not forbidden by divine or human law—it would still not be licit to establish a right of annuity upon oneself while remaining free. This is because just human law forbids placing a right of <term>pledge</term> <note>original: "prenda." A security or lien placed on property to guarantee a debt.</note> upon a free person (while they remain free); consequently, it forbids a right of annuity, which is greater than that of a pledge. There is no doubt that the legislator, if asked about this, would respond the same regarding the

f And thus it must be held as law, according to the singular gloss on the law *Tale pactum*, paragraph *Qui provocavit*, in the Digest, on agreements.

annuity as he did regarding the pledge **f**. Furthermore, † even if the reasons of the opposing side were to prove that (before God) this <term>redeemable annuity</term> <note>original: "censo al quitar." An annuity that the debtor can cancel by paying back the principal sum.</note> could licitly be established upon the person alone without designating other assets, before men it must be presumed that it is established fraudulently to cloak usury. For if the purchase of an estate with a pact to be able to redeem it whenever one wishes is often presumed usurious **g**, and if very grave doctors have held that the purchase of a new annuity (even if it were perpetual and established upon estates alone without personal obligation) must be presumed usurious; and if many others have held that at the very least the purchase of redeemable annuities must be presumed as such—even if made with the aforementioned conditions that diminish this presumption—and the doubt was so great that two Popes had to declare that those made with those conditions were licit **h**: how shall we dare to say that the purchase of an annuity established upon the person alone is not,

<margin>95</margin>
or should not be presumed, usurious? Furthermore, † the contrary opinion closes the door to the petitioning of a <term>gracious loan</term> <note>An interest-free loan given out of charity or friendship.</note>. For one will be ashamed to ask for it from someone who can respond that he should ask for it as the price of an annuity, which he can easily establish upon his own person for that purpose. It closes the door to charity and gives wings to greed; for few will lend graciously when they can give it for the price of an annuity that can so easily be established upon the borrower. It opens the door to <term>cloaked usury</term> <note>original: "usura paliada." Usury hidden under the guise of a legal contract like a sale or an insurance agreement.</note>, so that with banners flying it may occupy the whole world; for everyone, without fear of any penalty, could give money upon a redeemable annuity established upon their persons. It opens † the door to introduce

<margin>96</margin>
much greater usury, without regard for any interest, than that which the Roman laws permitted. For since an annuity is worth so much less as it is less secure—and that which is placed upon the person alone (especially if they are poor and of little industry or value) is less secure than that placed upon an estate—and as there can be an annuity justly established upon an estate at one for ten <note>A 10% rate, following the papal decrees mentioned in margin 94.</note> (as the said *Extravagantes* prove), so one could find an annuity justly established upon a person at one for six or seven. Consequently, it would come out to fourteen or fifteen percent per year, which is greater than the greatest rate the civil law permitted, even to those who lent with security, which is the <term>hundredth</term> <note>original: "centesima." In Roman law, a 1% monthly interest rate, totaling 12% annually.</note>; which as we said above **i** is twelve percent per year.

<margin>
97
i Above, in this same commentary, number 14.
</margin>
It opens † the door for the whole world to walk in debt. For as pride and trickery have grown, and as there are few needy people who will not take money if it is given to them on the sole obligation of their persons and goods, so there will be many who will establish annuities upon themselves to be redeemed if anyone wishes to buy them; and as greed is so heightened, there will be no lack of those who want to buy them. For if usury were permitted, there would be many who would borrow under it; and in this regard there is little difference between usury and an annuity, for commonly he who obligates his person to usury will also establish an annuity of the same amount upon his person; and he who lends at usury without pledges or sureties will also com-

<margin>98</margin>
monly buy a personal annuity. And † thus will come almost all those who little fear the hardness of the end of vices and enjoy the sweetness of their beginnings, to eat, dress, idle, and play the rogue, loading themselves with annuities—which these would establish upon themselves without the others knowing, and those without these knowing. Afterward, being unable to pay the annuities and even less to redeem them, they would walk like slaves; and for shame before the world and fear of jail, they would go to foreign lands, leaving their wives and children lost. As we see many do, finding themselves burdened by annuities they placed on lands they did not have (and feigned to have) or that were only worth as much as the annuity; and it seems to them that they cannot free themselves from the obligation to pay, seeing their persons obligated, and those of...

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on "personal annuities" (debt secured by a person’s labor rather than property) and begins the analysis of "exchanges" (currency trading). Azpilcueta argues that easy credit leads to social collapse, bankruptcy, and vanity across all social classes.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>On Chapter 1. 14. q. 3.</header>

<margin>99</margin> ...their heirs to the payment of the **annuity** <term>censo: a financial contract where a person "sold" the right to collect annual payments from their property or labor in exchange for a lump sum; a common way to bypass usury laws</term> that they sold upon it. Let us therefore embrace the common opinion, which is so useful to souls, honors, and estates. Let us flee from these novelties, which are most pernicious to all three. Let us persuade † the governors of the republic that they should not allow the enforcement of obligations for **redeemable annuities** <term>censos al quitar: a loan-like annuity that the debtor could "buy back" or cancel by returning the principal</term>, even those established upon real estate, unless they meet the conditions under which those approved by the <term>Apostolic See</term> <note>The central authority of the Catholic Church in Rome.</note> were established. And even less should they permit personal annuities, which Spain has never seen until today—at least not since it has been Christian. Let us help as much as we lawfully can to remove the ease with which these are given and taken. Let us consider that this ease is a great cause of the disorder that grows daily in our Spain: how some become merchants using only the wealth of others, and by adopting the house, dress, and lifestyle of rich men, they gain credit with which they deceive and rob everyone. Afterward, they go bankrupt and flee, not only from their land and the grace of their King and his governors, but even from heaven and <margin>100</margin> the grace of Him who governs it. Let us consider † that this same ease is the cause of the disorder in which many gentlemen and honored men add expenses to expenses and debts to debts for the vanities of superfluous feasts, households, clothing, and finery. With these, they diminish the necessary payments of their debts, the wages owed to their servants, the maintenance of their horses, and the exercise of arms necessary to their station. Let us look at how this same ease is the cause of the disorder where many laborers and tradesmen eat, drink, dress, and idle too much with a pride abominable to God, pretending to have much while secretly having everything tied up in annuities. If these people could not find annuities, usury, and **fictitious sales** <term>mohatras: a fraudulent practice where a debtor "buys" an item on credit at a high price and "sells" it back immediately for cash at a lower price to hide a high-interest loan</term>, they would get by (as Plutarch <margin>a) In the book *On Avoiding Usury*, where you may gather many things suited to our purpose.</margin> a) well signifies) with eating and drinking less and dressing simply, suffering their poverty with a restraint and patience very pleasing to the Divine Goodness. May He give us grace to know our own smallness and the need to humble ourselves and stay within our shells, rather than exalting ourselves and leaving them only to lose our way. Amen.

# Resolutory commentary on Exchanges, regarding the beginning of the final chapter on usury.

To establish the foundation for what we have agreed to say regarding the **Exchanges** <term>Cambios: the business of currency exchange and transferring funds between different locations, often involving complex fees that touched upon usury</term> of our time, we declare the beginning of the last chapter on usury, the words of which are these:

->Gregory IX in the final chapter on usuries.<-

> *To one navigating or going to the fairs, he who lends a certain quantity of money, because he took the risk upon himself, if he is to receive something beyond the principal, must be judged a usurer.* <note>original Latin: "Nauiganti, vel eunti ad nundinas certam mutuans pecuniæ quantitatem, eo quòd suscepit in se periculum, recepturus aliquid vltra fortem, vfurarius est censendus." This decree by Pope Gregory IX (c. 1234) was a cornerstone of medieval finance law, suggesting that even if a lender takes on the risk of the voyage, taking a profit on a loan is still usury.</note>

Whoever lends a certain quantity of money to one who sails or goes to the fairs: because he took the risk upon himself, hoping to take something more than what he lent, he must be judged as a usurer.

<meta>The text transitions from discussing the social impact of debt to the legal definitions of usury in international trade and currency exchange.</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta concludes his critique of annuities by warning that easy credit leads to social decay, bankruptcy, and vanity. He then transitions to the topic of currency exchange, citing a papal decree that labels even risk-bearing loans to merchants as usury.</summary>
<keywords>annuities (censos), social disorder, bankruptcy, vanity, money exchange (cambios), Pope Gregory IX, usury laws, mohatras, Plutarch</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of "personal annuities" (p. 31) into a detailed analysis of "exchanges" (currency trading) and "nautical usury" (loans involving maritime risk). Azpilcueta is specifically interpreting a decree by Pope Gregory IX regarding whether charging for the risk of a journey constitutes usury.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>Resolutory Commentary on Exchanges.</header>

## S U M M A R Y

1. A person is a usurer if and when they lend money while taking the risk upon themselves for one who must navigate or transport the loan elsewhere. Number 1 & 2. Also, if they lend something that is not money. Number 6.
2. This chapter has two interpretations, and which is the better one. Number 1 & 2.
3. An example does not restrict a rule. Number 2.
4. One who affirms something of one person does not deny it of their peer; nor the reverse. Number 2.
5. Gregory IX was concise, pithy, and brief. Number 2. He is accustomed to determining doubtful matters. Number 3.
6. "This is" signifies truth, and "must be considered" (*censendus*) signifies a presumption. Number 3.
7. What is **nautical usury** <term>nautical usury: Latin "foenus nauticum," an ancient type of loan where the lender assumed the sea-risk and charged a higher interest rate in return</term>. Number 3. It is forbidden today. Number 4. In this manner. Number 6.
8. Insuring, by charging a just price for it; to whom it is lawful and to whom it is not. Number 5.
9. One who lends is in a worse condition—when and why—than one who does not lend. Number 5.
10. A guardian, curator, or judge may not buy the assets of a subject, ward, or minor. Number 5.
11. What a penitent who confesses to having lent and insured shall be commanded. Number 6.
12. "Money" (*Pecunia*) in Latin; how it signifies all temporal goods. Number 6.
13. A **presumption of law** <term>presumptio iuris et de iure: a legal presumption that is so strong it cannot be rebutted by evidence</term>; what it is, and the nature of the one in this chapter. Number 6.
14. One is a usurer even if they lend to the rich for profit. Number 7.
15. A guarantor may charge something for providing a guarantee, except when, etc. Number 7.
16. **Exchanges** <term>cambios: the business of exchanging different currencies, often involving bills of exchange between cities</term> are lawful. Number 9. As is declared from there onward. Number 8.

---

<margin>
a Glosses by Hostiensis, Joannes Andreae, Panormitanus, and the Common Opinion.
b In Book 4, distinction 15, question 31, near the end.
c Under the word "usury," question 2, section 35.
d In the Summary, under the word "external usury."
e In the Code regarding restitution, title on usury, restitution at the beginning, and later in the verse "Instead of."
f Book 6, question 7, article 1, on justice and law.
g Law regarding the "Price of Risk," in the Digest on nautical loans.
h Laurentius de Rodulphis in the chapter "He Consulted," part 3, question 1, number 8. Antoninus, part 2, title 1, chapter 7, paragraph 21; and Navarro here, number 37.
</margin>

<margin>1</margin>
The first thing we say for the clarification of this principle is that it has two interpretations. The first is from the ancient doctors <gloss>a</gloss>, according to which those words—<note>original: "Eo quòd periculum in ſe ſuſcepit"</note> "Because he took the risk upon himself"—must be joined with that participle <note>original: "recepturus"</note> "about to receive." The text should be ordered in this way: *One lending a certain quantity of money to one navigating or going to a fair, expecting to receive something beyond the principal <note>original: "sortem"; the base amount of the loan</note> because he took the risk upon himself, is to be considered a usurer.* In short, this means what Panormitanus says in his summary: that he is a usurer who receives more than he lent, even if he takes the risk upon himself.

The other interpretation is from some more recent authors, which we also followed when we lectured on this in an extraordinary session at this most famous University of Salamanca in the year 1530. According to this view, those words "Because he took the risk upon himself" must be joined with the participle <note>original: "Mutuans"</note> "The one who lends." Thus, the text should be ordered as follows: *One who lends a certain quantity of money—because he took the risk upon himself—to one navigating or going to a fair, expecting to receive something beyond the principal, is to be considered a usurer.* This would mean: anyone who lends money to someone who must transport it through dangerous places, on the condition that the lender insures it and the borrower gives him an extra amount for that insurance, is a usurer. This is how John Major <gloss>b</gloss> understands it, saying that the gloss speaks here in a disjointed manner. Sylvester <gloss>c</gloss> also seems to understand it this way, saying that the *Supplement* did not understand this text. Cajetan <gloss>d</gloss>, Medina <gloss>e</gloss>, and Soto <gloss>f</gloss> also seem to understand it thus. This way of understanding creates a problem: it seems to follow from the ancient view that anyone who insures any merchandise to be moved through dangerous places is a usurer if they charge anything for it. This is contrary to the practice of all Christendom, contrary to a law <gloss>g</gloss> which signifies that insurance has a valid price, and against the common opinion <gloss>h</gloss>.

<margin>2</margin>
Secondly, we say that although in the past we held this [second] interpretation because of that argument, now, however, that God grants us the grace to weigh these mat—

<meta>This page continues the technical legal and theological debate regarding nautical usury. Azpilcueta is specifically dissecting a decree by Pope Gregory IX (the "Naviganti" decree) to determine if a lender who assumes the risk of a journey can lawfully charge a fee for that risk, or if such a fee is inherently usurious.</meta>

<page-num>33</page-num>
<header>On the final chapter regarding usury.</header>

<margin>
From Panormitanus <note>Nicolaus de Tudeschis, a famous 15th-century canonist</note>, Johannes Andreae, and Petrus de Ancharano. For Johannes Andreae, for the sake of brevity, does not summarize. Argument from that place: If that which seems less likely to be included is included, then that which is more likely is also included, etc. Chapter *Cum in cunctis*, on elections. The *Authentica* "Much more," in the Code *On the most holy churches*. And he who speaks of one thing by way of example does not deny it of another, in the law "Stipulation for impending damage" in the Digest *On impending damage*. A distinct gloss on Chapter 1, *Ne clerici vel monachi*. And he who speaks of one thing does not deny it of another similar thing, nor the reverse. Dominicus de Sancto Geminiano in Chapter *Qualis*, distinction 25.
</margin>

In weighing the texts, the first interpretation seems better to us—the one that the gloss accepted <margin>a</margin> from Panormitanus and Johannes Andreae—which everyone here has followed. According to this interpretation, the summary is somewhat more cautious than that of others: That anyone who lends money to be carried elsewhere (even if they take the risk upon themselves) is to be judged as a usurer if they take anything more than what they lent. This summary cannot be criticized for being too general, even though the text only speaks of one who lends to a sailor or to one going to the fairs. The summary covers that person and anyone else who lends to whomever must carry the money elsewhere. This is because the text does not speak of the lender to the sailor or the fair-goer to imply that it does not apply to those who lend to others, but rather as an example, or to signify that it applies there by even stronger reasoning. For if one who lends to someone traveling by sea (where there are commonly more dangers) is not excused from usury even though he takes the risk upon himself, then even less should he be excused who, taking the risk upon himself, lends to another who will pass through fewer dangers. And if he who lends to one going to the fair—who is commonly a merchant who borrows to go to the fair and buy merchandise to earn more—is not excused, then he shall be excused even less if he lends to another who has greater necessity.

<margin>
a Argument from Chapter 4, question 3, and following, where we have commented on these Latin terms. See the previous Commentary.
3
b This sounds like the truth, just as the word *censendus* ("to be considered") sounds like a fiction or a presumption, according to the notes by Bartolus and Jason on the law *Si is qui pro emptore* in the Digest *On usucaption*.
c Which is something estimable. Law "The price of risk" in the Digest *On nautical loans*.
</margin>

¶ Thirdly, we say that this summary and this way of understanding it are supported by several points: First, that all who have commented here have understood it this way. Second, that the structure of this principle, plainly ordered, clearly says this; and it cannot say what others impose upon it without constructing it in a way that clearly shows they are destroying its sense, as anyone who constructs it according to the two interpretations without bias will experience. Third, that it is the phrasing of Gregory IX. Consequently, it is organized, dense, brief, and carefully considered, which does not suffer from improprieties or strange constructions. Out of a hundred men learned in Latin composition who read this text (without worrying about whether the merchants' insurance is lawful or not), barely three will say that this text does not speak of one who takes more than the amount lent in exchange for lending and insuring. Fourth, because if Gregory IX had wanted to say what those who give it the second interpretation claim, he would not have said, (*Eo quòd fuscepit in fe periculum*) <note>Latin: "Because he took the risk upon himself."</note>, but rather (*vt fusciperet in fe periculum*) <note>Latin: "So that he might take the risk upon himself."</note>. For they say he speaks of one who lends with a pact that the recipient provides insurance for the lender. Fifth, because according to the construction and order of the letter that the others provide, the text signifies that it speaks of one who insures before he lends; because it says (*Mutuans eo quòd fuscepit in se periculum*) <note>Latin: "One who lends because he took the risk upon himself."</note>. And the very ones who order the text this way say that it speaks of one who lends with a pact to insure the loan; and consequently, they presuppose that it speaks of when the loan precedes the insurance, and thus they contradict themselves without realizing it. And if someone should say that in some new books it does not say *suscepit* <note>original: "he took"</note> in the past tense, but *suspicit* <note>original: "he takes"</note> in the present, let him see that in the ancient ones and most of the new ones it is *suscepit*, and that it matters little <margin>3</margin> to the case, for if he looks closely, he will find the same meaning. Sixth, because Gregory IX is not accustomed to determining anything except doubtful things, and there was no doubt that it is usury to lend to another with a pact that the borrower obligates himself to do something for the lender's benefit <margin>a</margin> beyond paying back what he receives. And there is no doubt that this occurs when the one who borrows obligates himself to insure the lender. Lastly, something which few notice: Gregory IX did not say that the person he speaks of *is* a usurer, but that he is *presumed* to be a usurer. For he does not say (<margin>b</margin> *vfurarius est*) <note>Latin: "he is a usurer"</note>, but (*vfurarius est censentur*) <note>Latin: "they are to be considered (presumed) usurers"</note>. This gives us to understand that it may well be that before God, the person he speaks of will sometimes not be a usurer, but the Church must hold him as such. According to the other interpretation, he would have had to say that he is a true usurer before God and men. Furthermore, because according to this understanding, very apt reasons can be given for both the doubt and the decision, which, once heard, everyone will say is the truth. For the reason for doubting (according to common opinion and our understanding) was that since no text of the Canons specifically forbade the usury called **nautical** or **transportation usury**—which is that which is taken for lending and insuring, taking upon oneself the danger of the crossing and of being lost at sea—and since by civil law it is permitted with much greater reason than others because of the danger the lender takes upon himself <margin>c</margin>, it seemed that it would also be lawful according to the Canons. The reason for the decision, however, by which...

<meta>The catchword "no ob-" at the bottom indicates the next page begins with "no obstante" (notwithstanding).</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that charging extra for a loan based on the lender's assumption of travel risk is a form of usury, regardless of whether it involves sea voyages or land fairs. He emphasizes that the law creates a legal presumption of usury even if the lender's intent might not be sinful in the eyes of God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Pope Gregory IX's decree regarding nautical usury. Azpilcueta is arguing that the Pope's intent was to prevent "masked usury"—interest hidden under the guise of insurance premiums—and explains how this principle applies to contemporary Spanish and Portuguese law.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>Resolutory Commentary on Exchanges.</header>
<meta>catchword: court,</meta>

despite this doubt) Gregory IX determined the contrary. The reason was not what the gloss, Panormitanus, and others believe, but rather the necessity of preventing **masked or hidden usury**, which was practiced under the color of insurance. Many, seeing that canon law forbade usury in general but did not specifically forbid **nautical usury** <note>original: *nautica*; a type of high-interest loan allowed in Roman law for sea voyages because the lender assumed the risk of shipwreck.</note>—which seemed licit because the lender took the risk upon himself—began to lend by taking the risk upon themselves: whether there truly was danger or not, and whether the goods lent were to travel by sea or by land.

And many took loans saying they took them to carry them personally, or through others, beyond the sea, or beyond such and such mountains, or outside the kingdom, etc., in order to find someone to lend to them for the sake of what they would gain through the **feigned insurance**. Even others, who truly wanted to borrow to travel where they said but did not want to insure the loan, were forced to take insurance because others would not lend to them without profit. Since the lenders could not take profit for the loan alone, they wanted to mask and hide it with insurance. <margin>a Below in the same, chapter number 30. b According to the gloss on the word "feigning" in the chapter *Conquestus* on usury, which Antonius de Burgos said is commonly accepted in the chapter *Ad nostras*, etc.</margin> For this reason, Gregory IX ordered that whoever lends money and takes more back (even if they insure it) should be judged a usurer, even if it is said that the payment was given and taken for the insurance. This was certainly a provision of great prudence. For if nautical usury were permitted to the one who lends while insuring, everyone would soon give and ask for loans with insurance, some saying truly and others lying that they requested it to pass through the sea or through dangerous lands, etc.

<margin>column 15. on purchase and sale. Cresse in the word *usu* says Cassiodorus in decision 1 on usury.</margin> ¶ By the same † providence, it has been ordered (recently) in these kingdoms and in those of Portugal that there should be no **exchange** <note>the practice of transferring money between cities for a fee, often used to bypass usury laws</note> from one city in the kingdom to another in the same, because they are presumed to be masked usuries, as we shall say shortly. By the same [providence] it is ordered that whoever buys something for less than it is worth, with a pact to return it whenever the seller wishes for the same price, it is presumed to be a loan and a pledge, and not a sale, in the **outward court** <note>original: *fuero exterior*; the public legal system or ecclesiastical court, as opposed to the "court of conscience" or private confession.</note>. <margin>c Digest and Code on nautical interest.</margin> The other reason is because not only are other forms of usury now forbidden by canon law, but even those called **nautical** <margin>d On this very point.</margin>, which are those mentioned above, as Hostiensis affirmed <margin>e Through his text in the *Authentica* "To these things." Code on usury, column 3.</margin>, whom no one here contradicts, and with whom Salyceto agrees. Saliceto’s words are commonly held, as affirmed by <margin>f In the present, number 3, citing Petrus de Ancharano in the final law of the Code on nautical interest, reproving Jacobus Butrigarius, who held the contrary in the first law of the Code on nautical interest.</margin> Johannes de Anania, concluding after them that by this chapter a title of the **civil law** <note>referring to Roman Law, specifically the Digest/Code</note> is corrected <margin>g Title on nautical interest.</margin>. If we held the other understanding, we would have to confess they are illicit; for this text would not prove them illicit, and there is no other in the world that proves them such, at least in their specific kind. Finally, one is compelled to hold this because this beginning of this very solemn chapter would serve no doubtful decision and would be useless and superfluous, since there is no student with three years of study in canons who doubts whether it is usury to lend money to another with the requirement that they be obliged to pay for insurance with it. To say such a thing of a text by Gregory IX is a disrespect and an insolent temerity.

¶ Fourthly † we say that the argument we made on the other side does not stand in the way, which at one time seemed insoluble to us, as it also seemed to the aforementioned who departed from this common understanding. Namely, that from our common understanding it follows that whoever insures merchandise that must pass through dangerous places is a usurer if they take anything for it. This would be against the practice of all Christendom, against a law <margin>a Law "The price of risk." Digest on nautical interest.</margin> which signifies that insurance has a value and a price, and against common opinion <margin>b Reported above, column number 2.</margin>. We say, then, that this does not stand in the way because we deny that this follows from our understanding. For it only follows that he who lends money and takes something more than what he lends (even if he insures it) should be held as a usurer. This differs from what the argument infers in three things. First, this does not include the one who insures without lending, whereas the other does. Second, this does not include the one who lends something other than money, whereas the other does. Third, to say this is not to say that such a person is a usurer, but that he should be **presumed** to be a usurer, while saying the other is to say he truly is a usurer. And if you reply against this with what Saint Antoninus pointed out—that the one who lends to do good should not be in a worse condition than one who does not lend, and consequently there is no reason why he cannot insure and take for the insurance as much as another—we will respond by conceding that before God, and in the court...

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that Pope Gregory IX's restrictions on nautical usury were designed to prevent "masked usury," where interest was disguised as insurance. He notes that similar laws now exist in Spain and Portugal to prevent fraudulent exchanges and predatory buy-back sales.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal and moral distinction between genuine nautical insurance and "masked usury." Azpilcueta is explaining why the law treats a lender who also acts as an insurer more harshly than a standard insurer, even if their intentions are honest, in order to prevent systemic fraud.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>On the final chapter of usury 35</header>

<margin>e In.4.de rest. in quaest. which begins. Occurrunt.
d c.17.n.242.
e l.Cum ipse. C.de contra.empti.
f l.Si in emtione. §.si.ff.eod. f l.1.C.de contract.indi. & l.Principalibus. C.si cert.petat.</margin>
In the **court of conscience** <note>The "internal forum" or the spiritual relationship between a person and God, usually handled in confession.</note> (where only the truth is considered and the penitent is believed), a person who lends and insures may lawfully charge as much for the insurance as another person who insures without lending. However, we deny that in the **external court** <note>The "external forum" or ecclesiastical and civil law courts, which judge based on evidence and legal presumptions rather than the heart.</note> he is not in a worse condition; this is to prevent the presumption that such insurance is being used to mask and hide usury—using a "good color" to take more for the act of lending than for the act of insuring. For this reason, Gregory did not say here that such a person *is* a usurer, but that he is *to be held* as a usurer. This was the sentiment (if I am not mistaken) of Hadrian, <gloss>e</gloss> question 5. 

To this end, consider the case of a merchant who sells a cloth on credit to a man at the highest just price, and that man immediately sells it back to him for less. If the merchant buys it back for the lowest just price, he would not commit usury or sin before God; however, before men, he would easily be presumed a usurer, as we stated in the *Manual* <gloss>d</gloss>. This is true even though, by selling it to him on credit at a just price, he did him more of a kindness than one who refused to sell at all. If someone else, who had not sold it to him or done him that kindness, bought it for even less than the merchant did, he would neither be, nor be presumed to be, a usurer. 

It is also for this reason that a tutor or curator cannot buy <gloss>e</gloss> the property of their minors as others can, nor can secular judges buy the property of those under their jurisdiction <gloss>f</gloss>. Even if they treat them better than others would, the law puts them in a worse condition than others in this regard to avoid fraud, at least in the external court.

<margin>4</margin>
¶ Fifthly, we say that from all this it follows that if a penitent confesses that he lent money to another who wanted to insure it to carry it by sea or through other dangerous places, and without any other pact or coercion he insured it for the same rate others charge, the confessor should not order him to restitute anything. But if he confesses that he charged something extra for having lent it—or as much for the loan as for the insurance—the confessor must order him to restitute that portion taken by reason of the loan. Likewise, if he refused to lend unless the borrower insured with him (or with someone else with whom he held a share), he must restitute, as this very chapter proves according to the other interpretation, which (in this regard) is true in itself.

<margin>a c.Totum.1.q.3.l.Quisquis.de leg.3.
b c.1.2.& 3.14 q.3.
c According to the extensive notes in c.Is qui fidem.de spons.
d ca.Que à iure cõi.de reg.iur.li. 6.l.Quod contra rationem.ff.eod.</margin>
¶ It also follows that this text does not apply to one who lends and insures other merchandise. Firstly, because it speaks only of one who lends money: for the Pope used the Latin word *pecunia* <note>original: *pecunia*; meaning money or property.</note>. Although this word (according to its general meaning) signifies money and any other goods, according to its specific meaning, it signifies only money <gloss>b</gloss>. To denote that the Pope was using it in this specific sense in this chapter, he did not use it alone, but with an addition, saying "a certain quantity of money" <note>original: *certam pecuniæ quantitatem*</note> to signify that he only intended to introduce this rigor for one who lends a quantity of money, and not for one who lends other goods. 

Secondly, because this text is "exorbitant" <note>In legal terms, "exorbitant" means deviating from the common or standard rule of law.</note> and veers away from the main road of the law, insofar as it introduces a new presumption—and even one they call *iuris & de iure* <note>original: *iuris & de iure*; a legal presumption that is absolute and cannot be rebutted by evidence to the contrary.</note>, the opposite of which cannot be proven <gloss>c</gloss>. This presumption—that whoever lends and insures and takes more than the loan is presumed to take it for the loan and for usury—must be interpreted narrowly <gloss>d</gloss> and not expanded. 

Thirdly, because the same reasoning is not found in one who lends money as in one who lends other things. Partly because other goods are commonly given at a set value and sold, not lent; and partly because so many frauds cannot be committed with them as with money. Few can give them, and few can take them for this purpose without notorious malice. Only traders—and not all of them, but those who trade by sea or across different kingdoms—can take them without the fraud being clearly visible. But money can be taken by the great, the small, and those in between, feigning that they need it to send to Flanders or outside the kingdom for relatives, friends, business, or estates, whether their own or others'. Finally, there is no reason to commit such frauds with goods: for if one wanted to give and take an unjust profit, they could simply add it to the price of the goods, etc.

¶ Sixthly, we say that from what has been said, one can gather how to understand what was stated above: namely, that "nautical usuries" are today forbidden by canon law in this singular text. It should be understood that they are entirely forbidden in the external court if they are taken for lent money; and also in the internal court, if and insofar as they are taken for lending money or any other thing. But they are not forbidden if and insofar as they are taken solely for insurance, without having respect (at least primarily) to the lending in such quantity as one might justly take—

<sig>C 2</sig>

<summary>Azpilcueta distinguishes between the legal presumption of usury in public courts and the actual moral guilt in the court of conscience. He argues that the Pope’s strict rules against nautical interest apply specifically to money loans to prevent easy fraud, and should not be extended to the trade of other goods.</summary>

Eighthly † then, we add that exchange—which in Latin is also called *Cambium*—is the bartering of one thing for another, which legal scholars commonly call "permutation." <gloss>a</gloss>

¶ From which follows, first: that an exchange strictly is not a purchase <gloss>b</gloss>, nor a sale, nor a deposit, nor that kind of loan called in Latin <term>loan for consumption <note>original: "Mutuum"</note></term>, nor that called <term>loan for use <note>original: "Commodatum"</note></term>. ¶ It follows, secondly: that exchange, taking it strictly and generally, is divided into the exchange of money and the exchange of other things. This is because, although the exchange of one natural thing for another natural thing seems a more natural barter, it is also true when one coin is given for another coin—not as a price or currency, but as a piece of gold, silver, or metal. But the barter of money for money can also strictly be called an exchange, even as currency, provided that one is not given as the price of the other, but as a trade for it. For everything salable is exchangeable <gloss>d</gloss>, and money is a salable thing, as will be said below <gloss>e</gloss>. # On the final Chapter, on usury.

<margin>

...all confess, and even (according to those of us who follow this view) in those of the same metal and value, when one is in one land and the other in another, and even when they are in the same place: but one is at hand, and the other is not; or one seems better because of its beauty, antiquity, or some other respect to the one who wishes to have it by exchange, as we see every day, that one *real*, one *ducat*, one *doubloon*, and one *angelot* <note>original: "angelote," a gold coin of English origin featuring the archangel Michael</note> seems more beautiful than another.

<margin>10</margin>
¶ It follows thirdly, that the common language of Spain and the common Latin used by some scholastics today do not use this word "Exchange" <term>Cambio: broadly meaning the exchange of one thing for another, but specifically applied here to currency</term> as broadly as its original meaning allows in one respect $^a$, while in another they use it more broadly. For according to that meaning, all and only barter is exchange, and all and only exchange is barter; but the said common tongue does not call all barters "exchanges," but only the exchange of money for money. And to many contracts that properly are not exchanges—but rather purchases, hirings, leases, and other unnamed contracts—it gives the name "Exchanges." Thus, exchange (taking it as the aforementioned common folk do) is any contract of money for money that is not gratuitous: whether it be barter, purchase, deposit, or any other. We said "the common language of Spain" because all the laws of the *Partidas* <note>The Siete Partidas, the celebrated 13th-century statutory code of Castile</note> call all and only barters and permutations "exchanges" $^d$.

¶ Exchange, then (as the common folk take it), is divided according to St. Antoninus $^b$ (whom the theologians writing afterward have followed) into "real exchange" and "dry exchange." Dry exchange <term>cambio seco: a deceptive financial transaction used to circumvent usury laws, often involving a fictitious bill of exchange</term>, according to them, is an imaginary exchange that is not truly an exchange; but Laurentius, who spoke of this earlier $^c$, says better that dry exchanges are those in which the exchanger gives before he receives; and because they give without receiving, they are called "dry." It is also divided according to Cajetan $^d$ into clearly just exchange, clearly unjust, and doubtful. Others divide it into pure exchange and non-pure exchange; some (like Medina $^e$) rightly call "pure" that which has no mixture of another contract, and "non-pure" that which is mixed with another contract. Soto $^f$, however, calls "pure" that which has no mixture of injustice, and "impure" that which does. All of these divisions are of little use in our opinion and cause great confusion. Therefore, it seems more useful to say that there are seven types, species, or manners of exchange: f. By office, or the labor of lending; g. By small change; h. By letters <note>bills of exchange</note>; i. By real transfer; k. By interest; l. By safekeeping; m. And by purchase $^n$. Barter, or another unnamed contract—these are more intelligible and open up the subject matter; and to these are reduced the "real" and the "dry," the clearly just, clearly unjust, and doubtful, and the pure and non-pure. We will speak of each one so that, through their decisions and foundations, the doubts of everyone may be resolved.

---

->centered text<- SUMMARY. <-

Exchange is older than buying and selling. no. 11.
For what purpose was money found? What is its principal end and use? no. 11.
What is the art of exchange? When and why is it licit? no. 11.

<margin>
11
o In law 1, of the Digest, on the permutation of things.
a As was said before, where mentioned above.
Panormitanus says, where mentioned above.
f Aristotle, *Politics*, book 1, chapter 6.
</margin>

**W**e say $^o$, that the exchange or barter of things that are not money (as the legal consultant Paul $^o$ elegantly put it) is a much older contract than that of buying and selling, which began after money was found. For before it, whoever had one thing and needed another sought someone who had it and wanted to trade it for theirs: just as he who had wine and wool, and not wheat or shoes, sought the one who had wheat and shoes and wanted to give them for his wine and wool—as even today some barbaric peoples do with whom the Spaniards and others trade. However, money was found later, which as certain was a very necessary invention on one hand; but on the other, I do not know if today it is what destroys souls through greed, bodies through wars, voyages, and terrifying pilgrimages, and even itself, and many fleets (in which it comes and goes) through horrible storms and shipwrecks. Thus, the first premise and principal end for which money was found was as a price for buying $^f$ and selling things necessary for human life $^a$: and so that it would be like a measure...

<page-num>38</page-num>
# Resolutory Commentary on Usury.

<margin>
b St. Thomas, Book 2 on the Government of Princes, ch. 13; and all recent authors following this matter, especially John Calderinus here and Laurentius in the chapter *Consuluit*. See also 2. q. 26. art 1 and the text in *l. Si ita ff. de fide iussor*.

c Aristotle, *Politics*, Book 1, ch. 6 & 7.

d 2. Secunda, question 77, article 1, commonly accepted.

e In the said article 1.
</margin>

...public measure of saleable things $^b$. Later, the exchange of money of one metal or value for that of another metal or value began: such as large denominations for small, and small for large. Afterward, because the money of one land was worth less in it than in another (as today almost all the gold and silver of Spain is worth less here than in Flanders and France) the art of exchange began. This is the art of dealing in money, giving and taking one for another, by which money began to be moved from where it was worth less to where it was worth more. In our own time, many have increased their estates significantly by carrying to Flanders and France ducats of two, four, and ten, some in small casks <note>original: "pipotes"</note> like olives, and some in pipes <note>original: "pipas," referring to large wine barrels</note> hidden in wine. In each of these they gained much, and brought back merchandise that was worth little there and much here, benefiting us greatly in the one but harming us much in the other. ¶ And although it seemed wrong to Aristotle $^c$ this art of exchanging and trading by swapping money—because this third use did not seem natural to him, nor did it bring benefit to the commonwealth, nor have any other end but profit, which is an end without end—for this reason alone St. Thomas $^d$ said that any art of trading whose principal end is to gain absolutely is illicit. But because the same St. Thomas says $^e$ that the art of trading is licit if the end is moderate profit to maintain oneself and one's household, and the art of exchange brings some benefits to the commonwealth, we say that if it is practiced as it should be, and the end of the profit sought through it is ordered toward honorably and moderately maintaining oneself and one's household, it is licit. Nor is it true that the use of money to gain by exchanging it is against its nature. For although it is different from the first and principal use for which it was invented, it is not different from the less principal and secondary use for which it is suited. Just as the use of shoes to trade them for profit is different from the primary use for which they were invented—which is to be worn on the feet—but it is not for that reason against their nature.

->SUMMARY<-

Money serves for many contracts, and for eight ends and uses. number 12.
A simulated contract is judged by what it truly is, not by what it is feigned to be. number 22.

<margin>
a Regarding which, Thomas, Book 2 on the Government of Princes, ch. 14.

b *l. 3. § siu. ff. commodo.* <note>Reference to the Digest regarding loans for use</note>

12 ¶ We say that money is used for eight ends; the first three are those mentioned above $^a$. The fourth is for the display of riches $^b$, showing it to some or others, or placing it on the table or in the square where one deals or exchanges. The fifth is to be worn as medals and ornaments on clothing. The sixth is to delight with its sight $^c$. The seventh is to heal certain infirmities with its broth—as they say $^d$ is the case with fine gold <note>A reference to "aurum potabile" or medicinal gold, a common belief in the 16th century that gold had curative properties when ingested</note>. The eighth is to give it as a pledge for a debt. For these five uses, money can not only be lent and exchanged, but even rented out. In this way, money can be given by way of many contracts: by way of the price of a thing bought; as merchandise sold for other money; by way of an unnamed contract of barter or another giving it for another thing or for other money; by way of a loan called <term>Mutuum: a loan of fungible items where the borrower returns an equal amount of the same kind, but not the specific items</term>, where that same money is not returned, but another like it $^g$; by way of a loan called <term>Commodatum: a loan for use where the exact same item must be returned</term>, so that the very same money given is returned; by way of a pledge for what is owed; and by way of a lease for a certain amount of rent so that the same money is returned after the one who took it has benefited from its use in showing his wealth, or delighting in its sight, or using its broth, or giving it as a pledge, etc. And by as many ways as it can be given, by just as many can it be taken $^e$. ¶ And because the nature of the said contracts by which money can be given and taken is diverse, so too must it be judged by various rules of law if and when it is licit or not. Because if it is given by way of purchase and sale, it cannot be given except for what it is worth $^f$; nor if it is given by way of exchange or barter. And if it is given by way of a loan (whether the same must be returned, or another like it), one cannot take a small thing nor a large one in interest $^h$; nor if it is given as a pledge for one's own debt. But if it is given by way of rental, to delight and honor with its sight and display, or to heal with its broth, or to put it as a pledge for someone else's debt, an honest rent can well be charged $^i$. For such is the nature of this contract, by which ownership is not transferred, but only the use, and it is valued according to the time for which it is taken. However, since more understanding must be given to what truly happens than to what is feigned $^k$, whenever it is truly understood...

<meta>catchword: understood</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta explains that money, while primarily a measure of value, has multiple uses—including medicinal and decorative—which allow for different types of legal contracts. He argues that charging a fee for certain uses of money (like rental for display) is licit, unlike usury in a standard loan.</summary>

...intends to make one of these contracts, and feigns another, he is not to be judged by the rules of the feigned one, but by those of the true one. Thus, if the money changer truly lends his money, he cannot take anything, even if he pretends he is exchanging it or renting it out.

*Exchange, or swapping of money, or other things of unequal value, is illicit. number 13.*
*A money changer, as such, cannot take more than what he gives, except for what is ordained. number 13.*

<margin>13</margin>
Eleventh: We add that, just as for a purchase and sale to be just, it is necessary that what is bought be worth as much as the price given for it—and conversely, the price should be as much as the thing is worth <gloss>a</gloss>. And just as for any lease to be just, it is necessary that the use of the leased thing be worth as much as the price given for it—and conversely, that as much be given for it as it is worth—so <gloss>b</gloss> for the exchange or swap to be just and licit, it is necessary that what one party gives to the other be of equal value with what he takes.

¶ From this it follows that, just as the purchase of a mule worth one hundred ducats for eighty or one hundred and twenty is unjust, and also the lease of a house whose use is worth fifty ducats a year for forty or sixty; so the swap by someone who gives a beast not worth six ducats for another worth ten is not just. Nor, consequently, is the exchange or swap of ten ducats in reals for twelve *tarjas* <note>A *tarja* was a small silver or copper coin used in the Kingdom of Navarre and Spain.</note> licit.

¶ It follows again that whenever money changers perform a true exchange and swap of money for money, they cannot take more than the value of what they give by reason of the swap and exchange, except for some small amount usually given for swapping one currency for another that is immediately counted out. However, it may be that at times, for other respects that are joined and make it not a pure exchange, something may be taken, as will be said later. Because if the exchange and swap of other natural things—among which swapping is more legitimate (or at least more natural <gloss>c</gloss>)—is made illicit by inequality in the things swapped, by even stronger reason will it make the swapping of money illicit. For money, insofar as it is money, consists of artificial things that were not primarily created to be swapped for one another, but to serve as a price <gloss>d</gloss> that could be carried anywhere to buy what was necessary.

*A money changer or swapper, simply by being such, cannot take more than what is for his office, etc. But he may well swap what he does not yet have for what the other has. number 14.*
