Twelfth: No money changer can take more than he otherwise could by reason of giving his money before the other gives him his, and waiting for payment for a month, or two, or more, or until the next fair; <gloss>e</gloss> nor, conversely, can another licitly give the money changer some money with a pact that in a year, or three months, or another fair, they return it with something more, or do something for him that is naturally worth money. Because whenever something more than the principal is taken or given by reason of time—and for waiting or advancing payment—it is a loan, or at least a disguised one <note>original: "paleado" - literally 'palliated' or cloaked.</note>, which contains disguised usury, as we have said elsewhere <gloss>a</gloss>. And just as he who gives a mule now so that another worth much more be given to him in three, four, or six months is a usurer, so he who gives some money now so that in three, four, or six months they give him other money worth more is a usurer <gloss>b</gloss>. However, it is not necessary, as some require <gloss>f</gloss>, that what one is to swap or exchange for another’s property already be produced and already belong to the one who wants to swap it. First, because there is no text or reason to prove this. Second, because just as one can buy <gloss>c</gloss>, pledge <gloss>d</gloss>, promise <gloss>e</gloss>, and order <gloss>f</gloss> that which is yet to be born, so one can swap—at least by a general swap—that which...

<header>Resolutory Commentary on Exchanges.</header>

which in this regard is equal with a special paragraph. Secondly, because the same Silvester ^h^ admits that for me to licitly trade and exchange ten <term>ducats</term> <note>A gold coin used throughout Europe.</note> from Lisbon with ten ducats placed here, it is not necessary that at the time you give me the ten ducats here, I already have them in Lisbon. For it is enough that I can find them there lent at interest, or in another manner, at the time I must deliver them to you there. Secondly, if I were to exchange with you one hundred pounds of oil that I have here for as many or more that you give me in Lisbon, it is not necessary that at the time I give them to you here, you already have them there. For it is enough that you have them when you wish to give them to me. It is no objection to say that for it to be a <term>swap</term> <note>original: "trueco" — a direct exchange of goods or specific assets.</note>, it is necessary that one specific thing be exchanged for another specific thing. First, because although this is required for a "specific swap," it is not required for a general one. Secondly, because if this were necessary, almost no merchant who takes money in Medina <note>Medina del Campo, the site of Spain’s most important financial fairs.</note> for Flanders, or conversely in Flanders for Medina, makes a true exchange ^i^; for no one (even if they have much money where they must give it) designates specific ducats, specific <term>reals</term>, or <term>testones</term> <note>Common silver coins of the period.</note> to be given. It is true that for the swap to be finalized by both parties so that neither can retract, not only is what they require necessary, but it is not necessary for the contract of the swap to be valid—as other <term>innominate contracts</term> <note>Legal agreements that do not fall under specific Roman law categories like "sale" or "hire" but are still binding once one party performs.</note> are valid—before delivery is made by both parties or by one alone ^k^.

Thirteenth: That there is great doubt as to whether the first exchange by office and labor of lending is licit. For Cajetan ^a^ <note>Thomas Cajetan, a prominent 16th-century Italian theologian.</note> says that some held that the money changer—insofar as he is a lender and offers to lend to those in need of money—may receive a certain amount for what is lent for a certain time (at the discretion of an honest man) for the labor and industry he applies in seeking, holding, and guarding much money, which are necessary for it, and afterwards keeping accounts, taking securities, and exposing himself to dangers and troubles. This is also held by Durandus ^b^ and Medina ^c^. Arguments in their favor: First, that such a lender does not receive payment for the lending itself, but for the labors to which he offers himself, which are undoubtedly very great; and it is certain that there is no usury when more than what was lent is taken for another just cause distinct from the lending ^d^. Secondly, that for one whose duty it is to swap one currency for another immediately paid, something may be given for that office and labor ^e^, and the same reason seems to apply in this case. Thirdly, that (according to the mind of Duns Scotus ^f^), the commonwealth can ordain that there be a lender of money at a certain rate for a certain time; and if the commonwealth can ordain it, it is licit; and if it is licit and not forbidden, anyone could take and use it, and take for it...

<header>On the final chapter of usury.</header>

for this reason it is just, according to the same <term>Scotus</term> <note>John Duns Scotus (c. 1266–1308), a highly influential Franciscan priest and philosopher.</note> §. Lo. 4 ^g^, that the judge, priest, and witness, who cannot receive anything for their sentences ^h^, sacraments, and testimony, can receive something for their sustenance and the labors they undertake in them. Lo. 5. that such a moneychanger, for the act of lending and forgoing other business, can consequently take his <term>interest of profit</term> <note>original: "interesse de ganancia" — a legal term for compensation for "lost opportunity" or profit the lender could have made elsewhere.</note>, as we say in another Commentary ^i^ and below ^k^. Lo. 6. that a cleric, for going to say a mass two leagues from here, or for staying in a place to say it there, may reasonably take more than if he said it here or by chance ^l^. Lo. 7. that in another Commentary ^m^ we have held that the <term>Mount of Piety</term> <note>original: "monte de la piedad" — a charitable pawnbroker run by the church to provide low-interest loans to the poor, intended to combat usury.</note> ^n^ is licit, and in it, it is permitted that the poor who receive loans give a certain amount for a certain amount, for each month they have it, as a salary for the one who has the duty of guarding it, governing it, and making the loans.

<margin>
l Innocent [IV], received in the chapter *Quoniam* on simony.
m Chapter 1, Question 14, Case 3, from number 64 up to 70.
n See <term>Cajetan</term> <note>Thomas Cajetan, a major 16th-century theologian.</note> in the aforementioned treatise on exchanges, chapter 2, whom <term>Soto</term> <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a Spanish Dominican priest and contemporary of the author.</note> follows; neither there, nor in any other place related in book 7, question 3, article 11, on justice and law.
</margin>
¶ **The contrary** †, however, is held by others. Because it seems that saying this is the same as saying that one may organize, and even without an ordinance take up the office and art of lending under **moderate usuries**. This appears contrary to the mind of the Gospel, and of natural and Canon law, and against that of all their interpreters and doctors: by which consideration alone they say all contrary reasons alleged are dissolved. For if the office is not licit, neither will it be licit to take anything for its salary, nor for the labors spent in exercising it and preparing the tools necessary for it. Nor can anything be inferred for this from the offices of judge, witness, priest, and chaplain: because those are licit, and this is not; and therefore it does not follow that if something can be given for their obligation, labor, and maintenance, it can also be given for this. And because they hold that the Mounts of Piety are not licit, they have no need to respond to the seventh argument, which seems among the strongest; but even holding that they are licit, we can respond that there is a very great difference between this and the Mount of Piety: because in this [private lending], profit is sought and desired, but there it is not; rather, it is for the indemnity of the one who has charge of it, so that he does not offer his estimable labors, cares, and diligence from his own house for free. Here the money belongs to the lender, and its guarding pertains to him; there it belongs to the poor, or to another for them, and its guarding pertains to them, and what they give or pay is very little, and resolves into a "collection" or just contribution, conforming to the benefit one derives from it; and for this and other respects, one cannot infer the former from the latter.

<margin>
a And so it is a <term>begging of the question</term> <note>original: "petitio principij" — a logical fallacy where the premise assumes the truth of the conclusion.</note>, or the same reasoning with the saying, against law 1, added gloss by Paul, Digest on exceptions.
b Book 6, question 1, article 2. To objection 6, on justice and law.
a In [the Sentences] book 4, distinction 15, question 2, article 2, paragraph "it follows."
b Chapter 1, Question 14, Case 3, number 66.
</margin>
37 ¶ **Nevertheless** † the other opinion does not seem as lacking in color to us as they make it out to be. Firstly, because theirs is founded principally on presupposing as settled that very thing which is disputed: For it is disputed (at least tacitly) whether that office is licit or not, and the contrary opinion holds that it is, and theirs that it is not ^a^. Secondly, because they do not respond to the first fundamental point: namely, that there is no usury where one receives no more than what is given for the loan, even if it is received for another good and just respect. Furthermore, because Soto himself confesses elsewhere ^b^ that one could licitly take a salary for obligating oneself to the <term>republic</term> <note>original: "republica" — the state or the common good of the community.</note> to lend it a certain amount each time it might be needed. Furthermore, because from what Soto says, it follows—what cannot be denied, in our view—that the republic could establish a certain salary for someone because he obligated himself to collect and keep ready a certain sum of money to lend to those in need of it each year, or for a certain time, and to collect it for another, and return to lend it to others: in such a way that he was obligated to do all this, and that he should take that salary each year, not for lending principally, but for being obligated to hold 그 sum to lend it, and to suffer the aforementioned labors and cares. Furthermore, it must be confessed to be licit and profitable to the republic that there be one obligated to lend graciously up to such a sum each year; and it cannot be denied that the republic can constitute a just salary for the one who takes such a just office, according to the excellent theory of the excellent Doctor Scotus ^a^. Furthermore, because if the office of lending graciously to the poor is licit, and for a licit office the republic can ordain a salary, it can ordain it for this one, and consequently collect that from those who benefit from that office and charge; and in conscience thereof, ordain that such people pay to the republic proportionally, at a rate per pound (according as they benefit more or less), a part of that salary, and consequently, so as not to wander in so many directions nor incur expenses, they should pay that to the one who held said charge according to whether they took more or less for more or less time. Furthermore, that the reasons and authority of the <term>Apostolic See</term> <note>The Papacy in Rome.</note>, with which in another Commentary ^b^ we conclude—

<sig>C 5</sig>

<summary>Azpilcueta debates whether charging for the labor of lending money is usury, comparing private moneychangers to the charitable Mounts of Piety. He argues that if the state deems the service necessary for the public good, a "just salary" for the lender's labor and risk may be licit.</summary>

we conclude that the <term>Mounts of Piety</term> <note>Charitable institutions that provided low-interest loans to the poor to prevent them from falling into the hands of usurers.</note> are licit, holy, and worthy of praise, they also conclude that this is licit. Furthermore, if small-scale exchange <note>original: "cambio por menudo"</note> is licit and profitable—as will be said later—it can be lawfully ordained that there be someone in charge of it who receives a salary, either from the commonwealth or from those who benefit from that office, according to the profit they derive from it. Just as this is already done, the aforementioned practice will be licit for the same reason.

<margin>18</margin>
<margin>c Where cited above.</margin>
<margin>d In book 3, distinction 37, question 2.</margin>
<margin>e On the restitution of things, at folio 147.</margin>
<margin>a In the beginning of this Commentary, numbers 3 and 4.</margin>
¶ For these reasons† (subject to due correction) we reconcile the two opinions in this manner: that the first opinion applies to the person who obligates himself to the commonwealth, with its authority, to hold the aforementioned office. Indeed, we even dare to wish that Kings and princes would provide their commonwealths with such lenders who were obligated to what has been said, and who, under great penalties, would not charge more than what was ordained by their Highnesses. But the habit of profiting greatly from ill-gotten gains will ensure that no one can be found who wishes to profit fairly either. The second opinion, however, applies to the person who, without obligating himself to this by private authority, takes up such an office of lending. Nor is it an obstacle to say that since the office is licit in itself, anyone could take it up without other authority and charge for their sustenance as much as would be reasonable for the commonwealth or its prince to guarantee them (according to the excellent theory of the same <term>Scotus</term> <note>Duns Scotus, the medieval theologian mentioned on the previous page.</note> ^c^). Consequently, the opinion of <term>Durandus</term> <note>Durandus of Saint-Pourçain (c. 1275–1334), a Dominican philosopher and theologian.</note> ^d^ and <term>Medina</term> <note>Juan de Medina (1489–1545), a Spanish theologian who wrote extensively on restitution and ethics.</note> ^e^ would apply in all cases. We say, then, that this is no obstacle. Firstly, because Durandus and Medina speak even of the person who does not obligate himself, in whom the same reasoning is not found as in the one who does; for this obligation is the principal reason why we say this office is licit and that a salary may be taken for it. Secondly, because although these reasons might prove that it would sometimes be licit before God and in the <term>forum of conscience</term> <note>The internal judgment of one's soul before God, as opposed to human legal courts.</note> to take up such an office with holy intention (even without obligation) and use it, charging something less than what the obligated person might charge: yet before men and in the <term>external forum</term> <note>The public legal system and church courts.</note>, it ought to be judged as <term>usurious</term> <note>Pertaining to usury, the sin of charging unfair interest on a loan.</note>. This is to avoid the great frauds that could be introduced this way under the color of piety, according to what we have said above ^a^ regarding the one who lends and insures.

Small-scale exchange (as they call it) is licit. It is very beneficial for the commonwealth. A public official may be appointed for it, with a salary, etc. number 19.
The <term>Contraste</term> <note>A public official in charge of weighing and assaying coins to ensure their value.</note>: what duties he has, and how he differs from a moneychanger. number 19.
Small-scale exchange may be conducted by an individual without being a public official. number 19.
Money may be sold according to its intrinsic value, even if by law it is not worth as much. number 20.
Currency valued by law may be worth more due to a specific personal interest. number 20.
Small-scale exchange becomes illicit for this or that reason. number 20.

THE FOURTEENTH: That the second type of exchange mentioned above, which they call "small-scale," is licit (according to everyone ^b^). This is the exchange of large-denomination currency for small, or small for large: such as one <term>ducat</term> <note>A high-value gold coin used across Europe.</note> for eleven <term>reales</term> <note>Spanish silver coins.</note>, or three hundred and seventy-five <term>maravedís</term> <note>A small Spanish copper coin used as a basic unit of account.</note>; or conversely, eleven reales or three hundred and seventy-five maravedís for one ducat, etc. And because it greatly benefits the commonwealth that there be someone who holds this charge, the state may ordain a just salary for the one who holds it ^c^, to be paid from the public revenues, or ordain that the person in need of the exchange or trade pay him a certain amount. As is ordained in these kingdoms ^d^: that for the exchange of one <term>castellano</term> <note>A Spanish gold coin.</note>, he may charge four maravedís; for a ducat or a <term>dobla</term> <note>An ancient Spanish gold coin.</note>, three; and for a <term>florin</term>, two. This duty belongs to any moneychanger, according to the intent of the <term>pragmáticas</term> <note>Royal decrees or statutes issued by the Spanish crown.</note> of these kingdoms ^e^, and the very word suggests it. Likewise, a <term>contraste</term> <note>From the Spanish "contrastar," to verify or assay.</note> is appointed, whose duty is to weigh ^f^ all gold and silver currency, declare how much each is worth, and settle the account between the parties who give and take it. In Seville, at least, he cannot be a moneychanger, nor keep money for exchange, nor take anything for weighing. Instead, he must have a house, scales, and a salary from the commonwealth ^g^. Although one and the same person used to perform these

Although both parties labor in the counting for the sake of the one requesting the exchange, both forms of labor are principally taken into account.

<margin>

d Royal Decree 124 and 129.
</margin>

¶ Cardinal Cajetan says, however, that no one else, unless they hold such a public office, can licitly take that surplus. To us, however, the contrary seems better, as it also seemed to Medina and Soto for their own reasons: and even because of the hindrance and labor that usually occurs in it—such as going up to the chamber, opening the chest, counting the money or watching it be counted, giving and receiving, and guarding the currency—which cannot be denied are things estimable in money <sup>b</sup>. Some say, however,<sup>c</sup> that it is forbidden in these kingdoms for any private individual to take anything for acting as a money-changer; but we do not believe them. First, because they do not cite the law that forbids this. Second, because the laws that speak of this only forbid <sup>d</sup> anyone from taking the office of a changer to exercise it publicly without public authority; nor can they be a foreigner, even if they have naturalization papers. Third, because the <term>Royal Decree</term> <note>original: "Pragmatica"; a sovereign ordinance that had the force of law in Spain.</note> <sup>e</sup> expressly says, in three places, that this fee may be taken by the changer and by any other person who provides the exchange. It could be forbidden, however (if it were deemed necessary), to prevent the price of money from being altered so much and to stop the <term>bullion</term> <note>original: "gruessa"; refers to heavy coinage or high-value metal.</note> from being taken out of the Kingdom. Indeed, because people were swapping their bullion for small coins for profit, we saw in Portugal that foreigners were privately giving the natives much more than the gold was worth in order to take the gold coins to other Kingdoms, causing great harm to that nation.

<margin>
f Argument of those things which we said in the Commentary on chapter 1, 14, question 3, number 45, following Thomas [Aquinas], Summa Theologica, Second part of the second part, question 77, article 1.
g Where cited above.
h Where cited above.
a The Gloss, Bartolus, Baldus, Decio, and others on Law 1, Code of sentences, concerning that which pertains to the interest of the plaintiff.
</margin>

¶ We also believe that he who has some coins of very fine gold can sell or exchange them as coins and pieces of gold, and take something more than what they are worth (according to the legal value) from the one who needs them for gilding, for medicines, and other things, if in truth they are worth that surplus due to their material: or if by giving them up, he loses some convenience that came from having them, which is worth as much or more than that surplus <sup>f</sup>. This was done every day in our time in Toulouse in France, where those who had them sold them for gilding to the cutlers, who bought the <term>cruzados</term> of Portugal (which are not found there) even more dearly than the <term>ducados</term> of these kingdoms with "two faces"—which no longer show us any of them! <note>Azpilcueta is making a joke about the "excelentes" coins of Ferdinand and Isabella, which featured two profiles; by 1556, these high-quality coins were disappearing from circulation.</note> although Medina <sup>g</sup> holds the contrary against all common usage, without a reason that (in our opinion) is conclusive. This opinion (which Soto <sup>h</sup> also holds) can be founded on the fact that although the commonwealth has valued that coin at a certain amount for its primary use—which is to serve as a price—and although no one may sell wheat (justly valued) for more than that price, and although no one can be compelled to give more for the money than its appraised value; nevertheless, for other uses and for other particular respects which the law calls <term>singular interests</term> <sup>a</sup> <note>original: "interesses singulares"; specific legal value or damages unique to an individual's circumstances rather than the general market value.</note>, the one who has the money may well take something extra from the one to whom he gives it <sup>b</sup>.

<margin>
b Argument of the Law: If in purchase. Digest, on money; and also what Thomas says in the Second part of the second part, question 77, article 1.
c Royal Decree 126 and 127, and better in 129; and because in one, he receives more than is just, in the other he gives less.
d Since equality must be preserved: Ethics, book 5, and above, number 15.
</margin>

¶ This exchange, however, which in itself is the most natural of all, becomes illicit if the changer takes more than what is owed to him by just law or custom: if he gives false, bad, broken, or non-current money to the one who asks for exchange: or if he deceives regarding the value of the gold piece, which the person exchanging it does not know, as indicated by the royal decrees of these kingdoms <sup>c</sup>. It is also illicit if the one who receives the exchange does not pay the changer what is owed to him <sup>d</sup>.

# Resolutory Commentary on Exchanges.

<margin>
21
</margin>
**15th Point.** It is also licit (according to everyone) to use the third type of exchange, which is called "exchange by bills." This is a virtual transfer of money, by which someone who needs funds for another land gives them in this land, or provides something of equal value—either in part giving and in part doing—to the exchanger or to someone else who has money or credit in that distant land. In return, the exchanger provides letters <note>Original: "letras," referring to bills of exchange, which were formal documents ordering a payment to be made at a later date in a different location.</note> by which a sum is paid there equal to the value of what was given or done here, plus a certain amount of profit for arranging the delivery there via those letters. It is called "exchange by bills" because it is commonly done through them, although it could also be done via a messenger or by one's own person, by going there and delivering the payment.

<margin>
a In chapter 1, on claiming more, number 9: calling it just, legally sound, necessary, and supported by natural reason.
b In Counsel 11, on usury.
c Because the acts of agents do not operate beyond their own limits. Law "Not every," in the Digest, on credited things; and the chapter "Since concerning," on the office of the delegate.
d Argument from Law 2, section "if a shipwreck," in the Digest, on hiring. The Institutes, on hiring, throughout.
e Law "Natural," in the Digest, on prescribed words.
f Law 1, section with the gloss and those things noted on it, in the Digest, on the basis for giving.
g According to the teaching of Bartolus in the said Law "Natural," section "but if I do," near the end.
22
</margin>
This contract is just, and Baldus <gloss>a</gloss> <note>Baldus de Ubaldis (1327–1400), a highly influential Italian jurist.</note> praises it greatly. Although he does not give it a special name, nor does it have one in our opinion, it conforms closely to that of Calderinus <gloss>b</gloss> <note>Johannes Calderinus, a 14th-century canonist.</note>, and we believe it would satisfy the most prudent jurists. For if it were to have a name, it would be that of purchase, sale, exchange, barter, loan, or the hiring of another’s works, labor, industry, and credit so that money may be provided where it is needed; but it is not purely or properly any of these. Firstly, because not all—nor only—the essential elements of any of those categories are present in it. Secondly, because out of a hundred people who exchange in this manner, there are not even four who think they are buying, selling, lending, or borrowing money, nor that they are bartering, nor even that they are hiring the works and labor of the exchanger to have money delivered there; and contracts depend upon the intention of the contracting parties <gloss>c</gloss>. Furthermore, if it were any of these, it would be the hiring of another’s labor and industry to move something from one place to another. This cannot be said, however, because in a hiring contract, the ownership of the thing to be moved does not pass to the person moving it, whereas in this contract it does: for the ownership of the money to be transferred, which is given to the exchanger, passes to him <gloss>d</gloss>. It is, however, one of those contracts that do not have a special name, which the legal experts call <term>innominate</term> <gloss>e</gloss> <note>From the Latin *contractus innominatus*. In Roman law, these were enforceable agreements that did not fit into standard categories like "sale" or "partnership" but relied on the principle of reciprocity: "I give so that you may give."</note>. Sometimes it is "I give that you may give"; other times "I give that you may do"; other times "I give that you may do and give"; or "I do that you may give or do"; or "I do and give, so that you may give and do," etc. I give you the money here so that you give me letters, or act so that you cause money to be given to me <gloss>f</gloss>, or you yourself give me an equal amount there, after you have been paid what is just <gloss>g</gloss> for your labor, industry, and the credit that you established before now for this purpose, and which you will now exercise and cause to be exercised to give the money to me there.

<margin>
h Argument from the Law "Of Risk," in the Digest, on nautical interest.
i Law "Carried across," in the Digest, on actions and obligations. Law "He who is at Rome," section 1, in the Digest, on verbal obligations.
k According to the notes in the Law "If money," with the gloss on the word "Repentance," in the Digest, on the basis for giving; and Law "By agreement," in the Code, on the exchange of things.
l In the fourth book of the Sentences, distinction 15, question 2, article 2, which is proven in the fifth book of the Ethics, and the writings of Thomas Aquinas, Second Part of the Second Part, question 58, article 6, and question 59, article 2.
23
</margin>
¶ And although in other matters, contracts known by a specific name differ from those that have no such name <gloss>h</gloss>, they agree in this: that (in order to be just) they also require that what is given or done by one party be worth as much as what is given or done by the other, according to that solemn rule of Scotus <gloss>i</gloss> <note>Duns Scotus (c. 1266–1308), a major medieval philosopher who argued that justice in exchange requires a balance of costs and labor.</note>: that in all things that are properly contracts, where one gives to another without the intent of making a free gift, there must be equality between what one party gives or does and what the other gives or does. Consequently, for this contract to be licit, it is necessary that what is given to the exchanger—for providing a note and arranging the delivery of money elsewhere—is a just salary for him, and that he does not take more than that <gloss>k</gloss>. However, to determine what is just and what is unjust—whether by deficiency or by excess—one must recur to the law, or in the absence of law, to custom, if there is any, and in the absence of both, to the judgment of a prudent and good man <gloss>l</gloss>.

<margin>
m Saliceto in the Authentic "To these things," question 11, on usury.
n Argument from chapter 1, on constitutions, and the chapter "Custom," book 1 of the Digest, on the right of deliberation; and the chapter "Concerning the causes," on the office of the delegate.
24
</margin>
¶ From this it follows: First, that those exchanges are unjust and so mortally sinful that they require restitution <gloss>m</gloss> when the exchanger takes more than a just salary <gloss>n</gloss>, even if he provides credit to a party who does not have the money to give him immediately; and they are all the worse the more he takes for granting a longer period of time for payment. Such also are those in which the exchanger car—

<summary>Azpilcueta defines "exchange by bills" (bills of exchange) as a licit "innominate contract" where a fee is justified by the exchanger's labor and credit, provided the fee is a "just salary" and maintains equality between the parties.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Azpilcueta's analysis of "exchange by bills" (bills of exchange). He is currently examining the conditions that make these financial contracts just or unjust, specifically focusing on the timing of payments and the honesty of the transaction.</meta>

# On the final chapter of usury, page 45

The exchanger takes more than a just salary <note>Original: "justo salario." In this context, it refers to a fair service fee for the labor and risk of moving money, rather than interest on a loan.</note> if he must provide the funds immediately in the place where they are requested; even so, he is satisfied with that fee if he is given a term of three or four months to make the delivery. Such are also the contracts in which, conversely, those who provide money a year or half a year in advance do so with a pact that the exchanger shall afterwards charge nothing as his just salary for delivering the funds there. In this, we see many—even the learned and religious—err gravely. It is clear that the aforementioned contracts are of this nature. Because in all these cases, either the just salary is not paid, or too much is paid; whether by giving or taking the money sooner or later, more or less than the just price is taken. And by a rule set down above <margin>a See Commentary, ch. 1, 14, question 3, number 26, and above in the same work, number 14.</margin>: All contracts in which equality is not maintained are unjust. And by another rule set down <margin>b c Above in the same, number 14.</margin> in this and in another Commentary: All contracts in which more than the highest just price for cash is taken, or less than the lowest just price for cash is accepted, contain formal or virtual usury.

25 <margin>b c Above in the same, number 14.</margin> ¶ Second, it follows that those exchanges we see every day being made with Kings, merchants, and others are bad (according to everyone) and clearly unjust, according to Cajetan <margin>d ch. 1, 14, q. 3, num. 4. with this previously explained; and held by Thomas [Aquinas] in the Second Part of the Summa, question 78, article 2.</margin>. In these cases, they take money from the exchangers and give them bills for Rome, Lisbon, Lyons, Flanders, Venice, or other parts, so that they may be paid there at a certain time or fair. Both parties know <margin>e In the treatise on exchanges, ch. 1. Which Sylvester resolves best of all under the word "usury," 4, question 9; and "dry exchange" according to everyone.</margin> that the one taking the money has no funds there, nor credit, nor any factor <note>An agent or business representative acting on behalf of a merchant.</note>, nor any intention of paying there, but rather here, where he takes the money at the price it would be worth there at the fair. These are even worse if the one taking the money here promises to pay the "exchange" for the outward journey and the "re-exchange" <note>Original: "recambio." This was a penalty fee or interest charged when a bill of exchange was not honored at its destination and had to be sent back to the point of origin.</note> for the return, should the bills not be fulfilled there. Later, the exchanger sends his bills there, and once they are notified to the intended recipient—with the response that they do not know the sender or do not wish to fulfill them—they return here re-exchanged. In the first of these two exchanges, only one usury is paid, but in the second, two. The same applies to the exchange in which one gives money to another to be paid at a certain time, such as during the fairs of Flanders or elsewhere, according to whatever the money happened to be worth there. To perform these exchanges is to seek means to deceive God and to show a lack of faith, or forgetfulness, or a poor memory of the fact that His divine wisdom sees all our works, along with all our bad and good thoughts, much more fully than we ourselves do.

26 <margin>a Cajetan in the treatise on exchanges, ch. 4.</margin> ¶ In † a single case they could be saved, at least from mortal sin and the obligation of making restitution for these three types of exchange: namely, when the exchanger finds someone who wants to take his money through a true exchange, but to succor the necessity of this person or that, he refrains from giving it to the first party. In doing so, he loses the chance to earn through a just exchange as much as he earns through this feigned one <margin>a Cajetan in the treatise on exchanges, ch. 4.</margin>: because this is nothing more than asking for his interest <note>Original: "interés." In 16th-century scholastic thought, this referred to "compensation for a loss incurred" (id quod interest) rather than profit on a loan.</note> <margin>b which we shall say is licit below in the same work, number 34.</margin>.

27 <margin>c in the present [work], num. 46.</margin> ¶ It is, however, to be noted † that although there may be statutes stating that bills of exchange have summary execution <note>A legal process allowing a creditor to collect a debt immediately without a lengthy trial.</note>, those of a feigned exchange would not have it, as Anania said <margin>c in the present [work], num. 46.</margin> is practiced in Bologna. If, however, the exchange contained in the bill were partly <margin>d Which Laurentius deduces at length in the said chapter, "He Consulted," part 2, question 135.</margin> true and partly feigned, it could be executed for the part that was true, provided the adversary at least confessed that it was true regarding that part. <margin>e Through what was said above, numbers 14 and 24.</margin> It follows, thirdly, that it is illicit for me to give you a thousand ducats now with a pact that you have them given to me in Rome a year from now, without any exchange fee, because of the profit you will derive from them in this interim. This is usury on my part; for by advancing the payment, I gain the salary <note>The fee I would normally have to pay an agent to move the money.</note> that I would have had to give you if you had delivered them to me immediately.

28 <margin>f Book 7, q. 3, art. 2 near the end; on justice and law.</margin> ¶ Fourth † follows: although the said Doctor Soto <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a hugely influential Spanish Dominican theologian and contemporary of the author.</note> determines in one part <margin>f Book 7, q. 3, art. 2 near the end; on justice and law.</margin> that nothing can be taken for this type of exchange when letters of credit are given from one city of a kingdom to another city of the same kingdom—such as from Medina to Toledo or Seville—in another part <margin>g in the same book 7, q. 6, art. 1.</margin> he said yes, and very well so. Firstly, because the reason stated above that justifies this contract from here to Rome also justifies it from here to Lyons, and the same from here to Pamplona, Burgos, Seville, and Toledo: provided it is done sincerely and without fraud, charging as much less as is reasonable for the shorter distance and fewer dangers, labors, and costs involved in transferring, carrying, holding, and guarding the money there than in other more distant parts. Secondly, because the reason that concludes that bills for outside the kingdom which mask usury are illicit concludes, conversely, that those for another city in the kingdom are licit if they are given sincerely (without fraud or deceit) for an honest salary. Some say, however, that by a new prohibition, these exchanges from one part of the kingdom to another in the same are forbidden, both here and in Portugal, because they were almost always done to mask usury. Which, in our opinion, ought to be limited, so that there would not be...

<summary>Azpilcueta condemns "dry exchange" and fictitious bills used to hide usury, arguing that timing and equality in contracts are essential for justice. He also debates whether bank transfers within the same kingdom are licit, concluding they are valid if they reflect actual costs and risks.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the justice of exchange contracts, specifically addressing when an exchanger receives money before or after delivering it elsewhere. Azpilcueta argues against broad prohibitions on domestic exchange, suggesting they harm travelers and students.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>Resolutory Commentary on Exchange.</header>

...there would be reason, when the exchanger takes before he gives, or causes to be given. Firstly, because few or no usuries are disguised when the exchanger receives before he makes the delivery: as is commonly done in this type of exchange. Rather, all the disguise is the other way around, when the exchanger gives first in order to receive later: which is a type of exchange that the very ancient Bolognese <note>Refers to the famous legal scholars at the University of Bologna.</note> call "dry exchanges" <term>cambios secos; a financial transaction that mimics a bill of exchange to hide a high-interest loan without any actual transport of funds</term>, as was said above <margin>a Above in the same, number 10.</margin>, citing Lawrence for this <margin>b In the chapter "He consulted." Part 3, question 1, element 1. On the constitution of the prince. Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica 1. 2e question 97, article 2.</margin>. Secondly, because this exchange is just in itself, according to divine, canonical, and civil law; and the law is not to be changed unless the utility and benefit that moves it is evident <margin>c</margin>. Such utility does not seem to exist in this prohibition. Instead, it would deprive students, pilgrims, and many other businessmen of a good means of transferring (almost without cost and danger) their provisions and money from Seville and other similar cities to this city of Salamanca, Burgos, and other parts; and from Burgos and such places to Seville and other very distant parts, between which there are dangerous passes.

¶ There is, however, great color and reason <margin>29</margin> to forbid within the kingdom those exchanges in which the exchanger gives first, in order to receive more later there or in another place: because certainly many usuries would be disguised in it. Although, in my weak opinion, little profit would be drawn from it. Firstly, because it does not take away from the profiteers <note>Original: "logreros," referring to those who seek excessive gain or usury.</note> who wish to use feigned exchanges the means to disguise their gains. Rather, it gives them occasion to do without embarrassment and with greater profit for outside the kingdom what they used to do with some fear, shame, and less gain for a city within the kingdom. Secondly, because a better remedy would be, and even now would be, to appoint honest judges to examine past and present exchanges; finding by the circumstances of the persons that they were feigned, they should punish those who made them, wherever they were for, executing the ancient laws that have not been repealed by this new prohibition <margin>d On the law "We command." Code of Justinian, on appeals; and chapter 1, on constitutions, book 6.</margin>, which is not contrary to them. Thirdly, because through this ban, the past remains ignored and almost pardoned, which is an "unjust mercy" <note>Original Latin: "Est iniusta misericordia."</note> <margin>e Chapter "It is an unjust mercy" at the beginning and at the end there: "the ease of pardon provides an incentive for offending." Dist. 4. Chapter "Do not think," with many following, 23, question 5. g On the contrary, the law "It is not doubtful." Code of Justinian, on laws; and the chapter "It is certain," on the rules of law, book 6.</margin>, for ignoring the past and forbidding the future gives occasion to do what is forbidden, in the hope of another such pardon; this is against clement justice, which, with harsh punishment of the past, restrains the wicked for the future <margin>f</margin>. It serves, nevertheless, to more easily verify the fiction of feigned exchanges: because it will be more easily seen that this Spaniard, who takes an exchange to pay in Flanders, has no money there, just as it could be seen that he does not have it in Seville. Although we have already seen frauds against this in Lisbon, where a knight who needed money did not take it himself for Medina, but begged some merchant to take it for himself, the knight obliging himself to pay it back there with the exchange. So true is that Italian saying, *Once the law is made, the fraud is found* <note>Original Italian: "Fata la legge, trobata la fraude."</note> <margin>g</margin>.

There follows <margin>†</margin> the fifth point: that His Majesty's intention was holy in having wished <margin>30</margin> to cut short in days past the disorder that existed in taking excessive profit through this kind of exchange, by ordering that for the exchange from these kingdoms to Rome, no more than 400 maravedís <note>The maravedí was a small copper coin used as a unit of account in Spain.</note> be taken per Chamber Ducat; nor from Rome to here more than 420. Nor from these kingdoms to Naples per large ducat, more than 400. Nor to Besançon per the *escudo de marco*, more than 375. Nor from Besançon to here per escudo, more than 390. Nor from here to Flanders per escudo of six shillings at sixty maravedís, more than 370. Nor from Flanders to here per escudo, less than 70 *grossi* <note>Original: "gruessos," referring to the Flemish groat or groot.</note>. Nor from here to Valencia for one golden *castellano*, more than 380. Nor from Valencia to here, more than 510 per castellano. Nor from here to Zaragoza for one escudo, more than one ducat given there, nor from Zaragoza to here, more than 400. Nor from here to Barcelona, except what has been given until now. Nor from here to Portugal per ducat, more than 370, which are worth 400 reales there. Nor from Portugal to here per ducat, more than 385. After this moderating provision, His Royal Majesty totally forbade exchanges within all of Spain. That is, that there be none from the kingdoms of Castile to those of Aragon, Catalonia, and Valencia, nor even within Castile, with certain small limitations; only in which it would be well to understand the exchanges where the exchanger receives the money before he has to give it, for the reasons aforementioned <margin>a Above in this same Commentary, number 18.</margin>. Would that it all be received, executed with as much vigilance, integrity, and constancy as the good intention with which it was provided. Although I fear it will not be so, at least in the...

# On the final chapter of Usury.

...in the exchanges, which are made from those kingdoms where money is worth more and there are more goods, to these [lands]. For those who have money in those kingdoms will not want to give their money beforehand to be paid in these lands less than what they are worth in those: as we will point out below regarding the exchange from Flanders and Portugal to here.

->SUMMARY<-

Exchange by physical transfer <term>traspaso real; the actual movement of physical coins or bullion from one location to another</term>: what it is. That it is pure purchase and sale, or pure barter. That it is just, provided equality is maintained, num. 31, and otherwise not, and provided just laws are followed, num. 22.
Money can be sold under many respects, but not insofar as it is the price [of other things], num. 32.

<margin>
31
</margin>
<margin>
a *Below in the same, number 41.*
b *Throughout the title on contracting purchase and sale, and on the exchange of things, and Law 1, with four signs, of the Digest on prescribed words.* <note>Standard references to Roman civil law found in the Digest of Justinian.</note>
c *Book 7, question 5, article 3. On justice and law.*
d *Argument from Law 1 of the Digest on the exchange of things; Law 1 of the Digest on contracting purchase; Law 3, final paragraph, of the Digest on commodatum <note>a loan for use</note>; and those things written by Cajetan in the treatise on exchanges, chapter 6, and Medina in the Codex on the restitution of things, at folio 148. Although regarding some things of little weight, they may seem different.*
e *Argument from Law 1 and 2 of the Digest on letting and hiring, and the paragraph "Likewise the price" in the Institutes on purchase.*
f *In chapter 1, 14th question, 3rd.*
g *As Lawrence in the chapter "He consulted," part 2, question 26.*
h *Law 2, title 6, Partida 5. Hostiensis in the Summa on the exchange of things, section "What however."* <note>Refers to the Siete Partidas, the famous 13th-century Spanish statutory code.</note>
i *Above in the same, number 24.*
k *By the same reasoning, Law "That" of the Digest on the Aquilian Law.*
</margin>
Sixteenth Point: That the fourth type of exchange is also licit (according to everyone). This is exchange by physical transfer, which is done by buying, bartering, or giving through another unnamed contract <term>contracto innominado; a legal agreement that does not fall under standard named categories like "sale" but is still binding</term> currency that is worth less in one land than in another. This might be because it does not circulate there, or because its metal is not worth as much there as elsewhere, or because it is broken, defaced, shaved <note>original: "rayda"; the illegal practice of clipping or shaving the edges of precious metal coins</note>, worn, or lacking weight; and by carrying it to another place where it is worth more, or because it is not weighed there, or because it circulates, etc. And one later commutes it for another currency that is worth more where the first was worth less, provided that the due equality is maintained. This is all purchase, sale, or barter, or another unnamed contract of "I give that you may give" <note>Latin: "do ut des"</note> or etc., as will be said below ^a. These are known to be licit if the due equality is observed ^b. Nor is it an obstacle to say that for the same thing for which less was given in one land, more is taken in another. Because that for which less is given in one land is worth less in it; and that for which more is later taken in the other land is worth more in it. And thus, what was bought for less in this land can be sold for more in the other; and what was bartered in this land for a thing of lower price can be bartered in another for a thing of more, as with all other merchandise—provided that one does not give so little in the one, nor take so much more in the other, that the <margin>32</margin> just price (as determined by a prudent man) fails to be observed. <insert>†</insert> It follows from this that money can be bought and sold, although Soto <note>Domingo de Soto, a prominent Spanish Dominican theologian and contemporary of the author</note> ^c holds the contrary. This is a very certain thing when money is not considered as "money" [a fixed measure of value], but as a piece of metal, such as gold, silver, or broken copper. And even when it is considered as money, under any of the eight respects through which we will say below that it can be worth more or less than the price the law sets for it; and even whenever it is proposed as merchandise and not as the price for other merchandise ^d, if it is weighed at its root. Because every time it is considered according to one of these respects, and not as the price for other things, it is merchandise that can be valued at something more or less, and consequently bought ^e. And the Archdeacon <note>Guido de Baysio, a famous canonist</note> ^f does not hold the contrary, though some attribute it to him ^g. For if one weighs the matter well, he does not say that money cannot be sold, but rather that its *use* cannot be sold insofar as it is money, without the money itself being sold. And because the law of the *Partida* ^h determines that everything that can be exchanged can be sold, and everything that can be sold can be exchanged (except for spiritual things, which can be exchanged but not sold), and everyone confesses that <margin>33</margin> money can be exchanged. <insert>¶</insert> It also follows that this kind of exchange will be unjust if the exchanger buys or barters what is worth less in one land for even less than what it is worth there, or sells and barters what is worth more for even more than what it is worth there—especially when this is done for advancing the price or for providing it on credit. This can easily be proven by the two aforementioned ^i rules. It could also be unjust if prohibited currency were transferred in a way that is unjust for others to transfer it ^k.

->SUMMARY<-

Exchange for interest: licit, and one may take something for interest, num. 34.
If by giving in exchange one leaves the trade that was determined to be held, and otherwise not, num. 35.
Doctors Antonio and Luis Coronel defended, num. 34.

<meta>catchword: Lo. xvij</meta>

<summary>This page explains that money can be treated as a commodity (merchandise) rather than just a fixed measure of value, making it licit to buy or barter currency based on its physical condition, metal content, or regional demand. Azpilcueta argues that such exchanges are moral as long as they follow the "just price" and are not used as a cover for usurious credit charges.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical and moral analysis of financial exchanges. Azpilcueta is currently defining the "fifth type" of exchange, focusing on the concept of "interest" as compensation for lost opportunity (lucrum cessans), a pivotal concept in 16th-century economic thought.</meta>

# Resolutory Commentary on Exchanges.

<margin>b In the Commentary on chapter 1, question 14, article 3, number 46 and following; together with this excuse.</margin>
<margin>c Because exactly the same reasoning and exactly the same law persuades <note>original: "Quia eadē omnino ratio, idem omnino ius suadet"</note> in the law *Illud* of the Digest regarding the Aquilian Law, and the chapter *Traslato* regarding constitutions.</margin>
<margin>d In the treatise on exchanges, chapter 1.</margin>
<margin>e Above in the same, number 26.</margin>
<margin>f As if God did not exist, or did not search hearts and minds <note>original: "corda, & renes" — literally "hearts and kidneys," a biblical idiom for one's innermost thoughts</note>, contrary to Psalm 75, the chapter *Novit* on judgments, and the chapter *Deus omnipotens*, question 2, article 1.</margin>

17. That the fifth type of exchange based on interest <term>interesse; here meaning compensation for the loss suffered or profit lost by the lender because they did not have the money available for their own business</term> is also licit. This means that if a money changer deals in merchandise and, by lending to someone in need, stops his own trading, he may take his interest, both for the profit [lost] and the loss [incurred]. This is because (as we have proved at length elsewhere b) any merchant may take such interest under certain conditions. We add to this anew that even if he does not deal in any merchandise other than his exchanges, if he stops dealing in those exchanges (provided they are licit) in order to lend, he may take the interest for the profit he ceases to earn in his office of just exchange c. For this, the singular decision of Cajetan d, referred to above e, is relevant: that whoever stops making a true exchange to help another with a feigned one may earn what he could have with the true one. But woe to him who does not actually stop his trading, nor ceases making as many true exchanges as before, yet takes feigned interest without any true or likely basis f. It is as if God did not exist, who searches not only our works, but even our hearts.

<margin>g Book 7, question 5, article 5, on justice and law.</margin>
<margin>h Where mentioned above.</margin>
<margin>i In counsel 11.</margin>
<margin>k In the repetition of the chapter *Consultis*, question 1, part 2.</margin>
<margin>l Part 2, title 1, question 7, paragraph 49.</margin>
<margin>m In the present, from number 46.</margin>
<margin>n Under the word "usury," 4, throughout.</margin>
<margin>o In the treatise on exchanges.</margin>
<margin>p In the Codex on the restitution of things, from folio 145.</margin>

¶ Through this type of exchange, one can also justify the response of the Doctors of Paris—among whom were those two renowned brothers Antonio Coronel and Luis Coronel (whose works and counsel aided us for some time)—which Lord Doctor Soto g criticizes. They argued that merchants may charge more if they wait for payment until the second fairs <term>ferias; seasonal markets that served as the primary centers for international trade and the settling of debts</term> than if they only wait until the first; and more if they wait until the third than the second. This is because the exchange of interest is greater the more one likely ceases to earn. It is certain that the trader who stops trading, and the changer who stops exchanging for two fairs with his money, loses more profit than if he stopped for only one fair. Nor is it believable that such learned doctors from such a great university understood this as that other type of exchange involving purchase or barter; even students of a few years know that buying or bartering more expensively because of a longer term is usury. And because they have been spoken of so little in the schools recently—as the same Lord Doctor Soto says, that until his time they were never understood there h—it seems to us that Gaspar Calderino i, Lawrence Rodulpho k, Saint Antoninus l, John of Anania m, Sylvester n, Cajetan o, and Medina p, and others, scrutinized them deeply enough, though they did not explain their concepts as extensively as we have ours.

<margin>q chapter 17, numbers 211.</margin>
<margin>a folio chapter 1, 14, question 3, number 49.</margin>

¶ Concerning q this exchange, he sins mortally—with the obligation to restore the money to the one who exchanged it—who, having removed his money from trade, leaves the art of merchandising entirely and takes up that of exchange, giving all his money to exchange from fair to fair for a certain or uncertain interest. This is done with a pact that those who take it pay him as much as others who trade in what he used to do might earn, or a determined amount of likely interest he would have earned if he had traded. Since he has already removed the money from trade and does not wish to trade, there is no such true or likely interest, as was also noted in the Manual a and in another Commentary b. Likewise, the money changer sins with the obligation to make restitution if, by giving money in exchange, he does not actually stop trading with the funds he has designated for that purpose, for the same reason. Therefore, there are many penitents enriched by these means, and even confessors who hear and have heard their confessions and absolved them without ordering them to desist or to restore what was gained in this way—or ordering it when the penitent is unwilling to do so—to the condemnation of both.

## S U M M A R Y.

Exchange for safekeeping, licit. n. 36. When may one charge for it? nu. 37.
Money changer receives and pays in cash and by bills of exchange. Whether he can receive something for paying in cash. num. 37.
Payment of five per thousand for cash, illicit, except in three cases. num. 37 & 38.
To earn a little justly, how much better than much with sin. nu. 39.
One who does not pay the changer, or takes cash from him, and the changer for allowing it, they sin. num. 40.

<meta>This page continues Azpilcueta’s systematic evaluation of different types of currency exchange. Having previously discussed exchanges based on interest and lost profit, he now moves to the "sixth type," which concerns the fees charged by money changers for the physical security and administration of deposits.</meta>

# On the final chapter of Usury.

<margin>
36
<margin>
c Law *Argentarius*, paragraph 1, and Law *Quedã*, paragraph *Numularios* of the Digest, on the production of documents <note>original: "ff. de edendo"</note>.
d The laborer is worthy of his hire. Luke 10 <note>original: "Dignus enim est operarius mercede sua. Luc. 10."</note>.
e Canon 1, question 2, case 13.
a Because that which can be done through the king can be done through the parties. Law *Nõ impossibile* of the Digest on compacts; chapter *Contractus* with the gloss, on the rules of the sixth book.
</margin>
</margin>

18. That the sixth [type of] exchange for safekeeping is also just. This means that when the law ^c, custom, or statute requires that the money changer be the guard, depositary, and guarantor of the money given or exchanged for those who need it—whether they give it or send it—and that he be obligated to pay the merchants or persons whom the depositors designate, he may lawfully take a just salary in such a manner, either from the commonwealth or from the depositing parties. This is because this office and burden is useful to the commonwealth and contains no iniquity; for it is just that he who works should earn his daily wage ^d. Such a money changer works by receiving, holding in deposit, and keeping ready the money of many merchants, and by writing, giving, and keeping accounts for one and the other, involving significant trouble and sometimes the risk of accounting errors or other issues. The same could be done by contract ^a, whereby someone obligates himself to various parties to receive and hold their money in deposit, to give, pay, and keep accounts with one another as they instruct him, etc. This is because this contract is one of hiring out his works and labors to others, which is a named contract <term>named contract; a contract recognized by a specific legal name in Roman law, such as "hiring and leasing"</term>, just and holy ^b.

<margin>
37
<margin>
b For it is a contract of hiring on the part of the money changer, and of leasing on the part of the others, with a fixed wage established. Laws 1 and 2 of the Digest on hiring; paragraph 1 of the Institutes on hiring.
c Soto, book 7, question 4, article 1, on justice and law.
d Royal Decree 127.
e Royal Decree 129.
f Rule: No one should be burdened by the hatred of another. On the rules of law, book 6; question 4, case 1, throughout; chapter *Si habes*, question 3, case 24.
</margin>
</margin>

¶ However, the amount of the salary for this labor is not determined in law. It should be noted that the money changer takes money in two ways: in cash, actually receiving the physical money; and by drafts <term>libranças; written orders or bills of exchange</term>, accepting notes from other exchanges or other persons, by which they promise and consign the payment to his bank for what they remit to him, so that he may pay it on their account. He also pays in two other ways: in cash, actually giving the money; or by drafts, remitting the payment to other exchanges.

¶ Some presuppose ^c that in these kingdoms it is ordained and determined that when the money changer pays anyone in cash, he receives five per thousand, and when by note, remitting to another exchange, nothing. We, however, find the contrary expressed by the royal decrees <term>pragmaticas; laws or edicts issued by the monarch on specific administrative matters</term> of these kingdoms. For in one ^d it is said that the Catholic Monarchs <note>Isabella I of Castile and Ferdinand II of Aragon</note> ordained in Seville in the year 1491 that the money changer could pay those who held drafts and others in deficient, broken, or worn coins, paying for the deficiencies; and that from whoever wanted their payment in sound, good, and selected coins, he could take five per thousand for paying it as such, and no more, even if the party wished to give him more. In another decree ^e, it is said that later the same Catholic Monarchs—informed that the money changers took occasion from the said law not only to take the said five per thousand in the said case, but even in all cases where they paid in cash in any coin, selected or unselected—revoked the said law in the year 1513. They declared it null (regarding this matter) and ordered that money changers may not pay in broken or worn coins, nor take anything from anyone to whom money was drafted in their exchanges, under heavy penalties. This provision was very holy and necessary. Because it is against all natural, divine, and human reason ^f for you to take from me or another five or ten per thousand for what our debtors or others have drafted to your bank or exchange, without you doing anything else for us except paying us what was drafted to you. And because it is not just ^g that we should pay you for the labor you spent in guarding what belongs to our debtors, or those who drafted to us through you, or in keeping accounts with them. Although some say there is an extraordinary provision allowing the said five per thousand to be taken, I do not believe it. For it would contain injustice outside of the three cases we will mention shortly.

<margin>
38
<margin>
g Argument from the Code: on the son for the father; nor the wife for the husband; throughout.
h Chapter *Peccatum* on the rules of law, book 6; with what we said in the manual, chapter 17, numbers 63 and 64.
</margin>
</margin>

¶ From this it follows that not only are the said five per thousand (when they pay in cash) not their salary, but rather they are theft and unjust extortion that binds one to hell or to restitution and entire penance to be free from it ^h, except in three cases. The first: when the payment is made to the same persons who deposited and gave their money in cash to the exchange, and they pay that amount as a discount for the labor and care the money changer takes in receiving and guarding their money and performing the aforementioned tasks. The second: when those to whom the depositors draft the payment for their merchandise sold the goods at a price higher by the amount they had to pay to the exchange for receiving cash, as a discount and discharge of what the depositors owe the money changer. The third: when those who receive the payments give that amount to the exchange of their own free will. Of these (in our opinion) there are very few, because they are not...

D

<summary>Azpilcueta discusses the legality of service fees charged by money changers, arguing that while they deserve a "salary" for the labor of safekeeping and accounting, a common 0.5% (five per thousand) fee is often an unjust extortion under Spanish law and Christian morality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Azpilcueta’s critique of the banking practices of his day, specifically focusing on the "sixth type" of exchange—the service of safeguarding deposits. He transitions from defining the office to detailing the specific ways money changers and their clients fall into the sin of usury.</meta>

# Resolutory Commentary on Exchanges.

<margin>
b Chapter *Because in all things* regarding usury <note>original: "c. Quia in omnibus de usur."</note>, chapter 1, from the title in book 6.
</margin>
<margin>
c By chapter 1, question 14, case 3, by the definition of usury and those things we placed there; indeed, it is the worst kind of usury. Hostiensis <note>A famous 13th-century canonist.</note> in his *Summary on Usury*, section "Whether by any," near the end.
</margin>
...to give them <note>The fees or concessions mentioned on the previous page.</note> even to those who would have left them to them, simply to avoid waiting eight or ten days for payment during the settling periods because the money changer is deferring it. This happens because they do not want to leave him anything for the spot payment <term>contado|Payment made in physical cash immediately, rather than through a credit note.</term> and they want the full payment of their bills of exchange <term>libranças|Written orders or bills of exchange used to transfer funds between accounts at a trade fair.</term>, as has happened to us ourselves. Their will is as coerced as that of one who pays interest to a usurer, which does not excuse them from sin, nor from the obligation of restitution <margin>b</margin>.

Others say that their salary is two, three, or four percent, depending on whether the money they lend or give in cash to various people until the next fair is more expensive or cheaper. That this is usury, and a mortal sin involving the obligation to make restitution, cannot be denied in any way <margin>c</margin>.

<margin>
d Because those things which are left confused by law must be determined by his judgment. Law 1 of the Digest <note>original: "ff." refers to the Pandects or Digest of Roman Law.</note> on the causes and office of a delegate.
</margin>
<margin>39</margin>
Therefore † we say that his salary is what each trader gives him, or ought to give him, according to the judgment of a good man <margin>d</margin> at each fair once their accounts are settled—more or less, according to how much more or less was processed by him or for him until that settlement. This is not a fixed amount, beyond what we are told: that some give them one, or one and a half per thousand, plus what they are given for exchanging one type of currency for another. And if you say that the latter—nowadays (when there are no gold pieces, or they are not being exchanged)—is little or nothing, and the former is too little to enrich so many people as quickly and as greatly as they are being enriched, we shall answer you. We say that (according to report) they have been a great part of the cause why there are no gold pieces, nor are they exchanged in the kingdom, having been the instruments for extracting money from it with a thousand arts and crafts—although I believe there has been another greater cause. We also answer that exchanges were not invented to enrich the money changers, but to provide a easier and more useful order to trade, so that there would be more merchandise and at cheaper prices. This would be the case if they exercised their office cleanly and were satisfied with a just salary, receiving it from those who owe it—those whose money they guard and whose accounts they keep—and not from those who do not owe it. They should remember what that great King and prophet said <margin>a</margin>: "Better is a little with justice than great riches with sin." And what the Author of the prophets said <margin>b</margin>: "What does it profit a man to gain the whole world and lose his soul for it?" They should not wish (contrary to the precept of the Psalm <margin>c</margin>) to imitate the wicked who enrich themselves through evil.
<margin>
a Psalm 36. Better is a little for the righteous than the many riches of sinners.
</margin>
<margin>
b Matthew 16. What does it profit a man if he gain the whole world, but suffer the loss of his own soul?
</margin>
<margin>
c Psalm 36. Be not envious of evildoers, etc.
</margin>

<margin>40</margin>
¶ Regarding † this type of exchange, not only do the money changers sin, but also—with the obligation of restitution—those who give them money to keep for them and perform the aforementioned tasks. These people then do not want to pay them anything, saying that what the changer earns with their money, and what they receive from those to whom they pay in cash, is sufficient for a salary. If the money changers ask them for anything, they leave them and move to deal with others; and so that they will not be left, the changers forgo the salary owed to them and take it from those who do not owe it. ¶ Those also sin who give the money changers some money in cash, and later, if they take it in bills of exchange for themselves or for others rather than in cash, make the changers pay the fee for having given it to them in cash when accounts are settled—which is at least two percent. This profit can be taken for no reason in the world as something owed, except for the benefit that the money changer has received or is to receive from that money given in cash. Thus it is clear usury, since the money changers who take the money put in the labor of receiving it, guarding it, keeping accounts, and having it ready for whenever it is requested or drawn upon, while the one who gave it does none of these things. ¶ The money changers commit another usury regarding this same matter: namely, that for a merchant who has placed cash in their power, bank, or table, the changer issues him that amount plus a certain amount of money in another bank for what he needs for as long a time as the changer has held his money; this is done on the condition that the merchant leaves the changer the profit that would have been paid by reason of the cash payment. This, at least in their intentions, is clear usury, because the trader leaves the money changer the profit which, in his view, he has earned by depositing in cash, so that the changer might lend him an equal or similar amount via a bill of exchange until the next fair. The money changer lends it to avoid paying that which, according to his evil custom, he thinks he owes the depositor. All of this is a great misery worthy of much lamentation.

# On the final chapter concerning usury.

In this regard they do not differ. n. 41. And for this reason, it matters nothing whether it is called this or that. It requires two things to be just. n. 42.

**Named and unnamed contracts**: in what do they differ, and in what do they not, regarding this purpose? n. 41.

**Commute**: this word, which includes all contracts. nu. 42.

**Profit** is drawn from the trade of money just as from that of other things. n. 43.

**Money**, for these eight respects, is worth more or less. n. 43. Regarding four of these. n. 44. Regarding the fifth. n. 45. Regarding the sixth. n. 46. Regarding the seventh. n. 51. Regarding the eighth. n. 62.

**Money**: how does it rise or fall with time? n. 46. And not by being entrusted for a longer time. n. 47. How, and when, must it be returned in the same currency and price in which it was lent? n. 48. & following.

A **Ducat** <note>A gold coin used throughout Europe.</note>, by rising in value, does not cease to be the same as before; although a **fanega** <note>A measure of grain, roughly a bushel.</note> does change if they increase its volume. n. 48. Because the price is an extrinsic thing to it, like wheat. n. 49.

Whoever lends something must receive another thing of equal **intrinsic goodness**. n. 50.

<margin>a Law *Iurisgentium* with the gloss, Digest, on pacts. Law *Ex placito*, Code, on the exchange of things, with the gloss. <note>original: "l.Iurisgẽ. cum glof. ff. de pact. l. Ex placito. C. de rerum permuta. cum glo."</note></margin>

<margin>d Which are many, according to the notes by Bartolus on the said law *Naturalis*, section *But if I do*, and by everyone at the beginning of the said law *Iurisgentium*, and by the gloss and others on the said law *Ex placito*. <note>Bartolus de Saxoferrato was a highly influential 14th-century jurist.</note></margin>
<margin>e Above in this same work, n. 14 & n. 24.</margin>
Eighteenthly, † we say that the justice of exchange by purchase and exchange by barter, or any other unnamed contract, must be weighed and measured by the same weights and measures. This is because, although purchase on one hand and barter—which is an <term>unnamed contract|Innominate contract; a contract that does not fall under a specific legal name like 'sale' or 'lease' but is still binding under the principle of 'I give that you may give'.</term> <margin>a</margin>—and the other unnamed contracts on the other differ in that purchase is a <term>named contract|A contract with a specific legal designation and established rules in Roman Law.</term> <margin>b</margin> and the others are not; and consequently, they differ in all those ways that contracts called "named"—because they have a specific name <margin>c</margin> in law—differ from those that do not, and are therefore called "unnamed" <margin>d</margin>. But regarding our purpose, which is to see how one can justly profit by buying, selling, or bartering money, there is no difference. For in this regard, it amounts to the same thing whether one says it is a purchase, or a barter, or a contract of "I give to you, so that you may give to me," or "I give to you, or I do, so that you may make someone give to me, or you give," etc. This applies to the contract by which one gives to another in **Medina** <note>Medina del Campo, the site of the primary Spanish trade fairs.</note> one hundred for one hundred and ten, to be given or made to be given in **Flanders**, or giving him in Flanders one hundred for one hundred and twenty, to be given in Medina. This is because two things, or one of them, make these contracts illicit: namely, the inequality of what is given and what is to be taken, and charging more or less for advancing or delaying, or granting a long or short term. It is certain that these two things, and each one of them, make the contract of barter and any other named contract just as illicit as that of purchase; and conversely, they make purchase illicit just as they do these others, for the reasons stated above <margin>e</margin>.

<margin>k Where cited above.</margin>
<margin>a Above in this same work, n. 21 & 22.</margin>

¶ The 2nd point follows: that to satisfy all opinions, we should use this word **commute**, which is general to all the aforementioned and any other contracts by which something passes from one person to another. ¶ The 3rd point follows: that the said exchange (whatever it may be called) is licit if it is done justly, and otherwise not. It is done justly when two things concur: First, that for the money being commuted, its just value is given. The other, that its value is not lowered because it is to be delivered later, as **Cajetan** <margin>b</margin> noted well, and before and better than all others, **Sylvester** <margin>c</margin>. These points, even if they or others had not noted them, are proven by the two rules set down above <margin>d</margin>.

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that for the purpose of moral and legal justice, it does not matter if a currency exchange is classified as a 'purchase,' 'barter,' or 'unnamed contract.' He establishes that profit in exchange is only licit if the money is traded at its current just value without charging for the element of time.</summary>

# Resolutory Commentary on Exchanges.

<margin>
a Book 2 of *On the Governance of Rulers*, chapter 14, which Anthony Cajetan and almost all theologians follow. The same is held by Calderino, Counsel 11, on usury, and Lawrence in the chapter *Consulted*, question 1, part 3, on usury. <note>original: "Lib. 2. de regi. print. c. 14. que Anto. Caie. et omues fere Theologi sequuntur. idẽ tenent Cald. Co- fil. 11. de vsur. et Laure. in c. Con fuluit. q. 1. parte 3. de vsur."</note>
f Above in the same, numbers 12 & 32.
