...or when it is worth less, in order to exchange it where and when it might be worth more: For as Saint Thomas rightly feels <gloss>a</gloss> and as was said above <gloss>f</gloss>, money (even as money) is exchangeable with another to be able to gain a profit by trading in it.

¶ The fifth point follows: that the solution to the said difficulty depends on knowing how and when a type of money, which is equal to another according to the common price set by law or custom at the time they were minted, is worth more or less in some respect than the other. For one cannot know if the exchange of receiving one money for another is just without knowing how much it is worth. Therefore, we say that this can happen for one of eight respects. The first, for not being of the same metal. The second, for not being metal of the same karat <note>The purity or fineness of the precious metal.</note>. The third, for not being of equal shape and weight. The fourth, for the diversity of the land in which they are located. The fifth, for the demonetization <note>original: "reprouacion"</note>, or doubt of the demonetization, rise, or fall of one. The sixth, for the diversity of time. The seventh, for the lack and necessity of it. The eighth, for the absence of one and the presence of the other.

<margin>
g Which experience, the teacher of things, teaches. Chapter *Quanto fit*, on elections, book 6, from your counsel 11, on usury, which is followed by John of Anania in the present, number 46 and following. <note>original: "Quod docet experientia rerũ magistra. c. Quã fit de electio. li. 6 a tu consi. 11. de vsur. quem sequi tur Ioan. ab A- nan. in presenti. n. 46. & sequẽt."</note>
</margin>
<margin>44</margin>
¶ Regarding the first <gloss>g</gloss>, which is not being of the same metal, sometimes a gold ducat <note>A standard gold coin of the era.</note> is worth more to the one who holds it than another in silver or base metal, because it can be better guarded or carried far; and conversely, sometimes a coin in silver or base metal is worth more than one in gold due to the lack of small change for daily spending. ¶ Regarding the second respect, which is that the two coins are not of metal of equal karat, it happens that of two ducats—which by law are estimated at the same value, such as the ducats of Castile, Portugal, Hungary, and Florence—one can be worth more than the other, even though they are in the same land.

¶ Regarding the third, not being of equal shape or weight: sometimes a ducat of the same minting is worth more than another if it has an extra grain <note>A tiny unit of weight.</note> and is well-formed, while the other lacks a grain, or is broken, cracked, disfigured, etc. ¶ Regarding the fourth, being in different lands, the same currency is worth more in one land than in another, according to the accepted Calderino <gloss>a</gloss>: either because its metal is worth more in one than in the other—as gold is worth more in Spain than in the Indies <note>The Spanish Americas.</note>, and in France than in Spain—or because the King or the custom of one land sets it at a higher price than the King or custom of the other. This occurred in the time when we studied and lectured in Toulouse in France; the King there greatly raised the prices of his *escudos del sol* <note>French gold coins stamped with a sun.</note> and the ducats of Spain, and they even say <gloss>d</gloss> he has raised them more since then; in all of which almost everyone agrees <gloss>b</gloss>.

<margin>
b For Anthony, Sylvester, Cajetan, Medina, and Soto agree, as above, and Lawrence Rodul., question 1, part 3, chapter *Consulted*, on usury, and John of Anania here, number 52. <note>original: "Conueniunt e- nim Ant. Sylue. Caie. Methi. & Sot. ubi supra & Laurent. Rodul. q. 1. 3. partis. c. Cõ fuluit. de vsur. et Ioan. ab Anan. hic n. 52."</note>
</margin>
<margin>45</margin>
¶ Regarding the fifth respect <gloss>†</gloss> of the demonetization, or the lowering of its value, or its rise, or doubt thereof: we saw in years past the *tarjas* <note>Small silver coins of low value.</note> at ten being worth less for a time than they were before. And in other lands where there are many lords who mint money, they often command that the coins of their neighbors do not circulate in their own lands. Others lower their price, and just as after commanding that it does not circulate it is exchanged for much less than before, so when there is talk of demonetizing or lowering it and there is doubt if it will happen, it is exchanged for somewhat less. And just as after it rises it is worth more, so when there is talk and doubt of it, it begins to be exchanged for somewhat more; because just as the price certainly rises due to an actual increase and certainly falls due to a decrease, so due to the doubt of one or the other, it rises or falls by some uncertain amount <gloss>c</gloss>. And because concerning the exchange of money that is worth more or less, these five respects commonly deal with the exchange of physical transfer (of which we spoke above <gloss>d</gloss>), I refer to what was said there.

<margin>
c Argument from the law *If the cast of a net*, Digest, on the action of purchase, and the present chapter with those things noted. <note>original: "Arg. l. Si ia- ctum retis. ff. de act. emptio. et c. praesen. cũ ei an- notatis."</note>
d Above in the same chapter, number 31.
e Above in the same, number 43.
</margin>
<margin>46</margin>
¶ Regarding the sixth <gloss>†</gloss> respect of the diversity of time, by which the value of money rises or falls: a hundred gold ducats, or a hundred silver ones, or a hundred of base metal, or a hundred absolutely in quantity, are worth more at times and less at others now than they will be worth a year from now. Because (based on what was said above <gloss>e</gloss>) they would be worth more if, for any one of many causes that might exist—namely, for having been taken out of the land to buy provisions, to wage war, or to help friends who were waging it, etc.—there was now a lack of some or all of them, and a year from now an abundance should follow, either because of having sold the provisions and other merchandise of the land, or because the King paid the soldiers and servants well, or for other similar causes. And conversely, they would be worth less now if there were an abundance now and a lack a year from now. Just as a load of wheat is not commonly worth as much in August, when there is an

47 ¶ Money is never properly said to be worth more or less simply for being given earlier or later, or for a long or short period of time, unless some other respect of the eight aforementioned reasons for the rise or fall of money is joined with that time, according to the common opinion of almost everyone <gloss>b</gloss>. ¶ From which it follows: First, that all money changers, merchants, and any others are in error who think it is licit for them to take something more than what they lent on the grounds of having their money "dead" <note>Idle capital that is not being invested or used to generate profit.</note> for a long time without making use of it. Consequently, those money changers err who measure and count the time until the fair, or until the payment deadlines when they are to be paid, in order to charge more or less for the exchange.

48 ¶ It follows † secondly, that he who lends one hundred pieces of gold to another, and later their price rises, may licitly demand them back with the gain of that additional value they carry when he collects them compared to when he lent them. This is because he does not take the extra for the sake of the passage of time alone, but because of the increase in value that the King or custom placed upon that which was owed to him as time passed. This is a conclusion drawn from many parts of Bartolo <gloss>e</gloss> <note>Bartolo de Sassoferrato (1313–1357), a highly influential legal scholar whose opinions often carried the force of law.</note> and is commonly received <gloss>d</gloss>. There is no doubt in this if he intended to keep the money until then, as this chapter <gloss>e</gloss> well proves, along with Juan Calderino, and in other parts Gaspar Calderino <gloss>f</gloss>, Lawrence Rodulph <gloss>g</gloss>, and Silvestro <gloss>h</gloss>. The latter is reprimanded by Soto <gloss>i</gloss> <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a Spanish Dominican priest and scholastic theologian.</note> without Soto citing anyone to support his view, although Francisco Curcio the Elder <gloss>k</gloss> and others whom he mentions held his opinion previously. But Silvestro deserves no reprimand, because he speaks of one who lends ducats <note>A gold coin used for trade throughout Europe.</note> that he intended to keep, and because Soto’s analogy (in our view) is not conclusive. For example, Soto argues that just as one who lends a bushel <note>original: "hanega"</note> of wheat of twelve *celemines* <note>A dry measurement; there are 12 celemines in one fanega/bushel.</note> should not later be returned a full bushel of thirteen, even if it is ordained that a bushel should now contain that many; likewise, he who lends a ducat of eleven reals should not be returned one of twelve, if it is ordained that it be worth that many.

<margin>
k In the said law *When something* in the treatise on money, column 3.
l In the chapter *When canons*, where it is noted in the gloss, and in the chapter *Formerly*, and in the chapter *On the part* regarding census.
a In the law *That which you*, number 7, in the Digest on credited things.
b For Molina says this has been observed for these last three centuries in the *Book on Commerce*, number 696.
c Arguments from the law *If in* in the Digest on the action of purchase, and this chapter.
</margin>

49 ¶ This † analogy is not conclusive in our view because when the bushel of twelve *celemines* is made into thirteen, it changes its form and matter, and ceases to be the same thing it was before. However, just because a ducat rises from eleven reals to twelve by mandate of the prince, it does not change its matter or form, nor does it cease to be the same as it was before. What changes in it is something extrinsic and accidental, and not of its essence, as held by Bartolo <gloss>a</gloss> and commonly received <gloss>b</gloss>. And because a grain of wheat does not cease to be the same wheat as before even if its valuation has increased or decreased; therefore, he who took a borrowed bushel of wheat must return another of wheat just as good in its essence, even though it may be worth more or less in price, which is an extrinsic matter to it. And as for the metaphysical rebuttal that could be made—that the price is of the essence of the ducat insofar as it is a ducat and currency—one can respond with the commonly received Bartolo: that even insofar as it is money, it is founded more on its natural being than on its artificial being, as has been said. ¶ Furthermore, we say that such a lender could take that excess even if he had not intended to keep the coins, if it was agreed that they should be returned in as many and such pieces as those he lent, whether they were worth more, or less, or the same—at least if he had no more certainty that the price would increase than that it would decrease, according to this chapter. This is because that was like a form of venture, luck, and wager, or a transaction regarding doubts of fact and law that might occur, all of which is licit <gloss>c</gloss>.

<margin>
d Law *Of the risk* in the Digest on nautical interest, and number 7, and in laws 1 and 2.
</margin>

50 ¶ Furthermore † we say that according to the common opinion of Bartolo <gloss>d</gloss>, which is commonly received <gloss>e</gloss>, to whom one lends one hundred gold ducats, one hundred must be returned in gold as good as those were, without discounting anything from their price, even if their value grows and even if he did not intend to keep them, nor expressly agreed that they should be returned in such and so many pieces as those he lent, whether they rise or fall. Because to the one who lends a thing, another of the same kind must be returned as good as what was lent (regarding intrinsic goodness <gloss>f</gloss>), and the intrinsic goodness of money is not the price, which the re-

<summary>This page analyzes whether a lender can profit from a legal increase in currency value during the term of a loan, arguing that since a coin's essence (its metal) remains the same despite changes in its legal price (extrinsic value), the lender is entitled to the same number of coins originally lent.</summary>

# Resolutory Commentary on Exchanges

<margin>Bartolus on the law *That which*, number 7, in the Digest on credited things; which Molineus says has been observed for these three centuries in his book on commerce, number 696.</margin>
<margin>a In the said law *When something*</margin>
<margin>b Where cited above, question 90, from number 694.</margin>
<margin>c In the Authentic *In addition*, question 17, on usury.</margin>
<margin>d Number 707.</margin>
the state <note>original: "republica"; referring to the governing authority that sets the face value of coins.</note> assigns to it, but rather the quality and goodness of the material from which it is made, according to the truest and most received opinion of Bartolus <gloss>g</gloss>. Although this common opinion could easily be maintained in all cases, it seems more equitable to us that it should proceed in only three. The first: when the one who lent the coins was intended to keep them until their price rose. The second: when he expressly stated that they should be returned as such, in the same number of pieces as he lent—whether they rose or fell in value—thus exposing himself to the risk of loss as much as the hope of gain. The third: when they rose in value so quickly that even the person who borrowed them had not yet spent them, and thus he spent them and profited from them at the higher price to which they rose. Outside of these three cases, it is sufficient to pay him back in the same pieces, or other similar ones, or of the same metal as those he lent, in the same amount that they were worth at the time of the loan, counting them at the price they have at the time of payment. We are moved to this: partly by what Bartolus and the common opinion hold <gloss>a</gloss>; partly by what Charles Molineus <note>Carolus Molinaeus, a highly influential French jurist.</note> alleges <gloss>b</gloss>; and partly by the great equity written of by Baldus <gloss>c</gloss>, who declares it well <gloss>d</gloss>. And our desired brevity does not permit us any more (nor even as much as we have already said).

### S U M M A R Y

Money, how it rises or falls in its value, by abundance or lack. num. 51.
Merchandise rises and falls by its abundance or lack. num. 51.
Money is merchandise. num. 51. Its rise lowers everything else. That of each metal rises by a lack of it; all of it, by a lack of all. num. 52, 54, & 56. What is its principal purpose? What is the other? num. 55.
Ducats of merchants and of the people seem different. num. 53. But they are not. num. 54 & 56.
Money, the price of other things. Other things can be its price. num. 55. How do they rise? num. 57. Their regulated price <term>tassa</term> <note>The official valuation of a commodity set by the state.</note>. num. 58.
Usury, how it is to give merchant ducats to be paid in others. num. 56.
Selling for more than the thing is worth to others, when it is licit. num. 58.
Money of the fair does not rise by fake exchanges or monopolies <term>manipodios</term> <note>Illegal price-fixing agreements or cartels.</note>. num. 59.
Ducats and reales in Portugal, compared to Castile. num. 60.
Maravedis and coronados of Castile, and reales and ceitis of Portugal, equalized. num. 60.
Ducats and wheat lent where they are worth more, if when paid they are worth less. num. 61.

20. We say † that regarding the seventh respect that makes money rise or fall—which is there being a great lack and need, or an abundance of it—it is worth more where, or when, there is a great lack of it, than where there is abundance. This is held by Calderinus <gloss>e</gloss>, Laurentius, Rodulphus <gloss>f</gloss>, and Sylvester <gloss>g</gloss>, with whom Cajetan <gloss>h</gloss> and Soto <gloss>i</gloss> agree. This opinion is supported first by this: that this is the common concept of almost all people, both good and bad, throughout all of Christendom, and thus it seems the voice of God and of nature <gloss>k</gloss>. Secondly, and very strongly: all merchandise becomes more expensive through great need and a small quantity of it <gloss>l</gloss>; and money, insofar as it is a thing that can be sold, bartered, or exchanged by another contract, is merchandise, according to what was said above <gloss>m</gloss>; therefore, it too will become more expensive with great need and a small quantity of it. Thirdly: that (all else being equal) in the lands where there is a great lack of money, all other salable things, and even the hands and labor of men, are given for less money than where there is an abundance of it. As is seen by experience in France, where there is less money than in Spain, bread, wine, cloth, labor, and the work of men are worth much less. And even in Spain, in the time when there was less money, salable things and the labor of men were given for much less than after the Indies <note>Referring to the Americas, which brought a massive influx of gold and silver into Spain, causing significant inflation.</note>

## On the Final Chapter of Usury

<sig>D 4</sig>

...the discovered Indies covered it with gold and silver. The cause of this is that money is worth more where and when there is a lack of it, than where and when there is an abundance. What some say—that a lack of money lowers everything else—stems from the fact that its excessive rise makes everything else appear lower; just as a short man, standing next to a very tall one, seems 

<margin>a Laurentius, question 1, part 3; Ancharano here at number 52.</margin>

52 shorter than he would next to his equal. Fourthly, † that because of the lack of gold coins, their value may reasonably increase so that more silver coins, or coins of another metal, are given for them ᵃ. We see this now: because of the great lack of gold coins, some give 22, and even 24 or 25 reals for a doubloon, even though the law and price of the kingdom do not value it at more than 22. We have even seen in Portugal people giving eleven and a half, or even twelve ducats in silver for one ten-ducat gold piece. Likewise, because of a lack of silver coins, their value can be raised so that more gold or other metal is given for them than used to be the case. Even for the lack of small copper coins, or other base metals, their value can be raised so that more gold or silver is given than was usual. As we saw in Portugal, they gave us one hundred and six *maravedís* in *cetis* <note>The *ceti* was a small Portuguese copper coin of low value.</note> when there was an abundance of them, for a *teston* <note>A silver coin.</note> which is worth no more than one hundred; later, when a scarcity of them arrived, we gave one *teston* for ninety-four in *cetis*. Thus, it seems that due to the lack of money in general,

53 ¶ Against this † opinion, however, many considerations are made, which at one time made it seem vain to us. The first: that no matter how much lack or surplus of money there is, the ducat is never worth more or less than eleven reals and one *maravedí* here; nor in Rome, Flanders, or Lyon is it worth more or less than what the Pope, the King, or custom has set as its fixed rate <term>tassado</term> <note>Referring to the "tasa," a legally mandated price ceiling intended to ensure stability.</note>; nor will he from whom you buy anything take it for more. <margin>b According to the mind of Innocent; common opinion in the chapter *How much*, on the law of oaths. Thomas, book 2 of *On the Governance of Princes*, chapter 13. Gabriel treats this in 4, distinction 15, question 9. Charles Molinaeus, *On Commerce*, number 195.</margin>

two types of ducats and escudos. One is the merchants' type for their exchanges, which rises and falls according to whether there is much or little money available, and consequently whether many or few people want to give or take on exchange. The other is the ducat or escudo for spending, used by the common people and even by the same merchants for their expenses outside of exchanges; this is always of one price commonly, which seems like a new and vain imagination, because neither Roman, ecclesiastical, nor secular jurisprudence ever imagined it ᵃ. Also, because merchants do not have the power to raise or lower public currency ᵇ; and because it seems a thing of thin air—a trick, simulation, and a masking of usury—to invent ducats or escudos in the air and imagination of a certain value, which no one selling bread, wine, meat, fish, cloth, or anything else would take except by way of exchange to pay you back in another fair or another place. solid reason appears to exist why, due to a general lack of money, ducats or escudos of a greater quantity should be created in imagination alone, only for the purpose of exchanging without having any other use for them, and to deceive. By changing them, a cloud is placed to cover the loan made with usury beneath it. Another point against the said opinion is that money, insofar as it is money, seems to be the price of all other merchandise ᶜ and is not merchandise itself; and its price in every kingdom is fixed. Consequently, it cannot rise more than wheat does when it is fixed by the state.

<margin>d According to the mind of the text, Innocent and others in the chapter *How much* on the law of oaths.</margin>

54 ¶ Notwithstanding all of this, however, and the contrary opinion of Doctor Medina ᵉ <note>Juan de Medina (1490–1546), a prominent Spanish theologian who wrote on economics and contract law.</note> (which at one time seemed better to us), we hold the first opinion, for the new reasons and considerations made for it. And to the first argument, which seems unsolvable, one can respond anew: that although when there is a lack of money in general, the ducat is not worth more reals than when there is a surplus, nor the real worth more *cuartos*, nor the *cuartos* more *maravedís*, nevertheless all the money is worth more. This is because more salable things are found for a certain amount of money then than before, all else being equal. Nor does it hinder us to say that this comes from the drop in value of other things; because that drop is born from the rise...

He addresses counter-arguments that distinguish between "merchants' money" and "spending money," ultimately concluding that scarcity increases the overall purchasing power of currency.</summary>

# Resolutory Commentary on Exchanges

...of money, as is considered in the third argument we have made. Regarding the second argument, which seems unsolvable, one can respond by denying that it is necessary to defend this position by positing imaginary and chimerical <term>ducats<note>The ducat was a gold coin used as a standard for international trade across Europe.</note></term> and <term>escudos<note>The escudo (meaning "shield") was another high-value gold coin used in the Spanish Empire.</note></term>. These would be like Plato’s Ideas <note>A reference to Plato's Theory of Forms, suggesting that these "imaginary" coins exist as perfect concepts but not as physical objects.</note>, existing in their genus and species but not as individuals. The arguments conclude this well, and it is effectively confirmed by the consideration that whoever says such a thing must confess that almost as many imaginary ducats would have to be created as there are places where money is given and taken at the <term>fair<note>The great trade fairs, such as those at Medina del Campo, were the 16th-century equivalent of stock and currency exchanges.</note></term>. For almost every place has its own price: one for Flanders, another for Rome, another for Lyon, another for Lisbon, another for Valencia, another for Zaragoza, etc. This is quite laughable when combined with the consideration that it does not seem sensibly said that the ducat or escudo is worth "this much" at the fair, unless it is worth "this much" for one specific place and "that much" for another. Even those who say this mean that the ducat is given for a specific place in exchange or at a price so that, in that location, a certain amount is given for it. To the third argument † we respond

by denying that money (insofar as it is money) is always considered only as a price. Even as money, it can be exchanged through purchase, barter, or another named or unnamed contract, as was stated above <gloss>a</gloss>. For although the first and principal end and use for which it was invented was to be the price and measure of sellable goods <gloss>b</gloss>, its secondary and less principal use—which is to profit with it by trading money for money—is not to be a price, but to be <term>merchandise<note>Azpilcueta is making a revolutionary economic argument: money is not just a fixed measure of value, but a commodity that can be bought and sold like any other good.</note></term>. This is like how the principal use of footwear is to wear it, but the secondary use is to profit by trading it, buying and selling it. Regarding the matter of the <term>tax<note>The *tasa*, or legal fixed rate set by the crown for the value of currency.</note></term>, it will be answered below <gloss>c</gloss>.

<margin>56</margin>
¶ From this† the following deductions follow. First, that gold money, due to its particular scarcity, can be worth more than it would if there were an abundance of it; and silver money for its own particular scarcity; and base metal money for its own; and all money generally for its general scarcity.

<margin>d Book 7, question 5, article 1, near the end, *On Justice and Law*.</margin>
¶ Second, that there is no need to invent imaginary ducats or escudos for merchants that differ from those used by the common people. Without them, one can clearly agree upon the price to be given for a ducat or escudo from one place to another. In fact, it is better not to invent them, so as not to give occasion to those who unjustly lend and give money so that they may be paid back later at that [inflated] value—a point which the Lord <term>Soto<note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a prominent Spanish Dominican priest and scholastic philosopher who also wrote on economics.</note></term> tacitly understood <gloss>d</gloss>.

<margin>a Below in the same, number 65.</margin>
<margin>b Above in the same, number 26.</margin>
¶ Third, that it is clear <term>usury<note>In this context, usury refers to any illicit profit made from a money exchange that lacks a just cause or exceeds the legal interest rates.</note></term> in the exchange of many who (as they say) give ducats or escudos to various people from one fair to the next, to be paid at the price they are worth when given, or what they will be worth in the marketplace for merchants when they are to be paid. This is because no such ducats or escudos exist in the world. Furthermore, even if they did exist, they would have as many different values as there are cities for which they are exchanged. For some, they are exchanged at par, as is often done from Medina to Lisbon; for other parts at ten or twenty <term>maravedís<note>A small Spanish unit of account; 375 maravedís usually equaled one gold ducat.</note></term>; for others at 30, 40, 50, etc. And they sometimes give them at the rate they are exchanged for the city where they are most expensive. Additionally, the reason that justifies the exchange of a certain amount of money to be given in a distant city does not justify the exchange of the same amount to be given in the same city, for reasons that will be stated below <gloss>a</gloss>. Although it must be admitted that someone who finds someone else to take his money through a true exchange, but declines that profit to give it to his neighbor or another nearby person who is in great need, could in this way earn from it what he ceases to earn from the other, for the reasons mentioned above <gloss>b</gloss>.

<margin>57</margin>
¶ Fourth,† that the value of money can rise or fall not only insofar as it is a piece of metal, but even insofar as it is money and a price for other things. Most of the aforementioned eight factors why money rises or falls are factors that affect money insofar as it is money and the price of sellable goods. They conclude that, as money and price, it is worth more in one land than in another, and even in the same land, more at one time than at another.

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that money's value fluctuates based on local supply and demand (scarcity), functioning as both a measure of value and a tradable commodity. He rejects the use of "imaginary" currencies in banking, warning that such abstractions can lead to usury and economic injustice.</summary>

### ->On the final chapter of Usury.<-

...is fixed, as wheat usually is, it does not rise due to any shortage of it. Some of the aforementioned authors <insert>c</insert> <margin>c From among these is Caietanus in the treatise *On Exchange*, chapter 6.</margin> respond that although it is fixed insofar as it is a price, it is not fixed insofar as it is a commodity. But this is not satisfactory: because from what has been said above, it is clear that even insofar as it is money and a price, it rises and falls. <margin>d On the word *Usura*, question 4, article 3.</margin> Sylvester <insert>d</insert> <note>Sylvester Prierias (1456–1523) was an influential Italian theologian whose *Summa Sylvestrina* was a standard reference for moral law.</note> indicates that it is fixed insofar as it is the price for other saleable things, but not insofar as it is the price <margin>e Soto, book 7, question 5, article 2, *On Justice and Law*.</margin> of the same money. However, he does not provide a reason for this distinction. Others <insert>e</insert> <note>Specifically, Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a prominent Spanish Dominican theologian of the School of Salamanca.</note> feel that money is never "sold," and for this reason, some would say that in its exchange, no higher price is given. But <margin>f Bartolus on the law *Paulinus 1*, in the *Digest* on payments, notes 7 and 10; and Panormitanus on the chapter *Quanto* on oaths, note 13.</margin> this, on one hand, goes against the common opinion <insert>f</insert> <note>Referring to Bartolus de Saxoferrato and Panormitanus, two of the most cited medieval legal scholars.</note> which speaks of the purchase and sale of money; and on the other hand, it does not help them at all. For since they confess that it is swapped, and that it cannot be swapped except for what it is worth—and that its value grows due to its great scarcity, and that more must be given for it when it is worth more <margin>g All of the things mentioned above are admitted by Soto in the said article 2.</margin>—they must by necessity confess that its value grows notwithstanding its fixed rate. Thus, they face the same need to resolve the argument based on that fixed rate as do those who say it is "bought." Therefore, † we respond anew, conceding <margin>Thomas, *Summa Theologica* 2.2, question 78, article 1, reply 4; Cajetan in counsel 11 on usury.</margin> that money is fixed for one purpose and not for another. It is fixed for the purpose of compelling a person who sells something, or to whom something is owed, to accept it at that price, and they cannot be compelled to take it for more. But it is not fixed so that the person who has it cannot take less for it if they wish, nor so that they cannot take more if some <margin>58</margin> particular convenience results for them. However, this solution cannot satisfy the consciences of those who exchange it at a higher rate because of its scarcity without some convenience resulting to them from holding it, even if the person for whom it is exchanged benefits from obtaining it. Because the seller <margin>a *Summa* 2.2, question 77, article 1.</margin> cannot sell a thing for more because of the particular profit that comes to the <margin>b In the *Sentences* 4, distinction 15, question 2.</margin> buyer, although they can do so because of the loss they themselves incur in selling it, according to the accepted views of St. Thomas <insert>a</insert> and Scotus <insert>b</insert> <note>Duns Scotus (1266–1308), a major medieval philosopher and theologian.</note>. And <margin>c On the law *Paulus*, in the *Digest* on payments.</margin> we see every day that not only traders—to whom it rarely fails that some convenience results from holding their money when there is a great shortage of it, if only <margin>d In the chapter *Quanto*, note 13, on oaths.</margin> to buy things more cheaply—but even those who do not trade now exchange <term>doubloons<note>The *doblón* was a high-value Spanish gold coin.</note></term> at 24 and 25 <term>reals<note>The silver *real* was the basic unit of Spanish currency.</note></term>, even though they are fixed at 22, because of the great shortage <margin>e From the argument in the chapter *Ne quis*, question 22, question 2, etc.</margin> of them. And although it could be said that because of the intrinsic value of their gold, which is much higher than that of <term>crowns<note>The *corona* or crown was a different gold coin, usually of slightly lower purity or weight than the doubloon.</note></term>, they are worth that much more in relation to crowns, we could not say this of all other currencies. Yet Bartolus <insert>c</insert> and Panormitanus <insert>d</insert>, whom nobody contradicts, indicate that all currencies rise and fall every day. Therefore, it seems safer to respond that the fixed rate placed on money is set so that it is worth that much and no more while things remain in that state; but not so that if things change so much that there is a great shortage and need for that fixed money, it cannot be worth more <insert>e</insert>, as it would seem to wise and good men—at least for the purpose of exchanging it for other money, as Sylvester says <insert>f</insert>. <margin>f Where cited above.</margin>

<margin>59</margin> ¶ The sixth point is that it is no wonder that money (even as money) is worth more in <margin>g Cajetan in the treatise *On Exchange*, chapter 7, and Soto, book 7, question 5, article 3.</margin> one <term>fair<note>International trade fairs were the primary centers for currency exchange and wholesale commerce.</note></term> than in another, and more in one part of the same fair than in another. Because in one part of it, since there are few who want to take it in a <term>genuine exchange<note>A "genuine" exchange refers to a legitimate transfer of funds for trade, as opposed to a "fictitious" exchange used to hide a high-interest loan.</note></term> and many who want to give it, it may be worth less; and in the other part, the reverse: because there are many who want to take it in a genuine exchange and few who want to give it, it may be worth <margin>h Chapter *Ex tenore* on rescripts; chapter *Adversus* on church immunity.</margin> more. Thus, due to great scarcity and need, its price increases <insert>g</insert>. We said "genuine exchange" because, in our view, the price of money should not be raised because there is a multitude of those who want to take it through fictitious and illicit exchanges; because deceit <margin>i Extensively in Cajetan, *Summa* 2.2, question 77, article 1.</margin> and fraud should not benefit the one who commits them <insert>h</insert>. And because no commodity increases in price because there are many who want to steal it or illicitly usurp it, though it does if there are many who want to justly buy or swap it <insert>i</insert>. And because (as Lord Soto <insert>k</insert> <margin>k Where cited above.</margin> pointed out very well) money should not be considered more expensive at a fair because of a <margin>a From the argument of law 1, Code on monopolies.</margin> shortage of it, or a shortage of people willing to give it, when that shortage arises from a <term>monopoly<note>The original Spanish uses *monipodio*, a term for a cartel or conspiracy among merchants to fix prices or restrict supply.</note></term> of those who are to give it, and of the exchange-merchants, who openly or covertly agree not <margin>b Because fraud and deceit should profit no one; chapter *Ex tenore* on rescripts.</margin> to give it until it becomes more expensive <insert>a</insert>. Or when some of them have taken almost all of it more cheaply at the beginning of the fair for various parties, and later, since almost all of it is in their power, they do not want to give it except as they fancy. In which time and case, although those who were not at fault could give it according to its scarcity with a good conscience, those who were at fault could not <insert>b</insert>. This is a more daily occurrence than is necessary.

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that the value of money fluctuates according to market demand and scarcity, particularly at trade fairs, regardless of legally fixed rates. He warns that while natural scarcity can justify a higher exchange rate, price-fixing cartels and fraudulent exchanges are morally illicit.</summary>

### Resolutory Commentary on Exchanges.

<margin>

¶ The seventh point: it would be less of a wonder for the <term>ducat<note>The *ducado* (ducat) was a high-value gold coin used throughout Europe.</note></term> to be worth more in Portugal than in Castile, although there is doubt as to whether it actually is. Because some say it is not, for two reasons. First, because anyone in Portugal who owes 400 <term>reales<note>The *real* (plural *reales*) was a silver coin; its value relative to gold changed over time.</note></term> can pay there or here with one ducat worth eleven reales; yet someone who owes 400 <term>maravedís<note>The *maravedí* was the smallest unit of account in Castile, used to measure the value of larger silver and gold coins.</note></term> here cannot pay with one ducat, either here or there. This is a sign that the maravedís here are worth more than the reales there, but that the ducat is worth the same here as there, and there as here. The second reason is that in the moderate provision for exchanges issued by His Majesty <note>Referring to King Philip II's regulations on financial markets.</note> (the sum of which we referred to above <insert>c</insert>), it indicates that 370 maravedís here are worth 400 reales there. However, the opposite seems truer to us: namely, that the ducat from here and there is worth more there than here, and also the real from here is worth more there than here. For the ducat there is worth 400 Portuguese reales, and the real is worth 36 [units of account]; whereas here, the ducat is worth only 375 maravedís, and the real 34. That the reales there and the maravedís here are equal is gathered from the fact that just as one real is worth six <term>cetis<note>The *céti* was a small Portuguese copper coin.</note></term> in Portugal, so too the maravedí (currently in use) is worth six <term>cornados<note>The *cornado* was a small Castilian coin of little value.</note></term>, which appear to be equal to the *cetis*. This is quite effectively proven by Lord Archbishop Don Diego de Leyva y Covarrubias <insert>d</insert> <note>Diego de Covarrubias y Leyva (1512–1577) was a renowned Spanish jurist and bishop whose work on money and prices was foundational.</note>. Even today, in the Kingdom of Galicia (where they have *cetis* as in Portugal), six of them are worth one maravedí, just as in Portugal six are worth one real. Furthermore, the argument made by the opposing party <insert>e</insert> does not stand. For we deny that he who owes 400 reales in Portugal makes a just payment here with one ducat, unless the creditor is satisfied with it; nor can you pay someone here with eleven reales if you owe them eleven reales there. We also deny that he who owes 400 maravedís here cannot pay there with 400 reales. Finally, one can respond to the aforementioned moderate provision that those words were placed in it incidentally <insert>f</insert>; and if you replicate that its determination is founded upon it <insert>g</insert>, we will say that they concern the facts of another region, and the contrary can be proven <insert>h</insert>. We believe that although that provision might be accepted in these kingdoms for their benefit, it will hardly be accepted in foreign ones—even if they belong to His Majesty—to their own detriment.

<margin>
61
a Argument from the good in Law 3, section *Now concerning the office*, in the Digest on that which is in a certain place, and better in Law 4 of the same title.
b Argument from the aforementioned laws.
c Chapter 1, Case 14, Question 3, where we spoke extensively at number 7.
d Book 6, Question 5, Article 2, *On Justice and Law*.
</margin>

¶ The eighth point, which is of great importance: he who lends one hundred ducats in Portugal can take for them in Medina <note>Medina del Campo was the site of the most important international trade fairs in Spain.</note> more than one hundred, solely because they are worth more there than here <insert>a</insert>. ¶ The ninth point: he who lends one hundred ducats in Medina should not receive one hundred in Lisbon; because they are worth more there than here <insert>b</insert>, and he who lends cannot take back more than what was lent <insert>c</insert>. ¶ The tenth point: what has been said of Medina and Lisbon in these last two inferences should also be said of any other two cities where the same currency is worth more in one than the other. Consequently, to the one who lends one hundred ducats in Flanders, Rome, or Lyon (where ducats are worth more than in Castile), more than one hundred must be paid in Castile; and conversely, to the one who lends one hundred in Castile, one hundred must not be paid in Rome, as Lord Doctor Soto <insert>d</insert> <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a Dominican theologian at the University of Salamanca who wrote extensively on the morality of economics.</note> singularly presupposes. Because it would be usury to lend you a load of wheat in Salamanca (where it is worth two ducats) so that you pay me for it in Galicia, where it is worth four. Likewise, it would be usury to lend you a ducat here, which is worth 375 maravedís, so that you pay me in another place where it is worth 400. And just as (though it is not usury, it is an injustice) that for a load of wheat I lent you in Galicia, where it was worth four ducats, you should pay me with another in this Salamanca, where it is worth no more than two; so it is an injustice that for one hundred ducats you lent me in Rome or Lisbon, where they are worth 400, I should give you only one hundred in Medina, where they are worth no more than 375.

<margin>
e Law 3, section *Now concerning the office*, in the Digest on that which is in a certain place.
</margin>

¶ The eleventh point: just as someone who lends a certain quantity of wheat, wine, or oil where it is worth more, must receive a greater quantity if he is paid where it is worth less—in proportion to how much more it is worth where he lends than where he is paid <insert>e</insert>; and just as someone who lends where it is worth less must receive a smaller quantity if he is paid where it is worth more; so too, someone who lends ducats where they are worth more must receive more if paid where they are worth less, according to the greater value; and conversely, someone who lends ducats where they are worth less must receive less if paid where they are worth more, according to that higher value.

<margin>
f Book 7, Question 5, Article 1, *On Justice and Law*.
</margin>

¶ The twelfth point: for this reason, it will seem to some that there is no doubt in that conclusion of Lord Doctor Soto <insert>f</insert>: that he who gives a ducat in exchange in Spain, where it is worth only eleven reales, so that there is returned to him in Rome another of twelve or thirteen *carniles* <note>The *carnile* (or *carlino*) was a silver coin used in parts of Italy.</note> (which are equal to our reales or worth more than eleven), commits usury. This is because he seeks to take more than he gives, and...

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that the moral value of a loan depends on the purchasing power of the currency in different locations (Portugal, Castile, Rome), rather than just the nominal amount. He uses analogies with commodities like wheat to illustrate that failing to account for these regional value differences results in either usury or injustice.</summary>

...which he gives, and gaining 그 excess. However, neither this conclusion nor those that follow from it are inferred from our deductions, nor (in our opinion) are they firm. They are not inferred because the aforementioned three deductions speak of the person who lends money and of the loan which in Latin is called *Mutuum* <term>mutuum<note>A type of loan in Roman and Canon law where the borrower is expected to return the exact equivalent in kind and quality, traditionally required to be interest-free.</note></term>, the nature of which is to be gratuitous. And by virtue of it, nothing more should be taken than what was lent, as we said in another Commentary<gloss>a</gloss>. Conversely, his conclusion speaks of the one who gives in exchange, the nature of which is not to be gratuitous; and for this reason, it is not inferred from the others, as they speak of different matters<gloss>b</gloss>. That it is not firm is evident: because every day the contrary is practiced from Medina to Lisbon and Flanders, and from there back to Medina. This practice is licit both by way of a true sale and by way of barter and other innominate contracts, as we prove below<gloss>c</gloss>.

...another thing; but Cajetan <gloss>b</gloss> feels the same as we do, and before all of them Calderino <gloss>c</gloss> and Lorenzo Rodolpho <gloss>d</gloss>, and it seems to us to be legally sound. Firstly, because any absent merchandise that one buys for the place where it is located, considered absolutely, requires by its nature costs and labor estimable in money <gloss>e</gloss> to collect it and bring it. Nor is it an obstacle to say that the merchant has relatives, friends, or <term>factors<note>Commercial agents or representatives who conducted business on behalf of a merchant in a distant city.</note></term> who collect it for him in that absent place without cost or labor to him: because all of that is paid for in one way or another, and for all of it he remains obligated to do the same for them, at least by the obligation they call <term>antidotal<note>From the Greek 'antidosis,' referring to a reciprocal exchange of services or a 'gift for a gift'—essentially, a moral debt of gratitude where one expects to return a favor.</note></term> <gloss>f</gloss>. And because a work does not cease to have a value of its own just because someone happens to do it for free <gloss>g</gloss>. And because one cannot by justice take away anything from what is promised to someone for going from here to Rome by saying that on the road he found someone to cover his costs and even give him money to accompany him. Secondly, because no one denies that a mule in Seville is worth less to someone who is here than another present mule of the same quality and price, even if by some accidental case or by his own industry he could bring it here without cost, or it might be worth more there than here. And it is certain that if no industry, custom, or provision of merchants existed in this matter, the money of Flanders would be worth much less here than it is; and it is not just that his industry should harm anyone <gloss>h</gloss>.

<margin>
63
i Code on restored things, title on the causes for which money changers are accustomed to increase profit, page 150.
</margin>
Furthermore, it is no obstacle that Dr. Medina <gloss>i</gloss> said that absence, joined with the dangers that occur and the expenses made in collecting absent money, are sufficient cause for it to not be worth as much as present money: for from his statement ours follows. Since expenses and labors are, by their nature, as annexed to absence as we say and prove—even if some are accidentally detached from it.

<margin>
k book 7, question 6, article 2, on justice and law.
</margin>
Furthermore, it is also no obstacle that the Lord Dr. Soto <gloss>k</gloss> holds that neither absence by itself (as Medina says) makes it worth less, nor do dangers and expenses, since such things do not exist today among merchants: for from his statement ours follows. For *a contrario sensu* <note>original: "by the contrary sense"</note> he confesses that if they did exist, it would be worth more; and in the first fundamental point we proved that they do exist, considering the nature of the business, and even considering the cost of the factors and correspondents that the merchants have there where they take the money. Furthermore, his argument does not obstruct us: that if this were true, Flanders money would be worth less in Medina than the money of Medina itself—which is false, because as he says, a Flanders ducat is worth more in Medina (for which more than 400 maravedís are given) than a ducat of Medina itself, which is had for 375.

We say then that this is no obstacle, because we deny his inference: for we do not mean to say that all absent money is always worth less than present money, but that it is worth less *all else being equal*—that is, if what is present is worth as much where it is as the absent money is worth where it is, and not otherwise. Just as a load of wheat in Toro is worth less to someone who is here than another load present here, if all else is equal: that is, if both are of the same quality, and that one is worth as much there as this one is here. But not if the wheat from Toro were worth four ducats there, and only two here, and he could have it brought safely for one; rather, it would be worth more, though somewhat less than the four ducats for being absent. Likewise, if the Flanders ducat were not worth more in Flanders than the Medina ducat in Medina, one from Flanders would be worth less in Medina than one of its own; but it is worth so much more in Flanders than in Medina that even though its price is somewhat diminished by the absence, it is not so much that it does not still remain of greater value than that of Medina.

a In counsel 11, on usury.
</margin>
¶ From this follows: First, that Calderino <gloss>a</gloss> advised well that the purchase made by one who bought from another in Genoa for one hundred ducats, one hundred and six of Alexandria in Egypt, was good; because the hundred present in Genoa were worth more to the one who was there than the hundred absent that were in Alexandria, for the reasons stated.

¶ It follows secondly, that if they were not (as they say they are) forbidden, the exchanges from one part of the kingdom to another part of the same, one could buy in Burgos, Medina, or here, from a Sevillian with one hundred ducats, more than one hundred that were to be given in Seville: because the ducat is worth as much here as there, and no more; and the absence lowers the price of the money that is there.

¶ It follows thirdly, that the greater the absence is, and the more danger and cost in its collection and its necessary transport, the more the absence lowers the price of the money; and consequently, money that is in Medina will cost more in Salamanca than that which is in Burgos; and more...

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that "absent" money is inherently less valuable than "present" money due to the risks and costs of transfer, justifying exchange fees. He counters the opinions of other scholars like Soto and Medina by emphasizing the "antidotal" or reciprocal costs of business agents.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the geographic location of money affects its value. Azpilcueta argues that "absent" money (money located in another city) is naturally worth less than "present" money because of the difficulty and risk of collecting it. This logic is used to explain the varying exchange rates between major commercial hubs like Medina del Campo, Lisbon, and Flanders.</meta>

# Resolutory Commentary on Usury

### On the final Chapter regarding usury.

Burgos; and more so that which is in Burgos than that in Seville; and more so that in Seville than that in Alexandria, Rome, Flanders, or Lyon. This is because the further away it is, the more difficult its collection becomes, and the greater the costs inherent to its nature, the further away it is located; and the easier and lower the costs, the closer it is. We said "by its nature" because it may happen accidentally that what is further away can be collected more easily; but one must attend more to the nature <gloss>a</gloss> of the business than to its accidents.

<margin>a Chapter *De occidentibus* <note>original Latin: "concerning accidents"</note> distinction 23, question 5. Chapter *Saepe*, distinction 50.</margin>

65 ¶ It follows fourthly, that the absence of money located in Flanders causes it to be worth less in Medina to the person who is there and buys it, than it would be worth in Flanders to someone who is there and buys it. However, it is commonly not worth so much less that it does not still value more in Medina than the Medina <term>ducat<note>A gold coin used widely in Europe; here, the author compares the value of the same denomination of currency in two different markets.</note></term>; for although absence (all else being equal) makes the absent thing worth less than the present, it is not by so much as the ducat is worth more there in Flanders than here in Medina.

¶ It follows fifthly, that the reason why Flanders ducats commonly cost more in Medina than those of Medina itself, is that ducats are worth much more there than here; and although absence takes away something from their price, it does not take away so much that they do not always remain much more expensive.

¶ It follows sixthly, that the reason why exchange is cheaper from here to Flanders than from Flanders back here is that one hundred Medina ducats cost less in Flanders than one hundred Flanders ducats cost in Medina. And the reason for this reasoning is that the price of one hundred Medina ducats offered for sale in Flanders is diminished for two reasons: The first is for being absent, and the other because the ducat is worth less in Medina than in Flanders. Conversely, the price of Flanders ducats offered for sale in Medina decreases for only one reason—namely, absence. While absence makes them worth somewhat less, it is not as much as they are worth more there than here.

66 ¶ It follows seventhly, the reason why from Medina to Lisbon exchange is often done **at par**; that is, as many ducats for just as many: one hundred in Medina for another hundred given in Lisbon, no more and no less. The reason is that the price of Lisbon money offered for sale in Medina is worth less than it is in Lisbon because it is absent and outside the Kingdom. And the reason why it is never, or rarely, exchanged for Flanders **at par** (even though it is absent and outside the kingdom) is that it is worth more in Flanders than in Lisbon. Although absence and being outside the kingdom are enough to equalize Lisbon money with that of Medina within Medina, neither absence nor being outside the Kingdom is enough to equalize the price of Medina money with that of Flanders.

<margin>b In the treatise on exchange, chapter 7.</margin>
<margin>c Above at the same number, 31.</margin>

67 ¶ It follows eighthly, that the aforementioned does not apply in an exchange made in such a way that the money of one is to be delivered to the other in the same place, and the other's to the first likewise—whether the place designated for this is where the agreement is made, or another place far or near it. Thus, it only applies when it is agreed that the money of one is given in one place to the other, and in another place the other's to the first, as Cajetan <gloss>b</gloss> rightly pointed out. Although this was already sufficiently indicated on its own: because the reasoning of costs, labor, and risks—on which the decrease in value of absent money is founded—does not apply when the delivery of both occurs in the same place, except (at most) for the purpose of paying as much for it as is paid for <term>petty exchange<note>Small-scale manual currency exchange, as opposed to large mercantile bills of exchange.</note></term>, of which we spoke above <gloss>c</gloss>.

68 ¶ 22nd and lastly, we say that it is not without reason that learned men doubt whether the trade currently practiced from Medina to Lisbon, Flanders, Lyon, and other similar cities is lawful; and from those to Seville, Medina, and others, by which many live (whom I know) without any other business. It is of this manner (which I have learned at my own expense): a person who has money gives it at the end of the **May fair** in Medina del Campo (which ends at the end of July) for Lisbon, to be paid within a month; sometimes **at par** (that is, as many ducats for just as many) and sometimes at one percent. Then in Lisbon, they give it back for the **October fair** in Medina at five, seven, or more percent. And at the end of that fair (which is at the end of December), they give it back for Lisbon and the twentieth of January, sometimes **at par**, sometimes at one or more percent. Then at the end of January, they give it back for the **fair of Villalón** or **Medina de Rioseco** at five or seven percent; and almost the same is done in the other fairs of other cities and kingdoms for the...

<summary>This page analyzes the economic factors that determine exchange rates between major European trade centers, specifically citing distance and local currency value as justifications for price differences. Azpilcueta also begins to describe the cyclical "fair-to-fair" exchange system used by merchants, questioning its moral and legal standing.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of currency exchange between major European financial centers. The previous page established that the "absence" of money—the physical distance from the owner—reduces its present value. Here, Azpilcueta examines the specific mechanics of exchange between Medina del Campo, Flanders, Lisbon, and Rome, weighing traditional theological prohibitions against practical economic necessity.</meta>

# Resolutory Commentary on Exchanges

<margin>c Book 7, question 5, article 2 of *On Justice and Right* <note>original Latin: "de iusti. & iure"</note></margin>
...for those of these, or of others. Others give (according to Lord Doctor Soto <margin>c</margin>) <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a Spanish Dominican theologian whose work *De Iustitia et Iure* was a standard reference for economic morality.</note> their money in Medina, for Flanders, giving in the former 410 <term>maravedis<note>A Spanish copper or silver coin used as a unit of account.</note></term> per <term>ducat<note>A high-value gold coin used throughout Europe for international trade.</note></term>, which they are to receive there at 360; and there they give it back for Medina, giving one ducat of 300 maravedis there to receive one of 375 here.

<margin>d Second part of the Second Part, question 77, article 1, accepted by all. e On the 4th [Book of Sentences], distinction 15, question 2. f Above in the same, numbers 14, 24, and 41.</margin>
<margin>69</margin>
¶ Against † this trade, the following points are made: First, it seems that it cannot be defended by way of the purchase and sale of money; because every purchase of a thing of greater price for a lesser one is licit, according to St. Thomas <margin>d</margin> and Scotus <margin>e</margin>, who are accepted by all, as was said above <margin>f</margin>. In this trade, one hundred ducats of Medina are bought in Lisbon for less than ninety-five, and in Flanders for less than ninety. Secondly, it seems that one must necessarily confess that either the purchase you make in Medina for Flanders or Lisbon, or in Lisbon and Flanders for Medina, is of things of greater price for a lesser price. For if the just price of one hundred ducats that you give me in Medina is the one hundred or one hundred and one that I am to give you in Lisbon within one month, then the price of one hundred and seven that I must give you for the October <term>fair<note>International trade fairs, like those in Medina del Campo, were the primary venues for settling debts and exchanging currency in the 16th century.</note></term> for only one hundred that you give me in Lisbon would be unjust. For it seems that if my one hundred and one in Lisbon were worth only your one hundred in Medina, then your one hundred in Lisbon cannot now be worth one hundred and seven of mine in Medina. And if you have justly sold me in Lisbon in past years 400 maravedis of Rome for 375, you have unjustly bought from me 400 of Lisbon for 400 that you give me in Rome; and if for the 410 that I give you in Medina, you justly sell me 360 that you have in Flanders, you unjustly sell me in Flanders 300 that you have there for 375 that I am to give you here.

<margin>a Book 7, question 5, article 2 of *On Justice and Right* <note>original Latin: "de iust. et iure"</note></margin>
<margin>70</margin>
¶ Thirdly, † it follows that this cannot be saved by the reasoning with which Lord Doctor Soto <margin>a</margin> wishes to save it: namely, by way of pure exchange and barter, considering that a smaller sum of money in a land where there is a great shortage of it is worth more than a larger sum in a land where there is greater abundance. I say, then, that it cannot be saved by this way. Firstly, because the said Doctor Soto expressly affirms that one cannot licitly exchange except that which is worth a certain amount in one land for that which is worth the same amount in the other, and no more; and that the money given in Spain must be worth as much and no more at the time it is given as that which is to be given for it in Flanders at that same time—whether it is to be given in eight days, one month, four months, or a year. Secondly, because he himself says that no reason can be given why, by the sole way of exchange and barter, you licitly take 410 maravedis in Spain for 360 that you are to give me in Flanders, and then there you give me 300 for 375 that I must give you here. For the exchange or barter from here to there, or from there to here, is unequal.

<margin>b Second part of the Second Part, question 78, article 1, reply to objection 7. c On the 4th [Book of Sentences], distinction 15, question 2, article 1, and it was said above in the same, numbers 14 and 24, and in the commentary on Chapter 4, question 14, question 3, number 26, and it is proved in the chapter *Ad nostram* regarding purchases, and in the chapter *In civitate* above in the same.</margin>
<margin>71</margin>
¶ Fourthly, † it is a most established conclusion of St. Thomas <margin>b</margin>, Scotus <margin>c</margin>, and everyone, that any trade in which more is taken by reason of a longer wait and delay is usurious. And it seems that in this trade, more is taken by reason of time and waiting. For whoever gives his ducats in Medina for Lisbon for one month gives them at par, or at one percent; and if he gives them for two months, he takes more; and if for three, more still. And if he gives them in Lisbon for Medina at a time when there are four months until the fair, he takes more than if there were only three; and if there are three, more than if there were only two; and if two, more than if there were only one. And to the person who gives money in Spain so that it may be given to him in Rome, they give it to him cheaper for three months from now than for right now. For these reasons, it seemed to us at one time that this trade could not be sustained.

<margin>a In Council 11 regarding usury. b Above in the same, number 62 and following. c Above in the same, number 51.</margin>
<margin>72</margin>
¶ Notwithstanding † all these points, however, we believe that it is licit. First because, as Calderinus <margin>a</margin> <note>Johannes Calderinus (d. 1365), an influential canon lawyer.</note> says, it seems an absurd thing to condemn so many good merchants who do this, and thereby harm the whole world. Secondly, without this trade, commerce with foreign kingdoms would perish, and one’s own kingdoms would be impoverished. Thirdly—which is the entire foundation of this trade—absent money is not worth as much as present money, as was proved above <margin>b</margin>; nor is it worth as much when there is an abundance and plenty of it as when there is a shortage and necessity, as was also proved above <margin>c</margin>. For this reason, someone who has money in Medina can justly buy, or seek to obtain by barter and exchange, other money that is in Flanders for less than what it is worth there; and afterward collect it there, and buy or seek to obtain by barter and other unnamed contracts with it there other money that is in Medina, for less than what it is worth.

<summary>Azpilcueta examines the theological objections to international currency exchange, specifically the argument that fluctuations in rates over time constitute usury. He ultimately defends the practice as licit, arguing that the social and economic necessity of trade—and the differing value of money based on location and scarcity—justifies these transactions.</summary>
<keywords>Medina del Campo, Flanders, Lisbon, Rome, ducats, maravedis, exchange, barter, usury, St. Thomas Aquinas, Duns Scotus, Domingo de Soto, Johannes Calderinus, merchants</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical rebuttal of arguments against the "absence" of money as a factor in its valuation. Azpilcueta is defending the practice of currency exchange between major fairs, specifically addressing the theories of Domingo de Soto regarding the equality of present and absent funds.</meta>

# On the Final Chapter regarding Usury

...they are worth there, and in this manner increase their money. Likewise, one who has money or credit in Flanders <note>At this time, Flanders (the Low Countries) was a primary hub for international banking and textile trade under the Spanish Crown.</note> can buy or procure through exchange to have money in Medina <note>Medina del Campo was the site of the most important financial fairs in 16th-century Spain.</note> outside the fair, or at the beginning of it (if there is an abundance of them) cheaper, and later buy or exchange them more expensively during the fair, or at the end of it, if there is a greater shortage: provided that he gives what is just for the absent money in present money, and for the present in absent money.

<margin>73</margin>
<margin>a Above in the same, number 60 and following.</margin>
<margin>b Above in the same, number 63.</margin>
¶ The fourth † point made here is that by this third foundation, the first two arguments of the opposing party are resolved. For it follows from this that, while we confess there is no just purchase without maintaining equality between the price and the merchandise, we can and must deny that (all else being equal) one hundred present units are not worth more than one hundred absent. We deny that one hundred absent units cannot be bought for less than one hundred present ones, provided each is worth as much in its own place as the other is in its own. We also deny the premise on which those arguments rest: namely, that if the just price of one hundred ducats absent from Seville in Medina is ninety-nine present ducats, then one hundred ducats of Seville present in Seville would also be the just price of ninety-nine ducats absent from Medina. On the contrary, ninety-nine present ducats of Seville would be the just price in Seville for one hundred absent from Medina. We said "all else being equal," meaning each is worth as much in its own place as the other is in its own—as those of Seville are worth in Seville, and those of Medina in Medina. For if one is worth more where it is than the other is where it is, what happens every day can occur: that the absent ones are worth more than the present ones. This has commonly been the case in our days with funds absent from Flanders in Medina, which were worth more than those present in Medina; and often, funds absent from Lisbon in Medina were worth as much as those present in Medina. And for this reason, we deny that if the price of one hundred ducats absent from Lisbon is one hundred present in Medina, then one hundred present in Lisbon would be the just price of one hundred absent from Medina. Because the <term>ducats<note>A high-purity gold coin that served as the standard for international trade in Europe.</note></term> of Lisbon are worth more in Lisbon than those of Medina in Medina, as was said above <margin>a</margin>. Therefore, the present Lisbon ducat is worth more in Lisbon than the absent Medina ducat for two reasons: for being present, and for having a higher inherent value there. Thus, it may well be that one hundred present Lisbon ducats are worth much more than one hundred absent Medina ducats; even though the mere absence of the Lisbon funds does not make them worth less in Medina than Medina's own funds, due to the counterbalance of the higher value the ducats have in Lisbon, as declared above <margin>b</margin>.

<margin>74</margin>
<margin>c Above in the same, number 14, where the first is refuted; and the other two are refuted from number 62.</margin>
¶ The fifth † point that justifies this trade is that, by the said third foundation, the third argument of the opposing party is also resolved. For it follows that this trade can also be defended by way of <term>barter<note>original Spanish: "trueco," referring to the direct exchange of currencies.</note></term> and by way of another <term>innominate contract<note>In Roman and Canon law, a contract that does not have a specific name but is binding because one party has performed their side (e.g., "I give so that you may give").</note></term>, such as "I give that you may give," etc. It follows that a smaller amount of present money is a just exchange, swap, and equivalent for a larger amount of present money—deducing it all, neither more nor less, as has been deduced regarding the purchase. However, we do confess that by the method of defense used by Lord Doctor Soto, this trade—conducted from one place to another and then back again as is done every day—cannot be defended, as argued in the fourth argument against his method of defense. And this is because he presupposed three things from which his total destruction follows. The first: that the barter or exchange of money cannot be done justly except with money that already really belongs to the two parties between whom it is exchanged. The second: that absent money is not worth less than present money. The third: that from these it follows that present money cannot be swapped or exchanged for absent money, unless one gives enough present money to be worth as much where it is as the absent money is worth where it is. From these three things, a fourth necessarily follows: namely, that if one hundred ducats are a just barter and exchange in Medina for ninety in Flanders, neither more nor less, then ninety in Flanders would also be the just price for one hundred in Medina. And from this follows a fifth: that by such a trade, no one could increase their money, nor even consider it, except with great danger, cost, and care—for no one wants them without <margin>c</margin> some profit. Consequently, this whole trade would perish.

And those who until now have held it would be obliged to <term>restitute<note>To return profits considered to be "filthy lucre" or usurious under Church law.</note></term> what they gained by it. But, because we concluded above that none of the said three things are proved by law—rather, their opposite is in accordance with it—we say,

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that the geographical location of money ("absence" vs. "presence") and its local purchasing power are valid factors in determining its value. He refutes Domingo de Soto's strict view, arguing that if exchange were not profitable, international trade and banking would collapse.</summary>

...we say that the said transaction, neither more nor less, can be justified by way of exchange, barter, or another innominate contract <note>An "innominate contract" is a legal term from Roman law for an agreement that does not fall under standard categories like "sale" or "lease" but is still legally binding.</note>, just as it was stated above that it could be justified through the law of purchase and sale.

<margin>

b Book 7, question 5, article 2, *On Justice and Law*; and the chapter *Concerning Causes*, *On the office of the delegate*.
c Argument from Law 1, *Digest*, *On the right of deliberation*; and the chapter *Concerning Causes*, *On the office of the delegate*.
d The chapter *Custom*, distinction 1; Law *Concerning those*, *Digest*, *On laws*.

¶ The sixth † point justifying this transaction is that the fourth argument of the opposing party can be dismissed by denying that in this transaction (when performed as it ought to be) anything is charged for waiting or delay. One reason is that among just merchants, the entire duration between payment periods is considered as a single day and as the present time for sending bills, preparing payments, and executing them—as Doctor Soto <note>original: "D. Soto"; referring to the influential Dominican theologian Domingo de Soto (1494–1560).</note> explained well, although he did not provide the underlying reason, which seems to be this: by law, some time must be granted to perform these tasks. Since this time is not strictly defined by the law itself, it must be determined by statute or by the judgment of a prudent man; and custom—which serves as law where law is absent—has determined it. This custom was established by the judgment of prudent merchants regarding the aforementioned period, though sometimes less time is sufficient and at other times more is required. 

The same argument is also resolved by considering that it is one thing to buy or sell something for its just price (at least the "pious" or moderate price) to be delivered three months from now, which is licit—for it is licit to sell on credit and to sell that which is yet to be born <note>Referring to future harvests or livestock offspring.</note>, and even to barter, as was said above, which is what occurs in this transaction. It is quite another thing to buy it for less than the just price because the money is advanced, or to sell it for more than the rigorous just price because it is sold on credit; the latter is illicit, as the argument proves and as we ourselves confess. Therefore, just as one may justly buy or obtain through barter the wool and herbs of the following year before Christmas at a just price, so too may one buy or obtain through barter in the Medina fair the money of Flanders at its just price, to be delivered at the first, or even second or third fair: provided that one does not charge more than the rigorous just price simply because it is to be delivered later than it would have been at the first fairs. We concede, however, that every time something significant is charged beyond the just price due to waiting and delay, one sins and is obligated to make restitution.

<margin>
76
a Otherwise it would not be a purchase or an exchange. Argument from Law *Not all*, *Digest*, *On things credited*; chapter *On the superior*, *On the office of the delegate*.
b Argument from Law 1, *Digest*, *On the right of deliberation*; chapter *Concerning Causes*, *On the office of the delegate*.
c Above in the same, number 34.
d Usurpation without a just title is illicit. Chapter *Penal*, 14, question 5.
</margin>

¶ We conclude † then, that the said transaction is licit, provided these conditions are kept. 
The first: that the exchange is not a sham. That is, that the person giving the money truly intends for it to be given to them in the place for which it is taken, and reasonably believes that the person taking it has, or will have, the money, estate, credit, or power to provide it there. 
The second: that for the absent money, one gives as much present money as is just, and that the price is not lowered excessively due to the absence. All of this must be estimated according to the judgment of a good man. 
The third: that one does not charge more because there is more time until the delivery or the scheduled payments than if it were to be delivered immediately in the place where it is to be paid. 
The fourth arises from this previous one: that one does not sell, barter, or give it for more by selling, bartering, or giving it for the second or third fair than if one gave it for the first. We say "for more" because if one wished to give it until the second or even third fair for the same amount that could justly be charged until the payments of the first fair, this can certainly be done and would be a work of charity and friendship; but one could not charge more. This is because, while that which is given by way of a true or likely exchange of interest can be given more expensively for two fairs than for one, and more for three than for two (as said above), this is not the case by way of an exchange of purchase, barter, or other innominate contract of which we speak here.

<margin>
77
e Above in this same Commentary, numbers 21 and 22.
f But every contract in which equality is not observed is illicit. Scotus in Book 4, distinction 25, question 2, article 2; and stated a little before in numbers 13 and 24.
</margin>

¶ From this † we infer the following. First, that one may reasonably doubt a case about which we were asked in Lisbon regarding a Castilian who wanted to give certain ducats there to a Portuguese merchant, so that they would be paid back to him with a certain profit at the first fair of Medina del Campo, which was to be three or four months away—a deal that was very convenient for the one giving the money to bring it back to Castile. On one hand, it seemed it was not licit, because no reason could be seen for which he could charge a profit; rather, it seemed he should pay the merchant, since it suited the Castilian to bring his money from there to here, and the merchant provided the industry and labor of giving it to him here, in accordance with what we have said regarding the justice of exchange by bills of exchange. Furthermore, because there seems to be an inequality and injustice if the merchant gives as much here as he takes there, and additionally applies his industry and labor, and yet pays a profit. Finally, because the merchant would not want to pay a profit if he had to give the money immediately in Medina...

<summary>Azpilcueta outlines the conditions under which currency exchange and future-delivery contracts are morally and legally licit, arguing that custom allows for a grace period in payments without it being considered usurious. He concludes by questioning a specific case in Lisbon where a merchant was offered profit on a transaction that seemed to lack the required equality of exchange.</summary>
