# On the final chapter on usury.

...but having to give them three or four months from now, and enjoying them in that intervening time; and consequently, he paid for the delay of time, which is usury, as stated above in § and elsewhere <note>original: "alibi"; Latin for 'elsewhere'.</note>, and this view Cajetan <note>Tommaso de Vio (1469–1534), known as Cajetan, was a highly influential Italian Dominican theologian and cardinal.</note> seems to hold.

<margin>78</margin>

<margin>
g Above in the same commentary, number 23.
b In the commentary on chapter 1, 14, question 3, in the treatise on exchange, final chapter.
a Book 7, question 3, article 1, *On Justice and Law*.
b Where cited above.
</margin>

¶ To † many, however, it will seem that the Lord Doctor Soto <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560) was a prominent Spanish Dominican theologian and a key figure in the School of Salamanca.</note> holds the opposite, saying that if it suited the merchant to carry his money from Medina to Lisbon, just as it suited the other to bring his own to Medina, he could well take the profit that can be taken through the exchange of letters <note>A "bill of exchange" (*letra de cambio*) allowed merchants to transfer funds across distances without moving physical gold, often involving a fee for the service.</note>. Cajetan also holds this in this case, although he does not cite it. To us, however, it does not: it seems that five ways should be distinguished by which the aforementioned Castilian could give the said ducats <note>The *ducado* (ducat) was a gold coin used for international trade throughout Europe and the Mediterranean.</note>. These are four without consideration—at least primarily—of the long or short time remaining until the fair <note>The trade fairs of Medina del Campo were the financial heart of 16th-century Spain.</note>, and one with this consideration.

The first, without the said consideration, is by way of a loan. The second, by way of exchange by letters, so that the merchant might transfer his ducats here. The third, by which he might transfer his own funds to the merchant, from Medina to there. The fourth, by way of purchase, barter, or another innominate exchange <note>An "innominate contract" is a legal term from Roman law for a binding agreement that does not fit into standard categories like "sale" or "lease."</note> of the absent ducats that the merchant held in Medina, for his own present ones held in Lisbon. The fifth is with primary consideration of the time and deadline remaining until the fair, by any of the said ways, charging him more or less according to the greater or lesser time remaining until then.

In the first case, if he wished to give them by way of a loan, and with a pact or primary intention that they be paid to him in Medina, it was usurious: because he wished to gain something through a loan—namely, the obligation that they be paid to him in Medina, and with a profit, having lent them in Lisbon, which is a gain measurable in money <note>original: "ganancia estimable a dinero"</note>.

<margin>c And consequently, usury. Chapter 1, 2, section 5, question 14, 3, as I stated more extensively in said chapter 1, number 5.</margin>

If, however, he wished to lend them without such a pact and intention of being precisely obligated to pay in Medina, but rather either in Lisbon, equal for equal, or in Medina with that profit as compensation for the fact that money was worth more there than in Medina, he could licitly take that excess, if the ducats were worth that much more there than here, according to what was said above.

<margin>d Above in the same, number 61.</margin>

In the second case, if he wished to give them by way of exchange by letters, so that the merchant might transfer his money to Medina, the Castilian was obligated to give someone else a reward for it, according to what was said above.

<margin>e Above in the same commentary, number 21.</margin>

Although it could be agreed in the contract that for his salary he might take the amount by which money is worth more there than here, or such a part of it as might be just, according to the aforementioned.

In the third case, if he wished to give them by way of transferring the money from here to there, the merchant could take as much salary as the banker could justly take for transferring it to him.

In the fourth case, if he wished to give them by way of purchase, barter, or another innominate contract of "I give to you so that you give to me," etc., he could charge more for two reasons: because the merchant's money is absent and therefore worth less;

<margin>a Above in the same commentary, number 61.</margin>

and by way of the fact that money is worth more there than here, as was said above.

In the fifth case, if he wished to give them by any of the said ways, with primary consideration of the time remaining until payment, wishing to charge more or less according to whether more or less time remained, we say that without doubt it was illicit for him.

<margin>b Above in the same commentary, number 47.</margin>

Because it remains resolved above that not only the contract of a loan, but every other contract in which more or less is taken because there is a longer or shorter deadline until payment, is formal or virtual usury.

<margin>79</margin>

¶ The † second point follows: that proportionally to this distinction, one must distinguish when someone else wishes to give money in Medina, where it is worth less, for Lisbon or Flanders, where it is worth more; or in Seville for Medina, where it is worth the same. To avoid prolixity, we do not explain this further.

¶ The third point follows: what ought to be said regarding that determination of Saint Antoninus <note>Antoninus of Florence (1389–1459) was an archbishop and legal expert famous for his works on moral theology and economics.</note>.

<margin>c Part 2, title 1, chapter 7, section 50.</margin>

Namely, that it is usurious for a changer or banker who gives someone in Rome one hundred or one thousand ducats for their business, to be repaid six months from then in Paris to whoever holds his power of attorney, with a pact that he will pay him five or eight percent more there.

<margin>d Under the word *Usury*, 4, question 13.</margin>

Which Silvester <note>Sylvester Mazzolini (1456–1523), an Italian theologian known for his "Summa Sylvestrina."</note> follows, and both are approved by the most learned Licentiate Gregorio López,

<margin>e Law 31, fifth *Partida*, 11.</margin>

who is satisfied with this title, being of the Council of the Indies <note>The Council of the Indies was the most important administrative organ of the Spanish Empire for the Americas and the Philippines.</note>, and having also deserved that of "Doctor," as shown by the great labors and erudition with which he has composed the very apt, discreet, and useful glosses <note>Legal commentaries written in the margins or between the lines of a text.</note> on all the *Seven Partidas* <note>The *Siete Partidas* was a foundational 13th-century Castilian legal code.</note>, which he published last year for the great benefit of the Republic. He printed them while being in this very same cell, although not as invisible as we are. For it follows,

<sig>E</sig>

<meta>This page continues the technical legal and moral discussion regarding currency exchange contracts, specifically focusing on the distinctions between a legitimate exchange and usurious loans. Azpilcueta concludes his commentary with a moral warning to confessors and merchants.</meta>

# Resolutory Commentary on Exchange.

...it must be said: First, that this view is true, because in that contract (as it is currently performed) those five or eight percent are taken for the waiting and consideration of the time that passes between the loan and the repayment, which is manifest usury <note>The School of Salamanca, to which Azpilcueta belonged, argued that charging for the mere passage of time was usury, as time belongs to God, not the lender.</note>. Second, that such a contract could not be made lawfully by way of a loan for Paris, even if it were done without respect to the time and deadline, but it could for Spain. This is because, since nothing should be expected in return for a loan, and money is worth more in France than in Rome, it is illicit for two reasons: namely, because one receives more than what was lent by reason of the place where it is to be paid, and because one receives that extra five or eight percent. For Spain, however, it could be done, not by taking five or eight percent, but by taking as much more percent as the money is worth less here than there, to be paid immediately here. Third, that such a contract can be made lawfully by way of purchase, barter, or another innominate contract <note>An "innominate contract" is a legal term for an agreement that does not have a specific name in Roman law but is still binding because both parties have fulfilled an obligation.</note>, giving there—with consideration for the time—those hundred <margin>a Above in the same commentary, no. 64.</margin> present ducats for as many, and some more, absent ones, provided the aforementioned **a** four conditions are kept. However, one could take more if they were given for Spain than if they were given for France, because Spain is further from Rome than France. For this reason, <margin>b Above in the same, no. 64.</margin> money "absent" from Spain is worth less in Rome than money "absent" from France, because of what was <margin>c Under the word "usury," 4th question, 13.</margin> said above **b**, and because money is worth less in Spain than in Rome, and in France more than in Rome and Spain. This (in our opinion) is what Sylvester <note>original: "Syluestro"; referring to Sylvester Prierias (1456–1523), a Dominican theologian who wrote extensively on cases of conscience.</note> **c** meant to say, stating that the said contract, as it is performed, was usurious, but that it could be performed well.

<margin>d in the treatise on exchange, chapter 7.</margin> <margin>e Above in the same commentary, no. 34.</margin>
¶ The † last point follows: it is not exchange but usury disguised with the name of exchange, namely that practiced by those who, when the fair <note>Fairs, such as those in Medina del Campo or Lyon, were the primary centers for international trade and the settlement of bills of exchange.</note> and the time for payment arrive, give the debtors (who do not pay) a delay and wait until the next fair, provided they pay a certain amount of "re-exchange," as Cajetan <note>original: "Caietano"; referring to Tommaso de Vio, a cardinal and theologian who was an authority on economic morality.</note> **d** noted well. Although it cannot be denied that, by way of exchange for interest <note>original: "interesse"; in this context, "interest" refers to compensation for a loss incurred or a profit missed (*lucrum cessans*) due to the delay, rather than a predetermined fee for a loan.</note>, they could be charged what the creditors cease to gain through true exchanges that were offered to them, which they missed because the debtors did not pay them at that time, provided they had that money. This is based on what was said above **e**.

¶ This is what, under due correction, has seemed to us regarding exchanges, in good faith and without deceit before God. This is the furthest extent to which the profits from them can be stretched. We have extended it as much as possible to justly defend the souls, honors, and estates of so many principal and honorable people. We desire that those who are outside of this trade feel no envy toward those who live by it, even those who are highly exalted. We advise the confessors of those who live by it to most gravely dissuade them from exchanges and feigned interests. They should persuade them that the temptations therein make them walk toward Paradise along high and stony slopes, from which the stumbles of great love and affection for large profits can easily cast them down into such deep valleys of sin, and such thick briars of restitution <note>Restitution is the moral and legal requirement to return ill-gotten gains (like usury) to the original owner as a condition for divine forgiveness.</note>, that they may late or never rise and free themselves from them. May it please Him who was crowned for all with a crown of briars and thorns to raise and release those who have already fallen into them, and those of us who have so often fallen into others, and may He lift us all to the most free heights of the heavens; for the love of that most glorious mother of His and Queen of them, the eighth day of whose most joyful Visitation the Catholic Church celebrates today. Amen. ->*Salamanca, July 8th, in the year*<-
->*from the birth of the same Virgin, 1556.*<-

<summary>Azpilcueta defines the narrow conditions under which currency exchange and "interest" are morally lawful, distinguishing them from usury. He concludes with a stern moral warning to merchants and confessors about the spiritual dangers of seeking excessive profits.</summary>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>Regarding the final chapter on Simony.</header>

# Resolutory commentary on mental Simony, and the understanding of the final chapter on Simony, for the clarification of a certain passage in the Manual for Confessors.

<note>An ornamental woodcut initial 'P' depicts a scholar or cleric seated at a writing desk in a vaulted room, holding a quill and looking at an open book. Architectural elements and foliage are visible in the background.</note> Continuing the review of the *Manual for Confessors and Penitents*, we came across that obscure passage on mental <term>simony</term> <note>Simony is the act of buying or selling sacred or spiritual things, such as church offices or sacraments. "Mental simony" refers to the internal intent or desire to do so, even if no formal agreement is made.</note>, and we decided to recall and print something of what we noted in the year 1532 in the last chapter on simony, after holding the Chair of Decrees and before attaining the <term>Prima chair</term> <note>The 'Prima' chair was the most prestigious and highest-paid professorship at the University of Salamanca, so named because the lectures were held at the first hour of the day (Prime).</note> of this most renowned University of Salamanca, to which—and to its governors, professors, and students—I owe so much. We decided, then, to do this to clarify that passage, which is considered inexplicable, and to defend the said chapter and its common interpretation, and what we said in the aforementioned *Manual* following it, and what has been taught and maintained for so many years throughout the world by illustrious authors regarding this most everyday matter, against the arguments and new way of understanding of certain modern, though very learned, men; using that extreme, careful, and sought-after brevity which we used in the said *Manual*.

### GREGORY IX IN CHAPTER XLVI.
*Which is the last of the title on Simony.*

> <note>Original Latin: "Mandato noſtro recepto, vt cum monachis, qui per Symoniam dato aliquo locum in monaſteriis ſunt adepti, ſecundum conſtitutionem generalis concilij diſpenſares..."</note> Having received our mandate, so that you might dispense with those monks who, by giving something through simony, obtained a place in monasteries, according to the constitution of the general council: and below. ¶ Responding briefly to your consultation, we say that the apostolic mandate also extends to abbots.
> ¶ And to the resignations of spiritual and temporal things which are acquired on both sides in such a way that no pact preceded it, but only a preceding impulse of the mind <note>This refers to "mental simony," where a gift is given with the hope of a spiritual office in return, but without a verbal or written contract.</note> (in which case it suffices for the delinquents to satisfy their Creator through penance alone), they are not held [liable] for simony of this kind.

<note>Azpilcueta provides his own Spanish translation of the Latin decree above, here rendered into English.</note> Our commission having been received, so that according to the constitution of the general council you might dispense with the monks who, by giving something through simony, gained a place in the monasteries: and below.
¶ Responding briefly to your consultation, we say that the apostolic commission also extends to abbots. ¶ And to the renouncing of spiritual and temporal things, which are acquired without a pact preceding them, though with the will and intent of both parties (in which case it is enough for the offenders to satisfy their Creator through penance alone), they are not held [liable] for such simony.

### SUMMARY.

Whoever allows a dispensation for monks, allows it for abbots. no. 1. And the reason. no. 2.
Doctor Miranda Sancho de Carranza of Navarre, teacher of the author. no. 1.
An abbot does not cease to be a monk, if he was one before. no. 2. He is understood to be a monk, even in unfavorable matters. no. 5. Which grace should be expanded, and which narrowed. no. 2.

<header>Resolutory Commentary on Exchanges.</header>

A dispensation regarding the monks of a monastery includes the monk-abbot. Number 6.

<margin>
1
</margin>
FIRST, † it is gathered from this chapter that notable conclusion: that whoever has a commission and power from the Pope to dispense with monks, can also dispense with abbots. This is gathered from it because it clearly states that whoever has received a commission from the Pope to dispense—in accordance with the General Council—with monks who were received into monasteries through <term>simony</term> <note>The buying or selling of spiritual offices or roles.</note>, can also dispense with the abbots. For this reason, I marvel at how the very sharp John Major <gloss>or</gloss> <note>John Major (1467–1550) was a renowned Scottish philosopher and theologian.</note> (whom I usually cite with pleasure as he deserves, and because I was a student in arts and philosophy and much beloved by his illustrious disciple, the most learned Doctor Miranda—Sancho de Carranza Navarro, the great glory of the University of Alcalá and of the magisterial canonry of Seville) said that this chapter was written to determine that the chapter *Quoniam*—found under this same title and speaking of monks—applies to abbots. He said this because those words, "having received our mandate," clearly prove that it speaks of the interpretation of the commission that Pope Gregory IX sent for dispensing, and not of the interpretation of the said chapter *Quoniam*. That chapter was ordained in the General Council long before Gregory IX was Pope, during the presidency of Innocent III, who was succeeded by Honorius III, and he by Gregory IX. It is true that although this chapter was not written for what he says, it could well be gathered from this induction: that whoever says one thing is seen to approve of what that thing presupposes. P. And that Gregory IX said that his commission to dispense according to the form of that chapter is understood to include abbots. This could not be unless that Council, which speaks only of monks and nuns, also included abbots and abbesses. It can also be gathered by way of even stronger reasoning, considering that a provision of the common law is more favorable than one of a Papal commission <gloss>a</gloss>: and this text says that in the commission sent regarding the matter of the said chapter *Quoniam* for monks, abbots are included. Therefore, by even stronger reasoning, they would be included under that <gloss>c</gloss> which speaks of monks.

<margin>
2
b In the title "on those things done by prelates without consent of the chapter."
c Argument from chapter *Sedes* on rescripts, where Innocent notes this.
d Section 1, on the rule.
e *Magna*, after the middle of Clementine 1.
f Ibid, opposition 6.
g Argument from the chapter *Cum ad monasterium*, final section, on the status of monks...
h And therefore it is favorable, chapter *Cum dilectus* on donations.
i Law 3, section *Si quis a Principa*, Digest...
k Chapter *P. & G.* on the office of the delegate...
l Chapters *Sanam* and *Porro* on principles.
m According to the gloss on the section *Nisi*, on rigor...
n Chapters 1 & 2 on the sons of priests.
</margin>
¶ Neither the † gloss here, however, nor John Major there, touch upon the reason why the commission speaking of monks is understood to include abbots—it appearing that abbots are one thing and monks another, and even their chapters are distinct <gloss>b</gloss>. And it being certain that by a <term>rescript</term> <note>A formal written reply or decree from the Pope or Emperor answering a legal question.</note> obtained against monks, one could not proceed against abbots <gloss>c</gloss>; and even appearing that a <term>Clementine</term> <note>A decree or collection of laws issued by Pope Clement V.</note> <gloss>d</gloss> speaking of the religious does not extend to prelates, as its gloss <gloss>e</gloss> and the Cardinal <gloss>f</gloss> upon it signify. Rather, the reason for this fine conclusion is composed of two or three notable things: First, a monk does not cease to be a monk by being made an abbot <gloss>g</gloss>. Second, the aforementioned commission of Gregory IX, by which he gave power to dispense according to what the law ordained, was a favor <gloss>h</gloss>. Third, it was a favor that prejudiced no one, nor was it against the law, nor did it give occasion for ambition. We have added this last part because a favor that prejudices a third party must be interpreted strictly <gloss>i</gloss>, just as rescripts for lawsuits that derogate from the jurisdiction of ordinary judges are narrowed <gloss>k</gloss>. So too are privileges that go against the law, and expectations and beneficial graces, for they give occasion for ambition. And if you should say that he who dispenses and relaxes laws prejudices the common law <gloss>m</gloss>, and consequently the commission in this text speaking of dispensation should be narrowed and not expanded: it shall be answered that it is one thing to *dispense* (of which your objection speaks, and which lacks the said qualities and should be narrowed <gloss>n</gloss>), and another thing is the *power to dispense* (which possesses those qualities our text speaks of, and for that reason should be expanded).

<meta>catchword: ¶ From all [De to]</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that a papal power to grant dispensations to monks should be interpreted broadly enough to include abbots, as an abbot remains essentially a monk and such a power is a legal "favor" that harms no one.</summary>
<keywords>canon law, dispensation, abbot, monk, Gregory IX, John Major, papal commission, simony, legal interpretation, favor (merced)</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the legal discussion regarding the interpretation of ecclesiastical dispensations. Azpilcueta is refining the rule that while a dispensation itself is a restriction of the general law (and thus "odious"), the power or commission granted to a subordinate to grant dispensations should be interpreted broadly as a "favor."</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>On the final chapter regarding Simony.</header>

<margin>
Or chapter 1 and 2, *De filiis presbyterorum* <note>original: "de fil. prasby." — regarding the sons of priests</note>, book 6.
According to the gloss on chapter 1, *De officio vicarii* <note>original: "de offi. vica." — regarding the office of the vicar</note>, as Panormitanus and Ravernas said there, and the Cardinal with Felinus in chapter *Perpetuus*, *De fide instrumentorum* <note>original: "de fide in stru." — regarding the authenticity of documents</note>, and others elsewhere have said individually.
</margin>

¶ From all of this, it is gathered that although the act of dispensing is an "odious" thing and worthy of being interpreted strictly ^o <note>In canon law, an "odious" matter is one that deviates from common law or restricts rights; therefore, it is interpreted as narrowly as possible to protect the general rule.</note>; yet the commission to dispense is a "favorable" thing and worthy of being interpreted broadly ^p. To this end, here and in many other places, this text is weighed. However, we now point out something never noted until here: that this chapter does not prove the said conclusion in such a general way. For it only proves that the commission which the Pope gives to dispense in cases where the law commands it to be done, ought to be expanded. This is because such was the commission spoken of in the text, if one carefully weighs those words: *You should dispense according to the Constitution of the general council* <note>original: "Secundum Constitutionem generalis concilij dispensares"</note>. Nevertheless, the said conclusion, stated generally, must be upheld (even if this text does not prove it by necessity), since giving power to someone so that they may dispense is a favor; it prejudices no one, is not against the law, nor does it provide matter or occasion for ambition—which are the four aforementioned qualities that induce favor and extension.

<margin>
According to the unique saying of Hostiensis in the final chapter, *De transactionibus* <note>original: "de trãfactio." — regarding settlements</note>.
Number 5.
</margin>

¶ From this conclusion, some useful things are again inferred which Felinus refers to here, limiting it without necessity (though not without truth): that it does not apply to natural defects. Even the "suppletion" <note>A legal remedy where a superior, like the Pope, fills in a technical or legal defect to make an act valid.</note> by which the Pope generally tends to supply the defects of some statute does not extend to these ^a. He limits it discreetly, however, stating that it does not apply when the persons to be dispensed are specifically named, as Panormitanus said here ^b and as one gloss felt ^c; because then, it seems more like an already-granted dispensation awaiting execution than a simple power to dispense.

<margin>
In the chapter *Literas*, under the word "ordinary," *De filiis presbyterorum* <note>Regarding the sons of priests</note>.
In the chapter *Dudum*, 2, *De electione* <note>Regarding elections</note>, near the end.
</margin>

¶ He also limits it † more usefully than pertinently: that this broad interpretation should not extend so far as to remove from the commissioner the necessity of knowing the *cause* <note>The legal or moral justification for granting an exemption from the law.</note> for the dispensation. It is never presumed in doubt that the Pope wishes to remove this requirement. Thus, all delegates and "ordinaries" <note>An "ordinary" is a cleric, such as a bishop, who has standard jurisdiction over a territory.</note> must inform themselves of the cause for the dispensation before they grant it, as Innocent uniquely stated ^d.

<margin>
In the chapter *Cum ad monasterium*, *De statu monachorum* <note>original: "de stat. monacho." — regarding the state of monks</note>, and in the chapter *Veniens*, *De filiis presbyterorum*.
</margin>

¶ From this, we can infer the errors that many nuncios, many Counts Palatine, and the ordinaries in cases permitted to them commit—with great harm to the commonwealth—by dispensing without cause, or without sufficient knowledge and information regarding it. They do not consider that they sin gravely in this, usurping the authority of the Pope. He alone, as the sovereign Vicar of Jesus Christ, can dispense without such knowledge and information. His dispensation alone, made without cause, is valid regarding general human law; it belongs to him alone to judge if, and when, he sins in doing so. Bishops, however, and others neither greater nor lesser than they, cannot dispense without cause, nor without knowledge or information regarding it, concerning the sacred Canons—even in cases permitted to them, as Innocent uniquely determined, and as is received by all.

<margin>
In the chapter *Veniens*, *De filiis presbyterorum*.
As cited above.
</margin>

^e Though they might do this regarding their own synodal constitutions, in which something is ordered outside of the Common Law. And yet, many—with great disrespect for the Holy Apostolic See, the burdening of their consciences, and damage to the commonwealth—dispense as easily against the cases in which they are permitted as they do against their own constitutions. Many of these should be rebuked and even (as Innocent says ^a) punished.

<margin>
In the Clementines, 1, *De regulis* <note>Regarding rules</note>.
In the law *Si servus communis*, 1, in the Digest, *De stipulatio servis* <note>original: "ff. de stipe. su." — regarding the stipulations of slaves</note>.
In the law *Cum lege*, in the Digest, *De testamentis* <note>Regarding wills</note>.
In the chapter *In isteis*, *De testamentis*.
In chapter 2 and the chapter *Quia in tantum*, *De praebendis* <note>Regarding prebends</note>.
</margin>

¶ It is also inferred from the aforesaid that a dispensation speaking of a "monk" does not include the "abbot" if the matter is not favorable, as everyone here and elsewhere seems to feel ^b. But (in our opinion) it is not necessary for the matter to be favorable; it is enough that it is not "odious" and restrictive. Therefore, if one were to vow, or in some other way bind himself, to provide food, or specific clothing, or specific books to the monks or friars of such a monastery, he would be obliged to give to the abbot or prelate as much as to any other monk, as the accepted Bartolus feels ^c. And we even add that it is not enough for the matter to be "odious" in any way if it cannot be gathered by certain legal conjectures that the intention of the one disposing was not to include the abbot. For we always follow the conclusion that words placed in a disposition (even if it is "odious") are to be understood as broadly as their proper meaning extends. We discuss this at length elsewhere ^e (following Aretinus and other more recent authors). And it is certain that the abbot is properly a monk, and that the name "monk," by its proper meaning, includes one who is truly a monk, even if he holds another dignity.

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that the authority to grant dispensations should be interpreted broadly as a "favor," but warns that officials must still have a valid "cause" before exempting anyone from Church law. He also concludes that legal terms like "monk" should naturally include "abbots" unless specifically excluded.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the interpretation of legal language in ecclesiastical dispensations. Having established that commissions to dispense should be interpreted broadly, Azpilcueta now applies this logic to specific roles within a monastery and moves into the definition and gravity of "mental simony" (simony of the mind or intent).</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>Commentary resolving mental Simony.</header>
<sig>I 3</sig>

<margin>6</margin>
¶ From this we infer another conclusion, which seems contrary to the opinion one might gather from the words of everyone here: that if the Pope today were to grant a dispensation to all the monks or religious of a certain monastery who fell into irregularity <note>Irregularity is a canonical impediment that prevents a person from receiving or exercising Holy Orders.</note> due to the violation of a certain interdict, he would be seen to have dispensed the abbot or prelate of that monastery, provided he was a professed monk. This holds even though the act of dispensing is "odious" and should be restricted according to everyone; this is because the word "Monk" or "religious," by its own proper meaning, includes the professed abbot, and there are no sufficient conjectures to presume that in this case the author of the dispensation did not wish to include him.

¶ We also infer from all this another new and singular conclusion: that neither this text nor other similar decisions apply to commendatory abbots <note>A commendatory abbot was often a secular cleric or even a layman who held the title and revenues of an abbey without being a member of the order or performing religious duties.</note> and prelates who never made a profession, nor are they truly monks or regular canons. This is because the primary reason for this text and similar decisions is that the abbot does not cease to be a monk by being made an abbot; this reasoning does not apply to the commendatory abbot or prelate who is not professed, since he cannot be called a monk or regular canon either properly or improperly.

->S U M M A R Y.<-

Mental Simony: is it a sin, and does it make one a simoniac? number 7. It consists of these two species, etc. number 8.
What is sin? Those of the will, speech, and deed are of the same species and malice. number 7.
Simony has these three species. number 8. And is there mental simony where there is an external promise without internal intent? number 9. Yes, there is. number 10. But it does not oblige one to make restitution. number 11.

---

<margin>7</margin>
<margin>
a Thomas [Aquinas], *Summa Theologica*, Part 1 of the 2nd Part, Question 72, Article 7; and in the *Manual*, Chapter 16, number 1, near the end.
b Chapter *Tanta est*, under the title *On Simony*.
c Paragraph 1, Question 15, Case 1, upon which commentary we declare this.
d In chapter *Consule*, under the title *On Simony*.
e In [the commentary on the] *Sentences*, Book 4, Distinction 25, Question 7, near the end.
f Against the chapter *Intelligentia*, under the title *On the Signification of Words*.
</margin>
The second point † gathered from this text is that mental simony is a sin. This is because it calls those who commit it "delinquents" <note>original: "delinquentibus"</note> through that very word, and because it clearly signifies that it is necessary for them to perform penance for it, through those words: *it is enough for delinquents to satisfy their Creator through penance alone* <note>original: "sufficit delinquentibus per solam pœnitentiam suo satisfacere creatori"</note>. Furthermore, sins of the will, speech, and deed are of the same species and malice <gloss>a</gloss>, and it is well known that simony put into action is a very grave sin <gloss>b</gloss>. It also meets the entire definition of sin with which Saint <unclear>Augustine</unclear> defined it <gloss>c</gloss>: to be the will to attain what justice forbids; and mental simony is such a will, as is clear. ¶ However, the second gloss of this chapter, with which another agrees <gloss>d</gloss>, seems to feel the opposite, insofar as it says that no one is a simoniac solely by the will to commit simony. It is clear that by mental simony alone, no one commits any sin other than simony, and therefore [it would seem] no one sins at all. But in truth, the gloss does not mean this, regardless of what John Mair <gloss>e</gloss> <note>John Mair (c. 1467–1550) was a prominent Scottish philosopher and historian known for his influence on scholasticism.</note> says, weighing the words more than their sense <gloss>f</gloss>. This is because the gloss itself says that he sins; in saying that he is not a "simoniac," it did not mean to say that he does not commit the sin of simony, but rather that he is not among those whom the law commonly calls "simoniacs"—those who put it into action and incur the penalties established against it. In effect, it means to say that he is not an *actual* simoniac, but only a *mental* one.

<margin>8</margin>
<margin>
g Chapter 22, number 103.
h As above, number 99.
i Ibid., from number 100.
</margin>
¶ From which † it follows that there are many species of simony: namely, purely mental; mental-conventional; and real, as we said in the *Manual* <gloss>g</gloss>, after declaring what simony is <gloss>h</gloss>. What is a spiritual thing? How many types of it are there <gloss>i</gloss>? What is each of these three simonies? Mental simony is the desire to give or take some temporal thing as the price for a spiritual thing, without actually giving or taking it; or wanting to take or give, and actually taking or giving, without an express or tacit declaration of that evil will; and consequently, without an express or tacit pact.

¶ To which we now add that mental simony is divided into two: namely, mental simony that does not reach the effect of taking or giving anything; and mental simony that does reach the point of taking or giving something without declaring, either expressly or tacitly, the evil will to give or take a spiritual thing.

<summary>This page explores the legal and moral definitions of simony, specifically focusing on 'mental simony'—the intent to trade spiritual favors for material gain. Azpilcueta argues that while mental simony is a sin of the will, it is legally distinct from actual simony, and he clarifies which ecclesiastical figures (like professed vs. commendatory abbots) are subject to specific dispensations.</summary>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a discussion on whether dispensations for "monks" included "abbots" (concluding they do) and excluded "commendatory abbots" (who are not truly monks). This page shifts to a specific legal and moral analysis of "simony"—the buying or selling of church offices—focusing on whether an agreement counts as simony if one party has no intention of fulfilling their side of the bargain.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>On the final chapter of Simony.</header>

...ritual for temporal things. We said "expressly or tacitly" to signify that it is not merely mental simony, but real simony, to place a bet (with someone who holds a vacant benefice <note>A church office, such as a parish or a cathedral position, that provided the holder with an income.</note> and can grant it) of one hundred ducats <note>A gold coin used as a standard currency in 16th-century Europe.</note> that he will not give it to N., his son or relative, whom he wishes to receive it. If, to avoid losing the bet, he gives the office, it is simony; this is because they have tacitly agreed that one gives one hundred ducats so that the other grants the benefice to the person he chooses. Another such case would be that involving a great and wealthy lord who made an offer to a great King. The King desired greatly that the debts left by a certain Bishop—amounting to more than twenty thousand ducats—be paid. The lord told him that if His Highness were pleased, he would pay all those debts from the inheritance that a certain son of his was to receive from him and his mother. Since the son was a cleric, highly learned and virtuous, it was tacitly understood that he did this so the King would present the son for the Bishopric, which was vacant due to the death of the man who owed the debts.

<margin>
a Book 3, Volume 2 on Simony.
b Book 9, Question 5, Article 11 on Justice and Law.
c Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica, Prima Secundae, Question 20, Articles 1, 2, and 3.
d Per the gloss in the Summa, Question 1, Article 1, and in the *Manual for Confessors*, Chapter 23, Number 90.
e Cited above.
</margin>
9 ¶ We also add that there is a doubt whether it would be "mental conventional simony" or "real simony" to promise you a certain number of ducats, and to bind oneself by a legal instrument <note>A formal legal document or contract.</note> to pay them to you, so that you would give him a Bishopric or some other benefice, while having no intention—great or small—of actually buying it or paying what was promised. To this, the most learned Cardinal Cajetan,<sup>a</sup> followed by Master Soto,<sup>b</sup> responds that it is not simony. This is because the guilt and naming of outward works descend from the nature of the inward intentions.<sup>c</sup> Thus, there can be no true real simony where there is no mental intent. Furthermore, simony is defined<sup>d</sup> by a studious will to buy, etc. In this case, there is no true purchase or sale: where there is no true will to buy, there can be no true purchase; and where there is no true purchase, there is no true sale, but only the appearance of one. Consequently, there is no true simony here, only the appearance of it. From this, Cajetan infers<sup>e</sup> that although such a promisor sins by participating in the mental simony of the one who wishes to sell him the spiritual office—defaming himself, scandalizing others, and lying...

<margin>
a For by "purchase" and "sale" in this matter, we understand every contract that is not free of charge, as we have said more extensively in the heading above and touched upon in the *Manual*, Chapter 23, Number 103.
</margin>
10 ¶ ...he does not sin by committing simony. Furthermore, both [Cajetan and Soto] infer that he is not obliged to give up the benefice he acquires through that deception. However, the first thing they say does not seem right to us, nor does what Cajetan infers from it. We believe that both parties commit simony—both mental and conventional. For an act to be the sin of simony, a will to buy or sell in the true and proper sense is not necessary; the will to do or give some temporal thing to obtain a spiritual one is sufficient.<sup>a</sup> This is found in this case. For one of these parties wants to sell the spiritual office, and the other—even if he does not want to buy it through a true purchase—wants to perform, and does perform, a temporal act so that the other will give him the spiritual one. He wants to make, and does make, an outward promise and obligation, and consents to a legal document for them, which is a temporal thing that can be valued in money. This is confirmed because no one would deny it is simony if I were to give you a benefice because you bound yourself by a legal document to give me or another person a thousand ducats, in such a way that you could be compelled to pay them, regardless of what your internal intent might be; for I give you the benefice in exchange for something valued in money.

<margin>
a All authors teach this in Book 4, Distinction 25.
b Below, Number 28 and following. See also *Casito* in decision 5 on pacts, and *Gomes* in the rules on three-year possession, Question 12.
c Chapter 45, Number 105.
e In the chapter *Si quando* on Rescripts, page 11.
</margin>
11 ¶ Regarding the second point that both infer—namely, that one would not be obliged to leave the benefice by reason of this simony—this seems right to us. However, it is not for the reason they give (that it is not simony), but rather because it is no more than mental and conventional simony, and not "real" simony. The latter (as we shall say below)<sup>b</sup> does not oblige one to make restitution <note>Restitution here means the legal and moral obligation to give up the office or return the profits gained from it.</note> according to the authors<sup>c</sup> whom we follow in the *Manual*<sup>d</sup> and elsewhere.<sup>e</sup>

Mental simony does not oblige one to make restitution. Number 12. With the defense of this position, Number 13 and following. This holds even though it is forbidden by natural and divine law, and it does not oblige even in the forum of conscience. Number 26. This is the case even if the act is carried out by both parties. Number 27.
A Papal declaration requires obedience and the submission of one's understanding. Number 15.

<summary>This page analyzes the nuances of "mental" vs "real" simony, arguing that even a fake promise of payment to obtain a church office constitutes simony because a legal obligation has temporal value, though it may not require the offender to resign the office.</summary>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the legal definition of simony—the sale of spiritual things—specifically focusing on cases where a "tacit" or unspoken agreement exists, such as giving money to a King with the silent expectation of receiving a bishopric for one's son. This page continues that analysis by distinguishing between "real" simony (with a pact) and "mental" simony (intent alone).</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>
# Resolutory Commentary on Mental Simony.

Inept understanding of this chapter by certain Theologians. Number 16 and the three following.
The Church does not impose a penalty for a purely mental act, nor for an act that is evil only because of intent. Number 20.
Which mental sins (even if damage follows) do not require restitution. Number 24.
Why mental simony <note>The internal intent to buy or sell a spiritual office or grace without a formal agreement.</note> does not require restitution, while mental usury does. Numbers 22 and 24.
Restitution is required by commandment when one wrongly takes from one who wrongly gives. Number 23.
Restitution is not owed where there is no outward injustice. Number 25.
Mental and conventional simony do not require restitution before, etc. Number 28.
The granting of a benefice is not suspended.
A benefice <note>An ecclesiastical office, like a parish or bishopric, that provides an income.</note> is feigned to be vacant. Number 30.
Emphyteusis <term>a long-term lease of land where the tenant must improve the property</term> is not considered vacant without the lord’s consent. Number 30.
Excommunication is not considered incurred regarding pensions until, etc. Number 31.
The author desires a declaration regarding conventional simony. Number 32.

<margin>
a On which see chapter *Tanta* regarding simony, and other places cited there by Innocent, Panormitanus, and others.
b *Extravagantes* 2 regarding simony. <note>original: "Extrauagan. 2. de symo." referring to papal decrees outside the main collections of Canon Law.</note>
c Argument from chapter *Cum in cunctis* regarding elections and the *Authenticum* *Multo magis* in the Code regarding sacred matters.
</margin>
THE third point † gathered from this text is that mental simony does not require restitution of what was acquired through it, whether it be spiritual or temporal. From this it follows that it will not cause one to incur any other penalty ordained in law against simoniacs ᵃ: because the obligation to restore what was gained by simony is not a punishment according to some, but a debt contracted by unduly taking what one should not have. And according to those who call it a punishment, it is not as external or odious as the other penalties of suspension, excommunication ᵇ and deprivation; and since the obligation of restitution is not incurred by it, the other penalties will be incurred even less. ᶜ In this, all agree: and also, that this conclusion holds in the first of the two above-mentioned mental simonies: which does not reach the effect of giving or taking anything. <margin>12</margin>

<margin>
d Among whom are John Major in book 4, distinction 25, question 7; Adrian in *Quodlibet* 9; Soto, book 9, question 8, article 1 on justice and law.
e Chapter *Consuluit* regarding usury; and more extensively as we said in chapter 1, 14, question 3, above, with this printed commentary.
</margin>
¶ There is, however, great difficulty as to whether this third conclusion and its corollary hold in the other type of mental simony, which, beyond the evil will, proceeds to giving or taking, or to giving and taking something for it, without expressing it formally or virtually, and without an express or tacit pact. Some authors ᵈ are of the opinion that it does not [hold]: seeing that mental usury <term>the hidden intent to charge interest on a loan without an explicit contract</term> requires the restitution of what was taken for it, ᵉ and they find no sufficient difference for this between it and mental simony. To us, however, the common opinion always seemed correct, which holds the opposite, and we have maintained it here and elsewhere for many reasons.

¶ First † because this text says it so clearly in the second part that it seems a great boldness to us to say it does not say so by giving it glosses <note>Interpretive notes or commentaries.</note> that in no way fit it, and giving an opening to say as much of many other texts, and to deny that they determine what is clearly determined in them. <margin>13</margin>

<margin>
f Word: Simony, question 20.
g *Summa Theologiae* 2nd of the 2nd, question 100, article 6, reply 6.
h Book 9, question 8, article 1 on justice and law.
</margin>
¶ Second, because until today the gloss and almost all the Doctor Canonists and Theologians have understood this text this way, among whom is Innocent IV here, whom Sylvester ᶠ wishes to claim as being on our side, for he exemplifies this text in two cases: one involving a man who served in order to obtain a benefice, and the other who gave it to him for having served him, without either declaring their evil intentions to the other, and thus clearly understands it as the said second type of mental simony. Among them is also Saint Thomas, ᵍ whatever the Lord Soto ʰ may wish to say. For his words, translated from Latin into Spanish <note>Azpilcueta is translating Aquinas from the original Latin.</note>, are these: "The will alone makes a man simoniacal for the purpose of being punished by God; but not for the purpose of incurring an ecclesiastical penalty, and for this reason he is not obliged to restore the benefice acquired through mental simony, and it suffices for him to perform penance for his evil intention."

<summary>This page explores the legal and moral nuances of "mental simony," arguing that while an unspoken intent to buy or sell church offices is a sin before God, it does not trigger the legal obligation of restitution or formal Church penalties because no outward contract exists.</summary>

# On the final chapter regarding Simony.

intention. Although the desire that the aforementioned Lord Soto <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a prominent Spanish Dominican theologian and contemporary of the author.</note> had for that most learned and holy man <note>Saint Thomas Aquinas.</note> to be on his side against the common opinion, made it seem to him that his words should not be understood as referring to mental simony <note>The internal intent to buy or sell a spiritual office without an outward agreement or payment.</note>, of which the Common Opinion speaks, but rather of another kind. This can in no way be maintained, because he clearly speaks of it: both in the proposition of the sixth argument and in its solution. For he clearly speaks of the simony that is committed by internal intention alone without explaining it outwardly, of which only God is judge and of which he says is a sin. This is evident because he says one must perform penance for it, and he speaks of mental simony through which something was acquired: because he says that one is not obliged to renounce the benefice <note>An ecclesiastical office that provides an income.</note> acquired through it. And besides this, which is a necessary conclusion, who among those who have a due estimation of the wisdom of that sovereign doctor would dare to say that he raised a doubt, providing an argument and its solution separately, regarding something that neither the learned nor the unlearned ever doubted? Namely, whether merely wanting to buy a benefice without actually buying it, or giving or taking anything for it, obligated one to restitution?

<margin>14</margin>
14 ¶ Nor does it hinder † what Soto says—that if he had felt that way about it, he would have cited this final chapter, since it was already written in his time. For in a thousand places Saint Thomas determines many things already determined by express canons without citing them, following the custom of the master theologians, even though he often does cite them with great reverence and little of the boldness found in the "Glosses of Orleans," <note>Referring to legal commentaries from the University of Orleans, which Azpilcueta suggests sometimes distorted the original meaning of texts.</note> which destroy the text. And so, the same Cajetan <note>Thomas Cajetan (1469–1534), a major commentator on Aquinas.</note> says there that the said Holy Doctor gathered his answer from this chapter. ¶ Among them are also Hostiensis <note>Henricus de Segusio (c. 1200–1271), a preeminent canonist.</note> and Johannes Andreae, regardless of what Sylvester <note>Sylvester Mazzolini da Prierio (1456–1523).</note> might say. I cite these classic doctors because some say they feel otherwise. As for the rest of the *great multitude that no one can number, I shall keep silent.* <note>original: "turba multa quam dinumerare nemo potest, callola." ¶ The third reason that moves us to this conclusion is that the cause which has led some to depart from this common opinion, and from the declaration of this text with glosses that confuse it, is the inability to find a sufficient reason why mental usury obliges restitution and mental simony does not. For Panormitanus <note>Nicola de' Tudeschi (1386–1445), a famous canon lawyer.</note> shows that the reasons given before him were not sufficient; and he implies the same regarding the others that he himself gives, and with reason: because the last of the three reasons he gives—which seems to be the one he considers best and which no one reproaches—is itself worthy of reproach. For he says that this chapter is understood to refer to one who did not have a primary intention, but only a secondary one, of giving or taking something temporal for something spiritual. And it cannot be understood this way, because this text speaks of the simoniac who sins and offends by conceiving the simony of which it speaks, as proven by that word *offenders*, <note>original: "delinquentibus"</note> and that phrase *to satisfy through penance*. <note>original: "satisfacere per pœnitentiam"</note> And the simoniac of whom Panormitanus speaks does not sin according to his own feeling, and rightly so (for the reasons we prove elsewhere) <margin>a In the Commentary on chapter 1, question 14, question 3, number 19.</margin> since it is not a sin to lend primarily for something other than gain.

¶ This reason, however, which has moved someone to hold against this common conclusion, <margin>15</margin> 15 should move no one to it. † Because we must submit our understandings to the declaration of the Pope, believing with humility that even if we do not grasp <margin>b Argument from the chapter *Ego solis*, distinction 9, where it says: "or I will not doubt that I have understood very little."</margin> the reason for what he declares, it would not be lacking to him. Just as it is to be believed that it was not lacking to the most learned Gregory IX and his wise men. For this reason, Johannes de Anania said here that the sufficient reason for this declaration should be asked of the one who made it. And because he seems to want to know *more than is appropriate*, <note>original: "plusquam oportet"</note> he who wishes to twist the text, as if it were a Lesbian rule, <term>a leaden mason's rule that could be bent to fit any shape; metaphorically, a standard that is altered to suit one's desires</term> so that it says what seems right to him, because what the text says does not seem right to him. Furthermore, a sufficient reason for it will be given shortly. ¶ The fourth thing that should move us to this is that this text cannot be understood in the way that John Major <margin>c In book 4, distinction 25, question 7.</margin> understood it, boasting that when there is doubt, he cares nothing for the glosses or the doctors. And so, laughing at Johannes Andreae and Panormitanus, he says they could not bring their understanding to port because the contrary wind had risen against them. And for this reason, he holds that this chapter has no place except in the first of the two mental simonies mentioned above, by which nothing is taken, even if one wishes to take it. And those words *They are acquired on both sides*, <note>original: "Vtrinque acquiruntur"</note> placed in the text, which clearly contradicted him, he explains as: *that is, what a man wished to have through simony.* <note>original: "id est, Quod homo habere vellet symoniacè."</note> And I marvel that he destroys the text as much as

<summary>Azpilcueta defends the traditional legal view that internal "mental simony" (intent without an overt act) does not require the restitution of a church office, refuting the interpretations of Domingo de Soto and John Major. He argues that the Pope’s legal declarations must be obeyed even when the underlying theological rationale seems difficult to distinguish from that of usury.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: Azpilcueta continues his critique of contemporary theologians like Domingo de Soto and Hadrian VI, arguing that their interpretation of Pope Gregory IX's Decretals regarding "mental simony" is grammatically and logically flawed.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>
# Commentary on the Resolution of Mental Simony

<margin>16</margin> regarding the words, and as for the meaning, it argues that the Pope is ignorant, wordy, and empty,† though he was the most learned, and the most concise and substantial in his rulings of all the Popes whose decrees are recorded in the Decretals <note>The *Decretals* of Gregory IX (1234) are a fundamental collection of canon law.</note>. Indeed, that exposition implies that the Pope did not understand the difference between acquiring something and merely wanting to acquire it. It implies that Pope Gregory IX doubted, or believed that the learned doubted, what no canon law student of three years' standing doubts: namely, whether the mere will to steal or take wrongly without actually taking it obliges one to make restitution <note>The legal and moral obligation to return stolen goods or pay compensation.</note>. It implies that he adds superfluous laws, for whoever says that Gregory IX determined with such purpose and so many words that the will to sell or buy a spiritual thing for a temporal one—without actually buying, selling, giving, or taking anything—does not oblige restitution, must say that the Pope either doubted it or believed it was much doubted. Because of these and other similar interpretations that some have given and continue to give to the texts, we have the law in such a state of confusion. We must consider that there are not just one or two, but many motives against any legal decision made to clarify doubts, compared to other better ones found for that purpose, and the legislator held them as such; for this reason, we must not depart from what the text plainly says because of some appearances that present themselves to us, without thoroughly searching out the contrary views.

<margin>17</margin> ¶ The fifth point† that moves us to defend the said conclusion is that this text cannot be understood in the way that Hadrian <margin>a Quodlibet 9.</margin> <note>Hadrian VI (1459–1523), the only Dutch Pope and a former professor at Leuven.</note> felt, whom Soto follows <margin>b Where cited above.</margin>, without manifest violence and corruption of its context, and without clearly seeing that they force and twist it to say what it does not say. For one thing, to make the text say what they want, they command the removal of the red mark <note>The pilcrow or paragraph symbol (¶), often printed in red in early law books to denote a new section.</note> that signifies a paragraph and division, which is placed before those words, "And to, etc." <note>original Latin: *Et ad, &c.*</note> without authority or any example from a book or author among the many who have written on it. Thus, they tacitly command the changing of the capital or large *E* that has always been placed in the aforementioned conjunction "And" <note>original Latin: *Et*</note> into a small *e*, against what has always been used since Gregory IX, without citing any example for it, as we see it changed recently in a Paris edition. For another, they want the verb "To be extended" <note>original Latin: *Extendi*</note>, which is placed in the first clause, to extend to the following one, contrary to all use and custom. This cannot be done without a solecism <note>A grammatical error or an absurdity in language.</note>, for good phrasing and the manner of Latin speech do not well tolerate that "And" <note>original Latin: *Et*</note> after that "Even" <note>original Latin: *Etiam*</note> which precedes it, nor that the verb "To be extended" <note>original Latin: *Extendi*</note> be placed between those two conjunctions. For it is clear that according to good phrasing and style, it should be placed before or after both of them; and no one can deny that the phrasing and eloquence of the Decretals of Gregory IX are very orderly, and that they were composed with the greatest vigilance <note>original Latin: *cum summa vigilancia*</note> and very substantial brevity.

<margin>18</margin> ¶ Furthermore, † because according to their way of understanding, those words "In which case" <note>original Latin: *In quo casu*</note> mean "in the case that the commissary and delegate for granting dispensations should dispense with them," which is an absurd thing; no learned person of clear judgment would have left such a redundant circumlocution <note>The use of many words where fewer would do.</note>. And because according to that ridiculous, superfluous, and non-substantial supplement, his decision would be against the style of all the Decretals of Gregory IX. For it would mean that those who commit mental simony, for whom someone with sufficient power from the Pope granted a <term>dispensation</term> <note>A formal exemption from the requirements of church law.</note>, would not be obliged to renounce their monastic status or the rights they obtained by being in the monasteries through that mental simony. It seems clear that such a decision would be ridiculous, wordy, superfluous, and lacking substance; for it is not obscure that no one ever doubted whether monks who had entered monasteries through mental simony could remain in them after someone with sufficient power from the Pope had granted them a dispensation for it. Indeed, it was never even doubted whether monks who had entered through conventional and actual simony <note>Simony involving an explicit external agreement or payment.</note> could remain in them after such a dispensation. Furthermore, because it is clear that the Pope intended to say there that mental simony does not incur as many penalties or obligations as conventional and actual simony, yet according to their understanding, all must be measured with the same yardstick. Finally, because it is repugnant to the text where it says that in the case of which it speaks, it is enough that through penance alone he satisfies his creator; for it says: "it is sufficient for the offender to satisfy his creator through penance alone" <note>original Latin: *sufficit delinquentis se per solam pœnitentiam suo satisfacere creatori.*</note>. And according to this [other] understanding, it is not enough; rather, it is necessary for a dispensation to intervene from someone who had the power for it; and consequently, beyond penance, dispensation and rehabilitation are required.

<meta>catchword: Lo otro †</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that the interpretations of Hadrian and Soto regarding mental simony are grammatically incorrect and logically redundant, as they ignore the intended brevity and legal clarity of Pope Gregory IX's Decretals.</summary>

-> # On the final chapter concerning Simony. <-

<margin>
a Chapter 23, number 104.
b Number 105.
e Decision 5 on penance, distinction, question 12. Rule on three-year possession.
</margin>

19 ¶ Another reason † is because, according to this interpretation, the text would imply that dispensation <note>An official exemption from a church law.</note> and penance would not suffice for one who commits "conventional simony" <term>conventional simony: an explicit agreement or contract to trade money or favors for a church office</term>. This is entirely false, according to the opinion of all authorities. Furthermore, dispensation is not only unnecessary in cases of "mental simony" <term>mental simony: the hidden intention to pay for a spiritual office without an explicit contract</term> to retain the benefit obtained through it, but it is not even necessary for conventional simony if the act was not fully completed by both parties. We stated this in the *Manual* ^a and in chapter 23, number 104, ^b following Cassiodorus, ^c and Gomez, ^d and we shall discuss it further below. ^e 105. Another reason is that, according to this interpretation, one would have to say there was some prior doubt ^b e (when this chapter was written) as to whether a person with the Pope’s authority to grant dispensations to monks—allowing those who entered monasteries through gifts to remain there—could also dispense those who entered through mental simony. It is laughable to suggest such a thing, as there is no doubt, nor was there ever in law, that he could dispense even those monks who committed conventional and actual simony. Finally, beyond all this (a third of which would be more than enough to reject this interpretation), Pope Hadrian—and those who followed him—failed to see that under his interpretation, it is impossible to provide a tolerable construction for those final words of the text: *Eos pro symonia huiusmodi non teneri* <note>original: "Eos pro symonia huiusmodi non teneri," meaning "They are not held liable for simony of this kind."</note>, as anyone who tries to arrange the sentence will see.

<margin>
e In the Second Part of the Second Part, question 100, article 6, reply 6.
f Chapter *Cogitationis*, on penance, distinction 1, where we spoke most extensively and from the root.
a In the chapter *Venerabilibus*, final paragraph, with notes on it; regarding sentences of excommunication, book 6.
</margin>

20 ¶ The sixth point † that moves us to hold the common conclusion is the reasoning of Saint Thomas Aquinas ^e. He clearly feels that restoring what is acquired through simony is an ecclesiastical penalty; and the Church cannot impose a penalty for "mere bad will" ^f nor, ^f consequently, for mental simony <note>Azpilcueta cites the legal maxim *cogitationis poenam nemo patitur*: "No one suffers punishment for their thoughts."</note>. It is no use arguing that this mental simony mentioned in this chapter is not one of those mortal sins that stays hidden within the will, but rather one that "sprouts" and emerges through an action, even if the bad intent is not expressed. I say this argument does not stand because, just as the Church cannot punish a purely internal bad act, neither can it punish an external act that is only "bad" because of its relationship to a disordered internal will. This was suggested by Pope Boniface VIII ^a and expressed by certain Parisian scholars ^b <note>Likely referring to the faculty at the University of Paris, known for rigorous moral analysis.</note>. Such is the nature of mental simony. For this reason, we stated many years ago that this is a unique text for supporting the determination of those aforementioned Parisians.

<margin>
b Jacques Almain, *On the Authority of the Church*, chapter 3.
c *Cùm ministros*, 23, question 5.
d In the *Manual*, chapter 16, number 15, which was previously stated by Cajetan, Part 2 of the 2nd Part, question 40, article 1.
</margin>

21 ¶ The seventh point † that moves us to this is that, just as there is "mental simony" which is only evil because of a hidden internal intention in the soul, so too is there "mental homicide." This is only evil because it is done with a bad intention. An example ^b is an executioner who kills a man legally sentenced to death, but does so out of private hatred and revenge. Another example is a soldier who, out of hatred, kills an enemy in a just war. It is certain that the executioner is not obliged to return the clothes or other gains he received for the execution (even though he performed it with a bad heart); nor must the soldier return the weapons, horse, and property he gained by killing the enemy (even though he fought with a bad heart), as we have stated elsewhere ^d. The same can be found in all sins. Thus, the same must be said not only of mental simony, but of all other mental sins. For if I gave you a hundred ducats to go to a just war and, following the laws of war, you were to kill a certain person who is among the enemies, and I do this with a hidden evil intention for private revenge or to inherit from him, but I do not tell you this; and if you kill him also with a hidden bad intention of revenge or inheritance, you would not be liable to any penalty in the "external forum" <note>The public legal system and church courts.</note>, nor would you be required to return the hundred ducats in the "internal forum" <note>The forum of conscience and one's relationship with God.</note>. Nor would I be required to give up the inheritance I received, even though we both sinned most gravely with our internal bad wills. This is because that homicide is not evil except in relation to the bad, hidden intentions and wills that we conceived internally. For the same reason, if I serve you solely to be paid with a church office (a "benefit"), yet I tell you I wish to serve you without any salary, and you give me the office solely as payment for my service while saying you give it because I deserve it—without either of us expressing these disordered internal wills—we both sin mortally. However, in conscience, you are not obligated to pay me for my service, nor am I obligated to give up the office.

<margin>
e In chapter 3, *On Usury*.
</margin>

22 ¶ The eighth point † that should move us to hold this common opinion is that a sufficient reason can be given as to why Pope Urban III ^e declared that "mental usury" <term>mental usury: the hidden expectation of interest or profit on a loan where no interest was explicitly agreed upon</term> requires the restitution of what is gained through it, while Pope Gregory IX declared the opposite regarding mental simony; because...

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that "mental simony"—hidden intent without a formal contract—does not legally require the forfeit of a church office, comparing it to an executioner who performs a legal act with an evil heart. He maintains that while such intent is a mortal sin before God, the Church cannot impose legal penalties or demand restitution for purely internal thoughts.</summary>

<page-num>76</page-num>

<header>Resolutory Commentary on Simony.</header>

<margin>
b. Cajetan, *Summa*, 2nd part of the 2nd part, question 100, article 6.
c. In chapter 23, number 103.
d. Book 9, question 8, article 1, *On Justice and Law*.
</margin>
mental: for the reasoning of Cajetan <note>Thomas Cajetan (1469–1534), a major Italian theologian and commentator on Thomas Aquinas.</note> seems quite sufficient, which we referenced more briefly in the *Manual*—namely, that what is given as usury <term>usury: the practice of charging interest on a loan, which was legally and morally prohibited at this time</term> is given involuntarily; whereas what is paid for simony is given voluntarily, like one who buys or sells. It is true that the aforementioned Domingo de Soto <note>A prominent Spanish Dominican theologian and contemporary of the author.</note> rejects this reason, saying that the one who pays usury and the one who gives something for a [church] benefit are in equal positions. He argues that just as the former would rather have his money lent freely, the latter would rather have the benefit without giving anything. And just as the former gives more than what he received in the loan because they will not lend to him for free, so the latter gives something so that they will grant him the benefit, which he believes they would not give him for nothing. However, this reasoning is not conclusive against Cajetan’s, because there is a very great difference between the two. For he who gives or takes by way of simony consents through a consent of buying and selling, or exchange; this is enough so that one is not obliged to make restitution, even though the buyer would prefer to be given the item for free without paying a price, and the seller would prefer to be given the price without giving his merchandise. But he who gives or takes by usury, gives or takes by a consent that is involuntary and forced, which is not enough to release the one who takes it from the obligation of restitution.

<margin>
e. Chapter 17, number 32.
f. 2nd part, title 2, chapter 5, at the beginning.
g. Under the word: *Restitutio* [Restitution], 2.
h. Under the word: *Restitutio*, 6. *Turpe* [Shameful].
i. Under the word: *Restitutio*, 2, 6, 2 and the word *Eleemosyna* [Alms], section 4.
</margin>
¶ The ninth reason <insert>9</insert> that moves us to the same conclusion is that opinion which we held and proved by many reasons in the *Manual*, following St. Antoninus, Monaldo, Angelo, and Sylvester: <note>These are standard "summas" or legal reference works used by confessors in the 16th century.</note> namely, that no one is bound by precept to restore what is voluntarily given and taken for a bad purpose <note>original: "mal," referring to a sinful transaction.</note>—in such a way that both parties voluntarily commit a shameful act—unless a specific law expressly commands restitution. From this opinion, it follows that the simoniac would not be obliged to restore what he takes from one who gives it voluntarily, unless there were a specific law commanding it. And it is well known that although there is a law <margin>k. Chapter *De hoc*, on simony.</margin> that commands the "conventional and real" simoniac <term>conventional simony: a simony involving an explicit agreement or exchange of money for a spiritual office</term> to return what he took for it, there is no law that commands this of the "mental" simoniac. On the contrary, there is the express law of this chapter, which declares he is not obliged to do so.

<margin>
l. Chapter *Debitores*, on oaths.
a. Chapter 17, number 37, through the chapter *Non sanè*, 14th question, 5.
b. In chapter 17, number 32.
</margin>
¶ The tenth reason <insert>10</insert> that moves us to the stated conclusion is that another sufficient reason can also be given <insert>24</insert> as to why mental simony does not oblige one to restore, while mental usury does. Namely, that usury is among those things that are "given well and taken badly": because it is not a sin to give or pay usury, but it is a sin to take it. Therefore, by natural and divine law, it must be returned to the one who gives and pays it, according to that rule we placed in the *Manual*: namely, that whoever takes something badly from someone who does not give it badly is obliged to restore it. However, that which is taken through mental simony is "taken well from one who gives it badly," because the receiver does not know it is being given for that reason; or at the very least, it is "taken badly from one who gives it badly," and for this reason, the receiver is not obliged to return it to anyone—at least if he performed the task for which it was given. This follows the rule we placed in said *Manual*: that he who takes something (well or badly) from one who gives it badly is not bound by precept to restore it; at least if he did that for which it was given, and when there is no special law commanding such a thing. And in this case of mental simony, there is no law that specifically commands it, as was said in the preceding argument. We gave this reason for the diversity of the two cases long ago in this chapter.

¶ The eleventh reason <insert>11</insert> that moves us is that it could be said that he who takes by mental simony alone <insert>25</insert>—although he takes badly in terms of the effect of sinning and offending the Divine Majesty—does not take badly in terms of being obliged to restore it. For the purpose of someone being bound to restore, it is not enough that they sin in taking it, considering only the bad intention of the taker. Rather, it is necessary that he take it badly considering the justice of the external acts of giving and taking, or the lack of consent. For example: If you return to me the sword that I lent you, and I take it with the bad intention of killing you with it, I am not obliged to restore it to you <note>Since the legal ownership and the physical act of returning were correct, despite the murderous intent.</note>. If I buy something from you on a feast day, neglecting to hear Mass to make that purchase, or with a desire to deceive you significantly without actually deceiving you: I sin, but I am not bound to restore it to you. If I despoil you with bad intention in a just war, according to the laws of war: I sin, but I am not bound to restore anything to you. Thus, by the same reasoning, if I take something from you that you give me voluntarily, without any injustice in the external act of the gift or the manner of giving, nor any defect of consent in the will for it to be acquired by me: I shall not be bound to restore anything to you, even though in your hidden internal intention or mine there may be some evil end of vengeance, hatred, fornication, adultery, simony, or the like. For restitution is an act of commutative justice <term>commutative justice: justice concerned with the fairness of exchange between individuals, requiring that things of equal value be swapped</term>, and the obligation for it arises solely from <margin>justice</margin>

<meta>catchword: justice</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that "mental simony" (having the secret intent to pay for a church office) does not require restitution of funds because, unlike usury, the act involves voluntary consent and lacks a specific law mandating repayment. He emphasizes that while a bad internal intention is a sin against God, restitution is only required when external commutative justice is violated.</summary>

<keywords>simony, mental simony, usury, restitution, commutative justice, Thomas Cajetan, Domingo de Soto, canon law, voluntary consent, intent vs action</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>Continuity: Azpilcueta continues his argument that while "mental usury" (the internal intent to profit from a loan) requires restitution, "mental simony" (the internal intent to buy or sell a sacred office) does not necessarily require the return of the office or benefit obtained, provided no formal contract was made.</meta>

<header>On the final chapter of Simony.</header>

<margin>c In chapter 17, number 6, following Thomas Aquinas, *Summa Theologica*, 2nd part of the 2nd part, question 61, article 1, and question 92, article 1.

e 2nd part of the 2nd part, question 58, articles 2, 7, 8, 9, and 10.</margin>
...real justice arises, as we stated in the *Manual* <note>original: *Manual de confesores y penitentes*, Azpilcueta's most famous and influential work for confessors</note>. ^c And justice or injustice does not consist in reconciling internal passions, but in reconciling external things and actions through which exchange occurs between different persons, as Aristotle says, ^d and as Saint Thomas <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the preeminent scholastic philosopher and theologian</note> ^e explains, whose view is commonly accepted.

<margin>a In the repetition of chapter 2, 14, question 3, number 13.
b Therefore, it is to be understood generally. Law: *De pretio*. Digest, *on public actions in rem*. Chapter: *Si Romanorum*, distinction 19.
