26 ¶ Let us conclude † therefore, that our third conclusion is true: namely, that **mental simony** <term>mental simony: the secret or internal intent to exchange a spiritual good for a temporal one, without an explicit agreement or contract</term> does not oblige one to restore what was acquired through it, whether it be spiritual or temporal. This is so even though mental usury obliges the restitution of what was taken through it, as we have stated more extensively elsewhere. ^a And we add that this conclusion must be understood to apply not only to mental simony forbidden by human law alone, but even to that forbidden by natural and divine law. This is because this text speaks generally without making any distinction. ^b And because it deals with simoniacs who gave something temporal for a spiritual state of religion, which is a simony forbidden by divine law. 

¶ This also extends to both "forums"—that of the conscience [confessional] and the judicial [legal]—regardless of what [Bartolomé de] Medina <note>A Spanish Dominican theologian (1527–1581) known for his work on moral theology</note> might say. ^c I do not know how he failed to weigh those words: "In which case it is sufficient for the offenders to satisfy their Creator through penance alone" <note>original: *In quo casu delinquentibus sufficit per solam pœnitentiam suo satisfacere creatori*</note>. By these words, it is clearly signified that he who commits mental simony satisfies God through penance alone, without restoring anything of what he gained or acquired through it. ¶ It also extends to cases where not only one party commits mental simony while the other does not, but even to those where both had corrupt intentions, regardless of what some may say. For the text speaks clearly of what both parties acquired in these words: "They are acquired in such a way on both sides" <note>original: *Vtrinque taliter acquiruntur*</note>. Nor should any heed be paid to the exposition of John Major <note>Ioannes Maior (1467–1550), a Scottish philosopher and theologian</note>, ^d which is ridiculous; but rather, great heed should be paid to the example given by Innocent IV <note>Pope Innocent IV (1243–1254), a major figure in the development of Canon Law</note> regarding one who serves in hopes of a [church] benefit, and it is given to him because of having served.

<margin>e Argument from the chapter *Si Romanorum*, distinction 19.
f For there are three things in this matter: Chapter *Sunt nonnulli*, 1, question 1.</margin>
27 ¶ It also extends † not only to the person who gained something spiritual through mental simony without giving anything temporal, and to the one who gained something temporal without giving anything spiritual, but even to the one who gained one by giving the other, regardless of what the Lord [Dominic] Soto <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a major figure in the School of Salamanca</note> feels. For it clearly says that those who have committed mental simony are not obliged to relinquish the spiritual or temporal things that were gained on either side through mental simony. ^e And also, because it does not distinguish between temporal things, it should be understood generally ^e as applying to every temporal gift, whether it be of the tongue [praise], of service, or of the hands [money or physical goods]. ^f Thus, it is to be understood in all cases where one party through mental simony acquires something spiritual and gives something temporal, or vice-versa, acquires something temporal for something spiritual. And so Innocent provided the example of one who served someone for a benefit, and the person gave it to him because he had served.

<margin>a In the *Summa*, under the word *Symonia*, and the 2nd part of the 2nd part, question 100, article 6, reply to objection 6.

d *Extravagantes* 2, on simony.

28 ¶ It also extends † to mental and **conventional simony** <term>conventional simony: simony involving an explicit agreement or contract, even if not yet fulfilled</term> that has not yet reached the point of giving or taking on either side, according to almost everyone. And even to mental and conventional simony that has reached the point of giving a temporal thing from one side, but not the giving of the spiritual thing from the other, according to Cajetan ^a <note>Thomas Cajetan (1469–1534), an Italian cardinal and theologian</note> whom Soto follows. ^b It does not, however, extend (according to them) to mental and conventional simony that reached the point of giving and taking the spiritual thing, even if it had not reached the point of giving and taking the promised temporal thing. Rather, the said Doctor Soto adds that those who said the opposite have been deceived. For he says that Saint Thomas holds it to be simony to give a benefit for future services, and because selling on credit is still selling. But (in our opinion) there was no deception in this; for rather, he is deceived who thinks that any of those most learned men ^c (whom he does not cite) thought that it is not simony to give a benefit for the promise of a temporal thing, even if it were never paid, or that giving a benefit at a credit price is not simony. Rather, they only say that the penalties of Canon Law which are incurred *ipso iure* <note>By the law itself; automatically</note>—which are the nullity of the collation [appointment] and excommunication ^d—are not incurred by simony that is not completed and carried out by both sides, which is a very different thing. In favor of this opinion is the fact that, as they testify, this is how the ancient style of the Roman Court <note>The Curia, the administrative apparatus of the Holy See</note> maintains and interprets it, which constitutes law ^e; the knowledge of which we learned from them and have valued greatly, as we stated long ago elsewhere. ^f

<margin>f in the chapter *Si quando*, on rescripts, page 11.</margin>
29 ¶ It also follows † that, as the most grave Soto confesses, selling and immediately delivering a benefit for a credit price is mental and conventional simony completed by one side; so too must he confess that giving money and paying immediately for a benefit promised on credit for when it should become vacant, is mental and conventional...

<meta>catchword: conventional</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that "mental simony" (intent without contract) does not require the restitution of acquired benefits in either civil or religious law. He refutes contemporary theologians like Soto and Medina, distinguishing between internal sin and the specific legal penalties of Canon Law.</summary>

...the agreement being fulfilled by one party. And since he <note>Referring to Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a Spanish Dominican priest and scholastic theologian</note> denies that penalties are incurred for this until the benefit <term>benefit: an ecclesiastical office, such as a parish or a prebend, which provided an income (a "benefice")</term> is delivered, it follows that to say the opposite regarding the other case, it is not a sufficient reason to say that Saint Thomas says that it is simony to give a benefit for future service, nor to say that it is a sale to sell at a credit price <note>original: *precio fiado*; selling something now to be paid for later</note>. For Saint Thomas would also say that it is simony to give and take services for a future benefit, and that it is a purchase to buy and pay immediately for a benefit granted on credit. And even the same Soto must confess that it is a purchase to pay immediately for merchandise that perhaps has not yet arrived, or has not even been born <note>A reference to "future goods" in contract law, such as an unborn calf or an unharvested crop</note>. Yet he still denies that whoever buys, paying immediately for the benefit that is to be given later, incurs the said penalties. It helps this argument that the same Doctor Soto confesses—and rightly so—that the nullity of the transfer of the Lordship <note>original: *Señorio*; the legal right or ownership</note> of the benefit conferred by simony is not induced by natural or divine law, but by human ecclesiastical law. And the same must be said of the transfer of the lordship of the price given for it (whatever he may say) according to <margin>a In this chapter, etc. On this, of simony.

It also matters, as few have considered, that the *Extravagante* <term>*Extravagante*: a term for Papal decrees that circulated outside the major official collections like the *Decretum*</term> which induces these penalties does not induce them against all those guilty of simony, but only against those who commit simony regarding orders or benefits; and even then, not against all of them, but only against those who commit it by giving or taking, such that it has no place except in simony that reaches the point of giving or taking. And they cannot say that it is enough to have giving on one side only, because both confess that it is not enough for the taking and giving of the price to occur unless the giving and taking of the benefit also occurs. And since the *Extravagante* does not extend further to the one than the other, neither will the giving and taking of the benefit suffice without the giving and taking of the price.

<margin>30</margin> ¶ Much more † than his arguments, other more profound ones stand in the way, which a great consistorial lawyer <note>An advocate who practiced in the Roman Curia</note> made in that most grave court of the Roman Rota <term>Roman Rota: the highest appellate court of the Catholic Church</term>, which the said Cassiodoro refers to and releases; and much more than all of them, there is one we noted regarding the same *Extravagante* in Coimbra <note>Azpilcueta held the chair of Canon Law at the University of Coimbra, Portugal</note> (days ago) for our students, so that they might exercise themselves in searching for the solution—which they never found, nor did I give it. It is necessary to know that the collation <term>collation: the formal act of appointing a person to a church office</term> of the benefit must be pure and without condition, and it cannot be suspended; rather, it is either valid or null from the start <margin>d Argument from chapter 2, *on elections*, and extensively by those handed down by Felinus in the chapter *Constitutum*, *on rescripts*.</margin> <sup>d</sup>. And it seems they cannot deny that if today a benefit were given for one hundred ducats to be paid a year from now, and they were later paid, the collation of the benefit would have to be judged null from the date of the grant. Consequently, it seems they are obliged to say that it was null from the start, and that he who receives it remains excommunicated from the start, etc. Upon much reflection, however, one can respond that since the suspension of the collation of the benefit and the annulment of its title all depend on human law—which can order concerning them whatever is most convenient for the commonwealth—the Pope and his style <note>original: *estilo*; the customary legal procedure or practice of the Roman courts</note> can therefore introduce that the annulment of the collation *ipso iure* <note>original: *ipso iure*; by the law itself, or automatically</note> and the censures do not take place until the simony is fulfilled. So much so, that if he does not declare it during the life of the <margin>g Which was extensively handed down by Castro in decision 2, *on location*.</margin> emphyteuta <note>the holder of the lease</note> who stopped paying, he cannot declare it afterward.<sup>g</sup> Nor can the successor of the <margin>h Which the same Cassia. affirms in decision 3 of the same title, *on location*.</margin> Lord during the life of the emphyteuta, if the same Lord in whose life the emphyteusis fell into default <note>original: *commisso*</note> did not declare it before he died.<sup>h</sup> ¶ It also matters †, and even more closely, that although the <margin>31</margin> law wishes that whoever does not pay the pension commanded by the bulls <note>original: *bulas*; formal Papal decrees or mandates</note> within a certain term, under penalty of losing the benefit *ipso iure* upon which the pension was placed, and the one for whom it was placed has the right of return; yet by the style of Rome and the tacit will of the Pope, he is not to be reputed deprived of it, nor the other person in possession of it, until the other party desires it and has <margin>i Which also Cassia. affirms in the said decisions 2 and 3, *on location*.</margin> it declared.<sup>i</sup> ¶ It matters, and even more closely, that although whoever does not pay the pension in the term commanded by the bulls incurs excommunication *ipso iure* by the law once the deadline has passed, the style and the will...

<meta>catchword: of the Pope</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that ecclesiastical penalties for simony (like excommunication and loss of office) do not trigger automatically upon an agreement, but only when the act is fully "fulfilled" by both parties. He highlights that the Pope and Roman legal practice have the authority to delay the legal effects of such crimes to ensure orderly administration.</summary>

<header>On the final chapter of Simony.</header>

the Pope’s law is that one should not be considered excommunicated until the other party desires it and has it declared; so much so that after his life or renunciation, it cannot be declared. <margin>c Which Celsus also uniquely says in decision 4, *on leases* <note>original: *de loca*</note></margin> Thus we can say that that *Extravagant* <note>A term for papal decrees that "wandered" outside the standard, official collections of canon law</note>, interpreted and declared by ancient style and custom, and the tacit will of the Pope, ordains that the penalty of the nullity of the title and the excommunication—which are imposed by the very act—are not incurred until the simony is consummated and finished by both parties. After that, the title is considered null and the simoniacs <note>Those who buy or sell sacred things or church offices</note> are considered excommunicated from the date the title was granted. Nor is there more difficulty in responding to certain replies that could be made against this than to those that could be made against what was said above regarding the penalty of deprivation, recourse, and excommunication incurred *by the law itself* <note>original: *ipso iure*</note> for not paying the pension of the benefice.

23 ¶ I am not unaware that it would be more easily answered by saying that the nullity of the title and the excommunication are not incurred from the date [of the agreement], but from the moment the simony is completed by both parties. However, this answer does not seem as consistent with the intention of the aforementioned style, nor with the mind of that *Extravagant*, as what was said above.

¶ It also seems to us that it would not be bad if our Lord, the most holy Pope Paul IV <note>Pope from 1555–1559, known for his rigorous and often harsh efforts at church reform and the Roman Inquisition</note>—who is said to be truly dedicated to the reformation of the church—would clarify this matter further. He might ordain that these penalties be incurred for "conventional simony" <note>Simony based on an explicit agreement or contract</note> as soon as it reaches the stage of giving or taking something spiritual. But until he declares otherwise, it is best that we hold to what fits the words of the said *Extravagant*, and what the Holy Apostolic See has tacitly, and its ancient style has expressly, declared regarding the penalties that depend on its will and law. From the incurring of these, and all others—and much more from the sins for which they are incurred—may God deliver and absolve us through the prayers of that most blessed Cardinal Bishop and Seraphic Doctor, Saint Bonaventure, whose feast the Holy Mother Church celebrates today, July 15, 1556.

# Resolutory Commentary on the necessity of defending [others] from spiritual and corporal death, regarding the Chapter *Non in inferenda*, 23, question 3, for the clarification of certain passages of the *Manual for Confessors* that some have desired.

### Question 23, Part 3. Ambrose, *On Offices*, Book 1, Chapter 36.

The law of Virtue consists not in inflicting injury, but in repelling it. For he who does not ward off an injury from a companion, if he is able, is as much at fault as the one who commits it. Whence Saint Moses <margin>a Exodus 2</margin> first began his trials of warlike fortitude here: for when he saw a Hebrew receiving an injury from an Egyptian, he defended him, so that he struck down the Egyptian and hid him in the sand. Solomon also says: <margin>b Proverbs 24</margin> Rescue him who is being led to death.

<summary>Azpilcueta concludes his legal argument on simony by appealing for papal clarification and then begins a new commentary on the moral duty to intervene against injustice and physical harm, citing St. Ambrose and biblical examples.</summary>

<page-num>80</page-num>

<header>Resolutory Commentary on the defense of one's neighbor.</header>

<margin>
a For what is opposite in the opposite, that operates as the proposed in the proposed <note>original: "Nam quod oppositum in opposito, id operatur propositū in proposito"</note>, see the Digest *on common wills* and the Decretum, case 8, question 1.
b According to that saying of Cicero to Plancus, book 10: "You have achieved all things with virtue as your leader and fortune as your companion" <note>original: "virtute duce, comite fortuna"</note>.
</margin>

The law of fortitude <term>esfuerço; here meaning the moral strength to act rightly</term> consists not in inflicting injury, but in warding it off. For he who does not ward off an injury from his companion, if he is able, is in as much vice as he who commits it. For this reason, Saint Moses began the first trials of warlike strength here: for when he saw the Hebrew receiving injury from the Egyptian, he defended him; and in such a way that he struck down the Egyptian and hid him in the sand. Solomon also says: "Deliver him who is being led to death."

### SUMMARY

<margin>
c As is clear from chapter 36, *on duties* <note>original: "de offi."</note>.
d See Aristotle, *Ethics* book 2; Augustine, *On Free Will* book 2; Thomas Aquinas, *Summa Theologica* 1-2, question 55, throughout.
e Psalm 6; Romans 7, section 1; case 15, question 1; and Psalm 102: "Who forgives all your iniquities, who heals all your diseases" <note>original: "Qui propitiatur omnibus iniquitatibus tuis..."</note>.
</margin>

This chapter, *Not in inflicting* <note>original: "Non in inferenda"</note>, has been amended in three places. Number 1.
Fortitude, effort, and greatness consist in impeding injuries; weakness consists in committing them. Number 2.
Strength is called virtue because every good habit is called a virtue. Number 2.
God cannot sin. To be able to sin is not "power"; to pride oneself on it is weakness. Number 3.

---

This chapter is originally from book 36 of *On Duties* <note>original: "libro de officijs"</note> by Saint Ambrose. Using the original version amended by Erasmus <note>Desiderius Erasmus, the famous Renaissance humanist who edited many church fathers' works</note>, we have corrected three errors found in many printings, even in the one made in Lyon—which was claimed to be highly corrected but lacked any colored rubrics. The first is at the beginning, where instead of *Not in inflicting* <note>original: "Non in inferenda"</note>, it says *Not inflicting*. The second is where, instead of *Warlike* <note>original: "Bellicæ"</note>, it has *weak* <note>original: "imbecillis"</note>. The third is where, after that word *of Fortitude* <note>original: "Fortitudinis"</note>, it has a superfluous *to repel* <note>original: "repellere"</note>.

<margin>
f Psalm 24: "Remember not the ignorances of my youth" <note>original: "Ignorācias meas ne memineris"</note>.
g See Sallust, *The Conspiracy of Catiline*: "In the greatest dignity, there is the least license."
h Chapter *final*, *on penitence*, distinction 2.
a Wherefore the Emperor Titus established that those who spoke ill of the Emperor should not be punished. Code, *If anyone speaks ill of the Emperor*.
b 1 Peter 2: "Who committed no sin, nor was deceit found in his mouth; who, when he was reviled, did not revile in return."
c Chapter *Let it be read*, case 24, question 2; and chapter *Pardon of sin*, *on royal rights*, book 6.
</margin>

One gathers from this, first in that word *virtue* <note>original: "virtutis"</note>, a conclusion most worthy of memory for any Prince or courageous man: that it is weakness, and not fortitude, to commit an injury. For since weakness and fortitude are opposites, and Saint Ambrose says here that the law of fortitude is to ward off and push it away, the law of weakness must be to commit it and bring it near. That Saint Ambrose understands fortitude by that word *virtue* is gathered both because he was an excellent Latinist and this is its proper meaning, and because he says this while specifically treating the virtue of fortitude.

Although, because some effort is put into acquiring and preserving good habits and dispositions of the soul, all of them are called "virtues"; just as all bad habits and dispositions are conversely called "weaknesses," "illnesses," and "ignorances." From this it follows how false an opinion some kings, lords, and other distinguished men hold—that it seems to them they have no power in the land where they reign, rule, or live, if they only do what is just and reasonable, unless they can also get away with what is against justice. For this reason, they seek by many ways to be considered so powerful that they succeed in everything they want, whether it be just or unjust. They want to be obeyed, served, or pleased in everything they desire, and they do not see that worth and fortitude (as Saint Ambrose says here) consist not in doing injustice, but in ensuring it is not done.

They do not consider that saying of Julius Caesar: "The greater one is, the less license one has to do evil." They do not see that the power to sin and do injustice is not "power" at all, but a lack of it, as Saint Augustine says. For this reason God, who can do all things, cannot do this. They do not see that it is greatness to forgive and forget an injury, vileness to commit it, and pettiness to avenge it. They forget what Holy Mother Church places before our eyes every day: namely, that sovereign and infinite fortitude of our Lord Jesus Christ, who never did any injury and yet suffered a hundred thousand. They do not see what no one fails to see: that they are in a state of eternal damnation, nor can they be absolved until they resolve never again to want to be obeyed, served, or pleased in any mortally injurious or unjust thing. Blessed is the voice of those who say: "God keep me from doing anyone injury, and for that which is done to me, give me good patience." Cursed is the voice of those <meta>catchword: who praise [themselves]-</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that true fortitude consists in defending others and resisting the urge to commit injustice, correcting several textual errors in St. Ambrose's "On Duties." He emphasizes that a ruler's power is measured by their adherence to justice, rather than their ability to act with impunity.</summary>
<keywords>fortitude, injury, justice, St. Ambrose, Erasmus, Moses, Julius Caesar, virtue vs weakness, power of kings</keywords>

See above, same question 1; below, same question 8; and Thomas Aquinas, *Summa Theologica* 2-2, question 108, article 1.
</margin>
...who boast: "No man ever did a thing to me that he did not pay for." If they understand this, as many do, as private vengeance, then it is a mortal sin.<gloss>d</gloss>

## S U M M A R Y.

He who does not prevent an injury sins, and is even presumed to consent to it. num. 4. Even if it is not personal. num. 5. And even if he does not actually consent, why is it so? num. 20.

A law ceases when its purpose ceases. num. 6. By which virtue does the law command the defense of another? num. 7.

The will is free; only God forces it. It can choose to want or not want anything. num. 6.

In what the virtue of fortitude <term>fortaleza; here referring to the moral strength to act rightly</term> is employed. num. 7. And better yet. num. 21. Distributive and commutative justice <term>Distributive justice concerns the fair allocation of goods in society; commutative justice concerns fairness in private exchanges and contracts</term>. The Ten Commandments are laws of justice. num. 7.

The law of charity rarely obliges, and when it does, it is to defend and to perform works of mercy. num. 8.

The Lady Joanna <note>Joanna of Austria (1535–1573), daughter of Emperor Charles V and Regent of Spain at the time of this publication.</note>, most high Princess for high reasons; may she be higher for another. num. 9.

He who consents to the sin, sins. All consent to sin is such. num. 9.

One who must defend another under penalty of sin. num. 9. & 10. Even with the loss of one's own property, etc. num. 10.

Defending one who can pay something, even if one is already obligated to do it. num. 11.

Whether defense is owed by charity, even with damage to honor and estate. What we may recover. num. 21. Whether we ought to ransom with money a condemned man who can be redeemed by it. Or, regarding scandal. Who owes defense by justice? num. 13. & 14. And why. num. 22.

He who does not provide a required defense is presumed to consent, even if he does not actually consent. num. 15. Except when he cannot do so without harm, through implications. num. 15.

He who transgresses a law seems to despise it, unless there is at least an unjust cause. num. 15. ☞

Leaving a defense without consenting—and even while consenting—differs from actively favoring the act. num. 16.

The commandments of the Decalogue <term>The Ten Commandments</term> are matters of justice, not those which are merely reduced to them. num. 16.

Restitution is not owed for failing to perform charity, but it is owed for failing to perform justice. num. 16.

Neglecting a defense while taking pleasure in the offense does not lead to a presumption of favor, nor to incurring punishment in the external forum <term>fuero exterior; the public legal system as opposed to the internal forum of the soul</term>, nor censure, nor irregularity <term>irregularidad; a canonical impediment that prevents someone from receiving or exercising Holy Orders</term>. num. 17. Unless four things concur. num. 18.

A unique understanding of the chapter *Quantae*, regarding the sentence of excommunication. num. 18.

Innocent, a great Pope and great doctor, clarified this matter which was poorly understood. num. 19.

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>Resolutory Commentary on the defense of the neighbor.</header>

<margin>
b Exodus 1.
c Proverbs ch. 2.
d above in the same, no. 2.
e chapter below in the same, cause 23, question 3.
f To the Romans 1. Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica 1-2, question 74, article 8; and 2-2, question 154, article 4.
g chapter "Negligere." 2. q. 7. chapter "Qui potest." below in the same. chapter 2. regarding heretics. chapter "Dilecto" regarding the sentence of excommunication, book 6.
h Regarding the sentence of excommunication.
i chapter "Sicut dignum" § "illi etiam qui." regarding homicide. To which Symmachus agrees § 1. 83. d. and others in other chapters of the same distinction.
k chapter "Pasce." 86. distinction.
l "To kill." Digest, regarding the recognition of children.
</margin>

His great authority sought to persuade us here; but he even went on to prove it with philosophical reason, with the example of Moses^b, and with the authority of Solomon^c. The reason is worthy of memory, as has been said^d, for all those who consider themselves courageous: namely, that the law of fortitude <term>fortaleza; the cardinal virtue of courage and moral strength</term> and effort commands it. Not content with merely saying that such a person sins, he adds these words: *He is just as much in the wrong as the one who commits the act* <note>original: "Tam est in vitio, quàm ille qui facit."</note>. By these words, he signifies that whoever does not defend another not only sins, but sins as much as the one who performs the injury. This conclusion is confirmed by our great father St. Augustine, whom St. Ambrose baptized, saying^e that whoever can prevent an injury and does not prevent it, consents to it; and all consent and favor toward sin is itself a sin^f. Pope Eleutherius and others^g said that one who does not prevent an injury not only consents to it but even encourages it. Furthermore, Innocent III^h appears to say that one incurs major excommunication <term>a severe ecclesiastical censure that excludes a person from the sacramental life of the Church</term> if the injured person he could have defended was a cleric. Alexander III states that whoever is able to defend someone from a murderer and does not, kills them^i. And St. Ambrose himself^k said that he who is able to feed someone dying of hunger and does not, kills them. St. Augustine calls him cruel who, being able, does not pull out (even by force) someone who is inside a house that is about to collapse.

<margin>
m chapter "Ipsa pietas." below. same question 4.
n Law "In servorum" Digest, regarding punishments.
o Exodus 23, Deuteronomy 22.
p chapter "Qui cum fure" regarding theft.
q Summa Theologica 2-2, question 70, article 1.
r in chapter "Inter ver." 11. question 3. numbers 7, 13, and 1 Manual, chapter 15, number 16.
s chapter 17, chapter 18 number 55.
</margin>

<margin>5</margin>
¶ All this is confirmed^† by considering that injury and damage to a person is a much greater harm than damage to property^m, and yet we are obliged to prevent damage to our neighbors' property. For Sacred Scripture^n commands that whoever encounters his neighbor’s lost ox (even if he is an enemy) must return it to him; and whoever sees a donkey fallen under its load must help it up. The glorious St. Jerome^o said that whoever knows of a theft and does not tell the owner who is looking for it, sins. And St. Thomas^p (whom we follow in other parts ¶) determines that whoever knows that some unjust harm will follow to another if he does not speak up, must arrange for it to be stopped, even if he is not pressured to do so. This is based on the words of the Apostle^t <note>St. Paul the Apostle, in his Epistle to the Romans.</note>: "They are worthy of death, not only those who sin, but even those who consent." Regarding this, the gloss <term>an interpretive commentary written in the margins of law or scripture</term> says: "To consent is to remain silent when you are able to rebuke." Finally, it follows that we are obliged to reveal the evils of person, honor, and property that are being prepared to harm the commonwealth <term>república; meaning the public good or the state</term> or any other individual, as we say in many places^a, extending this even to clerics and to those who have sworn to keep a secret, etc.

<margin>6</margin>
Against^† this conclusion, however, these doubts are offered. First, when the principal and expressed reason for a law ceases, the law itself ceases^b. The principal reason expressed in almost all the texts that establish this conclusion is that he who does not defend someone when he is able is seen to consent to and favor the injury. This reason ceases in the man who, before God, does not consent, nor wishes for the injury to be done—rather, he is saddened by it, or at least finds no pleasure in it—even though, through negligence, shame, fear, or some other cause, he does not prevent it. No one can deny that this is possible if one confesses (as one is obliged to do, under pain of heresy) the existence of free will^c, and that the will cannot be constrained except by God^d. The will is so free that whatever object the senses or the intellect propose to it, it can want it, or not want it^e—which in Latin we call *unwillingness*^f <note>original: "Nolle"</note>—or it can neither want it nor not want it, suspending its act, which the scholastics call a *purely negative not-willing* <note>original: "Non velle pure negativum"</note>. Therefore, he who does not consent, even if he does not defend, does not sin.

<margin>
t To the Romans 1. ch. 1. regarding the office of the delegate. chapter "Notum" 2. question 1. They are worthy of death, not only those who do it, but also those who consent.
</margin>

<margin>7</margin>
¶ The^† second doubt is that we should not hold ourselves obliged to that which no law obliges us^g. It does not appear that there is a law requiring this of us, because the law of fortitude obliges us^s; yet excessive fears prevent us from undertaking (or not undertaking) actions against right reason in the face of the dangers of death and other very great evils...

Digest, regarding the right of patronage. chapter "Cum cessante." regarding appeals.* & *chapter "Si enim." regarding penitence, distinction 2.* *verse: God created us with freedom of will, etc. § 1. 15. q. 1. chapter "Displicet" 23. q. 4.* d *Thomas Aquinas 1-2, question 82 & 83.* *According to Ecclesiasticus 15: God set man in the hand of his own counsel, where the gloss says: that is, in the liberty of free will.* e *Deduced from those beautiful sayings of Augustine in question 11. 5. question 1.* f *Law: "To want belongs to him who can also not want." Digest, regarding the rules of law.* g *chapter "Consulvisti." 1. q. 3. chapter 2. regarding the transfer of prelates. Law "Illam." Codex, regarding collation.*

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that failing to defend others from injury constitutes a sin of consent, citing various popes and Church Fathers. He then introduces a counter-argument based on the freedom of the human will, questioning if one can be guilty if they internally disagree with the harm but fail to act due to fear or negligence.</summary>

temporal evils $^h$. And it may be that one fails to defend the person who wants to offend him out of fear, through negligence, malice, shame, and for other causes, which are not passions that the virtue of fortitude <term>fortaleza; the cardinal virtue of courage and moral strength</term> governs. The third reason is that the law of justice does not oblige us to it either: partly because distributive justice does not deal with this, since it does not involve a common good that must be distributed to individuals $^i$: nor does commutative justice deal with it, since it does not involve the exchange of a thing from one person to another $^k$: partly because it does not appear that this is commanded by any of the precepts of the Decalogue <note>The Ten Commandments</note>: therefore, its transgression does not oblige one to make restitution, as we say in other places $^l$, following Alexander of Hales <note>A 13th-century Franciscan theologian</note>, whose view is commonly accepted $^m$.

8 ¶ The $\dagger$ fourth point is that no law of charity—to love one’s neighbor as oneself—seems to oblige us to this either. Partly because that law rarely obliges under pain of mortal sin, according to what was said in the manual $^a$: partly because we are not obliged to love our neighbors more than ourselves $^b$: for it is held as certain that we may allow ourselves to be killed by those against whom we could defend ourselves by killing them $^c$. The fifth point is that no one is obliged, under pain of mortal sin, to perform works of mercy for someone who is not in extreme necessity $^d$: and defending one's neighbor is a work of mercy, since it is done out of respect for the necessity and misery in which they find themselves: therefore, at the very least, we will not be obliged to defend our neighbors except when people seek to kill them.
This is confirmed by the example of Moses brought here, and the authority of Solomon, who speaks of the defense of the one they wish to kill. And consequently, whoever sees someone whom others wish to shove, slap, beat, or do other similar injuries to without danger of death, will not be obliged to prevent it, at least not under pain of mortal sin, which seems to contradict the aforementioned conclusion. The sixth point is that it seems we are more obliged to defend our own honor and property than that of our neighbor $^e$: and whoever defends the honor and property of a neighbor commonly loses, or puts his own at risk of being lost.

<margin>
d Chapter *Pasce fame esurientem* [Feed the one dying of hunger]. 86. distinction.
Which Thomas Aquinas understands thus: *Summa Theologica* 2-2, question 32, article 5, commonly accepted, and as we said in the *Manual*, chapter 24, numbers 3 and 4.
</margin>
9 ¶ To $\dagger$ properly resolve these contradictions and others, and to infer many everyday matters from their solutions, I would have wished for the necessary time, which the printing process, as it is catching up with me, diminishes for me, as does the sovereign command of the Princess, My Lady and Governor Doña Juana <note>Juana of Austria (1535–1573), sister of Philip II, who served as Regent of Spain. Azpilcueta suggests he is rushing to meet her deadlines.</note>, most high in many respects, through which in another way I hope to see her higher [in heaven]. From these we shall gather the answers to the six proposed doubts: from all of which we shall infer seventeen implications. The first declaration shall be that if the one who does not defend another consents to, and takes pleasure in, that injury being done, he sins, whether he is able to defend or not: and whether he is present or absent: because all consent by which one consents to sin is sin $^f$: and a sin of the same kind as that which is consented to $^f$.

<margin>
e Law *Presis*. Code, regarding services. Chapter *Si non licet*. 23. question 5.
f Through that of the Apostle: "They are worthy of death, not only those who do them, but also those who consent." To the Romans, chapter 2 [Romans 1:32].
Taught specifically by Thomas Aquinas, *Summa Theologica* 1-2, question 70, article 8, and 2-2, question 145, article 4.
</margin>
10 ¶ The second declaration $\dagger$ shall be that for a person to sin by not defending when they are able, it is necessary that they be obliged to do so. For as St. Thomas $^g$ said very well, and as Cajetan $^h$ <note>Thomas Cajetan (1469–1534), a major commentator on Aquinas</note> clarified: no one sins by not preventing something except when they are obliged to prevent it. And we add that, according to some, a person is not obliged to defend another if they cannot do so without damage to their honor, estimation, shame, or property, as resolved by Felino $^i$ <note>Felinus Sandeus (1444–1503), an Italian canon lawyer</note>, referred to by us elsewhere $^k$, whom no one contradicts, nor have we ever contradicted him from the chair <note>The professor's chair or *cathedra*</note>: But there are reasons why we might now contradict him: First, because we are obliged to run to the aid of one in extreme necessity even with the loss of all our property that is not necessary for the preservation of our own lives, if it should be needed, as St. Ambrose said elsewhere $^a$, and as we said in the *Manual* $^b$, following St. Thomas $^c$: and second, because not only does shame or some decrease in our reputation (as Felino says) not excuse us from it, but neither does the danger of losing our honor: because honor is also an external good, without which life can be sustained, and it is a lesser good than life, as we have proven at length elsewhere $^d$. Furthermore, because it can hardly be defended what Felino says, which is so approved in this matter, even regarding other goods:

<summary>Azpilcueta examines the limits of the moral obligation to defend others, arguing that while failure to intervene out of malice or consent is a sin, the obligation is tempered by the nature of the injury and the potential cost to the defender. He challenges the legal opinion of Felino Sandeus, asserting that life is a higher good than honor or property in cases of extreme necessity.</summary>

**Resolutory Commentary on the defense of the neighbor.**

...because those two authorities <note>original: "authoridades"</note> that we cited above $^f$, concerning the one who encounters his neighbor's ox wandering lost, or a donkey fallen to the ground under its load, prove that we are obliged to expend <note>original: "poner" — to put forth or contribute</note> something of our own property to prevent damage to that of our neighbor: for these things cannot be done without some loss of property, time, or hindrance to one's business.

¶ We said "expend" and † not "give": because he who does this may ask for what his labor, time, or hindrance deserves, if he so wishes. Just as he who aids someone in extreme necessity may also do so $^g$. For although the law obliges him to succor and free his neighbor from that harm, it does not oblige him to do so for free or as a gift. But he must expend it first. By this consideration, one can respond to some $^h$ who wish to prove that no one is obliged to defend another, because one can receive a reward for it $^i$, whereas no one can receive a reward for what they are already obliged to do $^k$. For the response can be that this should be understood of those who are obliged to do something freely, and not of those who are obliged to do it but not for free: like the physician, who is obliged to cure one in extreme necessity, but not for free—at least if the patient is rich $^l$. Likewise, the lawyer, the procurator, the notary, the innkeeper, and even the doctor <note>referring to a Doctor of Laws or Theology</note> are often obliged to use their offices, and can even be compelled to do so, according to what Decio <note>Filippo Decio (1454–1535), a famous Italian jurist</note> cites $^m$; but they are not obliged to use them for free, and therefore they may take money for their service $^n$. Furthermore, the reason Felino <note>Felino Sandeo (1444–1503), a canonist and bishop</note> is incorrect is that all those who are obliged by justice to defend others—such as judges and others we will specify later—are obliged to do so even with the inconvenience of their labor, property, and even person, though not recklessly, as we stated in the *Manual* $^a$.

<margin>
12
a in chapter 11, number 13, and chapter 17, number 136.
b Argument from Law 2, Digest *de iurisdictione omnium iudicum* and the chapter *Praeterea* in *de officio delegati*.
c By Exodus chapter 13 and Deuteronomy chapter 22.
d By "neighbor" said above in the preceding number, and more extensively in the *Manual*, chapter 23, number 96, and chapter 17, number 100.
e Argument from Law *Praeses*, Codex *de servitutibus*, and the chapter *Si non licet*, 23, question 5.
f In the chapter *Inter verba*, 11, question 3, numbers 116 and 217.
</margin>
¶ Let us resolve † therefore better than has been resolved until now, stating: **First**, that we can be obliged to defend our neighbor in two ways: through the precepts of charity and through those of justice. **Second**, that by the precepts of charity, we are obliged to defend the life of our neighbor if they wish to take it from him unjustly, and there is no one else who can or will defend him but us; and thus he has extreme necessity of our defense, even if we lose our property and even our honor for it, provided we do not venture our life. **Third**, the same must be said of his goods, without which he cannot preserve his life $^b$. **Fourth**, even to avoid other damages to his property, we are obliged to expend our labor and property as much as is necessary, if we can do so without scandal, when there is probably no one else who can or will free him from them $^c$. **Fifth**, we can however recover afterward what we expended for it $^d$. **Sixth**, that Felino’s statement should proceed only when the damage to the neighbor is so small that, in the judgment of a good man, it is not just for us to expend what is required to free him from it $^e$. **Seventh**, that it was not without cause that we said "of our labor and property," because we are not obliged to sacrifice our honor for his property, unless the greatness of the property and the insignificance of the honor suggest otherwise: for (as we prove elsewhere $^f$) honor is of greater value than property.

<margin>

g chapter *Non satis*, distinction 86.
h in *The Sentences* 4, distinction 15, question 3, article 6, regarding the second.
i In the *Manual*, chapter 15, number 21.
</margin>
¶ **Eighth**, neither did we say without cause "the life that they wish to take from him unjustly." For we are not obliged to ransom <term>rescatar; to pay for the release or preservation of a life</term> with our property the life of one who is justly condemned to lose it, even if the King, the law, the statute, or the sentence gives him the power to ransom it with money. And thus the aforementioned chapter of Saint Ambrose $^g$ must be newly limited. Although we know it can be replied that such a condemned man is in extreme necessity, and that having fallen into it by his own fault does not take away his privileges of necessity, and that we are obliged to help those placed in it by the most just judgments of our God. Because it is not a stretch that such a just condemnation relieves us of the necessity to ransom him, since it takes away from him the power to defend himself; and even the necessity to ransom himself, if one carefully weighs a doctrine of Soto <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a Spanish Dominican theologian and contemporary of the author</note> $^h$ cited by us elsewhere $^i$.

# On the chapter *Non in inferenda* <note>original: "Non in inferenda"</note>, Question 23, Case 3.

<margin>
a In the said three places of the Manual.
b In Book 4, on fraternal correction, column 11.
c From the argument in the chapters *Regum* and *Administratores*, Question 23, Case 5. To the Romans, chapter 13. Chapter *Ego*, title on oaths. Chapter *De forma*, Question 23, Case 5. Chapter 1, on the proper forum, in the *Usibus Feudorum* <note>The "Uses of Fiefs," a collection of feudal law</note>.
d Through the notes of Panormitanus <note>Niccolò de' Tudeschi (1386–1445), a leading canonist</note> in chapter 1, on the restitution of despoiled property, note 9. Based on the law regarding parents, Digest *de iustitia et iure* <note>On justice and law</note>. Chapter *Duo ista nomina*, Question 23, Case 4. Law 1, Code *de emendatione propinquorum* <note>On the correction of relatives</note>.
</margin>
...difference. For although those in Brazil who are ransomed through that method of purchase (not being slaves, but free people) so that they are not eaten <note>A reference to colonial reports of cannibalism used to justify the "ransom" or enslavement of indigenous peoples</note>, they can be freed by returning what was given for them; however, those who might sell themselves so they are not justly taken could not be freed by this means, if they sold themselves to be entirely slaves. This is because in their case, the reason fails which induces that equity mentioned there $^a$. Tenthly, we said "without scandal" so that through this, and through what we have said regarding honor, we may excuse (without sin) a grave man <note>An established, respectable man</note> who fails to defend a boy from being slapped, because he does not wish to run through the street after those who are doing it, which would cause scandal and the diminishment of his own honor and reputation. We also excuse the one who does not publicly respond that a preacher is not telling the truth when he falsely defames another from the pulpit; and likewise to excuse one who fails to free someone whom they are unjustly condemning $^b$, or another, so that it does not lead to the death of other innocents or a great riot of arms between those who wish to free him and those who wish to kill him. This was well-touched upon by Adrian $^b$ <note>Pope Hadrian VI</note>, saying that I would not be obliged to stop a lord from throwing himself off a cliff if I saw that his followers would kill me for detaining him, without believing the cause of my detention. Eleventhly: by the precepts of justice, kings and other superiors who have jurisdiction are obliged to defend one another and their subjects $^c$. Feudatories and their lords $^d$; parents and their children; tutors, curators, masters, tutors, priests, guards, and other similar persons are obliged to defend their children, wards, minors, servants, slaves, and parishioners $^e$, those under their protection, and others like them. All of these, by not defending one another when they are obliged to do so, not only sin against the law of charity by failing to defend the neighbors they ought to love, honor, and respect, but also against the law of justice, by not fulfilling what they owe to others by right.

<margin>
e All these, on penalties and precepts, 4. On the Decalogue, which is said to extend to them in the Manual, chapter 14, note 3.
a Because in doubt, the milder part is to be presumed. Chapter 1, on the rules of law. Law *Merito*, Digest *pro socio*.
b Celebrated gloss on chapter *Metropolitani*, Question 2, Case 7. Gloss on the word "contempt," chapter *Cum illorum*, on the sentence of excommunication.
c In the chapter *Nullus*, distinction 55.
d In the chapter *Quicunque*, distinction 81.
e Which is the mistress of things. Chapter *Quanto fit*, on elections, Book 6.
f Because regarding that which does not appear, and regarding things that do not exist, the judgment is the same. Chapter *Si omnes*, Question 6, Case 1. Law *Duo sunt* and Law *Secunda*, Question 34, on envy, Question 36.
</margin>
15 ¶ The third declaration † is that it is a possible thing, and even a daily occurrence, that one who is able does not defend another without actually consenting to the injury, as proven by the first argument formed against this text. However, it should be presumed that he consents, because all the aforementioned texts saying that he who does not defend or reprimand when able "consents" must be understood as a legal presumption of consent. This should be limited to when one can defend without any personal harm, and not otherwise. For even if one were obliged to defend even with the loss of all his property, it should not be presumed that he consents if he cannot do it without such damage. Firstly, because since he might fail to act either because the crime pleases him or simply to avoid that damage, it seems just to presume in case of doubt that he stopped defending for the latter reason and not the former $^a$. Secondly, because although he who contravenes a law unjustly without a just cause is presumed to do so out of contempt $^b$, this is not so if he had some other cause for it, even if that cause were unjust, as Dominic $^c$ <note>Dominicus de Sancto Geminiano</note> clearly explained following the Archdeacon $^d$ <note>Guido de Baysio, a famous canonist</note>. Furthermore, experience $^e$ (the mistress of things) teaches that many (especially those close to the great) fail to prevent a thousand things they are obliged to, even with the loss of property, and they do not prevent them—not because they find them pleasing, but so as not to lose the favor and goods they hope for. From this it follows singularly that although one may be obliged to defend a person in danger of losing their life injuriously, even at the cost of honor and property, he will not be presumed to consent to the injury. From this it follows again that such a person truly sins by not defending, and perhaps truly sins by consenting, but not *presumptively*. Consequently, he will not be punished in the external forum $^f$ <note>The public court of the Church or State</note> for consenting, though he will be in the internal forum <note>The court of conscience/God</note>; and more so if he truly consented than if he did not.

<margin>
g Book 3, Question 4, article 7, argument 3, on justice and law.
h As on hatred, note 1, Question 12, case 37 and following.
i Second part of the second part, article 2, Thomas Aquinas, Question 34.
</margin>
16 ¶ The fourth † declaration is that there is a great difference between simply not defending (or not defending and consenting) on one hand, and consenting and favoring on the other. Firstly, because merely not defending, and not defending and consenting without favoring, is a sin against charity or mercy and against the precept to love one's neighbor, as the Lord Master Soto <margin>g</margin> <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a major Spanish theologian of the School of Salamanca</note> rightly felt. This is proven because it is a work of hatred, envy, discord, contention, or other similar vices that are contrary to charity, or to its daughter mercy, or to its work beneficence $^h$. But consenting to and favoring the one who commits an injury is against the virtue of justice, because it is against the very precept that the injurer violates—and every injurer sins against one of the precepts of the Decalogue, which are matters of justice $^i$.

<sig>F 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: as,</meta>

<summary>This page distinguishes between the moral sin of failing to defend a neighbor (a lapse in charity) and the legal/moral sin of actively favoring an injurer (a lapse in justice). Azpilcueta also discusses legal presumptions of consent and excuses for inaction, such as the risk of personal scandal or major social upheaval.</summary>
<keywords>Brazil, slavery, ransom, defense of neighbor, legal presumption, consent, scandal, honor, Domingo de Soto, Hadrian VI, justice vs charity, internal forum vs external forum</keywords>

# Resolutory Commentary on the Defense of the Neighbor.

<margin>
i 2. Sec. q. 1. 22. art. 1. <note>original: "Secunda Secundae," referring to Thomas Aquinas's Summa Theologica, Part 2, Section 2, Question 22, Article 1.</note>
k in Manual. c. 14. n. 3. <note>Referring to Azpilcueta's own "Manual for Confessors."</note>
l in Manual. c. 11. n. 5. & 6. c. 14. n. 5.

b I cited Adrian on restitution, question 1, column 9. Where not cited, Soto says the same beautifully in Book 4, question 7, article 3, on justice and law. <note>Refers to Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a Spanish Dominican theologian.</note>
b Above in the same, numbers 7 & 8.
c In the Manual, chapter 24, number 5, and in the chapter *Inter verb.* number 9. 3. number 714. after others, especially Adrian in Book 4 on restitution, question 1, column 9. <note>Adrian refers to Pope Adrian VI, a significant theologian before his papacy.</note>
</margin>

...as Saint Thomas^i says: And to the one who says that the precept of loving one’s neighbor is also reduced to the fourth commandment^k of the Decalogue <note>The Ten Commandments; the fourth is "Honor thy father and mother," which scholastics expanded to include all social obligations.</note>, and is consequently a precept of justice: it shall be answered that it is one thing to be *of* them (which we deny^l), and another thing to be *reduced* to them (which we confess, and it does not hinder us). For almost all the other precepts of charity, and even of other virtues, are reduced to those of the Decalogue: such as the precept of loving God is reduced to the first commandment, without being one *of* them^m. Furthermore, failing to defend, or not defending and consenting without favoring, does not oblige one to the restitution of the damage that follows from not defending; however, consenting and favoring *does* so oblige. As we said in the Manual^a: For he who sins against only the precepts of charity and mercy is not obliged to restore the damage that follows from it; but he who sins against justice is, as was said above^b and as we say elsewhere^c.

<margin>
d law 1. Code *de donationibus promissis* <note>On promised donations.</note>
e noted by the Cardinal in the chapter *Quia circa.* regarding consanguinity.
f Which Panormitanus holds in chapter 1, on the restitution of despoiled property, although he says it is common. Decio in chapter 1, on the office of the delegate, and in the law *Culpa caret.* Digest *de regulis iuris* <note>On the rules of law.</note> and Taff. in the law *Ut vim.* Digest *de iustitia et iure* <note>On justice and law.</note>.
</margin>

¶ The fifth [declaration] is that † not everyone who is presumed to consent to an offense is presumed to favor the offender, at least not with the kind of favor that makes them a direct participant in the crime. First, because it seems a harsh thing to apply two special presumptions, especially to increase the severity of a crime regarding the same case^d, which would be done in this instance if both consent and favor were presumed. Secondly, because the common opinion^e holds that by civil law, one who does not defend another does not commonly commit a crime; and although they may commit a crime by Canon Law, they are not to be punished in the external forum <note>The public legal court, as opposed to the "internal forum" of conscience.</note>. And if we say that favor is presumed, we would have to say the opposite: for under both laws, those who favor a crime must be punished, according to everyone^f. Thirdly, because Innocent [III] and the commonly received opinion say that he who knows someone is plotting to kill another and does not prevent it is not "irregular" <note>A legal status in canon law that prevents a person from receiving or exercising Holy Orders.</note>; yet if it were presumed that he consented and favored it, he would be irregular, or presumed to be so, since it would be presumed that he directly participates in the crime as at least a partial cause of it. Fourthly, if we say the contrary, we must confess that all those who are able to defend but do not must be held in the external forum as transgressors, not only of the law of charity, but even of the law of justice; and consequently, they would be obliged to restore all damages that came to the offended party because of it, and they would have to be punished as those who command, advise, help, or harbor, which seems an unusual thing. Fifthly, because this interpretation of ours seems to be received throughout the world^1. Sixthly, because it is not enough for one to incur the excommunication of the canon^k merely to be pleased that a cleric is struck, if he was not struck in that person's name, nor had that person commanded it, nor given aid or advice for it, but only consented or felt pleasure out of pure malice; as is effectively proven by a saying of Boniface^l and the common sense of all, who would say you are not excommunicated even if you greatly desire that someone strike or kill a cleric in Rome or elsewhere, without expressing this to anyone, and they are killed, as happens every day.

<margin>
g in the chapter *Petrus* on custom.
h Since all those who positively help the wrongdoer are held accountable by the law. Case 17, Question 4.
t [Latin fragment regarding the presumption of rashness in laying violent hands on church rectors.]
lius sunt: Lest anyone's presumption think that only the authors of such violence are to be punished; those who act and those who consent are to be punished with equal penalty, as Catholic authority condemns.
</margin>

¶ The sixth declaration: this next conclusion must be limited when four things concur. First, the power to prevent it. Second, the obligation to do so. Third, that it can be done without harm to one's person, honor, or estate. Fourth, that the crime is manifest. That is, that it is obvious it is a crime, and that it is being committed openly, according to a decretal of Innocent III^a, which, by proving this limitation, also proves the limited conclusion. That it proves this limitation is clear, because if it did not, the limitation would be superfluous. For if one weighs it well, it brings no other benefit: because the first part does no more than signal how great a temerity it is to lay violent hands on the rectors of the church^b, which is to say nothing. The second part only contains that, so that no one foolishly thinks only the author of the violence should be punished, the Catholic authority commands that those who act and those who consent be punished with equal penalty^c, which is also not useful^d. The third part only says that it declares as "favorers" those who...

<summary>This page discusses the legal and moral nuances of failing to defend someone. Azpilcueta argues that while failing to act may be a sin against charity, it only requires legal restitution if it violates the law of justice through active favor or command.</summary>

# On the chapter *Non in inferenda*, 23rd question, 3rd distinction.
<note>original: "Non in inferenda" — A chapter in Gratian’s Decree, the foundational collection of Canon Law, which discusses the conditions under which force or defense is permissible.</note>

<margin>
e For these are his words: "We interpret those to favor offenders who, when they are able, cease to oppose a manifest crime." <note>original Latin: "Verba enim ejus sunt. Eos delinquentibus favere interpretamur, qui cum possint, manifesto facinori desinunt obviare." This is a key legal maxim from the *Liber Sextus* of the Decretals.</note>
f In the chapter *Error*, distinction 83, and the chapter *Qui potest* below the same, etc. As is worthy, paragraph *illi*, on homicide.
g In the chapter *Solet* on the sentence of excommunication, book 6.
h Chapter 1, on usury, book 6.
i Extravagant *Ad evitanda*, of which is treated in the Manual, chapter 27, number 35. <note>An "Extravagant" refers to a papal decree not originally included in the main collections of Canon Law.</note>
a Above in the same, number 15.
</margin>

19 ...those who, being able, do not oppose a manifest crime <margin>e</margin>; which would not be a very useful point, unless it meant what we have already said. For even before him, other Popes <margin>f</margin> stated that a person is not free from the <term>scruple</term><note>A feeling of doubt or hesitation with regard to the morality or propriety of a course of action.</note> and suspicion of being an accomplice to the wrongdoer if they do not oppose his manifest crime.

¶ Because that saying was obscure—as those words ("scruple" and "accomplice") could be understood in many ways—he, as a great doctor and Pope, clarified them singularly, saying: they mean that such people must be presumed and held to be <term>favorers</term><note>In a legal sense, a "favorer" is someone who encourages or permits a crime through their inaction or support.</note> of the offender. And lest anyone say that this <term>decretal</term><note>A papal decree forming part of Canon Law.</note> only speaks to the effect of incurring <term>excommunication</term><note>The harshest ecclesiastical penalty, excluding a person from the sacraments and the community of the Church.</note>: consider that although the beginning indicates the question he responded to was about excommunication (whether those who did not defend the clergy should or should not incur it), neither the response in the third part, nor the reasoning that moved the Pope in the second part, are restricted to it. We said (that proving the said limitation also proves the limited conclusion) because the said Decretal is useful in this regard: it declares as a favorer anyone who, being able, does not oppose what is a manifest crime. Consequently, it signifies that if the thing to be opposed were not a manifest crime, one would not be presumed a favorer. This is a very useful consideration for all cases in which there may be some doubt as to whether what is being done is an offense or not. Relevant to this is the established rule that when the offense is manifest, <term>absolution ad cautelam</term><note>Latin: "absolution for the sake of caution." A provisional forgiveness granted when there is doubt about whether a legal penalty was actually incurred.</note> is not given.

§ And the same applies to the manifest <term>usurer</term> <margin>h</margin>, <term>concubinary</term><note>A man cohabiting with a woman to whom he is not married.</note>, and the <term>excommunicate</term> <margin>i</margin>. However, for this limitation to apply, the four aforementioned things must concur, as is clear from the Decretal: first, the power [to act]; fourth, that the offense be manifest; second, the obligation; and third, that one can do it without harm <margin>a</margin>. This is proven because it was proven above that not only is one not presumed to favor who does not defend without obligation, or who cannot do so without harm, but further, they are not even presumed to consent.

<margin>
b above in the same, number 6.
c above in the same, number 9.
d in 2. distinction 11, number 20.
e Above, number 15.
</margin>

20 From these six declarations, the answers to the six doubts proposed above <margin>b</margin> against this second notable conclusion are gathered. To the first, we answer that the reason why one sins by not defending his neighbor is not because he consents to or delights in the offense. For, whether he defends or does not defend, whether he can or cannot defend, if he consents, he sins, as was said above <margin>c</margin>. The reason is, then, that he does not defend while being obliged to do so—sometimes by charity alone, and sometimes by both charity and justice—and sometimes even at the risk of his property and honor, and sometimes without such risk, as has been noted <margin>d</margin>. However, he sins more (all things being equal) if he consents to the act. The texts that say "he who does not defend, consents" do not mean that if he did not consent, he would not sin; rather, they mean that by failing to defend, he sins. And even when he can act and does not, it is presumed in the <term>external forum</term><note>The realm of public, ecclesiastical, or civil law, as opposed to the "internal forum" of the individual conscience.</note> that he consents and delights in the offense, as was noted in the fourth point <margin>e</margin>.

# Commentary on the Defense of the Neighbor.

<margin>21</margin>
<margin>*below at number 22.*</margin>
¶ To the second point, we say that we confess it to be just that we should not consider ourselves obligated to that which no law binds us. We deny, however, that there is no law that obliges us to defend our neighbor; for there is such a law, sometimes out of <term>charity</term><note>In this context, charity refers to the theological virtue of selfless love for others as an extension of love for God.</note> alone, and sometimes out of both charity and justice, as we shall soon <gloss>f</gloss> explain. We also deny that the law of <term>fortitude</term><note>One of the four cardinal virtues, involving courage in the face of fear and firmness in difficulties.</note> does not oblige us to this, at least indirectly, as our text states. For while we concede what is proposed in the doubt—namely, that the immediate office of the virtue of fortitude is to restrain boldness and fear so they do not make us undertake or abandon what reason commands—and that sometimes people fail to defend others out of malice rather than fear, it must also be confessed to us that defense is often abandoned out of fear of death, or some personal injury, or loss of honor or property. It is even abandoned at times out of shame, or for fear of losing the favor of men, which goes against the law of fortitude; for that virtue mandates that no fear should cause one to abandon what reason dictates.

<margin>22</margin>
<margin>*a above at the same, n. 14.*</margin>
¶ To the third point, we respond that the law of <term>commutative justice</term><note>Justice that governs the interactions and exchanges between individuals, ensuring that each receives their due according to a contractual or social obligation.</note> obliges many people, many times, to defend their neighbor. For as we have said <gloss>a</gloss>, Kings, prelates, judges, and others listed there are given a certain amount of honor, power, authority, income, stipend, or daily wage for their offices, among which is the duty to defend their subjects and those in their charge in peace, health, justice, and tranquility. The law gives a power, authority, and right to the father, the lord, the tutor, the curator, the priest, and other guardians—certain rights and powers over children, slaves, wards, minors, parishioners, and others entrusted to them—and thus it obliges them to their defense, as was stated above <gloss>b</gloss>.
<margin>b *above at the same, n. 14.*</margin>
<margin>c *above at the same, nu. 10. & 11.*</margin>

<margin>23</margin>
<margin>*d ch. 14. num. 8. e in ch. 14. nu. 8. f By the authority of Jerome in the chapter "No est nostrum." 23. q. 5. & Thomas [Aquinas] book 1. on the governance of princes, ch. 6. g ch. "Ad apostolicam." regarding rules. l. "Si quis in coscribé. do." Code on pacts. h law "Sed & si pater." i law "si." Digest on the Macedonians. ch. "Si diligenti." on the competent forum.*</margin>
¶ To the fourth doubt, we respond: First, that as already stated in the two preceding responses, the law of charity, which commands us to love our neighbor, obliges us to defend them as much and in the manner already described <gloss>c</gloss>. Second, although in few cases (as we said in the *Manual* <gloss>d</gloss>) are we obliged to love our neighbor with that sovereign love of charity, we are indeed obliged in the aforementioned cases to love them—at least with enough natural love to suffice for performing the defense mentioned above—or at least to do it without that love in order to avoid the <term>sin of omission</term><note>A sin committed by failing to do something that one is morally or legally required to do.</note>. This is a special note, worthy of being added to the general doctrine placed in the said *Manual* <gloss>e</gloss>. Third, we confess that we are more obligated to ourselves than to our neighbors, and that we are not commonly obliged to defend ourselves by killing the person who wants to kill us, as is proven in the doubt <gloss>f</gloss>. However, it does not follow from this that we are not obliged to defend a neighbor who desires our defense; for we cannot consent to another's injury <gloss>h</gloss> without their consent in the same way we might tolerate our own injury <gloss>g</gloss>.

<margin>24</margin>
¶ From this, it could be inferred that if the victim were to say they do not want us to defend them by the death of the attacker—and we saw that they said this with good intention, so that the other person would not die in sin—we would not be obliged to do so. Fourthly, we did not say idly that we are "commonly" not obliged to defend ourselves by killing the one who wants to kill us; for sometimes someone may be so obliged. We said this, and even wrote it long ago, while serving as the <term>cathedratico</term><note>A senior professor holding a primary chair (Cátedra) at a university.</note> of the Decree in this most celebrated University of Salamanca, in the presence of our Lord Emperor Charles V, ever August, on the day when, through his sovereign humanity, he was pleased to hear some of its professors. This occurred while we were lecturing on the chapter *Charitas est vt mihi videtur* <gloss>a</gloss> <note>original Latin: "Charity is as it seems to me."</note>. There we said that His Majesty, being so valiant, nor any other King useful to his kingdom, nor even other public persons uniquely useful to the state, could allow themselves to be killed without sin for the sake of not killing another. Nor could soldiers, who swear to fight for their King, allow themselves to be killed by their enemies for the sake of not killing them, as we proved more extensively there. We also disputed whether a simple man could justly kill a King who, without reason or cause, and without knowledge of the same, wished to kill him and would kill him if the man did not kill the King first.
<margin>a *On Penance, distinction 2.*</margin>

<margin>25</margin>
¶ To the fifth doubt we respond, conceding that, regularly, no one is obliged under pain of <term>mortal sin</term><note>A grave sin that, according to Catholic doctrine, results in the loss of sanctifying grace and leads to eternal damnation if not repented.</note> to perform a work of mercy for one who is not in extreme necessity, as is proven therein. But indeed, sometimes they are; as is proven by those two authorities from Exodus <gloss>b</gloss> and from the...
<margin>b *Chapter 23.*</margin>

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that the duty to defend others is rooted in both charity and justice, particularly for those in public or protective offices. He notes that while individuals can sometimes choose not to defend themselves, public figures like Kings and soldiers are morally obligated to protect their lives due to their utility to the state.</summary>

# On the chapter *Non in inferenda*. 23. q. 3.

<margin>c Chapter 22.</margin>
<margin>d In the following number.</margin>
and from Deuteronomy <note>original: "Deuteronomio"</note> ^c, which speaks of one who encounters his neighbor’s ox gone astray, or his donkey fallen under its load. From these, one could gather a unique rule, which we have never seen treated: namely, that every time a neighbor is in danger of receiving some notable harm from which they cannot free themselves—or it is believed they will not be freed by themselves or by another, but only by me—I am obligated to free them under pain of sin, if I can do so without receiving the same harm myself (of which we shall speak <gloss>d</gloss> next). Consequently, if someone wants to at least pull the hair of or slap an elderly, sick, weakened, or unaccompanied person, and they cannot be freed from this harm without my help (I being present), and I can free them without venturing much in the process, I am obligated to do it. All of this is a daily occurrence, yet poorly handled.

<margin>a Above in the same, number 11.</margin>
¶ To the sixth, I respond by conceding that no one is obligated to defend another (even when there is no one else to defend them) with the danger of losing as much in the process as the other stands to lose if not defended; nor even venturing less, but as much as is not reasonable to venture, according to the <term>judgment of a good man</term><note>The "judgment of a good man" (Latin: *arbitrium boni viri*) is a legal standard referring to the decision a reasonable, prudent, and honest person would make in a given situation.</note>. However, one must venture as much as a good and prudent man would say is reasonable, while retaining the right to recover from the person defended whatever was spent or lost in the effort, as was stated above ^a.

From this second † notable point, its foundations, its six declarations, and the six responses given to the six doubts raised against it, we draw seventeen inferences. The first is that none of the three solemn opinions existing on this matter fully hit the mark of the just understanding of the said texts, which speak of those who can defend and do not. For that of Bernard ^b—who said they refer only to those who hold an office of justice and, being able, do not defend—restricts them too much. It is clear that some of them speak of one who has no jurisdiction or public authority, such as our example of Moses, who had none at the time he defended the Hebrew; and that of Boniface ^c speaks of a neighbor who does not defend his neighbor. The other opinion of Johannes ^d, which says it must be understood of all persons, public and private, whenever they fail to defend, expands the meaning too much, as proven by the effective...

# Resolutory Commentary on the Defense of the Neighbor

<margin>
