<margin>27</margin>
<gloss>e</gloss> The reasons for the fifth declaration <gloss>e</gloss>. The third opinion, that of Innocent <gloss>f</gloss>, which says they speak of all those, and only those, who deceitfully fail to defend—although it is the Common opinion—seems less reasonable, for reasons we will state shortly <gloss>g</gloss>. The true and just understanding, then, will be that they speak of all persons, private as well as public: and thus of those who fail through negligence as well as through malice, without providing other favor: or providing it, but not effectively. It is not that all sin in the same way and incur equal penalties, but rather that all sin and all deserve penalties; however, some deserve certain penalties and others deserve different ones, according to the variety and diversity of the persons. For if they are prelates and judges or others whom justice obliges to defend, or if they are others who fail to defend while favoring the offender, they sin against charity and against justice, and must be regulated in one way. And if they are other persons who fail to defend without favoring the offender and without malice, they sin against charity alone, and must be regulated in another way. And if they sin without favoring the offender, but with malice: although they do not sin except against charity, they must nonetheless be regulated <gloss>a</gloss> in <gloss>b</gloss> another way, at least regarding the fact that they sin much more gravely.

<margin>
e Book 4, question 7, article 5, on justice and law.
f In chapter 27, number 78.
g Chapter *Quantae* on the sentence of excommunication.
h In the gloss of the said chapter *Quantae*.
a Above in the same section, number 26.
b Above in the same section, number 26.
c From which the meaning of the disposition usually depends <note>original: "Vnde disposicionis sensus pendere solet"</note>. Law *finalis*, Digest, on the institution of heirs; chapter *Quia propter*, on elections.
</margin>
<margin>28</margin>
The second inference is that each of the three aforementioned famous opinions is correct in something. For Joannes was correct regarding the sin against charity. Bernard was correct regarding the sin against justice. Innocent was correct regarding the greatness or smallness of the sin. This diversity of opinions (in our view) was born from not understanding or not noticing the difference that exists—regarding <term>censures</term><note>Ecclesiastical penalties, such as excommunication or interdict.</note>, <term>restitutions</term><note>The legal and moral obligation to restore goods or reputation to a victim.</note>, and other penalties—between sins that are against charity alone, and those that are against justice, which is a very great difference, as was said above <gloss>a</gloss> and elsewhere <gloss>b</gloss>. And in our view, the least reasonable opinion, and the one of most superficial consideration, is the Common one, insofar as it determines that the presence of deceit and malice causes one who does not defend to incur penalties, restitutions, and censures. For by "deceit" it understands malice, hatred, or bad intention; and it does not see that in saying that in case of doubt, deceit is presumed, it agrees with Joannes, at least regarding the <term>external forum</term><note>The realm of public, ecclesiastical legal proceedings, as opposed to the internal forum of the conscience.</note>. Nor does it see that malice does not make a sin be against justice if it is not otherwise so, but rather against charity; as Scotus felt <gloss>c</gloss>, and as Adrian <gloss>d</gloss> and Soto <gloss>e</gloss> explained well. Speaking of a neighbor who sees thieves robbing his neighbor and remains silent, being able to prevent the robbery by shouting, Soto says that whether he remains silent out of negligence, or out of malice and hatred of his neighbor, if he does not have a duty of justice, he only sins against charity and not against justice, and thus is not obligated to make restitution.

<margin>
d Above in the same section, numbers 14 and 22.
e Argument from the chapter *Marcion*, 1, question 1; and the chapter *Suggestum*, with its annotation by Panormitanus, on appeals.
f Law 1, § *Huius finalis*, Digest, on justice and law.
g Distinction 49, in the beginning.
h Chapter *Si quis suadente*, 17, question four.
i Chapter *Nullus*, on the competent forum.
k Chapter *Contingit*, on the sentence of excommunication.
e That is, *Si diligenti*, on the competent forum.
</margin>
<margin>29</margin>
The third inference follows what some desired in a certain part <gloss>f</gloss> of the *Manual for Confessors*: namely, what is the true understanding of a Decretal of Innocent III <gloss>g</gloss> which has not yet been found or well-declared. For Bernard <gloss>h</gloss> says its meaning is that only the person who, having a duty of justice, fails to defend a cleric is excommunicated. This seems too narrow for the reasons stated above <gloss>a</gloss>. Joannes said it means that anyone who can defend them and does not is excommunicated. This seems too broad for the reasons stated above <gloss>b</gloss>. Innocent IV, whom Panormitanus and the Common opinion follow, said it means that only and every person who deceitfully fails to defend is excommunicated. This is too broad on one hand, insofar as it includes all those who fail to defend with malice without giving any favor to the attacker. And it is too narrow on the other, insofar as it does not include those who, having a duty of justice or being otherwise obligated to it by law, do not do it without malice, but through carelessness or negligence. 

Another understanding passed through our thoughts, which seemed good to some: namely, that it only applies to those who fail to defend the rectors of the Church when they are able, because the preamble <gloss>c</gloss> speaks of them, and because defense is owed to them by justice according to what was said above <gloss>d</gloss>. But this would be to narrow that solemn text so much that it would be almost useless; and the reasoning and the response, which are general, do not permit it <gloss>e</gloss>. A more likely path was another: namely, that clerics are public property <gloss>f</gloss>; they are fathers and ambassadors of the people before God <gloss>g</gloss>; their privileges of the <term>canon</term><note>Specifically the 'privilegium canonis', which protected clergy from physical violence under threat of excommunication.</note> <gloss>h</gloss> and of the <term>forum</term><note>The 'privilegium fori', the right of clergy to be tried in ecclesiastical rather than secular courts.</note> <gloss>i</gloss> pertain more to the whole clergy than to each cleric in particular <gloss>k</gloss>. Therefore, it seems that laypeople are obligated by justice to defend them as their superiors and intercessors; and thus, by not defending them, they sin against justice for the reasons stated above. But because neither the reasoning of the text is founded on this, nor does the decision contain any word that tastes of it—and because it seems uphill to defend that every cleric, especially one of only the <term>first tonsure</term><note>The lowest rank of the clergy, often consisting of men who lived secular lives but claimed clerical legal privileges.</note>...

<meta>catchword: or less-</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta analyzes the conflicting legal opinions regarding the obligation to defend others, specifically debating whether failure to act constitutes a sin against charity or justice. He critiques the "Common opinion" and examines the specific legal protections and obligations owed to the clergy as public figures.</summary>

# On the Chapter *Non in Inferenda*, Case 23, Question 3

...or subordinates, be considered as a superior to every layperson for the purpose of obliging them to defend him as a superior. And even because, according to this, it would not include clerics who fail to defend other clerics, this does not seem to us a natural understanding.

l Above in the same section, number 18.
m *In the title on the sentence of excommunication.* <note>original: "f. in tit. de sententia excommunicationis."</note>
n *In the title on the sentence of excommunication.* <note>original: "f. in tit. de sententia excommunicationis."</note>
</margin>

30 ¶ Therefore, from what was said above, we gather that according to the literal sense of the same **Innocent III** <note>Pope Innocent III (r. 1198–1216), a major figure in the development of Canon Law.</note>, the author of this text—without looking at the intention and purpose for which **Gregory IX** <note>Pope Gregory IX (r. 1227–1241), who commissioned the *Decretals*, the primary collection of Canon Law.</note> placed it there—it means that whoever does not defend someone they are able to from a manifest injury (that is, an injury that is clearly an injury and is clearly being committed) is presumed to favor the one committing it. According to the intention of Gregory IX, however, who placed it in that location, it means a specific conclusion that follows from the general one: namely, that whoever is able but does not defend a cleric from an injury that is manifestly such and is manifestly being done to him, must be held as excommunicated, just like the one who commits the injury.

<margin>
a *Through the chapter "Dilecto" on the sentence of excommunication, book 6, as stated above in the same section, number 27.* <note>original: "Per. c. Dilecto. de sent. excom. libro. 6. ut supra eod. num. 27. est dictum."</note>
b *Second part of the Second Part, question 62, article 7, which we referred to shortly before in number 10.* <note>original: "2. Secun. q. 62. art. 7. quam paulō antè num. 10. retulimus."</note>
c *Above in the same section, number 14.*
d *Exodus 20.*
e *Above in the same section, number 17.*
f *Number 28.*
</margin>

31 ¶ The fourth point: everyone who fails to defend a cleric, being able and obligated to do so, against justice—whether truly or presumptively—is, or must be held as, excommunicated. We said "being able" generally, to include not only those who can do this by judicial authority, but even those who can do it by their own authority. We added "being obligated" because being able to prevent something does not induce sin in the one who fails to do so unless he is obligated to it, according to that aforementioned and singular doctrine of **Saint Thomas** <note>Saint Thomas Aquinas, specifically referring to the *Summa Theologiae*.</note>. We added "against justice" to include all those who are obligated to it by justice, most of whom we specified above. We also added it to include all those who fail to defend and expressly or tacitly favor the act in some way—by advising, commanding, exhorting, encouraging, or helping by some other art against justice, being present with their friends or their weapons, or giving signs that it should be done or that, if carried out, they would help, etc. All of this is express or tacit help against justice, which forbids all of it in the commandment "Thou shalt not kill" or "Thou shalt not steal," etc. It was also added to exclude those who, without being obligated to it by justice (though they might be by charity), fail to defend out of mere negligence, or hatred and malevolence, without giving any express or tacit favor or help. Such people sin against charity and mercy, but none of these will be excommunicated before God and in the internal forum <note>The "internal forum" refers to the court of conscience and one's standing before God, as opposed to the "external forum" of church or civil law.</note>, according to what was said above. We said "presumptively" to include those who do not truly sin against justice in this matter, but are presumed to: such as those who, without thinking or noticing, perform certain actions or give certain signs. Just as the offender interprets these in his favor, so the judge takes them as evidence to presume it. "Presumptively" was also said to include all those who, being obligated at least by charity and being able to do so without notable inconvenience, do not defend a cleric from an injury that is manifestly such and is manifestly occurring, as per what was said above. We put "is, or must be held as, excommunicated" so that "is" includes the one who truly sins against justice, and "must be held as" includes the one who presumptively sins against it.

<margin>
a *In the law "utramque," Digest, on the duties of a father.* <note>original: "In l. utram. ff. ad l. ope. de patri."</note>
b *Above, number 14.*
c *In the said chapter "Quantae."*
</margin>

32 ¶ We add that from this is inferred the reason (hitherto given by no one) upon which that harsh but just decision of **Bartolus** <note>Bartolus de Saxoferrato (1313–1357), a highly influential legal commentator.</note> can be founded: that although normally no one should be punished for merely knowing that a crime was being prepared and not revealing it, it is different when the one who knows it is a son, a subject, or a slave. If such a person does not reveal it, they can be punished for that alone, even with the death penalty. The reason for this may be that others commonly do not sin except against the precepts of charity, whereas these people sin even against the precepts of justice, as was said above. If **Baldus** <note>Baldus de Ubaldis (1327–1400), a student of Bartolus.</note> and those who follow him—and whom **Felinus** <note>Felinus Sandeus (1444–1503), a canonist.</note> cites—had considered this reason, they would not have reproached Bartolus as harshly as they do.

<margin>
d *Which Panormitanus follows in chapter 1, on the restitution of spoils, and Felinus in the said chapter "Quantae."* <note>original: "Quod sequitur Panor. in ca. 1. de resti. spolia. & Felin. in d. cap. Quantæ."</note>
</margin>

¶ The fifth point: the reason why a harsher punishment can be given to those who do not defend a magistrate <term>corregidor<gloss>a local administrative and judicial official representing the Crown</gloss></term> or an officer of the King than to those who do not defend other private men is that the former sin against justice, and for this, they must be punished in both forums, while the others do not, sinning only against charity.

33 ¶ The sixth point, which is the reason given by no one until now: why, normally, one who does not defend another is not punished in the external, civil, or canonical forum (according to the common opinion), even though he sins in doing so and is punished in the forum of conscience. The reason is that by merely not defending,

# Resolutory Commentary on the Defense of One’s Neighbor

...not defending, no one commonly sins against the precepts of justice, although they may sin against those of charity; and for this reason, they should not be punished with the penalties of the precepts of justice that are established against those who transgress them.

<margin>
e In the gloss of the said chapter *Quanta*.
f Concerning which, see above in the same section, numbers 18 and 19.

¶ The seventh [point]: that **Bernard of Parma** <note>original: "Bernardo"; a 13th-century canonist known for the *Glossa Ordinaria* on the Decretals.</note> correctly says that one who does nothing more than fail to defend a cleric is not excommunicated, if this is understood to refer to one who is not bound by justice to do so, and not otherwise; and if it is understood as true excommunication, and not otherwise. For it ought to be presumed that he is [excommunicated] when the four aforementioned conditions concur.

<margin>g Which **Hadrian VI** <note>original: "Adria."; Pope Hadrian VI (r. 1522–1523), a prominent theologian before his papacy.</note> beautifully proves in the 4th book on Restitution, question 1, column 9; with whom later authors agree, following **Alexander de Nevo** <note>original: "Alex."; a 15th-century Italian jurist.</note>, whom we cited above at the end of number 7.</margin>
¶ The eighth: that no one incurs an obligation to restore <term>restitution|The legal and moral obligation to compensate a victim for damages or lost property</term> anything to the offended party for having failed to defend them when they were able, if they were not legally obligated; not even if they were obligated by charity and mercy alone, even if they omitted the defense out of malice.

h And therefore it should not be said. chapter *Consuluisti*. 2. question 5. chapter 2. on the translation [of bishops]. <note>original: "Ideoque nō est dicendum. c. consuluisti. 2. q. 5. c. 2. de transluio."</note>

¶ The ninth: that no one truly incurs any censure <note>In canon law, a "censure" is a spiritual penalty such as excommunication or interdict.</note> established against those who do something against justice simply for failing to prevent it, or even for being pleased by it, unless it was done in their name or they were the positive cause of it through counsel, command, help, etc. This is because there is no text in the world that proves otherwise. For there is none but the aforementioned chapter *Quanta* that suggests such a thing; and that text does not say this, but only that one is presumed to favor [the crime], and consequently is excommunicated, if the said four conditions concur.

¶ The tenth: that we correctly answered in **Toulouse** <note>Azpilcueta taught at the University of Toulouse in the 1520s.</note> that certain students did not incur excommunication who were present in the Augustinian convent during a large gathering of university doctors and city consuls <note>City magistrates or officials.</note> regarding a certain privilege of the schools. Without the students providing any favor themselves, they were pleased because many others "plucked" <note>original: "repelaron"; likely referring to the aggressive pulling of hair or beards, a common form of assault in university riots of the period.</note> the consuls as they were leaving, as they wore their tonsures <note>original: "coronas abiertas"; the shaven crown of a cleric's head.</note> exposed. However, some regent doctors did incur it, because although they did not tell the students to assault them, they signaled to them that they would be pleased by it.

¶ The twelfth: that what we said in the *Manual* <note>Azpilcueta's *Manual de Confesores y Penitentes*, his most famous work.</note> is true (regardless of what others have written): that one is not truly irregular, nor so before God, who, while being able and even owing it, fails to defend one who is being killed—even if he omits the defense out of hatred, and even if the deceased is a cleric—provided that he gives no favor or help, tacitly or expressly, by commanding, advising, or helping in any way. This is because he does not kill, nor mutilate, nor is he the total or partial cause of it, for the reasons we alleged there; and because there is no text in the world that says irregularity is incurred in such a case. Irregularity is only incurred in the cases expressly stated for it in the law. We said "truly," because if he is so "presumptively," we shall speak of it next.

¶ The thirteenth: † that the opinion of some is not sound—namely, that one should not be presumed irregular who, by failing to defend the killed cleric, incurred excommunication (even if only presumptive). They argue that extraordinary laws should not be expanded, especially in penal matters involving "odious" <note>In legal terms, an "odious" penalty is one that is burdensome or restrictive, and thus must be interpreted strictly and not expanded to other cases.</note> punishments like irregularity, which is not incurred except in cases expressed by the law. Thus, even if we hold that the said Decretal of **Innocent III** induces a specialty whereby one is presumed to favor the act and sin against justice when they manifestly fail to defend though able and owing it (for the purpose of incurring excommunication), we should not therefore extend it to say that it also induces a presumption of said favor for the purpose of incurring irregularity. This [their opinion] is not sound; rather, the opposite follows effectively from what was said above. That Decretal speaks no more of excommunication than of other punishments (as noted) according to the letter of its author, Innocent. For it generally determines that one ought to be presumed and held as a favorer who does not prevent a manifest crime when able. And as the particular conclusion is gathered from this general one—that he who does not defend a cleric being manifestly injured should be presumed excommunicated—so it must follow by force that he is obligated to restore, and that he is irregular. For whoever is, or is presumed to be, a favorer of another's death, is, or must be presu—

<summary>Azpilcueta distinguishes between sins of charity and justice, arguing that failure to defend someone does not always trigger legal penalties like restitution or canonical irregularity unless a specific legal duty exists. He uses a university riot in Toulouse to illustrate how "favoring" a crime through silent approval can lead to excommunication for those in authority.</summary>

# On the chapter *Non in inferenda* <note>original: "Non in inferenda"; Latin for "Not in inflicting [injury]," a reference to Gratian’s Decretum, a foundational collection of canon law, specifically Causa 23, Question 3, which discusses the duty to ward off injuries from others.</note> 23. q. 3.

...one must presume that he is obligated to make <term>restitution|The legal and moral obligation to compensate a victim for damages or lost property</term>, and he is <term>irregular|Canonical irregularity: a legal impediment in the Catholic Church that prevents a person from receiving or exercising Holy Orders, often resulting from serious crimes or specific physical/social conditions.</term> just like the author of the act, although regarding other penalties, he should be punished somewhat less. <margin>b According to the chapter *Sicut*, distinction 4, [at the words] *illi qui* <note>original: "those who".</note>.</margin>

<margin>37</margin> ¶ The 13th [point]: † that from the two previous inferences follows the answer to what some have doubted in the *Manual* <note>Referring to Azpilcueta's own *Manual de confesores y penitentes* (1552), a widely used handbook for confessors.</note>: <margin>c ch. 27, nu. 248.</margin> <margin>d above in the same, nu. 35 & 36.</margin> <margin>e chapter *Is qui*, concerning the office of the ordinary, book 6.</margin> <margin>f in the chapter *Ex tenore*, concerning the time of ordination.</margin> <margin>g chapter *Inquisitio-ne*, concerning accusations.</margin> <margin>h Thomas [Aquinas], *Summa Theologiae* II-II, question 11, article 3.</margin> <margin>i Thomas, II-II, question 20, article 5.</margin> <margin>k chapter *Presbyterum*, concerning homicide.</margin> <margin>l chapter *Sententiam*, with the annotations there and the citations extensively brought in the *Manual*, in ch. 27, d. n. 106, in ch. *Nisi*, § 1, n. 11, title on dispensations, according to the preface number 17 in the distinct chapter *Nisi*, § 1.</margin> whether by those words we put there, namely, that "no crime or offense (no matter how grave) induces irregularity, except that which by special law has this effect," we meant to also include the unspeakable crime of Sodomy: for it follows that we must answer yes. Firstly, because (as has been said) irregularity is not incurred except in cases expressly stated by the law, and this is not one of them. Secondly, because those words are from **Innocent** <note>original: "Inno."; likely Pope Innocent IV, a major 13th-century canonist.</note>. Thirdly, because it matters little that the crime is very great, very filthy, and very abominable; for mental heresy is greater, and despair is greater, and the hatred of God is much greater—yet none of these induce irregularity <note>In canon law, "irregularity" was often attached to public or external acts rather than purely internal sins, even if those sins were spiritually more severe.</note>. Meanwhile, other quite small sins do induce it, and sometimes even virtuous acts. Fourthly, because the doctors who hold the contrary view do not bring foundations to prove it, nor even anything with which to respond to the aforementioned points; they merely followed **Bernard** <note>Bernard of Parma, author of the *Glossa Ordinaria*.</note>, **Hostiensis** <note>Henricus de Segusio, a famous 13th-century canonist.</note>, and the **Speculator** <note>Guillaume Durand, author of the *Speculum Iudiciale*.</note>. Although **Antonio** <note>Panormitanus (Niccolò de' Tudeschi), a prominent 15th-century jurist.</note>, who followed them in one part, departed from them in another because of the said words of Innocent. And the *Speculator* as a final opinion refers to that of **Vincencio** <note>Vincentius Hispanus, a 13th-century canonist.</note> which is contrary. And he does not consider that **Boniface VIII**—who declares that irregularity is incurred in no case except that expressed by the law—lived much after Bernard, Hostiensis, and the *Speculator*, and that he removed all these doubts if any remained. Just as **Bartolome Brixiese** <note>Bartholomew of Brescia, a 13th-century jurist.</note> in this same matter reprimands the opinion of the most learned and holy **Joanes** <note>Johannes Teutonicus, a notable canonist.</note>, saying that **Gregory IX** removed the doubts that existed until his time through one of his Decretals. It also matters that we have understood that in Italy—where, according to what is said, there is more of this evil than would be necessary—no dispensations are requested for this. And that through a high dispute, worthy of his genius, **Francesco Aretino** <note>Francesco Accolti, a famous 15th-century legal scholar from Arezzo.</note> concluded against the common [Franciscan] opinion.

<margin>38</margin> ¶ The 15th [point]: † that from this is inferred the reason and explanation of six conclusions which we briefly touched upon in the *Manual*, <margin>m ch. *Nisi*, § 1.</margin> <margin>n in this title on dispensations, according to the preface, nu. 17.</margin> <margin>o in the distinct chapter *Nisi*, § 1.</margin> <margin>p in the said chapter *Ex tenore*, concerning the time of ordination.</margin> <margin>q chapter *Is qui*, concerning sentences of excommunication, book 6.</margin> <margin>r in the chapter *Si*, 25. d. in the chapter *Si*, concerning the time of ordination.</margin> <margin>t In the chapter *Cum non*, concerning judicial order, column 9 & following.</margin> <margin>v book 5, q. 1, art. 9, on justice and law, ch. 17, n. 221.</margin> <margin>b final chapter 14, q. 6 up to number 13.</margin> <margin>c ch. 27, number 78.</margin> <margin>d chapter *Si quis suadente*, 17. q. 4.</margin> referring ourselves to this commentary. For the reason for the first—namely, that those who have a charge of justice incur the excommunication of the Canon <note>A penalty of excommunication specifically mandated by church law (the Canon).</note> if they do not defend when they are able—is that they sin in this against justice by failing to do what their offices obligate them to do. The same must be said of all those whom justice obliges; most of whom we expressed above. The reason for the second—namely, that others do not incur it by simple omission—is that they sin only against charity. Although this may be a mortal sin, it does not obligate one to restitution, nor to censure, nor to irregularity, which are established against those who trespass the precepts of justice, as was said above and mentioned in the *Manual*. The 3rd: that all who can act without harm are obliged to prevent [harm]; this is understood when extreme necessity arises, or such damage that it must be probably believed that another will not be able or willing to stop it, and not otherwise. The 4th that is touched upon: namely, that no one is obliged to it with harm to themselves; this is to be understood outside of the two said cases of extreme necessity and damage that cannot be remedied by others, or when it is known or believed it will not be remedied. And even in those two cases, for the effect that the harm is not presumed to please him; but not for the effect of excusing him from sin: for sin is not excused in extreme necessity, even by the loss of all property and honor; nor in the other aforementioned damage by the annoyance of applying some labor and cost which he can later recover. The 5th: that there is a difference in this between the <term>external forum|The public legal and disciplinary realm of the Church (ecclesiastical courts).</term> and the <term>internal forum|The realm of conscience and the confessional.</term>. It was understood that if the offense is manifest, he will not be excommunicated or irregular if he does not take pleasure in the crime nor favor the delinquent; but before the world, it will be presumed that he not only took pleasure but even favored him; and consequently, he must be held as excommunicated and irregular if death occurred, and obligated to restitution. The 6th: that before God there is a difference between failing to defend with bad intention and taking pleasure in the offense, or not taking pleasure in it; it is to be understood as the intention of favoring the delinquent with some tacit or express effect, and not simple complacency, as per what was said above.

<margin>39</margin> ¶ The 16th [point]: † that a harmony can be established between two accepted conclusions that <margin>e above in the same, number 14.</margin> <margin>f above in the same, nu. 18 & 19.</margin> seem contrary. ¶ Which we have often sought, and never until today, the feast of the glorious **Magdalene** <note>July 22nd; the author notes that he reached this legal reconciliation on the feast day of Saint Mary Magdalene.</note>.

<margin>num. 16. g 27. nu. 232. h above in the same, number 10 & 11. i better, number 25. i above in the same, nu. 18 & 19.</margin>

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that canonical irregularity only applies to crimes explicitly listed in law, notably excluding sodomy. He also distinguishes between those legally bound by justice to intervene in a crime (who face excommunication) and those bound only by charity (who may sin but avoid legal censure).</summary>
<keywords>canonical irregularity, sodomy, justice vs charity, excommunication, restitution, internal vs external forum, Innocent IV, Boniface VIII, Mary Magdalene</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>Continuity: Azpilcueta continues his legal and moral analysis of the duty to defend one's neighbor, moving from the legal consequences of failing to act to the specific moral obligations rooted in biblical law and Thomas Aquinas’s theology.</meta>

# Resolutory Commentary on the defense of the neighbor.

On the feast of Mary Magdalene in the year 1556, we concluded entirely. One conclusion is that no one is obliged to perform corporal **works of mercy** <note>Actions such as feeding the hungry or clothing the naked that address the physical needs of others.</note> for their **neighbor** <note>The term "proximo" refers to any fellow human being, following the biblical command to "love thy neighbor."</note> under penalty of mortal sin, except when that neighbor is in extreme necessity, as we state in the *Manual*, <margin>k In chapter 24, number 3.</margin> k and above <margin>l Above in the same work, numbers 8 and 25, according to the doctrine of St. Thomas, *Summa Theologiae* II-II, question 32, article 5.</margin> l.

The other conclusion is that everyone is obliged, under penalty of mortal sin, to return a neighbor’s ox if they find it wandering lost, and to help up the donkey—even belonging to one who hates them—if they find it fallen on the road and oppressed under its burden, as is commanded in **Exodus** <margin>a Exodus 23. In these words: "If you meet your enemy’s ox or donkey wandering away, take it back to him. If you see the donkey of one who hates you lying down under its burden, do not pass by; you must help him with it." <note>original: "Si occurreris boui inimici tui, aut asino erranti, reduc ad eum. Si videris asinu odientis te iacere sub onere, non pertransibis: sed subleuabis cum eo."</note></margin> a. This is not by a ceremonial or judicial precept, which have expired, but by a moral one, which endures forever <margin>b Final question 6, distinction [blank], chapter 1, on the purification after childbirth, with the notes on them.</margin> b. That the same must be done for clothing or any other thing that a neighbor loses is declared in **Deuteronomy** <margin>c Deuteronomy 22.</margin> c. This undoubtedly applies to the neighbor’s ox and donkey even if they do not have an extreme necessity for them. The reconciliation [of these two conclusions] is that it is one thing to give <term>alms|Limosna; a voluntary gift to someone in need, which the author argues is only mandatory in life-or-death situations.</term> to this or that neighbor, wherein they gain what they did not have (to which only extreme necessity obliges us, as the first conclusion says); it is quite another thing to prevent damage to what the neighbor already possesses, to which these precepts of Exodus and Deuteronomy oblige us. For this distinction, the laws make a very great difference between profit and loss—between damage to what has already been gained and damage to what is yet to be gained <margin>d [Legal references to the Digest]: Book 2, paragraph Portio, on the Rhodian Law; final law, on the risk and convenience of the thing sold; Law *Si is cui*, final paragraph, on theft; Law *Si sterilis*, paragraph *Cum per venditorem*, on the action of purchase.</margin> d. This must be limited, however, to proceed only in cases of damage where it should probably be believed that no one else could or will prevent it. This limitation is gathered from both said commandments, which do not speak of just any damage, but of that which probably cannot or will not be prevented except by the one who encounters the ox or the donkey. 

From this, many cases can be inferred in which we are obliged to prevent damage, and in which we are not. Walking along, I encounter a **muleteer** <note>A "recuero" is someone who drives a team of pack animals.</note> who has his mule stuck in the mud and cannot get it out: if it is a road where many pass, who I believe will help him, I do not sin mortally by not helping him; but if it is a place where no one or few pass, I do. I see beasts in my neighbor’s grain fields doing damage; I think no one will take them out if I do not; I sin if I do not take them out. I see that your houses, your grain, or your **household goods** <note>original: "axuar"; the furniture, clothing, and domestic tools of a home.</note> are beginning to burn, and there is no one to quench the fire but me, who easily can and do not quench it; I sin. I see that you are **gossiping** <note>original: "murmurays"; specifically, slandering or speaking ill of someone's reputation.</note> to the notable prejudice of a neighbor’s reputation, and although I do not believe you, you cause damage in the eyes of others who hear you, and I see that no one contradicts you, nor do I believe anyone will; I sin if I do not contradict you, as we have said elsewhere <margin>e In the *Manual*, chapter 18, number 36, and more extensively in the chapter *Inter verba*, Causa 11, question 3, number 349.</margin> e. I see you buying animals, pastures, lands, vineyards, or other things that I know are bad, or at least not as good as you think they are; and I see that no one will warn you if I do not; I sin if I do not warn you. Because although you are not in extreme necessity of my advice, you are in need of my help so that you do not lose your wealth. By these examples, many other very common things can be specified, which have not (to our knowledge) been well declared by anyone; and I affirm this as we said in another place <margin>f In the chapter *Inter*, Causa 11, question 3, number 71.</margin> f regarding witnesses.

### S U M M A R Y.

He who, being able, does not obstruct the mortal sin of his neighbor, sins mortally. number 41. Provided three conditions concur. number 47. And he who does not resist the slanderer, or does not deliver him who wishes to perish. number 42.

Future sins are to be avoided more than past ones are to be punished. And thus one inquires and arrests, etc. number 43.

He who does something by which his neighbor sins, sins as if mortally. number 44.

We must defend our neighbor so that he does not sin mortally, even with loss of wealth, honor, and physical life. number 45. And even outside of extreme necessity. number 46.

A work of mercy, not only spiritual but even temporal, is owed even outside of extreme necessity. numbers 45 & 46.

He who knows of an injustice can and should appeal the death sentence of his neighbor. number 6.

<meta>Catchword: Peca como</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta distinguishes between the general duty of charity and the specific moral obligation to prevent a neighbor from suffering a loss of property or reputation. He argues that while alms are only mandatory in life-threatening situations, protecting a neighbor's existing assets or preventing them from sinning is a duty under pain of mortal sin.</summary>
<keywords>restitution, mortal sin, property rights, works of mercy, Exodus 23, Deuteronomy 22, reputation, Martín de Azpilcueta, legal obligation, neighborly duty</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: Azpilcueta continues his analysis of the obligations of charity, specifically moving from the duty to protect a neighbor's physical property (like a lost ox) to the much higher duty of protecting their spiritual life and soul from sin.</meta>

# On the chapter *Non in inferenda* [Not in inflicting]. Case 23, question 3.

<margin>
a Chapter 14, number 26.
</margin>
One does not sin who uses their own goods and rights, even if another sins because of it. Number 48.

<margin>
b *Summa Theologiae* II-II, q. 26, art. 5.
c Chapter *Resuscitatus* [Resuscitated].
d Chapter *Omnis* [Every], *De Poenitentia*, distinction 1.
e Chapter *Ipsa pietas* [Piety itself], Case 23, q. 4. There: "What, therefore, must we expend for men for the sake of obtaining eternal life and avoiding eternal punishment... if true and kind reason compels us thus to help men for this salvation, not only temporal but also brief and for a tiny time?" <note>original: "Quid igitur de epere mie, quid pro vita eterna adipiscenda, & pænæ æterna vitandæ hominibus debemus impendere, indicandum est: si pro salute ista non solum temporali, sed etiã breui, & ad tempos exiguum liberanda, sic nos subvenire hominibus ratio vera, & benigna compellit?"</note>
</margin>
41 **T**he seventeenth and final conclusion † that is inferred from what has been said above is that the conclusion of the *Manual* <gloss>a</gloss> is true and ought to be declared, despite the doubts raised against it there by a man who is, without doubt, very highly regarded. Namely: that he who can prevent the **mortal sin** <note>In Catholic theology, a grave sin committed with full knowledge and consent that separates the soul from God's grace.</note> of a **neighbor** <note>The "proximo," meaning any fellow human being.</note> and does not prevent it, sins mortally. It is clear that this follows from what has been said. First, because it has been concluded above by this text and many others that **charity** <note>The theological virtue of "Caritas," or selfless love for God and neighbor.</note> obliges us all to defend the corporal life of our neighbor; and it is certain that it obliges us even more to defend their spiritual life of the soul, since we must love it more than their corporal life, and even our own, as Saint Thomas proves <gloss>b</gloss>. And the loss of one soul is a greater harm than the loss of many, and even innumerable bodies, even of innocent men, according to Saint Augustine <gloss>c</gloss>. The life of the soul dies through mortal sin <gloss>d</gloss>. In this way, Saint Augustine <gloss>e</gloss> argues with even stronger reason, saying in essence: "What must we do for the eternal life of our neighbor, if for their brief corporal life we are obliged to do this?"

<margin>
f *Summa Theologiae* II-II, q. 73, art. 4.
g In the chapter *Inter verba* [Between words], 11, q. 3, n. 547.
</margin>
42 It also follows † that he who does not resist one who **murmurs** <note>To "murmur" in this context refers to the sin of backbiting, gossip, or slander.</note> in his presence sins mortally, as Saint Thomas says <gloss>f</gloss>, referred to by us elsewhere <gloss>g</gloss>. We are also obliged to stop a neighbor who wants to throw himself down, cut his throat, hang himself, or kill himself in any other way <gloss>h</gloss>, and to pull out by force one who does not want to leave a house that is about to collapse <gloss>i</gloss>. These points show that even if there is no extreme necessity for our intervention, we are obliged to intervene: for if one is obliged to help a neighbor relieve the danger of losing a fallen donkey, a strayed ox, a garment, or another stolen or lost thing—even if the neighbor does not have an extreme or very urgent necessity for it <gloss>k</gloss>—how much more does the danger in which we see him of losing his soul oblige us?

<margin>
a Chapter *Nimium* [Too much], 23, q. 4.
i Chapter *Ipsa pietas* [Piety itself], 23, q. 4.
k Exodus 23 and Deuteronomy 22.
</margin>
43 It follows † that we are more obliged to prevent future sins than to seek the punishment of past ones <gloss>a</gloss>. It is clear that we are obliged to correct a neighbor for past sins and to seek with much love, much wisdom, and vigilance that he leaves the sin into which he has fallen <gloss>b</gloss>. Therefore, by even stronger reason, we must act to stop a sin that is yet to be committed. It follows that for the purpose of avoiding future sins, an inquiry may be made in any case, according to a unique saying of **Innocent IV** <gloss>c</gloss> <note>Sinibaldo Fieschi (c. 1195–1254), a Pope and highly influential jurist of canon law.</note> received by everyone; and a cleric may be seized by a layman according to **Panormitano** <gloss>d</gloss> <note>Niccolò de' Tudeschi (1386–1445), a famous Benedictine canonist.</note> and approved by others. This applies both to private offenses that harm the individual good, as well as public ones that harm the public; and to secret sins as well as notorious ones, as specifically stated by the Saintly Doctor **Soto** <gloss>e</gloss> <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a Spanish Dominican priest and scholastic theologian.</note>. It also follows that to avoid **scandal** <note>In a theological sense, "scandal" is an action that leads or tempts another person into sin.</note> and the stumbling block by which a neighbor might sin, we ought to do or refrain from doing everything that we can do without sinning <gloss>f</gloss>, even if they are alms or other works advised by the Gospel <gloss>g</gloss>. From all this, it is clear that the said conclusion of the manual is true <gloss>h</gloss>.

<margin>
a Argument from the chapter *Praeterea* [Moreover] regarding espousals; and in the *Manual* briefly in chapter 11, q. 3, from number 662.
b Matthew 18. Chapter *Si peccaverit* [If he sins], 2, q. 1, as stated briefly in the *Manual*, chapter 24, from number 17.
c In chapter 1, on the request of prelates, second column; there I gave it in chapter 3.
</margin>
44 However, † that it may require some clarification is shown by the foundations we wrote there. Namely: he does not sin mortally who asks a loan from a **usurer** <note>One who lends money at interest, which was traditionally considered a mortal sin by the Church.</note>, even if he believes the usurer will not lend except at **usury** <gloss>i</gloss> and is thus committing a mortal sin. Nor does he who asks his priest to baptize his child, believing the priest is in mortal sin and will baptize him without repenting of it. Nor the woman who applies makeup for venial vainglory, without any other mortal intent, even if she believes that some who see her will conceive mortal lust upon seeing her thus adorned <gloss>k</gloss>. Nor does he who (even being able to do so without harm to himself) fails to defend a cleric so that he is not struck, incur **excommunication** <note>Formal exclusion from the sacraments and community of the Church.</note>, at least as far as the **court of conscience** <gloss>l</gloss> <note>The "internal forum" or the private relationship between an individual's conscience and God, as opposed to the "external forum" of church law.</note> is concerned. And what is even stronger: no one is obliged to give alms under penalty of mortal sin unless the neighbor is in extreme necessity <gloss>m</gloss>. It is clear that one who willfully wants to sin mortally, knowing that it is such a sin and being able to avoid it, is not in the same state of "extreme necessity" for someone else's spiritual intervention.

<margin>
And the chapter on judgments; and in the book on the method of ruling secrets, member 2, q. 6.
</margin>
45 Therefore, † while affirming the said conclusion—that we are obliged to stop the mortal sin of our neighbor if we can—we add these clarifications. The first, that it applies not only

As notorious. Regarding the sentence of excommunication and the chapter *Cum non ab homine* [Since not by man], on judgments; and in the book on the method of ruling secrets, member 2, q. 6, page 15. f Chapter 2, on the notification of a new work. g said chapters 1, 2. h Chapter 14, number 16. i As above, number 27. k In the same *Manual*, chapter 23, number 13. l Above in the same, numbers 17 and 31. m Thomas, accepted, II-II, question 32, article 5, reported above, n. 59, and in the *Manual*, chapter 34, from number 3.

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that charity compels individuals to prevent the mortal sins of their neighbors, just as they would prevent physical harm or property loss. He balances this duty with practical legal and moral exceptions, such as borrowing from usurers or cases of personal vanity.</summary>
<keywords>fraternal correction, mortal sin, charity, spiritual life vs corporal life, usury, scandal, Panormitano, Domingo de Soto, court of conscience</keywords>

# Resolutory Commentary: On the defense of one's neighbor.

<margin>
u in the chapter *Quantae* [How great] on the sentence of excommunication and chapter 2 on heretics, cited and refuted above in this same work, numbers 10 and 11.
u In chapter 24, number 2.
b In book 4, on fraternal correction, column 11.
t Chapter *Nimium* [Too much] and chapter *Ipsa pietas* [Piety itself], Case 23, question 4.
</margin>
(as we stated there) if we can do so without harm, or shame and affront, according to Felino<sup>u</sup> <note>Felinus Sandeus (1444–1503) was an influential Italian canon lawyer whose commentaries were standard legal references.</note>, whom no one until us has contradicted. But further, we must act even if we cannot do so without damage to our property and honor, and even to our physical life, when the neighbor's soul is in extreme necessity of our assistance. We affirmed this in another part of the *Manual*<sup>u</sup>, stating that we are obliged to give **spiritual alms** <note>Spiritual alms refer to acts of mercy that assist the soul, such as instructing the ignorant, counseling the doubtful, or—as discussed here—preventing sin.</note> under pain of **mortal sin** to one who has extreme need of it for the salvation of their soul, even if we must lose our lives for it. The second declaration is that this obligation holds even when the neighbor is not in extreme necessity of our assistance, but—either through weakness, gross or affected ignorance, or other causes and occasions of sinning—is in such need that if we do not help them, they will commit a mortal sin and die spiritually, whereas if we help them, they will not. This is a singular doctrine of that most pious and learned **Hadrian**<sup>b</sup> <note>Hadrian refers to Pope Hadrian VI (1459–1523), a Dutch theologian and the last non-Italian pope before John Paul II; he was a key figure for the School of Salamanca where Azpilcueta taught.</note>. God knows how much consolation it gave us today when we found it almost by chance, though it discouraged us to see that he offers nothing to support it beyond inferring that spiritual alms are more strictly commanded than corporal alms, which also requires proof.

<margin>
d Solemn gloss, question 1, distinction 183.
e Law *Non tantùm* [Not only], Digests, on appeals; and law *Addictos* [The assigned], Codex, on the episcopal audience.
f Argument from Proverbs 24: "Deliver him who is being led to death," which is cited here.
</margin>
¶ We allege, however,<sup>t</sup> what we have never heard, read, or said on this subject: <insert>46</insert> that we are obliged to prevent for our neighbor the evils and damages that he could avoid but does not want to, if they are such that he cannot accept them without sin. For as we have alleged above, we are obliged to prevent a neighbor from hanging, stabbing, or otherwise killing himself, or cutting off a limb<sup>d</sup>. And even if we are physicians, we are sometimes obliged to cure one who does not wish<sup>d</sup> to be cured, and we are all obliged to forbid the sick from eating or drinking what we believe would kill them—and even the healthy from drinking poison—even though, if they wished, they could guard themselves against all of this. And so we say that in this there is no difference between spiritual and corporal alms or help. As Hadrian said (conquered, in our view, by the great force of the argument), when the help is such that the neighbor cannot renounce it, and without which they would die spiritually or physically, it must be given as a matter of **precept** <note>In canon law, a "precept" is a binding command or rule of conduct, as opposed to a mere suggestion.</note> by whoever is able, even if the neighbor does not want it. To this end, even if it grieves the person condemned to death, his neighbor can appeal the declaratory sentence<sup>e</sup>. And even if the condemned man failed to appeal because he wanted to die (as some slaves are wont to do), anyone who knew the sentence was unjust would be obliged to appeal.<sup>f</sup>

<margin>
a Chapter 14, number 17.
b *Summa Theologiae* II-II, question 33, article 2; and in Book 4, distinction 19.
c In the said article 2.
d In Book 4, on fraternal correction.
e Book on the reasoning of the law, member 2, question 2.
f In the chapter *Novit* [He knew], on judgments.
g Chapter *Si peccaverit* [If he sins], Case 2, question 1.
</margin>
¶ The third declaration † is that we are not obliged to the said prevention except when three conditions concur, similar to those already required to bind us to the precept of **fraternal correction** <note>The Christian duty to privately and charitably rebuke a fellow believer who is sinning.</note>. We placed these in the *Manual*<sup>a</sup>. The first: that it be certain the sin is mortal, or a dangerous venial sin. The second: that there be good hope that the sin will be prevented, or at least a belief that it will not make things worse. The third: that it be done with opportunity—not only by the person most obliged to it (or at least considering the negligence of those who are most obliged), but also at the proper time. All of this is gathered from the reasons for the said three conditions of fraternal correction provided by **St. Thomas**<sup>b</sup>, **Cajetan**<sup>c</sup>, **Hadrian**<sup>d</sup>, **Soto**<sup>e</sup>, **Innocent**, **Panormitanus**, and other **Decretalists**<sup>f</sup>, as well as the **Archdeacon**, **Dominicus**, and other **Decretists**<sup>g</sup>. <insert>47</insert> <note>The Decretists and Decretalists were the two main schools of medieval canon lawyers who commented on the foundational legal texts of the Church.</note>

<margin>
h Argument from Matthew 18: "Whoever causes one of these little ones to stumble," etc. Chapter 2, on the reporting of new work. Thomas, II-II, question 43, articles 7 and 8.
</margin>
¶ The fourth declaration: that we are also obliged to prevent the mortal sin of a neighbor by refraining from using our goods and rights, both spiritual and temporal, when we can refrain without sin, and where without refraining, we cannot prevent the mortal sin arising from the neighbor's ignorance or weakness<sup>h</sup>.

<margin>
i according to Matthew 15: "Let them alone; they are blind," etc. And cited by Thomas where above, and noted by our doctors in the chapter *Qui scandalizaverit* [He who causes scandal] in the rules of law, and the said chapter 2 on the reporting of new work.
</margin>
¶ The fifth declaration † is that we are not, however, so obliged to prevent another's mortal sin that we must refrain from using our goods and rights (even temporal ones) simply because we see that we could not otherwise prevent the sin of a neighbor who commits it out of pure malice and wickedness<sup>i</sup>. <insert>48</insert>

¶ By these declarations, the arguments raised against the conclusion are resolved. For the first two are resolved by the fourth and fifth declarations: the first speaks of the right one has to ask a loan from a neighbor, who sins out of malice and being in a bad state of usury and sin, and not out of ignorance and weakness. And the second speaks of one who uses his right and asks his priest to baptize his son, where the priest sins by his own malice and bad state, and not because of the rightful request. To the third argument, regarding she who dresses herself up vainly, etc., we respond that we did not say in our *Manual*...

<summary>Azpilcueta outlines the moral obligation to prevent a neighbor's mortal sin, even against their will, while establishing limits based on whether the neighbor sins out of weakness or pure malice.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual alms, fraternal correction, mortal sin, Pope Hadrian VI, St. Thomas Aquinas, moral obligation, legal appeal, School of Salamanca</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>Continuity: Azpilcueta concludes his discussion on the obligation to aid a neighbor’s soul, moving from general principles of spiritual alms to the specific case of whether a woman’s vanity makes her responsible for the lustful thoughts of others, and finally concluding this section of the commentary.</meta>

# On the chapter *Non in inferenda* [Not in inflicting], Case 23, question 3.

that a woman does not sin mortally when she adorns herself out of **venial vanity** <note>Venial vanity refers to a minor or pardonable desire for attention that does not reach the level of a grave or "mortal" sin.</note>, even if she believes that someone might be moved to **mortal lust** <note>original: "codicia mortal," referring here to grave sexual desire.</note> because of it. Rather, the case is different if someone were actually moved to mortal lust by her actions. Thus, we say that a woman who adorns herself for such vanity, believing that this or that specific man, through his own weakness, would be moved to mortal lust, and yet she presents herself before him, would not be excused from sin. We did not say "through his weakness" without cause: for she would not sin by adorning herself even if she believed that some man or men—who through their own malice are in a state of sin and have no care to guard themselves against sins of the flesh—look for temptations and opportunities even before they arise, seeking to delight in seeing and lusting after her and other beautiful, well-dressed women. And even if they did not lust after others but only her, this remains true as long as that lust proceeds not from weakness, but from malice alone, or from a mixture of malice and weakness where the weakness is not the cause of the sin, but merely a companion to the malice from which the sin arises, according to what **Saint Thomas** says regarding ignorance <margin>a 1st part of the 2nd part, question 76, article 1 and following.</margin><sup>a</sup> <note>Azpilcueta refers to Thomas Aquinas's distinction between ignorance that is "concomitant" (happening alongside an action) and ignorance that is the "cause" of an action.</note>. Neither did we say "N. N." <note>A placeholder for a specific person, like "John Doe."</note> instead of "anyone in general" without cause, because it seems she would not sin simply for believing that someone in general might be moved. <margin>b Argument from the chapter *Officii* [On the office] on election. And those things which we brought forward in the chapter *Si quis aut* [If anyone, or], on penance, distinction 7, regarding conscience that is distant in general only.</margin><sup>b</sup>

To the fourth argument, I respond that because of the **scandal of the neighbor** <note>In moral theology, "scandal" is an action or omission that provides another the occasion to sin.</note> which arises from malice, we are not obligated to cease using our spiritual or temporal rights, as has been said. To the fifth, we deny that we are only obligated to succor our neighbor when they are in such extreme necessity that they cannot escape without our help; for we are indeed obligated when and how it was stated in the first and second declarations.

To better establish all this, it helped us that it seemed right to the very Reverend Father **Friar Ambrosio de Salazar**, substitute for the first chair of Theology at this university, a man of singular life, wit, judgment, erudition, and energy in lecturing and preaching—qualities that rarely coincide in one person. Thus, the aforementioned conclusion remains defended against the arguments and doubts mentioned above, to the honor and glory of the most glorious **Saint Mary Magdalene**, whose feast the Church celebrates today.

May she negotiate with her beloved God and man, **JESUS**, so that just as He defended her more than once from those who whispered against her, He may likewise defend us from all mortal sins through her courageous intercession. And to defend one another spiritually and corporally, may He give us grace and perseverance in it, until we reach eternal glory. Amen.

Coment. resolut. on the defense of one's neighbor.

# Resolutory commentary on notable theft, regarding the final chapter 14, Question 6, for the clarification of certain passages of the Manual for Confessors.

## Final Chapter. xiiii. Question. vi.

->Jerome in the Epistle to Titus. Chapter 2.<-

> *For a thief is judged not only in major matters, but also in minor ones. For it is not what was taken by theft that is considered, but the mind of the thief. Just as in fornication, the act is not different whether the woman is beautiful or ugly, a slave or free, poor or wealthy: but whoever she may be, it is one and the same fornication. So in theft, no matter how much one takes, he incurs the crime of theft.* <note>original: "Fur autem non solum in maioribus: sed in minoribus etiam iudicatur. Non enim quod furto ablatum est, Sed mens furantis attenditur..."</note>

> *A person is judged as a thief not only for stealing large things, but even for stealing small ones: because one does not look at what is stolen, but at the intent of the one who steals. Just as fornication is not different because a woman is beautiful or ugly, a slave or free, poor or rich—rather, whichever she may be, it is the same fornication—likewise in theft, however much one steals, one commits the sin of theft.*

### S U M M A R Y.

What is theft? How it is divided (referred elsewhere). That every illicit usurpation and the will for it is forbidden by the seventh commandment "Thou shalt not steal." n. 1. That the large and the small are of the same species. n. 2. And are mortal sins by their nature. num. 5.

Which circumstance must be confessed? That of quantity is not such a circumstance. num. 3. Except when, etc. num. 4.

A sin that is mortal by its nature ceases to be so because of these three things. n. 5.

Small theft is not a <term>mortal sin</term><note>A grave sin that separates the soul from God's grace, requiring confession and penance.</note>. What qualifies as such? I say it is what a good man would judge. n. 5. How will he say it? num. 6.

Small theft with the intent to steal much is a mortal sin. n. 6. And otherwise not: although without harm to many, it will be so for another respect. n. 1. 7. And even if it causes great anger. When stealing from the poor is a greater sin, and when it is not. num. 8.

Theft of one hundred <term>maravedís</term><note>A small Spanish copper coin of low value.</note> and of two <term>reales</term><note>A silver coin worth 34 maravedís.</note> is considered a mortal sin in some parts. num. 9.

Theft of less than two <term>ducados</term><note>A high-value gold coin.</note> does not seem notable, just as deception in a lesser amount does not grant a legal action. n. 9. Which seems dangerous. n. 10.

Legal action is denied to the injured party in a matter that is not a mortal sin. n. 10. Although it can be granted for the retention of a single hen. num. 11.

General excommunication does not bind except for mortal sin: and for it, yes. n. 10. If it is not removed, does it bind for the theft of an awl? n. 12.

Theft of half a Real, or twenty maravedís, seems notable, and even from eight upward, and that of a hen, and of a dozen eggs, etc.

<meta>num. 14.</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta begins an analysis of theft, arguing that while the moral nature of theft depends on intent, the practical distinction between mortal and venial sin often depends on the value of the stolen item and the harm caused.</summary>

# Regarding the final chapter, Question 24, question 6. <note>Note: This refers to a specific section of Canon Law in Gratian’s Decretum. Though the previous page noted Question 14, the text here indicates Question 24.</note>

Item 11. And when that of an awl or a needle [is theft]. item 12.

Kings executed soldiers for stealing chickens and lesser things. item 11.

a in chapter 27, number 9.
b to which place we had referred in that [work]. From chapter 17, number 3, in the aforementioned Manual, chapter 17, number 2.
c in the addition to the chapter *Quando*, regarding consecration, distinction 1, number 231.

1 By † the original text of Saint Jerome, as amended by Erasmus, it is evident that this text has been changed somewhat. For from the verse "just as," it says in this manner: "just as in fornication and adultery, the act is not different whether the woman is beautiful or rich, deformed or poor, a prostitute or an adulteress. But whichever she may be, etc." <note>original: "quomodo in fornicatione, & adulterio, non idcirco diuersa fit forcatio, aut adulterium, si pulchra, vel diues, deformis, aut pauper, meretrix, vel adultera sit. Sed qualiscunque sit, &c." Erasmus was a famous Renaissance scholar who produced a more accurate version of Saint Jerome's ancient Latin writings.</note> But because this does not matter much to the case, neither regarding Gratian's purpose nor our own, we have translated it into the vernacular according to the letter of the same. In the revision of the *Manual for Confessors* <margin>a</margin>, we referred to this commentary the declaration of the quantity required for theft to be a mortal sin. Therefore, assuming the definition of theft and its division into mental theft, real theft, and other things we touched on there: we will only repeat here what we said there <margin>b</margin> and elsewhere <margin>c</margin>. Namely, that by the seventh commandment "Thou shalt not steal" <margin>d</margin>, not only is it forbidden to secretly take from one's neighbor against his will (which is properly called theft), but also everything else that is wrongly taken, wrongly held, and all harm that is wrongly inflicted. Consequently, this includes what is taken or held through deception, the force of unjust laws, or any other illicit usurpation of another's property <margin>e</margin>; and even every deliberate will to take, hold, harm, or illicitly usurp against the will of its owner. This is because, as we have said elsewhere, sins of the will, mouth, and deed are of the same kind, although those of the will alone do not obligate one to restitution as those of deed and mouth do.

<margin>
f in the Manual, chapter 11, number 9; and in the said addition, number 233; and in the final chapter regarding simony, number 7; following Saint Thomas [Aquinas], Prima Secundae, Question 72, article 7.
a Arguing from chapter *Marcio*, 1, Question 1, law *Non dubium*, Digest, regarding laws.
b It is not what was taken by theft, but the mind of the thief that is considered. <note>original: "Non est in quod furto ablatum est sed mens furantis attenditur."</note>
</margin>
2 ¶ Having assumed this, † let us note from this chapter that the theft of a large thing and that of a small thing are of the same kind and species. For in the beginning it says that he who steals a little is judged as a thief just as he who steals much. And at the end it concludes that however much one steals, he commits the sin of theft. It proves this even better in the middle, joining it with the end, where it effectively says that just as fornication with a beautiful or ugly, rich or poor woman, or a slave, is one—that is, of the same species and kind <margin>f</margin> of simple fornication—so the theft of a large thing and the theft of a small thing are of the same kind. And it is clear (as the gloss treats it here) that all else being equal, it is a greater sin to fornicate with one of the aforementioned than with another. Nor does it stand in the way to say that Saint Jerome’s intention—which is gathered from the reason he gives <margin>a</margin> to confirm his saying by those words <margin>b</margin> (Because one does not look at what is stolen, but at the mind of the one who steals)—was to say that the taking of a small thing is only theft when the will of the person taking it was to steal much. This does not stand in the way because it is answered that by the other reason and comparison he gives regarding fornication, and from the conclusion, it is gathered that his intention was to say what we have noted.

<margin>
c at the beginning of chapter *Consideret*, regarding penance, distinction 1.
d In the Manual, chapter 6, number 7.
</margin>
3 ¶ From this † follows the first daily conclusion, which we proved in one part <margin>c</margin> and placed in another <margin>d</margin>: that the circumstance of the quantity of the sin, although it increases it, does not change its species, nor does it commonly turn a venial sin into a mortal one. <note>"Venial" sins are minor offenses that do not destroy the soul's relationship with God, while "mortal" sins are grave matters that do.</note> Therefore, the penitent is not commonly obligated to confess it. Consequently, one is not required to say whether the theft was of the value of ten, twenty, a hundred, a thousand, or ten thousand ducats—provided that they confess it was of sufficient quantity to be a mortal theft. Because as Saint Jerome signifies here, the circumstance of the quantity of the theft, although it increases the sin, does not change its species, nor commonly make a venial sin mortal <margin>e chapter 6, number 3.</margin>. We are not obligated to confess all circumstances, but (as <margin>f in the said chapter *Consideret*, number 5.</margin> we resolved it in the Manual <margin>e</margin>) only those that cause the works to which they belong to be mortal sins, or those that are mortal sins of one species to be of another, or what is mortal in one respect to also be mortal in another—whether they change the works from one species to another or not, according to the common opinion, which we treated copiously elsewhere <margin>f</margin>.

<margin>
g In the same Manual, said chapter 6, number 7.
</margin>
4 ¶ It follows † secondly that what we said elsewhere <margin>g</margin> is also true: that although it is a laudable thing to confess the circumstances that aggravate the sin, making it go from lesser to greater...

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that the moral nature (species) of theft remains the same regardless of the object's value, comparing it to Jerome’s example of fornication. He concludes that while quantity increases the gravity of the sin, a penitent is not strictly required to confess the exact monetary value of a theft, only whether it was "notable" enough to be mortal.</summary>

# Resolutory Commentary on the Defense of One’s Neighbor.

But the more common and probable opinion is that it is not necessary, provided that such an increase [in the sin] is not the cause of a venial sin becoming mortal, or changing its kind, or affecting it in some other respect, 
<margin>b folio at the beginning of the said chapter "Consideret," number 12, part 4, page 36.</margin>
as we have proven extensively elsewhere <note>original: "alibi h"</note>. However, this does not apply in cases where the increase in the sin causes it to be "reserved"—at least by a synodal constitution—which sometimes reserves certain thefts or damages above a certain amount to a higher authority: or adds that absolution or restitution must be made in a certain way: and in cases where the sin has an attached excommunication, or when the attached excommunication is Papal. For example, by some letters of excommunication, those who have stolen up to a certain amount are excommunicated, while others are not, even though they may have sinned mortally in doing so. <note>"Reserved" sins are those that a standard priest cannot absolve without permission from a higher authority, like a Bishop or the Pope. Synodal constitutions are local church laws.</note>

<margin>
a chapter "Fures" and the three following, regarding theft.
b chapter 11, number 4.
c Second part of the Second part, question 66, article 6. <note>Refers to Thomas Aquinas’s Summa Theologica.</note>
d part 2, title 4, chapters 5, 6, 7, & 8.
e Quodlibet 8, page 11.
</margin>
¶ The third point follows: † that every theft, large and small, is mortal by its nature and species, because the largeness or smallness of the theft does not change its nature according to our notable author. And it is well established that the theft of large things is mortal ^a^; therefore, by their nature, small thefts will be so as well. But because in every matter of mortal sin, three things excuse one from mortal guilt—namely, the smallness of the matter, the lack of deliberation, and the lack of sufficient judgment to sin mortally, as we state in the *Manual* ^b^—likewise in this matter of theft, the smallness or insignificance of the act makes it so it is not mortal, according to Saint Thomas ^c^, Antoninus ^d^, and Hadrian ^e^, who are commonly accepted. However, because it is not determined by natural, divine, or human law what the exact quantity necessary is for a loss, or a detention, or a localized seizure of something to be a mortal sin, it is commonly held—and very rightly so—that it is a "notable quantity." What constitutes a "notable" amount is left to the judgment of a good man ^f^. <note>The "judgment of a good man" (arbitrio boni viri) is a legal concept suggesting that a reasonable, upright person should decide based on the specific circumstances when the law is silent.</note>

<margin>
f Since those things which are left undefined by law are to be defined by the judgment of a good man. law 1. Digest, on the right of deliberation; and Code, on the cases regarding the office of the delegate.
g Book 5, question 2, article 3, on justice and law.
h chapter 17, number 3; chapter 27, number 9.
</margin>
¶ But † some confessors cause us great trouble by asking us what quantity should be judged as notable, or what we ourselves would judge as such. This question occurs to us—as it has occurred more than once—and it has become even more burdensome to us because they think that the Lord Doctor Soto ^g^ has determined that it is two or three ducats, provided that the great poverty of the person from whom it is taken, withheld, or stolen does not suggest that a smaller amount would suffice. However, in our opinion, he does not say this as a rule, even though he uses it as an example. For the decision on this, which we refer back to in the *Manual*, we say ^h^: First, that whoever steals a little, intending to steal much, sins mortally, as Saint Jerome feels here: because the will to act and the act itself possess the same malice, according to the accepted teaching of Saint Thomas ^i^. <note>Domingo de Soto was a highly influential Spanish Dominican theologian and contemporary of the author.</note>

<margin>
i Second part of the Second part, question 20, article 3.
k Second part of the Second part, question 66, article 6.
l in the said article 6.
</margin>
¶ Second, † that whoever steals some tiny thing, without wanting to steal anything larger, nor intending to cause the neighbor more harm than that tiny thing is worth, does not commit a mortal theft—if he reasonably believes that the owner would be pleased if he knew of it. This is true even if he knows the owner would be annoyed if told, or would not want to give it to him, as Cajetan noted ^k^. Although Saint Thomas spoke somewhat obscurely on this point ^l^. <note>Cajetan (Thomas de Vio) was a major commentator on Aquinas.</note>

<margin>
a Chapter 17, number 3.
b Word: "theft" question 5.
c Book 5, question 2, article 3, on justice and law.
d Section "In double" and "In quadruple," Institutes, on actions.
e Argument from the final chapter on injuries; Law "He who kills," Digest, on the Aquilian law.
</margin>
¶ Third, that for someone who steals a tiny thing, such as an awl from a shoemaker or a needle from a tailor—because of which, and having no other awls or needles, they stop working—mortal theft is not committed, even if notable harm is caused to the owner by it, as we pointed out in the new editions of the *Manual* ^a^; even though Sylvester ^b^ feels the opposite, with whom Soto ^c^ agrees. My reasoning is, first, because he does not steal a notable thing. Second, because whoever steals such a small thing, even if it caused ten ducats worth of damage, would not be condemned to pay double or quadruple the total damage, but only double or quadruple the value of that small stolen thing, depending on whether it was an open or hidden theft ^d^. <note>Azpilcueta is distinguishing between the "matter" of the theft (the small needle) and the "consequential damage" (the loss of work).</note>

<margin>
f Which is easily gathered from the diversity of the titles on thefts and on injuries.
</margin>
¶ Fourth, we say that the act of taking that small thing would be mortal if the one who takes it knew, or should have known or believed, that such notable damage would follow for the owner from that tiny theft. This is not because the theft itself or the intent to steal is mortal, but because of causing notable damage ^e^, which are different things ^f^.

¶ Fifth, † that the same should be said of one who steals a tiny thing, believing that the owner would receive notable anger and distress from it. For example, I know of one who stole a very large quince from his friend, which the friend had in his garden and valued greatly to show off; he had told his servants that it would cause him very notable anger (as indeed he felt afterward) if they stole it from him. I say, then, that the man did not commit a notable theft, although by having caused notable anger because of it, he could have sinned mortally.

<meta>catchword: mortally.</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the moral distinction between venial and mortal theft, focusing on the "notable quantity" required for mortal sin and how contextual harm—like stealing a craftsman's tool—is evaluated by theologians like Soto and Aquinas.</summary>
