### Regarding the final chapter. 24. q. 6.

...mortally: since he believed, or should have believed, that the owner would take such an offense.

¶ Sixth: from these two points it follows that the reason why someone who takes a small thing from a poor person sins mortally, while one who takes it from a rich person does not, is not because the one commits a mortal theft and the other does not. Rather, it is because the first gives cause for notable offense and grief, and perhaps even bodily affliction such as hunger, thirst, heat, or cold, while the other does not; or because the one has reason to believe that the person from whom he takes it would grant permission, and the other does not.

<margin>
g Law "Inter omnes." Law "Rectè." Digest, on thefts. <note>Refers to the *Pandects* or *Digest* of Justinian's Civil Law.</note>
h And therefore he does no injury, nor deceit, chapter "To one who knows and consents." On the rules of law, book 6. <note>original: "Et ideò nō facit iniuriā, neque dolum, cap. Sciẽti & confentiẽti. de regu. iur. lib. 6." This is a fundamental legal maxim: no injury is done to one who knows and consents.</note>
</margin>

¶ Seventh: from this sixth point it follows that whoever takes a little or a lot, believing with good reason that its owner would approve, does not sin, because he does not commit theft g. Consequently, neither does someone who takes something that—given the amount and the condition of the person taking it and the one from whom it is taken—it is believable that the owner will not take it badly. This is because he does not steal, nor does he harm anyone against their will h. This holds even if he is perhaps mistaken in thinking the owner would approve; until he has reason to believe otherwise, he does not sin. We say "with good reason," because if he believed so foolishly, he would sin a.

<margin>
a By the same [author]. §. "rectè." distinction in the law "inter omnes." Digest, on thefts.
</margin>

¶ Eighth: once we set aside the conjectures regarding the harm, offense, and bodily or spiritual affliction that can follow from theft; and once we set aside the conjecture that the owner would approve; and once we set aside the will to steal more if one could—there is no difference in taking a thing from one person rather than another. Therefore, it is necessary to determine if there is absolutely any specific amount for which theft is a mortal sin, and another smaller amount for which it is not.

<margin>
b chapter "Nemo" and chapter "nullus." 11. q. 3. <note>Citations from the Decretum Gratiani, a collection of Canon Law.</note>
c chapter 2, number 9.
</margin>

9 ¶ Ninth: † in some dioceses <term>constituciones fynodales</term> <note>Synodal constitutions: laws enacted by a Bishop for his specific diocese.</note> declare that no letter of excommunication should be issued for a thing worth less than one hundred <term>maravedis</term> <note>A Spanish unit of account.</note>; and in others, that it should not be issued for a thing worth less than two <term>Reales</term>. However, it is not determined by this that such an amount is what is necessary for the theft or damage to be a mortal sin. For although excommunication cannot and should not be given except for a mortal sin b, as we state in the Manual c, it is not necessary to give it for *every* mortal sin. Thus, it can be understood that although one may sin mortally for less than those two Reales or one hundred maravedis, no one is to be excommunicated for less.

<margin>
d Book 5. q. 2. article 3. On justice and law. <note>Refers to Domingo de Soto's influential work *De Iustitia et Iure*.</note>
e Law "Si oleum." and the following law. Digest, on deceit.
f Note 135.
g Exodus 22. Law "furem." Digest, on the Cornelian law regarding assassins. Chapter "si perfodiens." On homicide. <note>Legal and biblical justifications for defending property against thieves.</note>
h Law "Si ex plagis." §. "tabernari⁹." Digest, on the Aquilian law.
</margin>

¶ Tenth: some gather from what the <term>S. Doctor Soto</term> <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a major Spanish Dominican theologian.</note> said d, referred to above, that a sum that is not two or three <term>Ducados</term> <note>Ducats: high-value gold coins.</note> is not [a mortal theft] in itself, without respect to the person from whom it is stolen. Their reasoning is that no legal action is granted for deceit committed in a matter not worth more than two Ducats e; and that <term>Matheo Mathefilano</term> f <note>Mattheus de Mathesillanis, a 15th-century jurist.</note> said that what is not worth two Ducats is a "vile" <note>low-value</note> thing. For this reason, no one can kill a nighttime thief for something not worth more, even though it is generally lawful to kill such a thief g. Furthermore, a lantern seems a vile and small thing to lay hands on the person who takes it from you, even if they refuse to give it up h; and it seems reasonable to believe that stealing one Ducat from the King, or another very rich man, would not be a mortal sin.

<margin>
i Second part of the Second part. q. 66. article 6. <note>Reference to Thomas Aquinas's *Summa Theologica*.</note>
a Law 2. Code, on rescinding sales. And chapter "cùm dilecti." On purchases.
b According to the accepted doctrine of Thomas. Second part of the Second part. q. 77. art. 7.
c Finally. 4. distinction. Thomas. First part of the Second part. q. 96. art. 2.
</margin>

10 ¶ Eleventh: † it seems to us that neither did <term>S. Doctor Soto</term> intend to say this, nor is it the truth. First, because <term>Saint Thomas</term> i does not excuse theft from mortal guilt except when it is of a minimal and very small thing, and to no one in this land will one Ducat appear as such when considered in itself. Second, because that most holy and learned man [Soto] released that statement (saying that the theft of a minimal thing is not mortal) with such careful measure that he signified it does not apply when the one who takes that minimal thing intends to harm the owner in that small matter against his will—although <term>Cajetan</term> <note>Thomas Cajetan (1469–1534), a major commentator on Aquinas.</note>, by force of effort and (in our view) rightly, stretches his words to say the opposite. Third, because it is one thing to deny a legal action for deceit against one who cheats for less than two ducats, and quite another to say that he who cheats for less does not sin mortally. For the law denies action against a buyer or seller who does not cheat for more than half the "just price" a, yet they do not for that reason cease to sin mortally b. Fourth, because Matheo Mathefilano does not bring necessary proof for his statement; and even if he did, it would not stand, for it is a poor logical leap to say: "One cannot kill this person in this case (especially by private authority), therefore he does not sin mortally." Furthermore, there are many mortal sins that human law declines to punish, which divine law does punish c. Fifth, because in some dioceses it is ordained with the counsel of learned and prudent men that letters of excommunication be given for the theft of one hundred maravedis...

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that while legal thresholds for lawsuits and excommunications exist (such as the two-ducat rule), they do not define whether a theft is a mortal sin in God's eyes. He contends that even small thefts can be mortal if they cause significant distress to the victim or if the stolen amount is not truly "minimal."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the monetary thresholds that distinguish venial theft from mortal sin, specifically regarding the imposition of excommunication. It elaborates on the "notable quantity" required for a sin to be considered mortal in the eyes of the Church and the law.</meta>

-># Resolutory Commentary on notable theft.<-

<margin>On chapter 17, number 9, following Paludanus <note>Petrus de Palude (c. 1275–1342), a prominent theologian and canonist.</note> on the 4th book [of the Sentences], distinction 18, question 1, article 1. In favor of this are the chapters "Nullus" and "Nemo," 11, question 3. Which we also extended in the chapter "Inter verba," same cause and question, number 480.</margin>
maravedis <note>A small Spanish copper coin and unit of account.</note>, and for that of two reales, which here are sixty-eight maravedis: and as we stated in the *Manual* <sup>d</sup>, the general major excommunication does not bind except for mortal sin.

<margin>11</margin>
¶ The twelfth† point is that (saving a better opinion) to anyone who confesses to me or seeks my advice, I would say these things. The first: that he should consider as a **notable quantity**—for the purpose of the taking or retention being a mortal sin—the sum of one hundred maravedis, or even fifty, or even thirty, or twenty. And he should not consider as such anything less than eight maravedis in this land, nor in any other where there is as much money as there is here; although I would order him to make restitution for anything from three maravedis upward. The second: I am more inclined to say that even eight maravedis and above is notable, although I would not condemn a somewhat learned penitent who felt the contrary. The third: I would tell him to consider as "notable" a kid [goat], a capon, and even a reasonable hen, even in a land where it is not worth twenty maravedis, nor even half a real; and even the taking of a dozen eggs. I would tell him the same regarding a *celemin* <note>A dry measure for grain, roughly 4.6 liters.</note> of wheat, and a *quarta* <note>A liquid measure, the fourth part of a *cántara* (pitcher).</note> of wine. Firstly, because any of these things is considered (at least commonly, and by most people) to be more than minimal. Secondly, because in the *repetición* <note>A formal academic lecture or dissertation defense.</note> that we performed on this very chapter without touching on any of this, in order to become a doctor for the second time in this most distinguished **University of Salamanca**—which, due to its greatness and authority, does not wish to incorporate doctors from other universities into its faculty rights, even though it had already granted us its Chair of Decretals with great honor. Although we defended that a legal action for theft (or any other) should not be granted for the taking or retention of such a small thing that it does not suffice to constitute mortal injustice, we held that it could be granted for the taking and retention of a hen. <margin>a Which is irrefragably proven to us by the section "Of Hens" in the *Institutes* on the division of things. <note>original: "Quod nobis irrefragabiliter probat. §. Gallinaru Instit. de rer. diuisio." A reference to Justinian's legal code.</note></margin> <sup>a</sup> Furthermore, because we have heard many prudent men praise a Christian King for having soldiers hanged who stole a pair of hens in his camp, and an infidel King for executing his soldiers for things even smaller than those. Although one could respond that the laws of war and the necessity of providing armies with victuals require this rigor, one could also reply that Christian meekness seems to conflict with laws that take a person's <margin>b in chapter 2, number 60 and following.</margin> life for something that is not, nor is presumed to be, a mortal sin, as was touched upon in the *Manual*. <sup>b</sup>

<margin>12</margin>
¶ The † fourth thing I would tell him is that stealing something of lesser quantity is a mortal sin when it causes damage of as much or more quantity than those mentioned: such as the theft of a needle, or an awl, or another tool, through the lack of which a tailor, a shoemaker, or another official loses as much daily wages as the quantities mentioned above. But it should be noted that if excommunication were given for the theft alone, and did not include other damages, the one who committed that theft would not be excommunicated. This is because, as we said above, the theft of a tool of such little value is one thing, and the damage caused by its taking or retention is another. For if he were condemned to pay double or fourfold for the theft (depending on whether it was manifest or non-manifest), the damage would not be doubled, but only the value of the stolen tool according to the mind of everyone <sup>c</sup>, <margin>c In the sections "In duplum" and "quadrupli." *Institutes* on actions.</margin> as was said above.

¶ The fifth thing I would tell him is that the general excommunication against those who steal, or do not restore what was ill-gotten, includes all those who take or retain unjustly a quantity sufficient to sin mortally. This is because only the words and the mind of the one who pronounces it are included, which is to pull souls out of mortal sin, or to ensure they do not fall into it <sup>d</sup>: <margin>d Argument from chapter 1 of "On the sentence of excommunication," Book 6; and chapter 2 of "On constitutions," in the same book.</margin> unless by the constitutions of the excommunicator, or by some other way, those who do not take up to a larger quantity are excluded. For if they are excluded, they will not be included, because excommunication does not bind more than whom and as many as the one who excommunicates (or the one who requests it) <margin>e chapter 27, number 11.</margin> intends, as we said in the *Manual* <sup>e</sup>. Regarding someone who very small things, many times <margin>f chapter 17, numbers 139 and 140.</margin> takes from his master, or from another, or from many, it was discussed in the *Manual*. <sup>f</sup>

### SUMMARY.

**A person is irregular if they accidentally kill another while performing an illicit act, or a licit act illicitly, number 13. Which is very well proven, whatever some may say, number 15.**

Let us now apply this text, and the aforementioned question of irregularity, which in this edition of the revised *Manual* <insert>a</insert> was mistakenly referred to the commentary on this chapter, though it should have been referred to another, <insert>b</insert> from whence we refer it here <insert>c</insert>.
¶ The question is whether a notable limitation by Lord Doctor Soto <insert>d</insert> <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a prominent Spanish Dominican theologian and contemporary of the author.</note> is true. For this, one must always keep in mind that affirmative rule regarding homicide which we placed in the *Manual* <insert>e</insert>: namely, that every accidental homicide—which is that which happens by chance, without the will for it—that follows from an illicit work, or from a licit work done illicitly, causes irregularity <insert>f</insert>.

¶ Lord Doctor Soto limits this, saying it only applies when the work, or the manner from which the homicide follows, is illicit by being of its own nature dangerous to life or causing mutilation, and for this reason forbidden, and not in other cases that are illicit for other reasons. Against which, and its foundations, the following points are made. First, that both the glosses <insert>g</insert> and Saint Thomas <insert>h</insert>, as well as all others without distinction, say that he who does any illicit work, or a licit one illicitly, is irregular if death or mutilation follows from it. Second, that Silvester <insert>a</insert> specifies that the cleric who kills by accident while engaging in trade—which is forbidden to him—is irregular, even though a layman to whom the same thing happened would not be. And he who, while cutting another’s tree, kills by accident, is irregular even if he uses as much diligence as would suffice to not be irregular if the tree were his own <insert>b</insert>. Even Cajetan <note>Thomas Cajetan (1469–1534), a leading theologian known for his commentaries on Thomas Aquinas.</note> (if one weighs it well) says that the cleric who, while hunting, uses as much diligence as a layman so that no deformity <note>In this context, a physical disfigurement or serious injury.</note> occurs, does not commit a greater sin of homicide than the layman, yet he incurs irregularity. It is clear that cutting a tree is not forbidden to a cleric, nor is trading forbidden because it is dangerous to life, but for other reasons; nor is the hunting of hawks, hares, and rabbits—which the law <insert>c</insert> forbids for pleasure, though not for recreation—forbidden for that reason. Third, there is no text that effectively proves this.

<margin>
e Which Johannes Andreae, Panormitanus <note>Niccolò de' Tudeschi (1386–1445), a famous canonist known as "Panormitanus."</note>, Arania, and Felinus prove.
f If, however, he did it for the sake of piety and not greed, and was skilled in the practice of surgery and applied all the diligence he ought to have used, he is not to be so strongly condemned for what happened through the woman’s fault against his advice, so that mercy cannot be shown to him after suitable satisfaction, enabling him to celebrate divine offices. Otherwise, the execution of the priestly order must be forbidden to him by the rigor of the law.

...foundation, he takes for certain what is so uncertain that common opinion holds the contrary—and even our own weak opinion does so with reason. This is because he presupposes that the text contains the idea that a monk who, practicing surgery out of piety alone, treats and opens a wound from which the patient dies (because the patient exposed themselves to the wind against the monk's orders), is not irregular; but if he treats for profit, he is. The text proves the opposite of this according to the common understanding ^e^, which says that in both cases he is irregular, although in one he is dispensed more easily than in the other. And they do not hold this without reason, because the text clearly says that the monk sinned in using a forbidden office, as stated in those words: <note>original: "Licet ipse monachus multum deliquerit, officium alienum usurpando, quod sibi minimè congruebat."</note> "Although the monk himself sinned greatly by usurping another's office, which was not at all fitting for him." And it also clearly says that if other factors concurred—namely, that he treated out of piety and not greed, that he knew the art well, and that he applied all due diligence—he should not be so condemned that mercy could not be shown to him, allowing him to celebrate after suitable satisfaction. This is to say that he had need of a merciful dispensation. For these are the words of the text ^f^.

a In the chapter *Sententiam*, "that clerics or monks [should not practice surgery]," where Panormitanus at number 11 affirms this, and Silvester under the word *Physician*.

¶ Fifthly: that if his induction were good, his limitation would be false. This is because his limitation holds that a person is irregular if they perform an illicit act that is forbidden to them because it is dangerous and likely to kill someone, should death or mutilation follow. And it is clear that the monk (of whom that text speaks) performed an illicit act, one forbidden to him as dangerous for causing death, since he practiced surgery by cutting, opening, or burning flesh. This is forbidden not only to monks but even to all those in holy orders, for being dangerous enough to cause death, as Panormitanus ^a^ and the Common Opinion hold. In such a way, if the premise of his induction were true, his limitation would be false; and if his limitation is true, his induction presupposes a falsehood.

¶ Sixthly ^†^: that he bases his argument on the idea that if an adulterer, being found by the husband with the wife, kills the husband to defend himself, he is not irregular. This is the opposite of what we held in the *Manual* ^b^, and what Saint Antoninus held before ^c^. This is proved by his own limitation: because adultery is an illicit act and dangerous for causing death, given the inclination of men and the custom of killing adulterers and their wives; and even considering the laws that do not punish fathers and husbands for it in certain cases ^d^. His limitation holds that accidental irregularity following an illicit and dangerous act makes the killer irregular. Seventhly: that he bases his argument on saying there is no text for the aforementioned general rule of the doctors (who say a man becomes irregular for any accidental death following an illicit act) except for two ^f^, which suggest it by saying that death following a licit act, licitly performed, does not cause irregularity. This foundation is weak: because the argument they call <term>a contrario sensu</term> <note>An argument "from the opposite sense," meaning that if the law defines one scenario, the opposite scenario is governed by the opposite rule.</note>, although weak in logic, is very strong in law ^g^, provided it is not taken to prove something whose contrary is already expressed ^h^. Since the contrary of the said rule is not found expressed, nor does he provide any other solution, it follows that the rule can be proven by it.

l Neither by will nor by act did he commit homicide, nor did he perform an illicit act.

For one of them ^k^ says that the chaplain spoken of was not irregular because ^l^ neither by will nor by deed did he commit homicide, nor did it happen to him while performing an illicit act. And the other ^m^ excuses one from irregularity because ^n^ he was not negligent in his work. Ninthly: that there are many texts which generally set down the rule that whoever "deforms"—that is, kills or cuts off a limb of a man, or is the cause of it—is irregular. This applies whether he does this in peace, in war, by throwing a stone, or by whipping a student carelessly ^o^. These texts set down such a general rule that they include even those who kill or are the cause of death justly and holily, directly or indirectly, as has been discussed at length...

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that clerics and monks who cause accidental death while performing forbidden acts, such as surgery or adultery, are legally "irregular." He critiques more lenient interpretations by emphasizing that the illicit nature of the initial act creates liability for any resulting death.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal and moral analysis of clerical "irregularity"—a status that prevents a member of the clergy from performing their duties. The author, Martín de Azpilcueta, is specifically examining how much "fault" or "guilt" must be present for an accidental death to result in this legal penalty.</meta>

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>On the final Chapter, 14th Question, 6th Section.</header>

<margin>
a Chapter 27, from number 206.
b Second part of the Second Part, question 64, article 8. <note>A reference to the *Summa Theologica* of St. Thomas Aquinas.</note>
c The Gloss on the law *Omnis definitio* [All definitions] in the Digest, "On the rules of law," and in the title "On the rules of law" in Book 6; accepted by all.
</margin>
We discuss this at length in the *Manual* <gloss>a</gloss>. And many doctors (among whom was Saint Thomas <gloss>b</gloss>) were of the opinion that even for a homicide that one commits for his own unavoidable defense, one became <term>irregular<note>In canon law, "irregularity" is a type of impediment that forbids a person from being ordained or, if already ordained, from performing the duties of their office (such as saying Mass). It is often triggered by involvement in a death.</note></term>. And it is certain that we must not depart from the rules of law except by express law, or a very necessary reason founded upon it <gloss>c</gloss>. And since the law does not exempt one from this irregularity except for the person who kills by accident without fault, or who is the cause of death while acting lawfully, or who kills on purpose out of necessary defense: the rule of the doctors remains proven, that an accidental death following a culpable act causes irregularity.

<margin>
17
d The law *si creditor* [if a creditor] regarding "those things which are fortuitous" in the Code of Justinian, on pledges; and the first law on commodities; and the final chapter on deposits; and "From which things they are null," the final chapter on homicide in Book 6.
e Petrus de Ancharano in the Perusian gloss; in the penultimate gloss of the final chapter on homicide in Book 6.
f The first chapter on commodities; the final chapter on deposits.
</margin>
¶ Because of these† foundations, I would not dare to hold the aforementioned limitation if it is (as he says) contrary to the opinion of the canonist doctors. Although it seems right to us if, and insofar as, it agrees with another opinion that they hold, and some even express it quite clearly <gloss>f</gloss>: namely, that accidental homicide does not make one irregular if he did not have a fault that was ordered and directed toward that end, either by its nature or by the intention of the guilty party. For example: I beg my companion to go with me toward the church, and I go there with some mortally sinful intention—to see, hear, speak, or do shameful things. While we are going there, a tile falls from the roof and kills my companion. I shall not be irregular. For although that death followed from what I begged him to do, and I sinned in begging him and in going there with him for that mortally sinful end, yet that malice and fault of mine was not ordered nor directed toward that death, neither by the nature of the act nor by the intention of the worker (which was me). I committed the sin in begging and in insisting that he go to where he died. This limitation and conclusion is supported, first, by the fact that no one deserves harm or punishment for what happens by accident <gloss>d</gloss>. Even if a fault precedes it, it does not count if the fault is not ordered or directed toward that accident, as some specifically say when speaking on this same matter <gloss>e</gloss>. Second, if I lend you my mule to go from here to Toro, even if you go there on it with some mortally sinful intention to say, hear, or do some mortal sin there, and on the road a bolt of lightning, a rabid dog, or another fortuitous thing kills the mule: you shall not be obliged to pay me for it. This is because although a person who borrows something is held responsible if it is lost—even if lost by a fortuitous event—when a fault preceded the accident <gloss>f</gloss>, and in this proposed case your fault preceded the accident, yet because your fault was not directed toward that loss by its nature or your intention, it should not harm you according to the mind and custom of all <gloss>a</gloss>.

<margin>
18
a In the two said chapters.
b In the said penultimate Gloss of the final chapter on homicide in Book 6.
c In the final chapter on homicide in Book 6.
</margin>
Third, Pedro† de Ancharano and Philippus Francus expressly decide this by commenting on a gloss. They say that when a gloss states that what happens by accident causes harm when a fault precedes the accident, it must be understood to mean when the fault is ordered and directed toward it; and the gloss is speaking on this very matter of irregularity. Fourth, the reasoning of an answer from Boniface VIII <gloss>c</gloss> seems clearly to prove this. For after he said that he who commands another to be struck, with the protestation that he should not be killed, is irregular if the agent kills the person, he gives as his reason that he was at fault in commanding it and should have thought that such a thing could happen. By this, he signifies that even though he was at fault in commanding it, if he had not been expected to think that death could result from his command, he would not have been irregular. From this, it can be gathered that if one were to send a servant from here to Medina to say or do some mortally sinful purchase or sale, and on the road a bolt of lightning, a lion, or a thief were to kill him, the sender would not be irregular. Because although he was at fault in sending him for a evil deed, he was not obligated to think that such an accident could happen from that message so as to refrain from sending him.

<margin>
19
d Under the word "Homicide," distinction 1, question 7, verse "Fourth."
e To the contrary, the law *Non aliter* [Not otherwise] in the Digest, on legacies 3; and the chapter *Praeterea* [Furthermore] on the meaning of words.
</margin>
Fifth†, the force of this fourth foundation will not be hindered by the person who says that Sylvester <gloss>d</gloss> says Boniface gave two reasons for his ruling there: first, because he was at fault in commanding it (which applies to every culpable command), and second, that he should have thought that it could happen (which applies to a lawful command). This argument fails, first, because his declaration is against the gloss, which, although he reprehends it, everyone commonly follows. Second, because it does violence to the text, turning the copulative "And" into the disjunctive "Or" <gloss>e</gloss> <note>original: "haziendo de la copulatiua, Et disiunctiua vel." Azpilcueta is arguing that the two conditions must be met together, rather than being alternatives.</note>. Sixth, we are moved much by the fact that Boniface's decision would be superfluous and a doubt where none existed if this limitation were not true. For if any fault at all by the person to whom it happens were enough to make them irregular for accidental homicide, it would be more than enough for it to happen through the fault of commanding someone to be struck (which is so close to killing); therefore, there would have been no reason to make that decree.

<meta>This page continues the legal and theological debate regarding "irregularity"—the clerical status that bars a priest from performing his duties. <page-num>106</page-num>
# Resolutory Commentary on Notable Theft.

<margin>f in the chapter *Ad audientiam* [To the audience], on homicide.</margin>
<margin>20</margin>
Seventhly, in our view, this was the intent of that most wise Innocent <gloss>f</gloss> <note>Pope Innocent IV (d. 1254), a major legal authority in the Middle Ages.</note> in the examples he provided for his solemn statement†—that it is not only the one who advises someone to kill who becomes <term>irregular<note>In canon law, "irregularity" is an impediment that prevents a cleric from serving in his office or being promoted to higher orders, often triggered by being the cause of a death.</note></term>, but even the one who advises someone to do something where death is foreseen. For he gives the example of one who advises someone to discharge a piece of artillery, or to strike another, or to go and take a castle by force, which likely cannot be taken without the deaths of men. However, he is very doubtful regarding the one who advises taking a castle through treason, because many times it is taken without deaths. If any fault at all—whether directed toward death or not—were enough to trigger this penalty, he would not have doubted the fourth example, nor would he have focused the first three on faults so closely tied to killing. Nor would he have said, in explaining why the penalty is incurred in those three cases, that what was commanded was "close to killing." Eighthly, this same view was held by Hostiensis, Johannes Andreae, and the common opinion. Along with Panormitanus <note>Nicolaus de Tudeschis, also known as Panormitanus, a famous 15th-century canon lawyer.</note>, he says the reason Innocent inclines toward saying the penalty is incurred even in the fourth case (treason) is that, although it is not as certain that deaths are foreseen in taking a castle by treason as by force, it often follows nonetheless. The common opinion <gloss>a</gloss> feels the same way when saying <margin>a In the said chapter *Ad audientiam*, on homicide.</margin> that the reason it concludes that someone who advises another to kill, and the other kills him, becomes irregular, is that he should have considered that such a result could follow from it, as Panormitanus <gloss>b</gloss> well declares. Ninthly, † it must be noted that if we held the opposite view and followed those <margin>21</margin> <margin>b In the said chapter *Ad audientiam*.</margin> who believe that even a venial sin is enough for this, <gloss>c</gloss> then nearly anyone who <margin>c Among whom is Cajetan, in the *Summa*, 2nd part of the 2nd part, question 94, article 8.</margin> spoke, acted, advised, or commanded anything would become irregular if a death subsequently occurred. This is because commonly (as we have said elsewhere <gloss>d</gloss>), although it is a Lutheran heresy <gloss>e</gloss> <margin>d In the chapter *Inter*, verse 11, question 3, number 464.</margin> to say that all our works are sins—at least venial ones—human misery is such <margin>e Which Alfonso de Castro, an excellent and most serious continuing writer, proves after the martyr (glorious to me) John Rochester, a magnanimous prelate. The former in his book *Against all heresies*, under the word "Works"; the latter in the *Assertion of Luther*, article 31. <note>John Fisher, Bishop of Rochester, was executed by Henry VIII and is here praised as a martyr for the Catholic faith.</note></margin> that I fear most of what we do is sinful. This is either because of the nature of the acts themselves, or because they lack some circumstance of time, place, person, manner, end, or others—all of which must concur for a work to be good. <gloss>f</gloss> If it is not good, it will necessarily be bad, at least venially. For there is no human work done with deliberation that, in its individual instance (which the jurists call the "species"), is indifferent—that is, neither good nor bad. <gloss>g</gloss> Consequently, almost all accidental homicides would happen to those committing venial sins. Tenthly, if what we claim were not true, it would follow that all those who go to see jousts, tournaments, cane games, or bullfights out of <margin>f Dionysius, Book 4, *On Divine Names*; Thomas, 1st part of the 2nd part, question 18, article 11.</margin> mortal malice or venial vanities—who, in our opinion, are the majority—and take with them wives, children, servants, friends, or other persons, would become irregular if any of them died or lost a limb from horse falls, wounds from spears, splinters, staffs, reeds, horse collisions, sword cuts, or other such things that might happen by chance. This would fill the world with irregular bishops, prelates, other high-ranking ecclesiastics, and honorable laymen. Finally, consider that beautiful division and solution which, in the new editions of this review, we added to the *Manual*, regarding the three types of deformation or homicide: entirely voluntary, entirely accidental, and mixed. We define "merely accidental" as that which is not directly intended in itself and follows from something that is in no way ordered toward it—such as the death caused by a lightning bolt striking someone who is sent somewhere, without any thought of harm. Regarding this, we stated absolutely there that it does not cause irregularity, and now we have proven it at length. <margin>g Thomas, 2nd part of the 2nd part, question 18, article 9.</margin>

<margin>h in chapter 27, number 220.</margin>
¶ † From all this, for further clarification, we infer these conclusions. First: that the Lord Doctor Soto <note>Domingo de Soto (1494–1560), a Spanish Dominican priest and influential theologian at the School of Salamanca.</note> spoke very well <margin>22</margin> in saying that Sylvester <note>Sylvester Prierias, a Dominican theologian who famously opposed Luther.</note> was not correct in saying that a cleric who, while cutting someone else's tree, kills someone by accident, incurs irregularity—even if he used as much diligence to avoid killing as would be enough to avoid the penalty if he were cutting his own tree. This is because the fault of cutting someone else's tree is not directed toward the death that occurred.
¶ Second: that the same doctor also spoke well in saying that a monk is not irregular if, while ringing a bell during a forbidden time, the clapper kills someone—provided he uses as much diligence as would suffice to avoid it if he were ringing it during a permitted time.
¶ Third: that Sylvester was not correct in saying that a cleric would be irregular for an accidental homicide occurring while he was engaged in prohibited trade, for which he would not be irregular if the trade were lawful; provided that the trade was such that, neither by its nature nor by the inten—

<meta>catchword: the inten—</meta>

<summary>Azpilcueta argues that clerical irregularity for accidental homicide should only apply if the initial act was inherently dangerous or aimed at violence, rather than merely being a minor sin or a prohibited activity like unsanctioned trade.</summary>

->*Non in inferenda <note>original: "Non in inferenda" [Not in inflicting], a specific legal heading within the Decretum Gratiani</note>. 23. quest. 3. And the final Chap-*<-
->*ter. 13. q. final.*<-

->To the very high, and very powerful lord Don Car-<-
->los, Prince of Castile, and of many<-
->other and very great Kingdoms<-
->our Lord.<-
<note>Don Carlos (1545–1568) was the eldest son of King Philip II of Spain and the heir apparent until his early death.</note>

->For a more complete explanation of what has been treated<-
->in his Manual for Con-<-
->fessors.<-
<note>Azpilcueta’s "Manual de Confesores y Penitentes" was one of the most influential works of the 16th century, used by priests to determine the severity of sins during confession.</note>

->Printed in Antwerp, at the house of the hei-<-
->rs of Juan Steels.<-
->1568.<-
<note>Antwerp (Anueres) was a major center for Spanish book production in the 16th century due to its advanced printing presses and its status within the Spanish Netherlands.</note>

<summary>This is the title page of Martín de Azpilcueta’s commentary on usury and canon law, dedicated to Prince Don Carlos of Spain. It serves as an expansion of his famous "Manual for Confessors," providing deeper legal resolutions on economic and ecclesiastical sins.</summary>
